# Heidegger, *Metaphysische Anfangsgründe der Logik im Ausgang von Leibniz* (GA 26): A Commentary

Source: Martin Heidegger, *Metaphysische Anfangsgründe der Logik im Ausgang von Leibniz*, Marburg lecture course, summer semester 1928, ed. Klaus Held, Gesamtausgabe vol. 26 (Frankfurt am Main: Klostermann, 1978). The commentary covers the main part of the course, from the Preliminary Remark (p. 33) through the appendix "Distance and Nearness" (p. 286); the Introduction (pp. 1–32) and the editor's afterword are outside its range. References are to the printed GA 26 pagination. English renderings of Heidegger's German and of the Latin he quotes are the commentator's own.

Several misreadings beset this course, and the commentary holds against them throughout. The "destruction" of Leibniz's doctrine of judgment is not a refutation or a demolition; it is a return through the doctrine to the metaphysical presuppositions that make it possible, and it treats Leibniz as a thinker who saw more than his logic could house. Heidegger does not side with the "logicist" Leibniz of Russell and Couturat, nor simply with their critics: he inverts the question, arguing that logic rests on metaphysics, and that metaphysics itself must be grounded anew in the analysis of Dasein. Transcendence is not the subject–object relation of epistemology and is not a synonym for intentionality; it is Dasein's surpassing of beings toward world, and it is what makes intentional comportment possible. The neutrality of Dasein is not an abstraction toward a bloodless "consciousness in general," and fundamental ontology is not philosophical anthropology. Freedom, in the second Main Part, is not a faculty of the will or a kind of causality; it is the origin of grounding as such. World is not the sum of what is present at hand. The *nihil originarium* of temporality is not the negation of beings and has nothing to do with nihilism. "Metontology" does not replace fundamental ontology with an ontic metaphysics; it names the turn by which ontology, brought to completion, turns back onto beings as a whole. Finally, Leibniz's "drive" (*Drang*) is an ontological determination of the being of beings, not a psychological urge.

Heidegger's own divisions are reproduced as headings. The title of the main part is set at H2, the Preliminary Remark and the two Main Parts at H3, the Sections of the Second Main Part at H4, and the numbered §§ at the next level down (H4 in the First Main Part, H5 in the Second); lettered subsections and the inserted headings ("In memoriam Max Scheler," "Summarizing Recapitulation," the appendix) sit one level below their host §; the closing appendix "Distance and Nearness," which follows the Second Main Part, is set at H3. Dasein, Existenz, and Leibniz's Latin terms of art (*inesse*, *veritas rationis*, *principium rationis*) are retained; other German terms are rendered in English with the original in parentheses where it matters.

## Metaphysical Foundations of Logic

### Preliminary Remark

Heidegger opens the main part by recalling that "metaphysics," as the Introduction established, unites ontology and theology. The title began as a librarian's label: the treatises placed "after the physics" (*meta ta physika*). Because those treatises had a subject matter of their own, the positional label hardened into a description of content, with *meta* now read as "beyond" and *physika* as beings of every kind. Metaphysics becomes the study of what "lies beyond" beings, though where and how it lies beyond is left unsaid. That beyond has two aspects: being as such, and beings as a whole. Heidegger compresses the history into one image: "the one-after-another of books becomes a one-above-another, an order of being and beings" (p. 33). The remark fixes the double sense of metaphysics that governs the whole course. The later distinction between fundamental ontology and "metontology" (pp. 196–202) is this duality of being as such and beings as a whole, reworked from the ground of Dasein.

### First Main Part: Destruction of Leibniz's Doctrine of Judgment with Respect to the Metaphysical Basic Problems

Heidegger denies that an account of Leibniz's theory of judgment is a piece of historical information about what one earlier philosopher taught. It is "a concrete path of reflection on what makes thinking possible at all" (p. 35). The historical material serves a systematic question, and the reader is told what to watch for: how Leibniz brings thinking into view, which basic structures he fixes, how far he grounds them, and what gives this characterization of thinking its footing. The last of these points already names the target. The destruction is aimed at the ground on which Leibniz's logic stands, not at its internal consistency.

Thinking is always thinking about objects, and so about beings. As "thinking about," it stands in relation to beings, and the question is how thinking and beings relate. Heidegger's answer relocates the problem: "Thinking is itself a comportment of Dasein and thus a way to be" (p. 35), namely the way a being (Dasein) is toward other beings. It follows that when thinking becomes a theme, relations of being become a theme. Logic, which takes thinking as its object, is therefore already a regional ontology whether it knows it or not. This thesis inverts the modern procedure in the next paragraph and anticipates the claim of the Second Main Part that intentional comportment presupposes transcendence.

Heidegger asks where the relation between thinking and being should be determined from, and what comes first. For Descartes, Spinoza, and Leibniz, thinking is what is nearest. If being comes after thinking, then beings must be thoroughly and in principle such that they can be determined in thought without remainder. Under this "postulate of thoroughgoing intelligibility," the conditions of truth become the presuppositions of being. Heidegger cites Leibniz's marginal note, "when God calculates and exercises his thought, the world comes to be" (*Cum Deus calculat et cogitationem exercet, fit mundus*; p. 35), which Couturat used as the motto of *La Logique de Leibniz*. The dictum makes the order explicit: one determines beings as such, and so being, from the essence of thinking and from what belongs to anything thought at all. The path runs from logic to ontology. Heidegger's point is that rationalism does not dispense with ontology; it decides ontology in advance by taking the thinkable as the measure of what can be.

The opposite possibility is that logic rests on ontology. The history of logic has been shaped largely by this dependence; that Aristotle's logic is grounded in his metaphysics is a commonplace. Modern rationalism, ruled by the *cogito sum*, sets itself the task of freeing logic from this bond. Heidegger places Leibniz within that effort, while the preceding paragraph has shown that the freeing is itself a metaphysical decision, the priority of thinking over being.

How logic and metaphysics relate in Leibniz is disputed. The notes identify the parties: Mahnke's study of Leibniz's synthesis of universal mathematics and individual metaphysics on one side, and on the other Russell and Couturat, for whom logic is the foundation of Leibniz's metaphysics. Heidegger calls Couturat's book "probably the most important on Leibniz at all" and adds that the attempted counterproof "has so far not succeeded" (p. 36, n. 3). He concedes the logicist reading its textual strength and then sets the alternative aside: disciplines cannot be treated as fixed labels, since it may be that neither logic nor metaphysics has been adequately determined. The relation between them must be decided from the problems and subject matters themselves. The controversy will only be touched on; the task is to present Leibniz's doctrine of judgment and its dimensions. This is the course's method in miniature. The quarrel over whether logic or metaphysics comes first is idle until both have been rethought from their common ground.

Heidegger defines the inquiry as a question about the dimensions of judgment, the contexts on whose basis anything like judgment is possible. Judgment has an internal structure; as determining assertion it aims at knowledge and truth; as a comportment it stands under certain principles; and as knowledge of beings it relates to beings as such. These four aspects yield a seven-step plan: the general structure of judgment (the inclusion theory); judgment and the idea of truth, with its two basic forms (truths of reason and truths of fact); truth and the principles of knowledge (identity, contradiction, sufficient reason, together with the *mathesis universalis*); the idea of knowledge as such (intuition); the essential determination of the being of genuine beings (the monad); the basic conception of being as such (essence and the *conatus existentiae*); and finally the relation of the theory of judgment to the conception of being, of logic to ontology. The plan moves from the surface structure of the proposition inward, to the kind of being that must be presupposed if propositions are to be true in the way Leibniz says they are. The sixth step was never carried out; the table of contents marks §6 "not executed," and §7 draws the conclusions directly from the analysis of the monad. A note lists the modern logic textbooks (Port-Royal, Kant's *Logic*, Sigwart, Lotze, Schuppe, Bergmann, Husserl's *Logical Investigations*, Pfänder) as the landscape against which the destruction is carried out.

#### §1. Characterization of the General Structure of Judgment

Heidegger situates Leibniz within the tradition. Current research increasingly shows how modern philosophy depends on medieval scholasticism and, through it, on antiquity, especially Aristotle. Leibniz's theory of judgment should therefore be expected to be shaped by tradition rather than wholly new. In his youth Leibniz studied scholasticism closely, above all Francisco Suárez, whose *Disputationes metaphysicae* (1597) systematized traditional metaphysics and strongly influenced Descartes and Protestant scholasticism; the note cites Leibniz's 1663 dissertation *De principio individui*. Leibniz also read Aristotle directly. The genealogy prepares a central claim of §2: the ideal of knowledge that drives Leibniz's logic is the scholastic doctrine of divine knowledge.

Following Aristotle, who saw in the *logos* an interweaving (*symplokē*, *nexus*, *connexio*) or a synthesis, the tradition defined judgment as *compositio* or *divisio*. Thomas speaks of the operation of the intellect "by which it composes or divides, affirming and denying" (*De veritate*, q. 14, a. 1). In its broadest formal sense, judgment is a relation between representations, a relationship of concepts. These formulas sound alike and appear to say the same thing, yet they conceal considerable vagueness and disagreement. Heidegger enlists Kant's complaint (B 140–41) that the logicians' definition of judgment as "the representation of a relation between two concepts" never satisfied him, because it leaves undetermined what the relation consists in. The theories remain disputed, and the root defect is that the conditions of the problem have not been clarified. The agreement of the formulas is thus superficial, and their shared failure is the absence of any account of the kind of being the "relation" has.

The subject–predicate relation is a *legein ti kata tinos*, saying something about something. What it is about is what underlies (*hypokeimenon*, *subjectum*); the predicate is what is said about it. Heidegger cites Aristotle's *De interpretatione* (3, 16b 10 f.): the verb is "always a sign of what is said of another, that is, of what is said of an underlying thing or is in an underlying thing." From this follows what looks like a trivial consideration: whatever can rightly, truly, be said of something must belong to the subject (*esse de ratione subiecti*), which is to say, must be contained in it (*hyparchein autō*). The whole Leibnizian doctrine will unfold from this apparent triviality. What makes it non-trivial is the double sense of "belonging" it slides over: the predicate may belong to the thing, or its concept may belong to the concept of the thing.

As soon as one asks what "subject" means, the consideration becomes ambiguous. In "the blackboard is black," is the subject the blackboard itself, the representation of it, the represented content, or the meaning of the word "blackboard"? The same question applies to "black" and its being contained in the subject: is this a containment of representations, of concepts, or of determinations of things? Is the containment thing-like, representational, or conceptual? Do the different containment relations correspond to one another, and if so, is the correspondence one of mirroring, depiction, or coordination? Heidegger multiplies the alternatives to show that the inclusion formula has no determinate sense until one says which kind of entity is doing the including. Every answer to that question is an ontological answer.

With a second pair of examples, "the blackboard is black" and "the chalk falls from the table," Heidegger presses the point. How are blackness and falling-from-the-table "contained" in their subjects, and what does "contained in" mean? He then asks whether any of this is actually said in the assertion, or whether talk of containment is merely a theory, an attempt to explain the judgment. The chalk example matters: an event does not lie inside a thing the way a property might seem to, so the inclusion model strains most visibly against what is contingent and temporal. That is exactly the difficulty §2 will locate in Leibniz's truths of fact.

Leibniz's conception of the *logos* moves in the direction of such a containment theory. It is an inclusion theory. If the label is more than external, the theory must develop a definite conception of containment itself, of *inesse*, and so of *esse*, of being. Leibniz does give the inclusion theory a specific form and grounding. Heidegger's inference from *in-esse* to *esse* is the hinge of the whole Main Part: a theory of how the predicate "is in" the subject is already a theory of being, and the destruction consists in making that theory explicit.

Heidegger introduces five pieces of textual evidence, beginning with §8 of the *Discourse on Metaphysics* (1686; first published in 1846), which he calls a principal text for the problem. Leibniz grants that an individual substance is a subject of which many predicates are said but which is itself predicated of nothing else, yet calls this a merely nominal definition. One must consider what it means for a predicate to belong truly to a subject. Every true assertion has "some foundation in the nature of things"; hence if a proposition is not identical, that is, if the predicate is not explicitly contained in the subject, it must be contained virtually. Philosophers call this *in-esse*. The term for the subject must therefore always include that of the predicate, so that whoever understood the subject's concept perfectly would judge immediately that the predicate belongs to it. The passage joins three claims that the rest of the course will pull apart: substance is defined from predication; truth is defined as containment; and the ideal knower sees all predicates in the subject at a glance.

The passage concerns the concept of individual substance, the monad, which Leibniz explicitly derives from Aristotle's definition of first substance (*prōtē ousia*, the *tode ti*). Heidegger cites *Categories* 5 (2a 11 ff.): substance in the most proper, primary, and highest sense is what is neither said of an underlying subject nor in an underlying subject, such as this man or this horse. He glosses Aristotle's *ousia* as "what is present in its own right, self-standing presence" (p. 41), a reading that anticipates the analysis of being as presence later in the course. On "in an underlying subject" he cites *Categories* 2 (1a 24 f.): what is in something not as a part and cannot exist apart from what it is in, that is, what is essentially dependent. *Metaphysics* Z 13 (1038b 8 ff.) confirms the point: no universal can be substance, because first substance is proper to each thing and belongs to nothing else, whereas the universal is common and belongs by nature to many. Heidegger's gloss on *ousia* as presence is the decisive addition. It indicates that the Aristotelian definition Leibniz inherits is already an interpretation of being as constant presence, which the Second Main Part will trace to its temporal root.

In the *Discourse*, Leibniz interprets individual substance through saying. The individual substance is essentially not sayable of anything underlying it; it is essentially self-standing and never a predicate. Leibniz sees that this interpretation of substance is oriented by predication, and he draws the conclusion that a radical determination of predication must yield an original concept of substance, namely that of the *subjectum*. In the same breath he states that in every true proposition the subject contains the predicate, explicitly or implicitly. Where the containment is explicit (A is A), there is identity; where it is not, there is hidden identity. A true proposition means that the predicate is inside the subject. Whoever sees through the subject completely can make every true judgment about it; he need only "unwrap" them. Leibniz does not merely inherit the Aristotelian orientation of substance by predication; he radicalizes it, turning a negative criterion (never predicated) into a positive one (containing all its predicates). This inversion is what makes the monad possible.

The *Discourse* passage came first because it names the central problem: the ambiguity of the concept of subject and its connection with the concept of substance. "Subject" means first the individual substance, the *hypokeimenon*, the self-standing individual being (the ontic subject); second, the subject of a proposition (the logical subject), whose concept is wider, since not every logical subject is a substance; and third, the I as opposed to the object, which is both the preeminent propositional subject and the preeminent self-standing individual. In Leibniz the ontic subject is understood from the logical one, but the reverse is also possible. Heidegger leaves the question open: which subject has priority, the ontic or the logical, or neither? The third sense, bracketed almost in passing, is the one that will decide the issue. The monad will turn out to be modeled on the self-knowing I (§5), and the "neither" points toward Dasein, which is neither a substance-subject nor a propositional subject.

The second piece of evidence comes from the fragment *De libertate*. Leibniz writes that he saw it to be common to every true affirmative proposition, universal or singular, necessary or contingent, that the predicate is in the subject, or that the notion of the predicate is somehow involved in the notion of the subject, and that this is "the principle of infallibility in every kind of truth for one who knows all things a priori." The new element is that the relation of inclusion is the ground of the truth of every kind of knowledge, and that it is so for an a priori knower. Heidegger underlines the qualifier. Inclusion is defined relative to a knower who knows everything in advance, so the theory of judgment already presupposes an idea of absolute knowledge, the idea §2 traces to the scholastic *scientia Dei*.

The third piece of evidence comes from the correspondence with Antoine Arnauld, Cartesian theologian and co-author of the Port-Royal *Logic*, which Leibniz began when he sent him the *Discourse*; Heidegger calls this exchange fundamental for the problem of individual substance. In the first passage (June 1686), Leibniz calls his decisive reason as good as a complete proof: in every true affirmative proposition, necessary or contingent, universal or particular, the concept of the predicate is in some way included in that of the subject, "or else I do not know what truth is." Inclusion is here equated with being true, and the concept of truth as such is defined through it. The phrase "or else I do not know what truth is" shows that for Leibniz the inclusion theory is not one hypothesis among others; it is his definition of truth, and to abandon it would leave him without any concept of truth at all.

The second Arnauld passage reads: "In saying that the individual concept of Adam includes everything that will ever happen to him, I mean nothing other than what all philosophers mean when they say that the predicate is in the subject of a true proposition." Here the ontic concept of a subject is plainly determined from the logical subject–predicate relation understood as an *inesse*. Adam's history, which looks like a sequence of contingent events, is folded into Adam's concept. The chalk falling from the table has become the paradigm case, and Leibniz accepts the consequence.

The fourth passage, from Couturat's edition of the *Opuscules*, states that every true proposition can be proved. Since the predicate is in the subject, "as Aristotle says," or the notion of the predicate is involved in the perfectly understood notion of the subject, the truth must always be demonstrable by resolving the terms into what they contain. Leibniz appeals to Aristotle for the *inesse*. Heidegger flags the entanglement of two things: the presence of the actual being thought of as subject, and the concept of the subject. Proof proceeds by *resolutio*, analysis of the subject into its elements, and the very idea of proof is grounded in the structure of the proposition as a relation of inclusion. Leibniz has made demonstrability a consequence of truth rather than an addition to it. If truth is containment, then to prove is to unpack, and every truth is in principle provable.

The fifth passage, from the short treatise *Primae veritates*, says that the predicate or consequent is always in the subject or antecedent, and that "in this very thing consists the nature of truth in general, or the connection between the terms of a statement, as Aristotle too observed." Heidegger's gloss: the predicate as consequent is inside the subject as antecedent, and the essence of truth consists in this being-in. The logical pair subject–predicate is identified with the inferential pair antecedent–consequent, so the proposition and the proof are the same structure.

Heidegger fixes the principle that runs through the evidence. The inclusion theory speaks both of the containment of the predicate concept in the subject concept and of the being of what the predicate means in the being the subject names. The inclusion is logical, since it is in the *logos*, and it is ontic, since it is meant as holding in the being itself. What characterizes the theory is that the two coincide. Whether they coincide rightly, and on what grounds, can be decided only once the ultimate metaphysical foundations of the theory have been reached. This is the problem the destruction must solve. The coincidence of logical and ontic inclusion will be explained in §5 by the monad, a being whose mode of being is itself representing, so that for it to be and to contain its representations are one.

It would be tempting to compare this theory with other conceptions of judgment. Leibniz himself appeals to Aristotle, though not wholly rightly. Heidegger postpones to the later "fundamental discussion" the question of how far the appeal is justified and why it finally is not. The implied reason, developed in the Second Main Part, is that Aristotle's *hyparchein* belongs to a thinking of being as presence that is not yet referred to the representing subject.

Descartes looks for the essence of judgment in an entirely different direction. For him, judging is not merely representing or combining representations; it is the knowing subject's taking a position on representations. To judge (*iudicare*) is to give assent to a relation of representations: to assent, to believe, to affirm to oneself. The opposition of assenting and rejecting, acknowledging and refusing, does not coincide with the division into affirmation and negation, positive and negative judgment. This conception is bound up with the way Descartes grounds first philosophy, through withholding assent, the attitude of doubt toward prior knowledge. Heidegger's point is that Descartes's theory of judgment is a consequence of his method: a philosophy that begins by suspending assent must locate judgment in the act of assent.

The Cartesian view returned in the nineteenth century with Brentano (*Psychology from an Empirical Standpoint*, 1874; *The Origin of Our Knowledge of Right and Wrong*, 1889). Brentano appeals to Descartes's threefold classification of mental acts in the Third Meditation: ideas, volitions or affects, and judgments. Descartes also offers a twofold division, perception and volition (*Principles* I, 32 and 34), in which judging counts as an act of will, and this ambiguity produced a controversy. Brentano argues that acts of judgment are a class of their own, and Bergmann follows him; Windelband, starting from Brentano, takes judgment to be an act of will. That is also the starting point of Rickert's *The Object of Knowledge* (1892), "the foundation of Rickert's theory of knowledge, indeed even of the philosophy of value as such" (p. 45), followed by Lask's *Doctrine of Judgment* (1912). The genealogy traces Neo-Kantian value philosophy back to Descartes's classification of judgment as volition. The "ought" that Rickert places at the root of truth is thus, on Heidegger's account, a late descendant of the Cartesian assent.

However much Leibniz argues with Descartes and the Cartesians, he treats this theory, designed around the judging subject's position-taking rather than the content asserted, as inessential, and Heidegger says he is right to do so: it leads away from the central metaphysical problems. The judgment by which Heidegger sides with Leibniz against Brentano and the Neo-Kantians is significant. Whatever its faults, the inclusion theory at least asks about the connection of what is asserted, and so about being; the assent theory retreats into the psychology of the act.

Kant's treatment of judgment in the Introduction to the *Critique of Pure Reason*, with its basic division into analytic and synthetic judgments, matters far more for assessing Leibniz. If Kant's distinction applies to Leibniz at all, one must say that for Leibniz every judgment is analytic. Yet Kant's concept of the analytic does not coincide with what Leibniz calls the analysis of notions (*analysis notionum*). The caution matters: Leibnizian analysis may run to infinity and is complete only for God, whereas Kant's analytic judgment is one whose predicate a finite understanding finds in the concept by explication. The two concepts differ precisely in their relation to finitude, which is the axis of Heidegger's own reading of Kant.

If a confrontation is needed, it must be with Aristotle and Suárez on one side and Kant on the other. In both cases the problem of judgment, of the *logos*, of logic, must be decided from the problem of ontology, of metaphysics as such. For now the task is to bring the metaphysical perspective of Leibniz's doctrine into view and thereby clarify the theory itself. The two confrontations mark the historical limits of the course. Leibniz stands between the ancient-scholastic ontology he inherits and the Kantian critique that his own identity theory provokes, a link stated outright at the end of §2.

Heidegger adds a determination from the *Definitiones logicae*: for A to include B is for B, the predicate, to be universally affirmed of A, the subject, as in "the wise includes the just, that is, every wise person is just." Leibniz equates being affirmed with being included throughout; affirmedness means inclusion pure and simple. The "is" of the example shows that this connection is a *connexio realis*, a material inclusion, and in this sense reality is the counterconcept to negation. Heidegger reads "real" in its scholastic sense, as pertaining to the content (*res*) of a determination, not as actual existence. On this reading, affirmation, inclusion, and reality form one field, and Leibniz's later equation of the positive with the real, taken up by Kant's category of reality, has its logical root here.

Heidegger closes §1 with two questions. Can this *includi* and *inesse* be characterized more precisely, and does Leibniz have a definite basic conception of the relation? And how does the interpretation of the relation bear on the essential character of judgment, its being true or false? The questions lead from the structure of the proposition to the idea of truth, the theme of §2.

#### §2. Judgment and the Idea of Truth. The Basic Forms of Truth

Tradition holds judgment to be the primary and proper bearer of truth, and truth to be a character of assertions, their being true. Heidegger separates three senses of "truth": the idea of truth, being-true as such; its concretion in true propositions, "truths" that are to be proved, whence talk of "basic truths"; and truth as an ideal, the whole of possible knowable truths. The first sense is at issue. If truth has its place in the assertion, then true assertions are themselves a truth, something that is true, and being-true is the being-true of the assertion. The essence of assertion must therefore be essentially connected with the essence of truth. The distinction is not pedantic. Leibniz's argument moves constantly between truth as a property of propositions and truths as items to be derived, and the third sense, the totality of truths, will turn out to be God's knowledge.

The concept of assertion must be determined with regard to the idea of truth, and conversely. The question is how Leibniz connects his theory of judgment, the inclusion theory, with his conception of the essence of truth. Answering it yields a fundamental characterization of the inclusion theory itself. Heidegger announces the result before reaching it: the inclusion theory will prove to be a theory of identity.

Leibniz answers the question about the essence of truth directly: "the ground of truth consists in the connection of the predicate with the subject" (*Ratio autem veritatis consistit in nexu praedicati cum subiecto*). The Arnauld letter said the predicate is in the subject, "or I do not know what truth is," and *Primae veritates* equated the nature of truth with the connection between the terms of an assertion. The nature of truth, the interweaving of the members of the assertion, consists in the *inesse*, and on this *inesse* rests the principle of infallibility. The three texts converge on a single equation, and Heidegger sets them side by side to show that it is Leibniz's settled doctrine, not an occasional formula.

To be true means to be interwoven, for a determination to be contained in what it determines; to assert means *connexio*, which means being true. Heidegger asks whether this is a circle: being-true is equated with the *inesse* of predicate in subject, and the *inesse* is explained as truth. There are, moreover, many kinds of assertion. The question is therefore what the original, basic form of *inesse* is, understood as *nexus* and *inclusio*. The basic form of being-true must show itself in the most elementary assertions, the simplest and most original true propositions, the primary "truths." Heidegger turns the apparent circle into a method. If truth and inclusion define each other, one must look for the case where inclusion is evident in itself, and that case will be the paradigm for all others.

Leibniz defines the first truths as those that "state the same of itself or deny the opposite of the opposite": A is A, A is not non-A; if it is true that A is B, it is false that A is not B or that A is non-B; each thing is as it is; each thing is similar or equal to itself; nothing is greater or less than itself. However these differ in degree of priority, all fall under the single name of "identicals" (*Primae veritates*). The first, original truths are identities: assertions in which something is determined explicitly as itself, in its sameness with itself. Leibniz's list includes both formal identity and the principle of contradiction in its assertoric form. That the latter counts as an identical truth is the premise of the claim in §3 that the principle of contradiction is only the negative expression of identity.

A further essential moment follows. All other truths are reduced to the first ones by means of definitions or the resolution of notions, "in which a priori proof consists, independent of experience" (*Primae veritates*). All true propositions are ultimately reducible to identities. At bottom every true proposition is an identity, although not necessarily one that lies open to view; in principle every truth is an identity. The phrase "independent of experience" carries the consequence Heidegger will draw out: if every truth is an identity, then every truth is knowable a priori, including truths about historical fact.

This means that the essence of truth as such, the *inclusio* of the *nexus*, lies in identity. "Being true means being identical; *inesse* means *idem esse*" (p. 49). The formula condenses the section. The little word "in" of the predicate's being-in has been interpreted as sameness, and the relation of containment, which seemed to require two terms, collapses into the self-relation of one.

Heidegger cites two more passages. The first: the ground of truth consists in the connection of predicate with subject, that is, in the predicate's being in the subject, "either manifestly, as in identicals, … or covertly" (*vel manifeste … vel tecte*). The second: in identicals this connection and comprehension of the predicate in the subject is explicit, in all others implicit, to be shown through the analysis of notions, "in which a priori demonstration lies." All propositions are identical, but "identicals" in the narrower sense are those whose identity is manifest. In the others the identity is hidden, and proving their truth consists in bringing the underlying hidden identity to light. Proof, on this account, is an uncovering. Heidegger does not yet say so, but the language of the manifest and the hidden (*manifeste*, *tecte*) is the vocabulary of unconcealment turned into a logic of analysis, and the Second Main Part will reclaim it.

The essence of truth is identity, quite apart from whether human knowledge ever succeeds in actually exhibiting all truths as identities. Truth characterizes the essence of judgment, and the nature of truth equals the *nexus*; therefore the inclusion theory is an identity theory. The syllogism matters because the qualification it sets aside, whether finite knowledge can carry out the reduction, is precisely where Leibniz's theory will be strained. Heidegger fixes the essence independently of its achievability so that the gap between them can be examined.

How Leibniz grounds the identity theory, what it presupposes, and how far it can be carried out for every possible judgment (how, for instance, "AB is B" can be reduced to "A is A") are postponed. Heidegger offers only an anticipatory hint, meant to show that with this theory of the *logos* and these logical problems one stands immediately within the most central metaphysical questions, that is, within the ontological problem, the question of being as such. The "hint" is the thesis of the whole First Main Part; everything that follows elaborates it.

The connection between the problem of judgment and that of individual substance has been indicated. Being true means being identical, *inesse* is *idem esse*. Identity counts as the essence of truth, and identity has counted since antiquity as a determination of being; Heidegger points to *tautotēs* in Plato's *Sophist*, where sameness appears among the greatest kinds. Truth and being are interpreted by looking at the same phenomenon, selfsameness or identity, so the question of truth and the question of being are immediately intertwined. The reference to the *Sophist* recalls Heidegger's lecture course of 1924–25 and places Leibniz within a tradition in which being and truth are read off the same phenomenon, identity in Plato, presence in Aristotle, and inclusion in the monad.

Because the theory has such scope for Leibniz's whole philosophy and is so plainly metaphysical, Heidegger documents it further from a longer passage (Gerhardt VII, 300). Identical propositions are the first of all and true through themselves; consequently, the virtually identical ones are true, that is, those that can be reduced by analysis of terms to formal or expressed identities. All necessary propositions, or propositions of eternal truth, are virtually identical. In general, every true proposition that is not identical or true through itself can be proved a priori by axioms and definitions. Whenever a predicate is truly affirmed of a subject, there must be some real connection between them, so that in any proposition "A is B," B is in A, or its notion is somehow contained in the notion of A. Heidegger draws out the structure: an expressed identity is a truth through itself; what is true is virtually identical; virtual identities are reduced to formal ones. Every true assertion, whether an original or a merely possible identity, is provable a priori, which means that all true knowledge is ultimately a priori knowledge. Heidegger calls the thesis "of enormous scope" (p. 50). If all knowledge is a priori, experience is only a confused or abbreviated form of rational insight, and the distinction between reason and sensibility becomes one of degree. That is the Leibnizian position Kant will attack by insisting on the irreducibility of intuition.

The identity theory is meant to delimit the essence of judgment as such, and so of every form of assertion. Because Leibniz distinguishes two basic forms of assertion, which by the earlier result means two basic forms of truth, the general doctrine of judgment must prove itself on both, or the difficulties must emerge precisely there. The two forms are the test case. A theory of truth as identity is at home with necessary truths; its credibility turns on whether it can also accommodate truths of fact.

Heidegger refines the earlier distinction between manifest and hidden truths using *De libertate*. Leibniz writes that on closer consideration the inner difference between necessary and contingent truths became clear: every truth is either original or derivative. Original truths are those "whose reason cannot be given," the identical or immediate ones. Derivative truths are of two kinds: some are resolved into original truths, others admit an infinite progress of resolution. The former are necessary, the latter contingent. Heidegger stresses that the original–derivative distinction is not simply the same as the manifest–hidden one. Original truths admit no account and no proof; they are immediately evident. Derivative truths divide into necessary and contingent. Necessary truths can be resolved into original ones, and although not every necessary truth is original, some need and admit derivation. The contingent derivative truths are also identities in essence, but their resolution, their proof, never comes to an end for a finite understanding. Heidegger concludes that this thesis also implies that every knowledge is a priori knowledge. The notion of an infinite analysis is Leibniz's device for keeping contingency within the identity theory. Contingent truths are identities whose identity cannot be displayed by any finite procedure, and the infinity of the analysis is what separates the contingent from the necessary.

The original truths and the necessary ones resolvable into them were called eternal truths in scholasticism, and this articulation of the basic forms of truth has a metaphysical, ontological background. The difference between eternal and contingent truths corresponds to a distinction among beings: the being necessary through itself and contingent beings, the uncreated and the created. Heidegger summarizes in a schema. Affirmation equals inclusion, inclusion equals being-true, being-true equals being-in, and being-in equals being-the-same (*tautotēs*): truth is identity, either formally expressed and true through itself, or virtual and implicit and true through reduction. Original truths are the expressed or immediate identities. Derivative truths are either necessary, virtually identical and resolved into original truths, derivable from identities "for us," or contingent, admitting an infinite progress of resolution, derivable from identities "for God." The schema's final line carries the argument: the difference between necessary and contingent truth is a difference between two knowers, finite and infinite, and not a difference in the essence of truth.

The eternal truths concern "primarily" the self-thinking of the uncreated; in the Platonist tradition they are the formal truths of thinking as such and of mathematics. The contingent truths refer to created beings that are present in time, now, formerly, or in the future. Because the eternal truths have their eternal origin in God's absolute reason, Leibniz calls them truths of reason (*veritates rationis*, *vérités de raison*); the contingent truths, referring to facts, are truths of fact (*veritates facti*, *vérités de fait*). *Monadology* §33 states that truths of reason are necessary and their opposite is impossible, while truths of fact are contingent and their opposite possible. Heidegger's characterization of contingent beings as present "in time" introduces the thread he will follow: the distinction between the two kinds of truth rests on the distinction between the eternal and the temporal, and so, ultimately, on an understanding of time.

Contingent truths are truths about what is not necessary, what could also not be. The basic intention of Leibniz's theory of judgment is to conceive these truths of fact too as identities, and so, at bottom, as original, eternal truths, attributing to them in principle absolute certainty and truth. Heidegger corrects his own phrasing: to speak of "assimilating" truths of fact to truths of reason is misleading, since truths of fact are meant to keep their distinctive character while having the character of identities, of judgments whose predicates can all be unfolded from the subject concept. More precisely, truths of fact are not necessary truths, but they are identical ones. The formula "identical but not necessary" isolates the tension. Identity normally guarantees necessity, and Leibniz must explain how a proposition can be true by identity and yet have a possible opposite. His answer, the infinite analysis and God's free choice among possible worlds, will lead into the metaphysics of the monad.

However strange this assimilation may seem, so that historical truth would be an a priori truth, it is not so unheard of if two things are kept in view. First, Leibniz's overall tendency is "rationalist," aiming to conceive being from reason, including the being of facts. Second, behind this tendency stands the scholastic tradition, which is at work centrally in this very problem, in that the ideal of knowledge as such is seen in God's knowledge (*scientia Dei*). The analysis now leaves Leibniz for Thomas. The explanation for Leibniz's identity theory lies not in a logical argument but in an inherited ideal of knowledge.

Scholasticism holds that the first truth, and so the source of all truth, is the absolute intellect of God (Thomas, *De veritate*, q. 1). Original truth, the totality of all true knowledge, is absolute, and it is so in God's knowledge, which Thomas discusses in the *Summa theologiae* (I, q. 14) and in *De veritate* (q. 2). Heidegger makes a strong claim: without knowing and understanding these connections, the basic problems of modern philosophy remain completely closed. Scholastic theology is the key not only to Leibniz's logic; Kant's *Critique of Pure Reason* and Hegel's *Logic* can also be grasped in their real driving forces only from here. He does not mean that this philosophy leaned on theology or borrowed propositions from it. The philosophical sense of the orientation toward God's knowledge is that it functions as "the construction of an absolute knowledge against which finite, human knowledge is to be measured" (p. 54). Whether and why such construction belongs to the essence of philosophy, and how far philosophical knowledge as such is construction, cannot be discussed here. The move is decisive for the course. The theological idea is treated as a methodological function, the projection of an infinite measure for finite knowing, and the unresolved remark on "construction" anticipates the role Heidegger will give to the projective character of transcendence and to his own reading of Kant, where finite knowledge is defined precisely against the idea of an intuitive intellect.

In a note Heidegger clarifies why he points to scholastic sources: not to diminish Leibniz's originality. To ask "Where did he get that from?" betrays "a shopkeeper's standpoint," and asking "From what psychological complex does this arise?" is equally superficial. The merit of productive work lies in being dependent in an original and central way, something the imitator can never be, because he does not want to be and must always be eager for originality. The orientation toward scholasticism serves something quite different, and Heidegger tells his students they are not on the wrong track if they are at first thoroughly surprised by a logic that discusses Thomas's concept of God. He regrets that he cannot introduce these problems with the thoroughness the matter demands and will give only the most necessary. The remark states Heidegger's conception of tradition: to belong to a tradition productively is to be claimed by its questions, not to borrow its answers. The same conception governs his claim, in the recapitulation, that historical orientation lives only by free re-enactment.

God's knowledge, which knows absolutely, embraces both the possible and the actual, and each of these needs a distinction. "The possible" means first the pure possibilities (*mere possibilia*), the essences of beings, the ideas of things, irrespective of whether any such idea is ever realized. To become actual, something must be internally possible, but not everything internally possible must be realized. God has knowledge of these pure possibilities as such, which correspond to Kant's transcendental ideal, the totality of reality (*omnitudo realitatis*). Better: it belongs to God's essence simply to think this totality of the possible. This knowledge of simple understanding (*scientia simplicis intelligentiae*) is necessary, natural knowledge, since it belongs to God's "nature" as absolute self-consciousness, the thinking of thinking (*noēsis noēseōs*): in knowing his thinking, God knows what is thought in it, the totality of reality, the pure possibilities. Heidegger links three figures in one sentence: Thomas's knowledge of simple understanding, Aristotle's thinking of thinking, and Kant's transcendental ideal. The link shows the continuity of the "construction" just named, from Aristotle's theology through Thomas to Kant.

"The possible" also means what is not yet in actuality but will be, or what is spoken from God's eternity before all time: that part of the possible which will be realized, everything that at some definite time will be a fact, everything not merely possible but determined. God as absolute spirit does not know a fact only after it has happened; otherwise he would be variable in his essence, dependent on something other than himself, and so finite. God has foreknowledge of everything actual, a vision (*visio*), a free knowledge (*scientia libera*), because what will be realized, and how, ultimately depends on his will. The second sense of possibility explains why God's knowledge of facts is called "free": it depends on his will, not on his nature. Leibniz's distinction between truths of reason grounded in the divine intellect and truths of fact grounded in the divine will (discussed below) is prefigured here.

To clarify the two kinds of divine knowledge, necessary and free, Heidegger surveys the relevant articles of the *Summa theologiae* I, q. 14. In article 9 (whether God has knowledge of things that are not), Thomas distinguishes what is not in a qualified sense (*non entia secundum quid*) from what is not absolutely (*non entia simpliciter*). The former is what is not now actual but is possible in that it has been or will be; its not-being is thought in relation to what happens to be present at the moment. The latter are the pure possibilities. God knows these through the knowledge of simple understanding, the qualified non-beings through the knowledge of vision (*scientia visionis*), which grasps in the manner of a present intuition (*praesens intuitus*). Heidegger's gloss that qualified non-being is thought "in relation to what is presently at hand" isolates the temporal horizon of the distinction. Even for Thomas, the being and non-being of facts are measured by the present.

The "present" is understood from eternity, "which, existing without succession, comprehends the whole of time." It means that this intuition is not a sequence of acts of intuition, in contrast to what proceeds with succession, but occurs in a single instant, in Eckhart's word a "Nu." This now is not the momentary now of time, the flowing now (*nunc fluens*), but the eternal now, the standing now (*nunc stans*). The distinction between the flowing and the standing now matters beyond Thomas. Eternity is conceived as a present that does not pass, which is to say as a heightening of the very presence that, on Heidegger's account, the ordinary understanding of being privileges.

Intuition (*intuitus*) means a direct look, a knowing that grasps the whole of time at one stroke without sequence. As present intuition, vision is a look that ranges over the whole, as lying before God, who has everything present before him. Heidegger quotes Thomas: God's present intuition extends to the whole of time and to everything in any time, "as to things present to it" (*sicut in subiecta sibi praesentialiter*). Heidegger hyphenates "present-at-hand" (*vor-handen*) in describing what lies before God, and the hyphen is deliberate. Divine knowledge is modeled on having beings lying before one, the same presence-at-hand that *Being and Time* identified as the guiding sense of being in the tradition, here raised to infinity.

The knowledge of simple understanding is not a vision but a simple grasping, because pure possibilities are not realized outside God; as thought, they remain immanent in God, whereas all seeing relates to something outside. Everything past, present, and future is looked at as presently present in the present intuition of vision. This perpetual look embraces the possible and the merely "contingent," conditionally actual facts, the truths of fact, "the historical in the broadest sense" (p. 57). Heidegger's gloss "the historical" signals where the problem lies for him. The theory of truths of fact is a theory of how history can be known, and scholastic theology answers by placing all history before a single timeless gaze.

It follows that in this intuition God knows not only what is actual but also what is contingent and future for us. Article 13 (whether God's knowledge is of future contingents) explains how. The contingent can be considered first in itself (*in se ipso*), as present, as "determined to one": it is contingent and could fail to be, but it is now factually present, and as such I can consider it "in itself." As actual, it has so to speak decided for one definite possibility and passed over into actuality, and to that extent it is determined to one. The contingent in itself can be subjected infallibly to certain knowledge, namely factual observation on the basis of sensory intuition; such knowledge poses no problem. The contingent that is already present is unproblematic for any knower, because its presence settles it. The difficulty arises only with what is not yet present.

The contingent can be considered second "as it is in its cause," as future, as not yet determined to one. Here it is seen from its cause, as something still to be realized, and so it is still open to definite possibilities. There can be no certain knowledge of such a contingent. Whoever sees the effect only from its cause has merely conjectural knowledge (*cognitio coniecturalis*): it may turn out one way or another. The two perspectives, the contingent in itself and in its cause, correspond to two temporal modes, present and future, and the certainty of knowledge follows presence. Where there is no presence, there is only conjecture.

According to Thomas, God grasps the future contingent the way the contingent is grasped in itself. He knows contingents "not only as in their causes but also as each of them is actually in itself," not successively but simultaneously, because he has already anticipated everything future in his eternal present. God sees everything evenly present in this presentness, "all things as they are in their presentiality," and Thomas stresses that all things are present to the divine gaze not only in their essential possibility but in their individual realization. On the ground of this presentness, the divine synopsis of everything proceeds infallibly. Thomas's image (in the reply to the third objection) contrasts the human and divine cases: someone walking along a road does not see those coming behind him, but someone standing on a height and overlooking the whole road sees everyone traveling it. God's certainty about the future is certainty about presence, since God's eternal now makes every future present. The image of the height encapsulates the whole construction: absolute knowledge is knowledge from a standpoint outside time that sees all of time laid out as present.

Article 14 (whether God knows enunciables) extends God's knowledge to "all enunciables that can be formed," all possible true propositions, all possible truths. A proposition is a *symplokē*, a *synthesis* and *diairesis*, a composition and division. Thomas's treatment becomes centrally important here, since the question is whether God knows possible propositions in the manner of composition and division. That is the character of finite knowledge, which proceeds successively: "our intellect runs discursively from one thing to another." In God there is no succession, as though he at first lacked something. The question joins the theory of divine knowledge to the theory of judgment: if God knows propositions, does he know them as propositions, by combining terms? Thomas's answer will provide Leibniz's model of truth.

When we know what a human being is, for example, we do not thereby understand the other things that belong to it. When human knowledge grasps a subject, it cannot draw all predicates from it at once; because of its successiveness it must bring what is known separately into one by composition or division, forming an assertion. God, however, knows the whole essence of each thing and "whatever can happen to it," since he knows through simple understanding, in which all predicates belonging to a subject are grasped at one stroke as being in it, in their *inesse*. What is for us a separation and succession of predications is for God originally one and selfsame; it is identity. This paragraph is the source of the Leibnizian doctrine. Composition in judgment is a mark of finitude, the finite intellect's way of reassembling what it could not grasp at once, whereas for an infinite intellect the predicates are already one with the subject, which is to say identical with it. Leibniz's identity theory of truth is Thomas's account of divine knowing turned into the definition of truth as such.

Article 7 (whether God's knowledge is discursive) confirms that divine knowledge is no laying apart and taking together. God's knowledge is in no way discursive (*nullo modo discursiva*); in keeping with his essence, it is an original seeing of the whole all at once and at one stroke: "God sees all things in one." The formula "sees all in one" repeats the identity doctrine from the side of the knower. What is one for the knower is identical in the known.

In summary, God has two kinds of knowledge, necessary and free, and both are absolute and a priori. God has in advance the full concept of everything factual and knows with absolute certainty what it will be and how; he possesses all truths about created things, and these are as necessary and certain for him as the truths about the possible. Both forms of truth involve knowledge from the concept of the being in question, a simple unfolding of the determinations it involves, that is, nothing but identities. The summary gives the exact structure Leibniz needs: contingent truths are truths whose subject concept contains all its predicates, known a priori, but only by an infinite knower.

Heidegger now names the decisive motive of Leibniz's assimilation of truths of fact to truths of reason. What holds for God's absolute spirit Leibniz takes at the same time as the ideal of human knowledge; he determines the idea and essence of human knowledge, and so of truth and of assertion, from the idea of God's knowledge. Because human knowledge is finite, it lacks absolute free vision. We do not have, for instance, before all experience, the complete concept of Julius Caesar as this particular individual subject, such that possessing it we could develop all of Caesar's predicates as necessarily belonging to him. The whole argument of §§1–2 lands here. Leibniz's logic measures human judgment against an infinite intellect, and that measure explains both the identity theory and its limit: a finite knower can have truths of fact only by experience, because it lacks the complete concept.

Leibniz states the essence of contingent truths more precisely in *De libertate*. In contingent truths, although the predicate is in the subject, it can never be demonstrated of it, nor can the proposition ever be brought back to an equation or identity; the resolution proceeds to infinity. God alone sees, not the end of the resolution, which does not exist, but the connection of the terms, the involvement of the predicate in the subject, since he sees whatever is in the series. Heidegger paraphrases: in contingent truths, although the predicate is in the subject (hidden identity), no strict proof can ever be given, since the relation cannot be reduced to a mathematical equation, and the resolution goes on to infinity. Only God sees, not the end of the process, since there is none, but the whole of the predicates' bond in the subject, because he sees what lies in the series of predicates, and sees it without running through it. Heidegger adds Couturat's citation of Leibniz: perfect demonstrations of truths do not require perfect concepts of things. God does not complete the infinite analysis; he does not need to, because he does not know by analysis at all. The infinite series that is inexhaustible for discursive thought is present all at once to intuition, which is why the Thomist distinction between discursive and intuitive knowledge carries the full weight of Leibniz's theory of contingency.

Through the concept of the infinite series, Leibniz relates this distinction among truths to number. Contingent truth stands to necessary truth as incommensurable magnitudes, which cannot be reduced to a common measure, stand to commensurable ones. Just as incommensurable magnitudes are subject to mathematics, which aims at equation and identity, so contingent truths are subject to God's vision, which dispenses with running through the series. The mathematical analogy shows that Leibniz thinks the contingent not as an irrational residue but as a determinate infinity, like an irrational number, fully defined yet never exhausted by finite steps. Contingency is thereby rationalized without being abolished.

Leibniz also formulates the distinction between truths of reason and truths of fact in another way, likewise prepared by traditional ontology: some propositions pertain to the essences of things and others to their existences. Essential propositions can be demonstrated by resolving terms (these are the eternal truths); existential or contingent propositions differ from them in their whole kind, and their truth is understood a priori by the infinite mind alone. Despite this difference "in their whole kind," the essence of essential and existential truths is the same, since both are identities. The pairing of necessary–contingent with essence–existence shows that the logical distinction rests on the ontological distinction between what a thing is and that it is. Leibniz's attempt to treat existential truths as identities is therefore an attempt to bring existence within the reach of essence, and the course's unexecuted §6, on essence and the striving toward existence (*conatus existentiae*), was to address this directly.

The origin of these truths differs. Necessary truths, like those of arithmetic, geometry, and logic, are founded in the divine intellect independently of the will, whereas contingent truths arise from God's will, "not his bare will, but from considerations of the best or most fitting, directed by the intellect" (letter to Bourguet, 1710). Necessary truths, which concern pure possibilities, are grounded in God's understanding independently of his will; Leibniz adds this against Descartes, who taught that even mathematical and logical truths depend on God's will. Contingent truths, the knowledge of what happens and how, and the being of what is known in them, depend on God's will, but on a will guided by his understanding, which is guided in turn by consideration of the best and most fitting, since the actual world is the best of all worlds. The distinction between intellect and will now carries the difference between essence and existence. Existence is what God's will adds to essence under the principle of the best, which is why the principle of reason, as a principle of the best, will prove more fundamental for contingent truths than the principle of contradiction.

Elsewhere Leibniz writes that necessary truths involve only the divine intellect, contingent ones the decrees of the will, and that in a contingent proposition the connection is not necessary but varies with time and depends on the supposition of a divine decree and on free will. Correspondingly there are two kinds of impossibility: the "logical" impossibility of what contradicts itself, and the imperfection of what, though not self-contradictory, is "morally" impossible. The phrase "varies with time" places the contingent in time, and the "moral" impossibility shows that the principle governing contingent truths is one of value, of the better and worse. Leibniz's logic of fact is at bottom a logic of choice.

Heidegger summarizes. Truth is identity, and in principle all truths can be reduced to identities. Derivative truths divide into two classes: necessary and conditional, truths of reason and truths of fact, truths of essence and truths of what is present at hand. The unity of all three classes of truth is oriented by God's knowledge. The rendering of truths of fact as truths "of what is present at hand" (*des Vorhandenen*) is Heidegger's own. It classes the existential proposition as a statement about presence-at-hand and anticipates the claim that Leibniz's ontology, like the tradition's, understands existence as presence.

The assimilation of truths of fact to truths of reason is the idea of a knowledge of factual beings, of what can be experienced empirically, from mere concepts of reason, a knowledge of beings from pure reason. This theory, which lies in the developmental tendency of modern philosophy, was to be struck by a critique that, as Kant's main work, calls itself a *Critique of Pure Reason*. That is the inner connection between Leibniz's theory of judgment and Kant's basic problem. Heidegger reads the *Critique*'s title against Leibniz: "pure reason" is reason that claims to know beings without intuition, and Kant's critique is directed at the identity theory's promise to know facts from concepts.

A twofold intention determines Leibniz's conception of truth: the reduction of all truths to identities, and at the same time the preservation of the distinctive character of contingent truths of fact. This leads him to determine the principles of knowledge accordingly. The tension between the two intentions is the motor of §3. The principles of contradiction and of sufficient reason are Leibniz's attempt to hold both commitments at once.

##### In memoriam Max Scheler

Heidegger interrupts the lecture on the news of Scheler's death (19 May 1928). Scheler has died in the midst of a large and wide-ranging work, at the stage of a new attempt to press into what is ultimate and whole, at the beginning of a new teaching position in Frankfurt from which he expected much. The broken sentence with which the paragraph ends conveys the interruption as well as announces it.

Setting aside the extent and kind of his productivity, Scheler was "the strongest philosophical force in present-day Germany, no, in present-day Europe and even in contemporary philosophy as such" (p. 62). The self-correcting superlative, moving from Germany to Europe to philosophy as such, is characteristic. Heidegger measures Scheler by philosophical force, not by results, a standard he makes explicit in the next paragraph.

Scheler's philosophical beginnings were guided by Eucken, and he set out from a positive science, biology. He received the decisive impetus from Husserl and the *Logical Investigations*. He saw the new possibilities of phenomenology clearly, and he did not merely adopt and apply them externally; he advanced them substantially and immediately brought them into unity with the central problems of philosophy, as his critique of ethical formalism above all attests. His confrontation with Bergson was also important. Heidegger adds a principle: occasions, impulses, and situations remain ineffective unless they meet a will that is already alive and are transformed within it. What matters is not results and progress; these exist only "in the field of what is most deeply irrelevant" (p. 63). Heidegger's account of influence in the memorial repeats the principle he stated about Leibniz's dependence on scholasticism. Real dependence consists in transformation by a living question, not in taking over results.

What was decisive and characteristic of Scheler was the totality of his questioning. Standing in the midst of beings as a whole, he had an unusual sense for all newly emerging possibilities and forces, and an indomitable drive to think and interpret always in terms of the whole. The phrase "in the midst of beings as a whole" is Heidegger's own metaphysical vocabulary. It describes Scheler's philosophizing in the terms Heidegger will use for Dasein's factical situation in the discussion of metontology.

Accordingly, Scheler, Catholic by background, took his philosophical path again, in a time of collapse, toward Catholicism as a world-historical power, not in the sense of the Church. Augustine and Pascal gained new significance, as an answer to and against Nietzsche. Heidegger frames Scheler's Catholic period as a response to the collapse after 1918 and to Nietzsche, not as a confession, which is consistent with his emphasis on the totality of questioning over doctrinal positions.

This possibility broke apart for Scheler as well. The question of what the human being is moved again to the center of his work, posed within the whole of philosophy in the sense of Aristotelian theology. Heidegger calls "immensely bold" the idea of the weak God who cannot be God without the human being, so that the human being is thought as God's "co-worker." All this remained far from a flat theism or a vague pantheism. Scheler's plan aimed at a philosophical anthropology, a working-out of the special position of the human being. Heidegger's praise for Scheler's placing of anthropology "in the sense of Aristotelian theology" connects with the Preliminary Remark's pairing of ontology and theology. The question of the human being is metaphysical only when it is posed together with the question of beings as a whole, a pairing that returns in the appendix on fundamental ontology, where Heidegger distinguishes his analysis of Dasein from anthropology.

Heidegger asks whether Scheler's capacity for change was a sign of lack of substance, of inner emptiness. He answers that one recognizes, as only a few experienced directly in days and nights of debate and struggle with him, a true possession by philosophy, which Scheler could not master and had to follow, and which, amid the fragmentation of today's Dasein, often drove him to impotence and despair. This possession was his substance. Through every change, he remained faithful to this inner direction in ever new attempts and efforts, and this fidelity must have been the source of the childlike kindness he sometimes showed. Heidegger rejects the stock charge of inconstancy by relocating Scheler's "substance" from a fixed doctrine to an inner direction, the same way he treats identity throughout the course, as something to be grasped from a way of being and not from an unchanging content.

No one seriously philosophizing today is not essentially indebted to Scheler, and no one can replace the living possibility of philosophy that has gone with him. This irreplaceability is the sign of his greatness. Greatness is measured by irreplaceability, not achievement, which is consistent with the rejection of "results" two paragraphs earlier.

The greatness of such an existence can be measured only by standards it must itself provide. The greatness of this philosophical existence lay in its reckless going-toward what the age is only now darkly rolling forward, what cannot be reckoned into the inherited, toward a humanity that will not let itself be appeased and leveled into "a shallow, antiquarian humanism" (p. 64). What Dilthey and Max Weber, each in his own way, went toward was powerful in Scheler in a wholly original way and with the greatest philosophical force. The polemic against a "shallow humanism" and the invocation of Dilthey and Weber place Scheler among thinkers who faced the historical crisis of the present rather than retreating into a cultural ideal. The phrase "going toward" (*Entgegengehen*) gives greatness the structure of a futural existence.

Heidegger closes: "Max Scheler is dead. We bow before his fate. Once again a path of philosophy falls back into darkness" (p. 64). The final sentence treats a philosophical path as something that, left unwalked, returns to concealment, the language of unconcealment and concealment applied to the history of philosophy itself.

#### §3. The Idea of Truth and the Principles of Knowledge

The essence of truth lies in the identity of subject and predicate, so knowing truths is grasping identities. Depending on whether the identities lie open or must first be exhibited, and in the latter case whether the truths are necessary or conditional, different marks and criteria of truth are needed. The principles of knowledge are thereby derived from the essence of truth. They are not external rules imposed on thinking but the criteria appropriate to the different ways in which identity can be given.

The usual view assigns two principles to Leibniz's two kinds of truth: truths of reason obey the principle of contradiction, truths of fact the principle of reason (*Monadology* §31 ff.). The Leibnizians Wolff and Baumgarten (*Metaphysica* §19 ff.) went so far as to prove the second principle from the first and to place all knowledge ultimately under the principle of contradiction. Taken as the negative expression of the principle of identity, this principle then becomes the principle of all knowledge, that is, of truths as identities. Heidegger grants the tendency a certain justification, then adds that it "goes entirely against Leibniz, and above all against the problems themselves" (p. 65). The school philosophy drew the obvious consequence of the identity theory and lost what was most important in it. The principle of reason cannot be derived from the principle of contradiction, and the rest of the section shows why its priority, which Leibniz glimpsed, is the thread leading to the Second Main Part.

The first class of truths, explicit identities or primal truths, carry their identity on their face. The mark of their truth is this evident identity (A is A). Put in the form of a principle, the criterion of the truth of primal truths reads: A is A, the principle of identity. The principle of knowledge for primal truths is itself nothing other than the most elementary primal truth. Heidegger stresses that the criterion, identity, is itself a first truth and a source of truth, and that this principle does not stand outside the propositions whose knowledge it guides; it belongs to them as their first proposition. For original truths, criterion and instance coincide. The principle of identity is not a rule applied to identities from outside but the first identity itself, and this self-grounding is the model the principle of reason will be measured against.

These truths are those "whose reason cannot be given." This does not mean they are groundless; on the contrary, they are themselves ground, and so need no grounding, no reduction: they are the original truths. All other truths, the necessary and the contingent alike, need grounding. They stand under the principle of reason, better, "the principle of reason to be rendered" (*principium reddendae rationis*), the principle that a ground must be exhibited, the principle of *resolutio*, of the need for proof. Hence not only the contingent class of derivative truths but every derivative truth stands under the principle of reason, by its essence. Nevertheless, a special principle is assigned to necessary truths: the principle of contradiction. Heidegger's reformulation shifts the principle from things to knowledge. As "the principle of the reason to be rendered," it demands of every non-original truth that its ground be given, which extends the principle beyond contingent truths to all derivative truth. The later title of the Second Main Part, the metaphysics of the principle of reason, begins here.

The second class of truths comprises the necessary truths, those directly reducible to identities. Heidegger quotes Leibniz: an absolutely necessary proposition is one that can be resolved into identicals, or whose opposite implies a contradiction; what implies a contradiction, or whose opposite is necessary, is called impossible. The criterion of necessary truths, by their essence, is reducibility to identities, and reducibility means agreement with them. What does not agree but conflicts with identities speaks against them and contains a contradiction. Reducibility to identities thus means freedom from contradiction. What includes a contradiction is what cannot be at all, since to be means to be-in, and to be-in means to be the same. What cannot be from the ground up is the impossible. Heidegger derives the principle of contradiction from the identity theory in three steps, and the last step is ontological: the self-contradictory is impossible because being is being-in and being-in is identity. The principle of contradiction is a principle of being before it is a principle of thought.

Yet because these necessary truths stand under the principle of non-contradiction as the principle of their reducibility, that is, their groundability, the principle of the reason to be rendered belongs to them as well. One can and must say conversely that this principle is more original than the principle of contradiction. Heidegger's argument turns on the concept of reduction. The principle of contradiction presupposes that necessary truths need to be reduced to identities, and that need is exactly what the principle of reason expresses. The demand for grounding precedes the test for consistency. This reversal of the Wolffian order is the first formulation of the thesis that grounding is prior to logic.

Leibniz does, however, write that a proposition is true absolutely and in itself only if it is identical or reducible to identicals, that is, demonstrable a priori, such that the connection of predicate and subject can be explained and "a reason always appears." Nothing at all happens without some reason; there is no proposition, apart from identicals, in which the connection between predicate and subject cannot be distinctly explained, since in identicals predicate and subject coincide. "This is among the first principles of all human reasoning, and after the principle of contradiction it has the greatest use in all the sciences." Heidegger objects that the ontological standing of the principle of reason remains unclarified here; only its "use" is mentioned. Yet the principle of reason is "the principle of the demanded disclosure of identity" (p. 67). The objection targets Leibniz's ranking of the principle of reason "after" the principle of contradiction. Heidegger reads it as a merely pragmatic ranking by use, and his own formula, "the demanded disclosure of identity," states what the principle is: the demand that the hidden identity be brought into the open. The word "disclosure" (*Enthüllung*) links the principle of reason to truth as unconcealment.

Conversely, since the principle of contradiction is at bottom the principle of identity, it cannot be reserved for one class of identities; it must refer to all identities, including contingent truths. In grounding them we make no real use of it only because it is impossible for us to carry out the reduction to identities. The asymmetry is again one of finitude. For God the principle of contradiction governs contingent truths as fully as necessary ones; for us it is idle there, because we cannot complete the analysis.

Two results follow. First, the relationship between the two principles, or rather the three, is not immediately transparent, not even to Leibniz. Second, although Leibniz himself often assigns the two principles to the two classes of necessary and contingent truth, he says in important passages of three major writings that both principles hold in both classes, that is, of all derivative truths. The lack of transparency belongs to the matter and not only to Leibniz's exposition, since identity, contradiction, and reason are three aspects of a single conception of truth whose unity has not been clarified.

The note documents the second claim. In the appendix to the *Theodicy* (1710), §14 of the remarks on King's *On the Origin of Evil*, Leibniz writes that each principle holds not only in the field of necessary truths but also in that of contingent truths, and that "in a certain way these principles are included in the definition of the true and the false."
