In *Primae veritates*, after showing that the essence of truth is identity, he says that from these considerations, insufficiently examined because of their very obviousness, many things of great moment follow: at once the received axiom arises that "nothing is without reason," or "no effect is without a cause." Otherwise there would be a truth that could not be proved a priori, or would not be resolved into identities, which is contrary to the nature of truth, which is always identical, expressly or implicitly. Heidegger paraphrases: the principle of sufficient reason springs from the definition of truth. If the principle of reason were not an original principle of all truth, there would be no a priori proof, no resolution into identity, which would contradict the essence of truth. Finally, *Monadology* §36 states that the sufficient reason must also be found in contingent truths or truths of fact, in the interconnection of all created things; Leibniz refers back to §33, where he had said that in necessary truths the reason can be found by analysis and brought back to identities. The *Primae veritates* passage is the decisive one. It derives the principle of reason from the definition of truth, so that the principle of reason is nothing but the essence of truth as identity turned into a demand, which is what Heidegger's formula "demanded disclosure of identity" expressed. The equation of "nothing without reason" with "no effect without a cause" shows how quickly the logical principle passes over into a principle of beings.

The survey of the principles of knowledge shows their connection with identity as the essence of truth, and identity is the basic character of the being of all beings. Among the principles, the principle of reason has priority, though unclearly. A connection of ground, truth, and being appears with regard to identity. The fundamental importance of the principle of reason becomes fully visible once one notices that the main principles of Leibniz's metaphysics are grounded in it and are even explicitly derived from it (*Primae veritates*). A far-reaching ambiguity thus enters the principle of reason: bound up with it or identical to it are the principles of harmony, continuity, fitness or the better (*melius*), existence, and the identity of indiscernibles. The triad of ground, truth, and being is the structure the Second Main Part will re-examine from transcendence, freedom, and temporality. The ambiguity of the principle of reason, which in Leibniz carries a whole metaphysics, is what makes it the "basic problem of logic" in the title of the Second Main Part.

On the whole, the tendency to assimilate the two basic forms of truth, truth of reason and truth of fact, and consequently the still obscure connection between the two basic principles, is oriented by God's knowledge, by the idea of absolute knowledge. It must therefore be asked whether and how Leibniz determines the idea of knowledge as such, and where he sees the essence of knowledge as knowledge. Since truth is an essential character of knowledge, false knowledge being no knowledge, the clarification of the idea of knowledge must bring into view the essence of truth, which has so far been determined as identity. Being true coincides with being included and being identical, so the clarification of the idea of knowledge must provide sharper insight into the basic connections within which the inquiry now constantly moves. The question is how judgment, truth, identity, knowledge, being, and the principles of knowledge are bound together in their essential structure. The paragraph transfers the burden of explanation from logic to the theory of knowledge, and from there, in §5, to the being of the knower. The chain of equivalences can be unified only by the idea of knowledge that underlies it.

In a note, Heidegger warns that the inquiry moves constantly in one and the same dimension of these basic phenomena without yet being able to recognize an unambiguous order of dependence among them; perhaps there is no order at all in the sense that one could be derived "in a straight line" from another. None of these basic phenomena is more original than the others; they are equiprimordial. That is exactly why two central problems arise: the inner constitution of this equiprimordiality, and the ground that makes it possible. At issue is "the essence of the solidity (indissolubility) of this equiprimordial dimension of the transcendental" (pp. 69–70). The note recasts the Leibnizian material in Heidegger's own terms. Equiprimordiality, familiar from *Being and Time*, rules out any deduction of truth from being or being from truth, and the "dimension of the transcendental" names the field the Second Main Part will identify with transcendence. The question about the "ground" of this unity is the question of the ground of grounds, which the course will answer with freedom.

##### Summarizing Recapitulation

At the start of a new session, Heidegger restates the aim. The course attempts a philosophical logic and thereby an introduction to philosophizing. What traditional logic treats in an external, technical way is to be grasped in its foundations, in the grounds at which what logic ordinarily treats begins. Only metaphysics reaches these grounds; hence the title, *Metaphysical Foundations of Logic*. The Introduction showed how the main phenomena of logic point back into metaphysics: truth to transcendence, ground to freedom, concept to schema, the copula to being. Because this connection is essential, it has constantly asserted itself in previous philosophy, however much the tendency has been, and today is more than ever, to detach logic completely and ground it in itself. The four correspondences are the program of the course, and three of them (truth and transcendence, ground and freedom, the copula and being) structure the Second Main Part; the pairing of concept and schema points to the book on Kant that Heidegger was writing at the time. The German title's *Anfangsgründe* is taken literally: the grounds at which logic "begins."

If one wants to ground logic metaphysically, one needs communication with the history of philosophy, because the grounding of metaphysics itself is thereby demanded anew. There is no finished metaphysics into which logic could be built. Instead, "the destruction of logic is itself a piece of the grounding of metaphysics," and this grounding is "nothing other than the fundamental confrontation with the entire previous tradition" (p. 70). The destruction is therefore not preparatory to a metaphysics to be supplied later; it is the work of grounding. Because the metaphysics that would ground logic does not yet exist, it can only be won through the confrontation with the tradition that the destruction carries out.

The course therefore first tries to press into the dimension of the metaphysical foundations of logic by a historical route. The orientation toward Leibniz is chosen not arbitrarily but for the reasons given. Hence the title of the First Main Part, the destruction of Leibniz's theory of judgment with respect to its metaphysical foundations, articulated in seven paragraphs, of which three have been treated: the general structure of judgment (the inclusion theory), the idea of truth (the identity theory) and the basic forms of truth, and the idea of truth and the principles of knowledge. What has become clear is the presupposition of the idea of absolute truth: knowledge is projected toward a definite ideal. The word "projected" (*entworfen*) recasts the result of §§2–3 in the vocabulary of *Being and Time*. The ideal of God's knowledge is not a borrowed dogma but a projection, and Leibniz's logic is the unfolding of that projection.

Heidegger closes the recapitulation with a methodological remark: every historical orientation is alive only if one learns to see that one always owes what is essential to one's own interpretation, in free re-enactment, which goes together with a detachment from everything merely learned. The historical path is justified only as appropriation. What a text yields depends on the interpreter's own questioning, which is also the standard Heidegger applied, in the note on Leibniz's originality, to Leibniz's relation to scholasticism.

#### §4. The Idea of Knowledge as Such

Heidegger recalls the result about God's knowledge: it is present intuition, with all things subject to it "presentially." Intuition is immediate looking, and "present" means present at once as opposed to successive. God's knowledge does not run through a sequence of intuitions of individual things; it occurs without succession, in a single instant (*Augenblick*) that does not last a moment but is the same from eternity. Hence its eternity: the standing now, the perpetual present. This constantly present intuition in God has everything lying "present" before it, the whole of past, present, and future actuality. Knowledge is intuition, vision, the immediate and constant beholding of the universe in its presence. The recapitulation makes the temporal structure explicit. Every determination of divine knowledge (instant, standing now, lying-before, presence) is a mode of presence, so the ideal of knowledge Leibniz inherits is an ideal of pure, unbroken presence.

This idea of knowledge follows from God's eternity. What is eternal is always everything at once, *totum simul*; eternity is the measure of time. Eternity itself is derived from immutability and simplicity. The construction runs: simplicity, immutability, eternity, present intuition. What is unchangeable in essence cannot change by acquiring knowledge; what cannot pass away is eternal and must have everything at once, free of change. Hence the knowing of so absolute a being must have the character of present intuition. Heidegger displays the order of derivation. The theory of knowledge is derived from a determination of being, simplicity, and not the other way round. The same order will hold in Leibniz, where the ideal of knowledge (§4) will be traced back to the being of the monad (§5).

Leibniz answered the question about the essence of knowledge in a short treatise that, unusually, he published himself: the *Meditations on Knowledge, Truth, and Ideas* (*Acta Eruditorum*, November 1684), two years before the *Discourse*. The theme was much discussed among the Cartesians. The questions entered a new stage with Arnauld's polemic against Malebranche, *On True and False Ideas*, which Heidegger calls a fundamental confrontation with Descartes and his principles of knowledge and an essential station on the way from Descartes to Kant regarding the problem of "categories." Leibniz begins with reference to these disputes: since eminent men now debate true and false ideas, a matter of great importance for knowing the truth in which even Descartes did not wholly satisfy, he will briefly set out his view on the distinctions and criteria of ideas and knowledge. Heidegger's placement of the dispute "on the way from Descartes to Kant" frames the *Meditations* as a stage in the problem of how concepts can have real content, which is the problem Kant will call the objective reality of the categories.

Leibniz's next sentence anticipates the whole treatise: knowledge is either obscure or clear; clear knowledge is either confused or distinct; distinct knowledge is either inadequate or adequate; and it is also either symbolic or intuitive. If it is at once adequate and intuitive, it is most perfect. Leibniz names pairs of opposed properties in progressive order, each time articulating the positive member further and ending with the idea of the most perfect knowledge, adequate intuition, direct beholding. Heidegger sets this out as a schema: knowledge (notion) divides into obscure and clear, clear into confused and distinct, distinct into inadequate and adequate, and adequate into symbolic or blind and intuitive (most perfect). The dichotomous form suggests a single scale of increasing perfection. Heidegger will show that the last division breaks the scale, because it concerns a different respect altogether.

A notion is obscure (*obscura*) when it does not suffice to recognize the thing represented. Heidegger's paraphrase: a familiarity with something is obscure if it does not allow me to recognize a being, when it is encountered, as what it is. Suppose I have seen a particular animal before; if it appears again and I fail to recognize it, perhaps confusing it with another, my acquaintance is obscure. Obscure notions bring together what is in itself different. Philosophy has obscure terms too, such as Aristotle's insufficiently explicated concept of *entelecheia*, or the concept of cause, and any proposition into which such a notion enters becomes obscure. Heidegger reads obscurity as a failure of recognition, of re-identification. The basic function of a notion, on this reading, is to let a being be encountered again as the same, which already ties the theory of knowledge to identity. His choice of *entelecheia* and cause as examples of "obscure" philosophical terms carries its own irony, since these are the concepts Leibniz's monadology will revive.

Knowledge is clear (*clara*) when I have the means to recognize the thing represented. A clear notion lets me recognize an encountered being as the same one I saw before, to grasp it again as the same, to "identify" it. The quotation marks around "identify" mark the thread: clarity is the most elementary achievement of identity.

I may have a clear representation, reliably distinguishing a hawk from a buzzard, yet be unable to state the particular marks that suffice to tell the two apart. A clear notion can still be confused. The example shows that clarity is practical and recognitional. It does not require being able to say what one recognizes.

Knowledge is confused (*confusa*), more precisely "flowing together," when I cannot enumerate separately the marks sufficient to distinguish the thing from others, although the thing really has such marks and requisites into which its notion could be resolved. Heidegger's gloss "flowing together" (*zusammenfließend*) restores the etymology of *confusa*. Confusion is not error but the fusion of marks that are really distinct in the thing and could be separated by analysis.

Heidegger adds a note. "Mark" (*nota*) means both essential determinacy and individual feature; "requisites" (*requisita*) are the moments into which the knowledge of a thing can be resolved (compare the twofold determination of distinct knowledge below). The relation between marks and requisites corresponds to the difference between implicit and explicit identity in §2. The equivocation in *nota* is important. It lets Leibniz treat the essential constituents of a thing and its distinguishing features as the same kind of item, the equivocation that permits the complete concept of an individual to include its whole history.

Leibniz gives examples of clear but confused representations. Colors can be distinguished clearly enough from smells and tastes, "but by the simple testimony of the senses, not by marks that can be stated." Red cannot be set before the blind by definition, only by "leading them to the thing present." Artists can know with complete clarity what has been done rightly or badly, but when asked why, they say only that something is missing, an "I know not what" (*nescio quid*). The three examples, sense qualities, the blind, and artistic judgment, share one feature: knowledge that is secure in presence but cannot be articulated in marks. For Leibniz, this is a defect of articulation, not of certainty.

Knowledge is distinct (*distincta*, articulated) when we have present the marks and tests sufficient to distinguish the thing expressly from similar things. We have such knowledge of what several senses share, what Aristotle called the common sensibles (*ta koina*): number, magnitude, figure. According to the *New Essays* (II, 5), these representations come from the mind itself and are ideas of pure understanding; Heidegger compares Kant's Inaugural Dissertation of 1770. We also have distinct knowledge of affects such as fear and hope. The lineage Heidegger draws from Aristotle's common sensibles through Leibniz's ideas of pure understanding to Kant's dissertation is the prehistory of the Kantian distinction between sensibility and understanding, which Kant first drew in the dissertation.

Of what is distinct we have a nominal definition, "which is nothing other than the enumeration of sufficient marks," the list of features sufficient for a clear distinction. We have distinct knowledge of what has a nominal definition, and also of an indefinable notion when it is primitive, "a mark of itself," that is, irresolvable, understood only through itself and therefore lacking requisites (compare "intuitive" below). The second case prepares Heidegger's argument that intuition is not a higher degree of analysis. A primitive notion is distinct without any enumeration, because there is nothing in it to enumerate.

Heidegger stresses that for Leibniz a nominal definition is not a mere explanation of a name or the attaching of a meaning to a word. It is knowledge of what is meant and named in naming, sufficient to distinguish it from others: knowledge with real content, though not primary knowledge. What characterizes the nominal definition is that the marks are only "enumerated." The nominal definition, as a mere list of distinguishing marks, must be kept apart from the real definition, characterized below. The warning heads off a nominalist reading. The nominal definition already knows the thing, but only as a juxtaposition of features, and its limitation is that it does not show how the features belong together.

Distinct knowledge not only distinguishes the named object clearly from others but can also expressly enumerate the marks of its distinctness. Staying with composite notions, as opposed to primitive ones, it may be that the individual marks can be enumerated and are distinct from one another, so that they stand in a relation of clarity to one another, while each mark is still confused in itself: the individual component marks are known clearly, "but nevertheless confusedly." What is clear can still be confused. Leibniz calls such knowledge, in which the marks are clear but only clear, inadequate. What lies in distinct knowledge of this kind is still limited and admits further clarification; the confused marks can each be resolved into distinct elements. The inadequate case shows that confusion recurs at each level of analysis. The marks that make a notion distinct can themselves be clear but confused, so distinctness is always relative to a level of analysis.

"When everything that enters into a distinct notion is itself known distinctly, that is, when analysis has been carried through to the end, knowledge is adequate." Adequate knowledge is distinct through and through; no confusion remains in it, and the resolution into marks and requisites is available as completed. Leibniz immediately remarks that he does not know whether humans can give a perfect example of it, though the knowledge of numbers comes very close. Here again, the primary interest lies in constructing the idea of adequate knowledge, irrespective of whether humans, as finitely knowing beings, can actually realize it. And again the mathematical ideal of knowledge appears in connection with this idea. Adequacy is defined by the completion of analysis, and Leibniz's doubt that humans can achieve it repeats the pattern of §2: the essence of knowledge is fixed by an ideal that finite knowers can approach but perhaps never reach, and mathematics is the nearest approximation.

In adequate knowledge the whole content of knowledge is supposed to be held in perfect distinctness. "Supposed to be," in idea; if it were so, we would have a kind of knowledge already familiar: all at once, all things present to it. At first, however, "we do not intuit the whole nature of the thing at once" but "use signs in place of things"; we do not hold a thing's whole content in one intuiting glance but use signs instead of remaining within the total intuition of the thing. When we think a chiliagon, for example, not only can we not picture one adequately, we do not always grasp at the same time the "nature" (essence) of side, equality, the number thousand, and so on. We use the words for what is named, with the accompanying awareness that we know the essence of these determinations and can call it before our eyes at any time. Those "natures" belong to the marks as requisites, which means that marks are not only or primarily individual properties (see below on possibility). Heidegger identifies the connection with God's knowledge. Adequate intuitive knowledge would be the finite mind's version of the *totum simul*, and signs are the substitute finitude uses for the unavailable whole. The chiliagon is Descartes's example (Sixth Meditation) of what can be understood but not imagined, and Leibniz turns it into an example of symbolic thought.

Adequate representation proceeds by using representative, indicating signs, symbols. Such adequate knowledge is therefore called symbolic or blind, because we do not behold the totality of distinct "marks" individually or as a whole; we know them, know of them, and can move among them, but we do not see them. Heidegger's phrase "move among them" describes calculation. Symbolic knowledge operates reliably on what it does not see, which is the epistemic basis of Leibniz's universal characteristic and of calculative thinking generally.

"And certainly when a notion is very composite, we cannot think all the notions entering it at once; but when this is possible," that is, when all are thought together, "I call the knowledge intuitive." It is the most perfect knowledge. Heidegger marks the "all together" with an exclamation. The criterion of intuitive knowledge is simultaneity, the finite analogue of the divine *totum simul*.

Heidegger insists that what Leibniz calls blind knowledge differs essentially from obscure and from confused knowledge. For knowledge to be blind, it must be adequate, perfectly distinct with regard to a composite notion, and only such adequate knowledge can in principle be blind. It takes a high, the highest, stage of the process of knowledge to be capable of blindness. It follows that the last two characters, blind or symbolic and intuitive, arise from a different respect of consideration than the preceding ones. The earlier pairs (obscure and clear, confused and distinct, inadequate and adequate) each concern a stage of analysis, a stage in the explicitness of marks and requisites. The final distinction concerns two possible ways of appropriating and possessing what is adequate, what has been completely analyzed as such. The point corrects the usual reading of the schema as a ladder. Blindness is not a lower rung but a way of having the top rung, so the opposition of symbolic and intuitive cuts across the scale of analysis instead of extending it.

That intuitive knowledge is not a new degree of analysis placed above adequate knowledge, but expresses the way what has already been fully analyzed is appropriated, shows itself in the fact that intuitive knowledge is possible at a stage before and below adequate knowledge, namely with distinct knowledge, when "the notion is primitive and lacks requisites." A simple representation can no longer be resolved; it is totally resolved, adequate, and the highest possible stage of analysis has already been reached. Hence Leibniz: of a distinct primitive notion there is no knowledge other than intuitive, just as the thought of composite notions is mostly only symbolic. The primitive notion is the decisive case. It is at once minimally complex and maximally analyzed, and it can be known only intuitively, which shows that intuition is a mode of possession and not a quantity of analysis.

Knowledge can be blind or seeing (intuitive). Intuition is not a still higher degree of analysis but a mode of appropriating the highest stage of analysis, its result, adequate knowledge. Intuition in Leibniz's sense presupposes adequacy, which lies in its concept. Intuition is not simply "intuition" but "adequate intuition": to intuit the whole distinctly at once, to bring it wholly into presence (*totum distinctum simul intueri; in totum praesens ducere*). Heidegger's Latin formulas, which are his own condensations, again define the ideal of knowledge as bringing the whole into presence.

Only now does it become fully clear that Leibniz's concept of intuition is not a general title for an indeterminate intuiting but means the manner of beholding, of having, an absolutely adequate knowledge. The concept of intuition is oriented by the ideal of the way God, as the simplest being (*ens simplicissimum*), knows. Intuition is, first, a direct grasping, and second, the grasping of something no longer further analyzable, in its wholeness. The two features, directness and wholeness, are the features of divine knowledge in Thomas. Leibniz's epistemology thus places the theological ideal at the summit of the scale of human knowledge.

The concept of adequate knowledge must not be taken as superficially as it usually is, when one speaks simply of stages and degrees of knowledge without attending to the kind of gradation. In adequate knowledge, compared with distinct knowledge whose marks are merely clear, the marks are also distinct, so no confusion remains. It looks as though adequate knowledge has only a little more distinctness, as though it were distinct with respect to the sum of all marks. But adequate knowledge has not merely "quantitatively more" distinctness; it has something essentially different. Analysis is carried to the end. All marks are present distinctly, among them the "natures," the essential determinations, what makes the thing itself possible. In this analysis, the distinct connection of marks that are distinct in themselves is revealed as a possible connection. Heidegger adds that in Leibniz the difference between essence and existence is again fluid. The step from distinct to adequate knowledge is a step from features to possibility. Adequate knowledge sees not just that marks co-occur but that they can belong together, which makes it knowledge of essence. The remark about the fluidity of essence and existence notes the cost: if the complete concept of an individual includes its existence, knowledge of possibility and knowledge of fact threaten to merge.

"Whenever adequate knowledge is had, a priori knowledge of possibility is also had; for when analysis has been carried to the end, if no contradiction appears, the notion is certainly possible." In adequate knowledge we grasp the "what," the material content, or, as Leibniz says in this article, the "reality of notions" (*realitas notionum*), what the concepts mean in their what-being, their essence. Possibility is the inner possibility of the thing. As knowledge of essence, adequate knowledge is a priori knowledge of what makes the known possible, for it is the distinct grasp of thoroughgoing compatibility, "when we resolve the notion into its requisites or into other notions of known possibility and know that nothing in them is incompatible." Two things are joined here. The test for possibility is non-contradiction, and the content of possibility is "reality," the positive determinations of the thing. Possibility therefore means the compatibility of realities, and adequate knowledge is the a priori grasp of that compatibility. The formula prepares Heidegger's reinterpretation of identity later in the section.

Merely distinct but not yet adequate knowledge corresponds to enumeration, the listing of marks sufficient to distinguish one thing from another. This enumeration, which does not penetrate to the distinctness of the individual marks, reveals only their juxtaposition, not their inner structure, their inner compatibility and connection in their possibility. That requires adequate knowledge, which gives insight into the reality of notions. Adequate knowledge therefore corresponds to the real definition, "from which it is established that the thing is possible." A merely distinct knowledge does not suffice for a real definition. There is thus an inner, fundamental difference between distinct and adequate knowledge, between nominal and real definition. The real definition does not list more marks; it shows how the marks belong together, and so it shows that the thing can be. The contrast between juxtaposition and inner structure anticipates the question of what unifies a manifold, which Leibniz will answer with the monad's unifying drive.

Kant draws almost exactly the same distinction in his *Logic* lectures (§106). Heidegger calls this "fundamentally important" for understanding Kant's concept of reality: ignorance of these connections misled the whole Neo-Kantian interpretation of the *Critique of Pure Reason* into looking for a theory of knowledge in Kant. Under the title "objective reality of the categories," Kant seeks a way to understand the essence of the categories so that they can be real determinations of objects (appearances) without having to be empirical properties of them. If determinations of being are not ontic properties of beings, how do they still belong to the *realitas*, the thinghood, of objects? Their reality, their belonging to the material content, is transcendental, "that is, finite, horizonal-ecstatic" (p. 81); compare Kant's distinction between the empirical reality and transcendental ideality of space and time. The paragraph compresses Heidegger's reading of Kant. "Reality" in Kant means thinghood, material content, not existence, and the problem of the objective reality of the categories is the ontological problem of how determinations of being belong to beings without being properties among others. His answer, "horizonal-ecstatic," refers this belonging to the temporal horizon of transcendence, the doctrine of the Second Main Part (§12), and to *Kant and the Problem of Metaphysics*.

Leibniz sees the ideal of knowledge in intuition. Intuitive knowledge is necessarily adequate where it concerns composite notions, or distinct where it concerns primitive notions, which need not require adequacy. But the construction of the ideal is not an assertion that it is or even can be realized. Realizing it would mean that we finite beings were equal to God, the absolutely knowing one, able to know what God knows and as God knows it, absolutely, and so to know God himself directly and absolutely. In any case, Leibniz does not venture to decide whether such knowledge, which would know the first essential possibilities of all beings, is possible for us: "whether humans can ever carry out a perfect analysis of notions, or reduce their thoughts to first possibles and irresolvable notions, or, what comes to the same, to the absolute attributes of God themselves, the first causes and the ultimate reason of things, I do not now dare to determine." Usually we are content to have learned the reality of certain notions from experience and then to compose others from them on nature's model. Leibniz equates the first possibles with the attributes of God, so the perfect analysis would terminate in knowledge of God. His modest conclusion, that we mostly take the reality of concepts from experience, concedes to finitude exactly what Kant will make a principle: concepts receive real content only from what is given.

It now becomes clear how far Leibniz's critique hits Descartes's doctrine at its center and grasps the problem of ideas more radically. Leibniz agrees with Descartes, and with the whole metaphysical tradition, that knowledge of beings presupposes the grasping of "ideas," knowledge of essence, whether conscious or not. But against Descartes he denies that the principle of knowledge Descartes stated as a general rule suffices for knowledge of ideas themselves: "I now seem able to establish as a general rule that everything I perceive very clearly and distinctly is true" (Third Meditation; *Principles* I, 43–45). Leibniz gives stricter concepts of clarity and obscurity, distinctness and confusion, and he shows that above them lies an essentially higher stage at which knowledge of essence is first attained, since only there is the whole of the requisites of realities disclosed. The general rule is not a criterion of knowledge of essence and ideas; it remains below it. Heidegger locates the critique precisely. Clarity and distinctness in Descartes's sense stop at the level of distinct knowledge, which enumerates marks without grasping their compatibility; they cannot certify the possibility of an essence, which is what knowledge of ideas requires.

Leibniz's critique does not reach the center either. Only Kant, who attacks Leibniz himself, grasps the problem of the possibility of materially contentful ontological knowledge of essence more radically, asking anew about the conditions of possibility and grounding ontological knowledge in the transcendental imagination; intuition takes on a constitutive character. Phenomenology today speaks of the "intuition" of essences. What this common but misleading expression aims at can be clarified only by radicalizing the whole problem. The sentence contains a critique of Husserl. The phenomenological "seeing" of essences is misleading because it inherits the Leibnizian orientation of knowledge by intuitive presence, and Heidegger implies that it too must be radicalized in the direction Kant opened, toward the finite, temporal ground of ontological knowledge.

Leibniz therefore says of Descartes's principle: "I see that people of our time abuse that much-vaunted principle, whatever I perceive clearly and distinctly of a thing is true or can be stated of it. For what seems clear and distinct to people who judge rashly is often obscure and confused. The axiom is useless unless the criteria of clear and distinct we have given are applied, and unless the truth of ideas is established." Heidegger renders the last clause as meaning "unless the axiom is established in its function as criterion for the truth of ideas," and calls the Cassirer–Buchenau rendering, "unless the truth of ideas has been proved," senseless. Elsewhere Leibniz compares the abuse of Descartes's principle with theologians' arbitrary appeal to the inner testimony of the Holy Spirit. Heidegger's correction of the translation is substantive. On his reading, the problem is not that the truth of ideas must first be proved before the axiom can be used, which would be circular, but that the axiom must itself be secured as a criterion. The comparison with the testimony of the Spirit indicates what an unsecured criterion amounts to: private certainty presented as evidence.

What does this discussion yield for the guiding problem? Earlier, truth equaled identity, being true equaled being the same. Now Leibniz says against Descartes, in substance if not in so many words, that being true is not being clearly and distinctly perceived but being adequately and intuitively perceived. Truth was first determined with reference to the assertion, as being identical; now it is determined with reference to the idea of knowledge as such. How does the earlier definition, true equals being the same, go together with the present one, true equals being adequately and intuitively perceived? Does being the same have anything at all to do with being adequately and intuitively perceived? There seems to be an insoluble difficulty: identity and adequate intuitive apprehension cannot be brought together as definitions of one and the same concept, the concept of truth. What does identity mean? "Idem" means the same; is it meant that several things are the same? The difficulty is real. One definition locates truth in the proposition, as a relation of terms; the other locates it in knowing, as a mode of apprehension. The question "what does identity mean?" signals that the solution will come from rethinking identity.

If one looks more sharply at what has been said, the difficulty is not only resolvable but puts us in a position to grasp more clearly what Leibniz means by "identity," without his discussing it explicitly. Heidegger claims to understand Leibniz's concept of identity better than Leibniz did, by reading it through the theory of adequate knowledge.

In adequate knowledge, what is known is the whole of the requisites, what as a whole constitutes the reality of a thing. What is known in this way is the true. This whole of requisites is possibility, what makes the thinghood of the thing possible. This content of the thing is compatible within itself, for only in compatibility can it make possible. Incompatibility, as conflict, breaks the essence of a thing apart; it disintegrates in itself and "cannot be." What adequate knowledge knows is the connection of mutually compatible determinations of a thing, and the thing, when adequately known, is known precisely in the compatibility of its realities. "Adequate knowledge is the total grasp of the accord of the manifold" (p. 84). The formula replaces the language of containment with that of accord. What adequate knowledge sees is not one term inside another but a manifold that holds together, and possibility is that holding-together.

Earlier it emerged that judgment is a connection, a *connexio realis*, and that this connection is conceived as identity. What stands in the nexus and is grasped in the connection does not fall apart or conflict; everything is at one within itself and, as a determination, concerns one and the same "what," the identical in its identity. Heidegger reads the identity of the judgment through the unity of the thing. What makes subject and predicate "the same" is that both determine a single, internally consistent content.

Identity therefore means at bottom not empty sameness devoid of all difference but, on the contrary, the whole wealth of real determinations in their compatibility, free of conflict. "Identity is not the negative concept of the absence of all difference but, conversely, the idea of the accord of what is different" (p. 84). This is the section's main result, and it reaches beyond Leibniz. Identity understood as the belonging-together of the different, not as the formal A = A, is a theme Heidegger will develop decades later in *Identity and Difference* (1957). Here it serves to show that Leibniz's identity theory of truth is richer than its formula: to be true is to state a determination that belongs, without conflict, to the unity of a thing.

It must be noted, and this is essential for Leibniz, that he at the same time uses identity in the empty formal sense (A = A) and equates the two, or tries to derive identity in the sense of the accord and belonging-together of the different from formal identity in the sense of empty sameness with itself. This is possible only in a certain way, in the ideal of God's simplicity: there, absolute simplicity and the whole of the totality of reality are united. The two concepts of identity are held together only in God, where the simplest is also the richest. Leibniz's logic therefore depends on his theology not only for its ideal of knowledge but for the coherence of its central concept.

If the unity of identity means the compatible oneness of what belongs together, then the two characterizations of the essence of truth, the true as being the same and the true as adequately perceived, go together and mean the same. Once identity is read as accord, the apparent difficulty disappears: what is adequately perceived is the accord of a thing's determinations, and what is identical is that same accord expressed in the proposition.

For Kant, the concept of truth and the concept of identity are joined in the original synthetic unity of transcendental apperception. Here too, by way of the truth of judgment, one goes back to the condition of the possibility of every act of knowledge. The "I" is the subject whose predicates are all representations, everything that enters into knowledge in any way. Heidegger transposes the Leibnizian structure onto Kant. The "I think" plays the role of the complete subject: all representations are its predicates, and its unity is the identity in which truth is grounded. This transposition anticipates §5, where the monad itself will be understood on the model of the self-representing I.

Only now does it become fully clear how the concept of knowledge is connected with the idea of what is in the highest sense and with its being. Intuition and identity, as essential characters of truth and knowledge, the "logical" in the broadest sense, are drawn from God's simplicity as the guiding ideal of what genuinely is. Because the identity theory of judgment points back into these metaphysical connections, into this conception of being, and because all judging and knowledge is knowledge of beings, it must now be shown how Leibniz orients the interpretation of being by the same ideal. Heidegger adds in parentheses that the exemplary being, God, also appears in the light of a definite conception of being as such. The parenthesis blocks the conclusion that God is the final ground. Theology does not explain the understanding of being; the idea of God is itself projected within a prior understanding of being as presence, which is why the analysis must go further than the theological ideal.

Moreover, the logical concept of subject and predicate refers directly back to the ontological-metaphysical concept (*Discourse* §8), and the metaphysical concept of the subject, the individual substance, is supposed to express what genuinely is. A connection between the interpretation of being and the theory of judgment must therefore show itself. The transition names the task of §5: to show that the monad, as the true subject, is conceived so that its being is the ground of the identity theory of truth.

#### §5. The Essential Determination of the Being of Genuine Beings

##### a) The Monad as Drive

Genuine knowledge is adequate knowledge; it contains in full distinctness the whole of the mutually belonging determinations that a being contains. Adequate knowledge is the complete or perfect notion (*notio completa seu perfecta*), in which all predicates determining the subject are available. This subject of the proposition is at the same time the subject as the underlying being itself, the individual substance. Hence Leibniz: "the complete or perfect notion of an individual substance involves all its predicates, past, present, and future" (*Primae veritates*); and if a notion is complete, such that from it a reason can be given for all the predicates of the subject to which it can be attributed, it will be the notion of an individual substance, and conversely. For an individual substance is a subject that is in no other subject, while others are in it, so that all predicates of the same subject are all predicates of the same individual substance. The logical and the ontological coincide exactly at this point. The complete concept is the concept of a substance and nothing else; conversely, a substance is whatever has a complete concept. The phrase "past, present, and future" gives the individual's whole temporal career to its concept.

The identification of the subject of true propositions with the individual substance, as what genuinely is, is clear. What remains undetermined is the being of this being, the substantiality of the substance. A further question is how the metaphysical characterization of the individual substance's substantiality is connected with the logical identity theory of judgment, or what "logical" then still means. The last clause anticipates the outcome of the First Main Part. If the identity theory turns out to rest on a determination of being, then "logic" in Leibniz is already ontology, and the traditional separation of the disciplines loses its sense.

The doctrine of the substantiality of individual substance is set out in the monadology, the core of Leibniz's metaphysics. The main thesis to be explained is: the individual substance is a monad. Leibniz's interpretation of being is monadological. The thesis identifies the answer to the question of substantiality, and the rest of §5 unpacks what "monad" means for the understanding of being.

The main texts are the *Discourse on Metaphysics* (1686), the correspondence with Arnauld (1686 ff.), *On the Correction of First Philosophy and the Notion of Substance* (*Acta Eruditorum*, 1694), the *New System of Nature and of the Communication of Substances* (*Journal des Savants*, 1695), the *Principles of Nature and Grace* (1714), and the *Monadology* (1714). Leibniz wrote the *Monadology* in French during his last stay in Vienna, two years before his death; it too went unpublished in his lifetime. A Latin translation appeared in 1721 in the *Acta Eruditorum* under the title *Principia philosophiae*, and in 1840 J. E. Erdmann published the original for the first time, giving it the title *La Monadologie*. Heidegger's inclusion of the 1694 article in the canon is pointed; he will make it the key text of his interpretation, whereas the usual reading centers on the *Monadology*.

Heidegger can present only certain features essential for the course, not the whole context and all the motives, especially since much that is essential in Leibniz is only indicated and not systematically worked out from the ground up. A note refers to the treatment of the monadology in the lecture course *Introduction to Philosophy* (winter 1928–29) and to the seminar on truth and certainty in Descartes and Leibniz (winter 1929–30). Here, as in other doctrines, much remains at the stage of first conception, as it mostly arose in polemic. There is a separate work, the *Monadology*, but characteristically it consists of ninety theses written one after another. Heidegger's emphasis on the unfinished, polemical, and aphoristic character of Leibniz's metaphysics licenses the constructive interpretation he is about to announce. Because Leibniz did not work his metaphysics out systematically, the interpreter must reconstruct its guiding intention.

The monadological metaphysics is therefore open to the most varied interpretations, each of which can appeal to Leibniz for some part, as well as the others. That, Heidegger says, is true of all genuine philosophy; only about sham philosophy is there unanimity, "the unanimity of the rabble" (p. 88). It would be a completely misguided idea of philosophy to think that by skillfully offsetting and balancing all interpretations of Kant or of Plato one could distill "the" Kant or "the" Plato. Nor would it make sense for Leibniz. What would result is something dead. "Kant in himself" is an idea contrary to the essence of history and even more to the history of philosophy. The historical Kant is always only the Kant who reveals himself in an original possibility of philosophizing, only in part, if you like, but a genuine part, in which the whole takes effect. Heidegger turns the plurality of interpretations from a defect into a mark of genuine philosophy. A "Kant in himself" is the historiographical counterpart of a thing in itself, a fixed object set apart from any questioning relation to it, and Heidegger rejects it on the same grounds.

The reality of the historical, especially of what is past, is not expressed in the most complete possible knowledge that "it was thus and so." The reality of what has been lies in its possibility, which becomes manifest only as the answer to a living question that a future present poses to itself in the sense of "What can we do?" "The objectivity of the historical lies in the inexhaustibility of possibilities, not in the rigid finality of a result" (p. 88). The paragraph applies the doctrine of *Being and Time* §76, that historical inquiry discloses what has been as possibility, to the practice of interpretation itself. The "future present" that questions the past is Dasein's own futurity, so the temporality of Dasein grounds the objectivity of history.

This must be kept in mind for the interpretation of the monadology that follows. Here above all an interpretation must be risked that goes beyond Leibniz, or better, goes back into him more originally, even at the risk of straying far from what he actually said. Heidegger announces openly that his reading of the monad will not be a report. Its legitimacy rests on the principle just stated: the past is real as possibility, and interpretation earns its right by retrieving a possibility the author did not himself fully grasp.

Two things must be kept in mind. First, as an interpretation of the substantiality of substance, the monadology is meant to determine the being of genuine beings. It is thus ontology, metaphysics, and indeed general metaphysics, since it seeks a concept of being that applies to every genuine being, whether physical nature, living things (plant, animal), what exists in the manner of the human being, or God. At the same time, this concept of being must allow the differences among these beings to be fixed within the unity of a general concept. Second, the essence of ontological knowledge, and so its explicit execution, remains largely obscure and groping in Leibniz. Hence his monadological interpretation of substance, precisely where it pursues more radical tendencies, must become opaque and mix with other things. Leibniz's thinking is only a preparation for the separation of metaphysical from non-metaphysical knowledge; it comes to light in Kant and is then buried again completely. Heidegger's vocabulary is exact: "what exists in the manner of the human being" reserves "existence" for Dasein, as in *Being and Time*, while listing it among the regions a general metaphysics must cover. The second point states the problem of the course's own method. Leibniz does not distinguish ontological from ontic questions clearly, so his ontology of force is constantly entangled with his physics, and the task is to separate the two.

Heidegger recalls the guiding problem. The monadology is to shed light on the being of beings (substance). What is known so far is that the being judged about, the subject, is determined by the whole of its mutually compatible marks, by their belonging together, by identity as the unity of this manifold. Is there a connection between the interpretation of beings as identity and their interpretation as monad, and if so, what is it? Does uncovering it give insight into what is sought, the metaphysical foundations of Leibniz's logic, and thereby exemplary insight into the rootedness of logic as such in metaphysics as such? The question joins the two lines of the inquiry. §4 reinterpreted identity as the unity of a manifold, and the monad will be a unity of a manifold. If the monad's unity turns out to be the ontological ground of the logical concept of identity, the destruction has reached its goal.

The very word Leibniz chooses for the substantiality of substance is telling. The Greek *monas* means the simple, the unit, the one, but also the single, the solitary. All of these senses will be used. Unity, simplicity, and solitude are each features of the monad: it unifies, it has no parts, and it has no windows.

Leibniz adopted the term only later, from 1696, after his metaphysics of substance had already been formed; he took it from F. M. van Helmont or Giordano Bruno. What Leibniz means by monad gathers all the basic Greek meanings: the essence of substance lies in its being a monad, which is to say that what genuinely is has the character of the simple "unity" of what is single and stands for itself. Heidegger anticipates his result: the monad is "what unifies simply, originally, and in advance, and in unifying individuates" (p. 90). The formula already turns the monad from a unit into an activity of unifying, which is the thesis of the whole subsection. That this unifying also individuates reinterprets the old problem of the principle of individuation, the topic of Leibniz's dissertation, in terms of the monad's own activity.

The direction in which Leibniz seeks to conceive the essence of substance more originally and universally becomes clear against the immediately preceding attempts of Descartes and Spinoza. Descartes (*Principles* I, 51) understands by substance "nothing other than a thing that so exists that it needs no other thing in order to exist." Here the characteristic feature is lack of need. Spinoza (*Ethics* I, def. 3) understands by substance "what is in itself and is conceived through itself, that is, that whose concept does not need the concept of another thing from which it must be formed." On this definition substance subsists in itself, but here too the essential remains negatively determined. Heidegger singles out the logical form of both definitions: they state what substance does not need. Neither says what substance positively is.

Subsisting in itself and underlying without reduction have always been thought in the concept of substance, as the term *hypokeimenon* shows. What Descartes and Spinoza add is either essentially negative or refers to the way substance is apprehended. Leibniz, in contrast, wants to determine the substantiality of substance positively. The question is how far characterizing substance as monad, as "unity," is such a positive interpretation, and what "unity" means here. Spinoza's addition, "conceived through itself," is the second case, a determination from the side of knowledge. Leibniz's demand for a positive determination is what drives him from independence to activity.

The monadology is general metaphysics; even so, or precisely because of this, it must be oriented by all beings. The problem of the being of nature, physical-inorganic and organic, which was burning in Leibniz's time, is therefore one guiding thread for conceiving the monadology, though neither the only one nor the ultimately decisive one. The qualification prepares subsection b, where Heidegger identifies the decisive guiding thread as the self-experience of the I.

Descartes sought the being of physical nature, the corporeal thing, in extension. Extension is the primary attribute, and all properties of physical things are supposed to be reducible to it. All determinations of natural events concern change, motion, and all motion is local motion, change of place; motion is to be considered scientifically only in this respect. So considered, it becomes accessible to what counts as the strictest theoretical grasp, the mathematical-geometrical. The Cartesians extended this theory above all to organic nature, to plants and animals, the latter also being regarded as nexuses of mere changes of place, purely mechanically: animals as machines. The mere reference to this region of beings, organic life, could set a limit to Descartes's ontology of nature. But it is inadequate even within physical-inorganic nature. Descartes explicitly tried to eliminate the concept of force from physics ("let us not inquire into the force by which local motion is excited," *Principles* I, 65). More precisely, he had no physically determined concept of force; by *vis* he thought of the "occult qualities" of scholastic natural philosophy. Heidegger's point is that Descartes's ontology fails on its own ground, inorganic nature, not only at the border of the living. The rejection of force was a rejection of scholastic qualities, which is why Leibniz can reintroduce force while claiming to have purified it.

Leibniz aims at a dynamics as a basic part of mathematical physics itself. But that does not capture his genuinely philosophical intention. The metaphysical-ontological intention always goes together with the physical-ontic one, without a clear separation of domains, kinds of questioning, and grounding. To assess the metaphysical significance and motivation of the tendency toward a dynamics in physics, one must attend to something else, namely what first demands the monadological structure of physical nature as well. Heidegger separates Leibniz's contribution to physics, dynamics, from his ontological motive, which is not a better account of motion but a problem that the concept of extension raises for the being of bodies as such.

If one accepts Descartes's ontological starting point, on which the essence of a natural thing lies in its extension, then every extended thing can ultimately be reduced to the last elements of extension, mathematical points. These are in a way "unities," but only as limits. This has two senses. Points are limits that are themselves divisible to infinity and never lose their character as limits. And a point, as limit, is no longer a cube, surface, or line, but only their boundary. Such unity is determined purely negatively, as later becomes explicit in Hegel's metaphysics of space: the point is simple negation, it is not all the other spatial figures in space, and as this primary "not" of the rest, each point is at the same time the negation of the others. The point is a unity only in the sense of a remainder of a total splintering of the whole, a remainder that is still left but in principle further dissolvable. The point is the limit case of division and so the product of the manifold it was supposed to found. A unity arrived at by dividing is always derivative of what it divides.

The basic element of extension has only the character of limit and negation. As this negative residue, it cannot be fit to unify a whole; it can only be something that enters into a unification and needs one. If extension is to constitute the ontological essence of a natural thing, then the original element of this essence, the mathematical point, can never make intelligible or ground the ontological unity of a being that is for itself. The decisive consideration is this: if extension constitutes the corporeal thing metaphysically, and every being is one (*omne ens unum*), then the basic element of extension, the mathematical point, must constitute the unity of a corporeal thing. Is that possible? No. If not, extension is not the essence, and a new determination of substance, and of what "unity" means, is required. The argument turns on the transcendental *omne ens unum*: unity belongs to being as such, so whatever constitutes a being must be able to confer unity. The point cannot, since it is at most the result of dividing a unity. The argument thus makes unity-conferring the criterion of substantiality.

Leibniz also argued against Descartes that what is conserved is not the quantity of motion but the magnitude of force, measured not by m·v (mass times velocity) but by m·v². He concluded that something belongs to body that lies beyond mass and local motion, beyond extension. The physical argument supplements the ontological one. Conserved force is a real feature of bodies not derivable from extension, so the essence of body must include something active.

In the *New System*, Leibniz writes that when he first freed himself from Aristotle's yoke, he favored the void and atoms, since these principles best satisfy the imagination (Heidegger corrects Buchenau's "sensible intuition"). After much reflection he saw that it is impossible to find the principles of a true unity in mere matter or in what is passive, since there everything is only a collection or heap of parts to infinity. Heidegger's gloss: the negative, the passive, which itself needs unification, cannot be the principle of unity, of what truly unifies. The principle of unity must therefore be sought in something positively unifying and thus active. Multiplicity is possible in its essence, its reality, only on the ground of a true unity. Leibniz continues: multiplicity can have its reality only from true unities, which have another origin and are something quite different from mathematical points, which are only limits and modifications of the extended and certainly cannot produce the continuum by composition. To find these real unities, he had to go back to a real and, so to speak, animated point, to a substantial atom that must include something formal (form-giving) or active in order to make a complete being. He was therefore compelled to recall the substantial forms so decried today and in a way to restore them to honor. The correction "imagination" for "sensible intuition" matters because atomism satisfies the picture-making faculty, not perception. Leibniz's rejection of atomism is a rejection of picturing as a guide to ontology. Heidegger's gloss states the principle that governs the rest of the section: unity must be prior to multiplicity and must be active, because only what unifies can ground what is unified.

Leibniz expressly notes that these substantial forms must not and cannot serve "to explain the particular problems of nature"; they have no ontic function in the positive science of nature. They are, however, "necessary for establishing true general principles." The "animated points" are thus an ontological-metaphysical problem. These points are unities, but animated unities: monads. The "formal atom" is not a remnant of matter, of what is determinable, but what determines. Heidegger reads Leibniz's disclaimer as the clearest evidence of the ontological status of the monad. Substantial forms explain nothing in physics because they are not causes among causes but determinations of what a body is, and the "formal atom" is formal in the Aristotelian sense of form as determining principle.

If one takes the doctrine externally, not in its purely metaphysical intention (which Leibniz himself does not always maintain), and understands the monadology in a popular, naive way, it looks like universal animation: everything down to the atoms of bodies teems with little souls and spirits (Leibniz himself speaks of "little gods"). One can then tell the stock stories: Leibniz has a dynamic instead of a mechanical worldview, he "represents" spiritualism instead of materialism, his explanation of the world is teleological instead of one-sidedly causal, and so on through "the nursery tales of the usual history of philosophy" (p. 94). The target is the textbook classification of philosophies as "worldviews" by opposed isms. Heidegger's objection is that such labels treat an ontological thesis about the being of beings as an ontic claim about what things are made of.

Against this, one must assume, and there is reason to, that the monadological interpretation of beings was undertaken with a genuine philosophical intention. In bringing out this intention, the problem of substantiality as a metaphysical-ontological problem becomes authoritative for the interpretation, although Leibniz himself is not clear in this respect everywhere, since the very concept of metaphysics is confused. The interpretive decision is explicit: the monad will be read as an answer to the question of being, even where Leibniz's own formulations point toward ontic speculation.

The methodological maxim is: first clarity about the guiding intention, then about what Leibniz himself says of the function of the monadological interpretation of nature. The task is to lay bare the genuinely philosophical core of the monadology. Only afterward may one note, in retrospect, how far Leibniz's intention was possible and carried out, and by what and why it was necessarily disfigured. This did not happen by accident, but because "every philosophy, as a human affair, fails at its innermost core; and God needs no philosophy" (p. 95). But for a philosophy even to be able to fail, the effort must be undertaken to make it succeed. The remark ties the failure of philosophy to finitude. Philosophy is a human, finite undertaking, and its necessary failure is the counterpart of the infinite knowledge that needs no philosophy, the same contrast that governed Leibniz's theory of truth in §2. Failure is no objection to philosophy but a mark of its seriousness, since only a real attempt can fail.

Heidegger explains that he deliberately began his first characterization of the monad, the "animated," animating points, with extension, to show that the monadological problem also arises where beings do not have the mode of being of life. The strategy blocks the reading of the monad as a projection of life onto matter. The problem of unity arises from the analysis of extension itself.

Three things are to be held fast about the idea of the monad. First, the unities, the points, do not themselves need unifying; they are what give unity; they are capable of something. Second, as conferring unity, they are themselves originally unifying, in a certain sense active; hence Leibniz calls them *vis primitiva*, *force primitive*, original simple force. Third, this conception has a metaphysical-ontological intention; Leibniz therefore also calls these points "metaphysical points," not mathematical ones, and "formal atoms," not material ones. They are not the ultimate tiny pieces of matter but the original, indivisible principle of forming, form, *eidos*. The three points state the logic of the concept: unity is something done, not something found, and what does it is a force. "Force" is thus introduced as the name for the activity of unifying, not as a physical quantity.

Every being for itself is constituted as a monad, which is to say that "what persists has a primitive force" (letter to de Volder, 21 January 1704). What does it mean that every being for itself is endowed with force? Understanding the metaphysical sense of the doctrine of monads depends on grasping the concept of primitive force correctly. The quotation joins persistence, the traditional mark of substance, to force: what endures does so by virtue of an active power. The question about the sense of force leads to the analysis of drive.

The problem of substantiality is to be solved positively, and for Leibniz it is a problem of "unity," of the monad. Everything said about "force" and its metaphysical function must be understood from this horizon of the positive determination of the unity of substance. The character of "force" is to be understood from the problem of unity that lies in substantiality. This is the interpretive key Heidegger uses throughout. Force is not an addition to substance but the answer to the question of how substance is one.

Only now are we learning again to see the fundamental importance and the difficulty of the problems of general metaphysics. Only the complete break of present-day philosophizing with the great ancient tradition explains its helplessness before metaphysical problems. This alone can explain why Leibniz's monadology has not been grasped radically from its essential intentions, although Leibniz unambiguously set out the general metaphysical sense and importance of the monadological problem of substance in the short article *On the Correction of First Philosophy and the Notion of Substance*, that is, on improving first philosophy, general metaphysics or ontology, by clarifying the concept of substance through a monadological interpretation of substantiality. Heidegger reads the article's title as a program for ontology: the reform of first philosophy through the concept of substance, which is what the course undertakes through the concept of Dasein.

The article covers one page in Erdmann's edition. Whether one penetrates to the ontological sense of the monadology or leaves it in the usual popular-philosophical shallowness depends on understanding it. Its theme is the nature of substance in general, substantiality as such. The weight placed on a single page is characteristic of the course's method: a short text read with maximal care in place of a survey of the doctrine.

Leibniz claims that his concept of substance is so fruitful that from it follow the primary truths concerning God, minds, and the nature of bodies, truths partly known but little demonstrated, partly unknown so far but of the greatest use for the other sciences. Heidegger glosses "God, minds, and bodies" as all beings and their regions, and "other sciences" as the non-philosophical ones. The substance concept thus proves to be the basic ontological concept as such. The claim of fruitfulness is the claim of a general metaphysics: one concept of being from which the regional ontologies follow.

Before his brief discussion of substance, Leibniz offers a general reflection on the need to clarify the basic metaphysical concepts, the difficulties of the task, and previous attempts. Mathematics, with its definitions, again serves as the model of clarity and distinctness, although here Leibniz sees in a certain way that clarifying and grounding the basic concepts of metaphysics has a different character. He observes that those who delight in mathematics mostly shrink from metaphysics, because they find light in the one and darkness in the other. The main cause, in his view, is that general notions, believed to be the best known to all, have been made ambiguous and obscure by human negligence and inconstancy of thought, and the usual definitions are not even nominal definitions and explain nothing. The true and fruitful notions not only of substance but of cause, action, relation, similarity, and many other general terms remain hidden. No wonder, then, that the principal science that goes under the name of first philosophy has remained among the things still sought. Leibniz's diagnosis matches Heidegger's own in *Being and Time* §1: the most general concepts pass as the most self-evident and are for that reason the most obscure. His claim that the usual definitions "are not even nominal" applies the scale of knowledge from §4 to metaphysics, which on this account falls below mere distinctness.

Leibniz continues: in metaphysics more light and certainty is needed than in mathematics itself, because mathematical matters carry their own tests and confirmations with them, the chief cause of their success, while in metaphysics this advantage is lacking. A special manner of exposition is needed, like a thread in the labyrinth, while preserving a clarity that makes no concession to popular talk. Heidegger's paraphrase: in contrast to what suffices for the progress of mathematics, metaphysics requires its own manner of presentation and clarification; this clarity is to be sought, which does not mean making concessions to popular chatter and so-called general intelligibility. The passage anticipates the course's argument that metaphysics cannot borrow its method from mathematics. Mathematics can check itself by construction, while metaphysics lacks any such external test and must find its own "thread."

Measuring general metaphysics by mathematics occurs in a way already in Plato, and since Descartes it happens as a matter of principle, so that even Kant must discuss the relation explicitly in the Doctrine of Method, "The Discipline of Pure Reason in Its Dogmatic Use" (A 712–38, B 740–66): "philosophical knowledge is rational knowledge from concepts, mathematical knowledge from the construction of concepts" (A 713, B 741). Heidegger adds that in this methodological reflection Kant did not reach the level of clarity present in his actual philosophical interpretation in the Schematism chapter. The judgment is typical of Heidegger's Kant reading: the schematism, where concepts are tied to time, contains Kant's real insight into ontological knowledge, and Kant's official methodology lags behind it.

Leibniz offers a foretaste: the notion of forces or power (which Germans call *Krafft* and the French *la force*), for whose explanation he has designed a special science of dynamics, sheds much light on the true notion of substance. Leibniz asserts only that the concept of force is constitutive for interpreting substantiality; he does not bring to light the inner connection between the character of force and the unity of substance, but limits himself to a preliminary task: making clear what he means by force. He does so by marking it off from the scholastic metaphysical concepts of bare (passive) potency, active potency, and action, which go back to Aristotle's discussion of *dynamis*, *energeia*, and *entelecheia*. Heidegger identifies the gap he will fill. Leibniz states that force explains substance but does not show how force produces unity; that connection is the task Heidegger sets for subsection c.

The theory of passive and active potency is a large and important doctrine in Aristotelian scholasticism. The distinction subdivides a more general one encountered earlier under the title of essence or reality: possibility in the sense of what makes up a thing's essence and makes it possible in its essential content, irrespective of whether this possible is ever realized. Scholasticism calls this possibility objective (or metaphysical) potency, "objective" in the old sense (as in Descartes's *realitas obiectiva*): the *ob-iectum*, what stands over against the mere thinking and imagining of a pure possibility, of the essence of a thing. Distinguished from this objective potency (possibility, essence) is subjective potency, "subject" in the old, not the modern sense: what underlies, what is present for itself; hence also physical potency. It is the capacity, "the ontological capacity of a thing for some perfection or act." The reversal of "objective" and "subjective" between scholastic and modern usage is Heidegger's point. Objective potency is essence as represented; subjective potency is the power residing in the thing that underlies. Leibniz's force belongs on the "subjective" side, in the old sense, as a determination of the underlying being itself.

Subjective (physical) potency is divided into active and passive. Thomas writes in the *Disputed Questions on Power* (q. 1, a. 1) that potency is twofold: active, to which corresponds the act that is operation, and to which the name "potency" seems first to have been given; and passive, to which corresponds the first act, which is form, and to which the name seems to have been extended secondarily. Heidegger interprets: "capacity" means first the capacity to accomplish, to which corresponds the accomplishing itself, act as action. The name "capacity" seems to have been given first to this phenomenon, the ability to carry out, and evidently this phenomenon is the primary occasion for forming the concept of possibility as such. Capacity in the other sense is the capacity to undergo, to let something be made of oneself: capacity as inclination toward something, as disposition. To it corresponds act as form, actuality. "Capacity" as the name of what can be actualized into something seems to be a derivative meaning. Heidegger emphasizes Thomas's genealogy: the concept of possibility originates in the experience of being able to act. The priority of active capacity over passive disposition is the priority Leibniz will radicalize, and it also underlies Heidegger's own concept of possibility as Dasein's ability-to-be.

The double concept of potency corresponds to a double concept of act. Thomas says in the same place: "Act is twofold: first, which is form; and second, which is operation." The first sense means having been effected, better, the actuality of something, "form"; the second means effecting, the activity of something already actual. The numbering follows the matter itself. The order "first act, second act" reflects that a thing must be actual before it can act. Heidegger's remark that "the numbering follows the matter" indicates that the scholastic terminology preserves a real ontological priority.

Passive potency corresponds to act as form, active potency to act as action. But the correspondence differs in each case. Thomas says (*Summa theologiae* I, q. 25, a. 1, ad 1): "Active potency is not divided against act but is founded in it, for each thing acts insofar as it is in act. Passive potency is divided against act, for each thing undergoes insofar as it is in potency." Heidegger explains: the capacity to accomplish is not distinguished from act as actuality; it is nothing independent, since something can accomplish only if it is itself actual. The capacity to become something first, to let something happen to oneself, is distinguished from actuality, since what becomes actual depends on the constitution of what lets something happen to it. The disposition toward something is distinguished from what something can be and become on the basis of that disposition; the disposition itself does not need actualization. The distinction is decisive for Leibniz. Active potency is always already grounded in actuality, whereas passive potency stands opposed to it, so a concept of active power that is itself a kind of actuality is already prepared in Thomas. Leibniz's *vis activa* will push this to the point where power and activity are no longer distinguished.

These distinctions are important in general ontological terms and have not nearly been adequately interpreted and taken up. One must see through the school form of merely listed distinctions. The remark gives the method of the course's engagement with scholasticism: the ordered lists of distinctions contain real phenomena, and interpretation consists in seeing the phenomena through the lists.

Leibniz now marks off his concept of active force, *vis activa*, from active potency. Literally, the two seem to mean the same. But Leibniz writes that active force differs from the bare potency commonly known in the schools, because the active potency of the scholastics, or faculty, is nothing other than a proximate possibility of acting, which still needs an external stimulus, as it were a goad, to be transferred into act. The scholastic active potency is thus a mere being-able-to-act, such that this being able is close to acting but does not yet act. It is "a capacity present in something present at hand, which has not yet come into play" (p. 102). Heidegger's gloss locates the defect of the scholastic concept in its mode of being. Active potency is conceived as a property present at hand in a thing, so the transition to action must come from outside. Leibniz's reform consists in thinking power as something that is not present at hand in this way.

"But active force contains a certain act or *entelecheia*, and is intermediate between the faculty of acting and action itself, and involves a striving (*conatus*)." Active force is already a certain acting, and yet not action in its actual execution; it is a capacity, but not a resting capacity. Heidegger names what Leibniz means "tending toward," better still, to express the specific, already somehow actual moment of acting, "pressing, drive" (*das Drängen, den Drang*). Neither a disposition nor a process is meant, but a being-concerned-with, namely with oneself: "applying oneself to oneself," as in the phrase "he sets himself to it," "being intent on oneself" (*das Sich-selbst-anliegen*). The coinage requires a note. *Sich-selbst-anliegen* plays on *anliegen*, to lie close to, to concern, to be someone's care; *es liegt mir an* means "it matters to me." Heidegger gives drive the reflexive structure of self-concern, the structure he ascribed to Dasein, whose own being "matters to it." Subsection b draws the connection openly: the monad's drive is modeled on the being of the self.

What characterizes drive is that it passes over into acting of itself, not occasionally but essentially. This passing over does not require an impetus from elsewhere. Drive is the impulse that by its essence is driven on by itself.
