Drive not only brings its own cause, in the sense of its release, with it; as such it is always already released, yet in such a way that it remains charged, tensed. Accordingly, drive can be inhibited in its pressing, yet even then it is not identical with a mere resting capacity to act. The removal of the inhibition can first free the pressing. Drive thus needs no additional external cause, only the disappearance of some present inhibition, or, in a fortunate expression of Max Scheler's, "disinhibition" (*Enthemmung*). Leibniz writes: "and so it is carried into operation by itself, and needs no assistance, but only the removal of an impediment." A drawn bow makes the meaning vivid. The drawn bow illustrates the structure exactly. It is not at rest, since it is under tension, and not in motion, since the arrow has not been released; its release requires no new force, only the removal of what holds it. Drive is thus a way of being that is neither actuality nor potency in the scholastic sense but a constant being-underway.

Having clarified active force as drive ("force" is a misleading translation, since it readily suggests a resting property), Leibniz reaches the essential determination: "I say that this power of acting inheres in every substance, and that some action always arises from it." This power inheres in every substance and constitutes its substantiality; it always gives birth to some acting. It is drive, which is to say productive. To produce (*producere*) means to lead forth, to yield from oneself and keep what is so yielded within oneself. The determination as drive holds also for corporeal substance; in the collision of bodies the drive is merely limited and restricted in manifold ways. Those who, like the Cartesians, placed the essence of corporeal substance in extension alone overlook this. Heidegger's gloss on "produce" contains the monad's immanence in advance: what the monad brings forth, its representations, remains within it. Collision between bodies is reinterpreted as mutual limitation of drives, which prepares the doctrine that monads do not act on one another.

Every being has this character of drive; it is determined in its being as pressing. This is the basic metaphysical feature of the monad, though the structure of drive itself has not yet been determined explicitly. The thesis is universal, and Heidegger marks what remains open: what drive is directed toward and how it unifies. Subsection c takes this up.

A metaphysical assertion of the greatest scope lies in this thesis. As a general interpretation, it must also clarify the possibility of beings as a whole. What does the basic monadological thesis say about the co-presence of several beings in the whole of the universe? If every being, every monad, presses of itself, then it brings with it the essentials of its being, what it presses toward and how. Every co-presence, better, co-pressing, of other monads is essentially negative in its possible relation to each single monad. No substance can give another its drive, its essence. All it can do is disinhibit or inhibit, and even this negative function it exercises only indirectly. The relation of one substance to another is merely that of limitation and so of negative determination. Leibniz says clearly that a created substance receives from another created substance "not the force of acting itself, but only the limits and determination of its own pre-existing striving or power of acting," and he adds that this will help solve the difficult problem of the mutual operation of substances. Heidegger calls "pre-existing striving" (*praeexistens nisus*) decisive. The consequence is the "windowlessness" of the monad, derived here not as a doctrine about perception but as a consequence of the concept of drive. If each being's essence is its own self-initiating drive, the relations between beings can only be limitations, and the unity of the universe becomes a problem that the concept of the monad itself must solve. The Second Main Part will contrast this with Dasein's being-with and being-in-the-world, which are not external limitations of an already complete self.

In a note, Heidegger observes that Leibniz also calls active force *entelecheia*, appealing to Aristotle, in the *New System* and the *Monadology* (§18), where the reason given is that monads "have in themselves a certain perfection" (*echousi to enteles*): each monad brings its positive content with it, in such a way that this content is, in possibility, the universe itself. This interpretation of *entelecheia* does not match Aristotle's genuine tendency, but Leibniz claims the title in a new sense for his monadology. The Renaissance already translated *entelecheia* in Leibniz's sense as *perfectihabia*, and the *Monadology* (§48) names Hermolaus Barbarus as the translator. Barbarus (1454–95) translated and commented on Aristotle and Themistius, aiming to set the Greek Aristotle against medieval scholasticism. His work was beset with difficulties; it is said that, at a loss over the philosophical meaning of *entelecheia*, he conjured the devil for enlightenment. Heidegger adds: "We are in the same situation today" (p. 105). The anecdote closes a serious point with a joke. Leibniz reads *entelecheia* as "having one's completion within," which fits the monad's self-contained fullness but not Aristotle's term, which names being-at-the-end in the sense of completed presence. The remark that "we are in the same situation" admits that the meaning of the most central Greek concept of being remains unclear, which is the premise of Heidegger's own work on Aristotle.

The concept of active force has now been clarified in general: active force means drive; this character of drive is supposed to inhere in every substance as substance; and from this drive an acting constantly springs. The summary keeps the three claims distinct: what force is, that it belongs to all substance, and that it is productive.

Only now does the genuine metaphysical problem of substantiality arise: the question of the unity of substance as what primarily is. What is not substance Leibniz calls phenomenon, which means emanation, something derivative. Regarding unity, the critique of Descartes's extended substance showed that "unity" is not the result of an aggregation, not something subsequent, but what gives unity in advance. As unity-giving, unity is active; it is active force, drive, "the first constitutive of the unity of substance" (*primum constitutivum*; to de Volder, 30 June 1704). Here lies the central problem of the monadology, the problem of drive and substantiality. The status of "phenomenon" completes the ontology: substances are unities of drive, and what is not such a unity, aggregates and bodies as extended, is derivative. The formula *primum constitutivum* makes the thesis exact: drive is not a feature a substance has but what makes it a substance, a unity, in the first place.

The basic character of this activity is now visible; what remains obscure is how something like drive can itself give unity. A further essential question is how, on the basis of this internally unified monad, the whole of the universe constitutes itself in its interconnection. First, an interim reflection is needed. The two open questions define the rest of §5. Subsection c will answer the first by showing that drive unifies as representing (*perceptio*), and the second by showing that each monad represents the universe. The interim reflection comes first because the answers depend on identifying the model from which Leibniz drew his idea of unity.

##### b) Interim Reflection on the Guiding Thread of the Interpretation of Being

The metaphysical sense of the monadology can be reached only if one risks constructing its essential connections and perspectives along the guiding thread that was decisive for Leibniz in projecting the monadology. The monadology seeks to clarify the being of beings, so it must have obtained an exemplary idea of being from somewhere, namely from where something like being reveals itself immediately to the philosophical questioner. The question is methodological in the strict sense of *Being and Time* §2: every inquiry into being is guided in advance by some exemplary being from which the sense of being is read off. Heidegger asks which being served Leibniz.

We comport ourselves toward beings, become absorbed in them and lose ourselves to them, and we are overwhelmed and captivated by them. But we do not only relate to beings in this way; we are at the same time a being, the one we ourselves each are, and not indifferently, but so that "our own being concerns us" (p. 106). Hence, other reasons aside, one's own being is in a certain way always the guiding thread, and so too in the projection of the monadology. What this function of guidance means, and what comes into view in advance through it (existence), both remain ontologically unquestioned. The paragraph restates in two sentences the starting point of the existential analytic: Dasein is the being whose being is an issue for it, and for that reason its own being always functions as a guide. The critique of Leibniz is contained in the last sentence. He uses the guide without asking what it is, and so he reads off the being of the self in the categories of what is present at hand.

The constant regard to one's own Dasein, the constitution and mode of being of one's own I, gives Leibniz the model for the "unity" he attributes to every being. Beings are interpreted by analogy with soul, life, and spirit, as many passages make clear, and seeing this guiding thread clearly is decisive for understanding Leibniz's fateful interpretation of beings. "Fateful" is precise. Through Leibniz, the interpretation of all beings by analogy with the representing self passes into German Idealism and becomes the metaphysics of subjectivity.

Heidegger cites the *New System*: by looking to the "soul" or "form," there arises the idea of a true unity corresponding to what in us is called the "I." Nothing of the kind is found in artificial machines or in material mass as such, which can only be regarded "like an army or a herd, or a pond full of fish, or a clock made of springs and wheels." Leibniz's examples of aggregates all share one feature: their unity is conferred from outside, by an observer who counts them together or a maker who assembled them. The I is the only unity given from within, and so it becomes the model.

In a letter to the Cartesian de Volder, professor at Leiden (20 June 1703), Leibniz writes: "Substance itself, if it has an original character of drive, I think of as an indivisible and perfect monad, comparable to our I." The analogy is stated outright, and it is tied to drive: the I is the model because it is a unity that is active.

A second letter to de Volder (30 June 1704) states that this principle of activity is "intelligible to us in the highest degree, because it forms in a certain way an analogue to what dwells in us, namely representing and striving." The two moments, representing and striving, reappear in subsection c as the structural moments of drive.

Heidegger draws two conclusions from the passages: the determination of substance proceeds from the analogy with the "I," and it possesses the highest degree of intelligibility precisely because of this origin. The second point connects with §§2–4. Leibniz measures being by intelligibility, and the self counts as most intelligible because it is closest to the knower. The priority of the I in ontology is thus the priority of thinking over being from the opening of the First Main Part, now at the level of the monad.

Leibniz writes to de Volder in 1705: "I presuppose everywhere only what we must all admit often enough in our soul, namely internal spontaneous changes, and with this single presupposition of thought I exhaust the whole sum of things." The only pre-supposition, the genuine content of the metaphysical projection, is therefore the idea of being drawn from self-experience, from the spontaneous change perceptible in the I, from pressing. Heidegger hyphenates "pre-supposition" (*Voraus-setzung*). What Leibniz presupposes is not a premise but what is set out in advance as the understanding of being within which all beings are interpreted, and that understanding is self-initiating change.

To Johann Bernoulli (29 July 1698) Leibniz writes: "If we think of substantial forms as something analogous to souls, one may doubt whether they were rightly rejected." This does not mean that substantial forms simply are souls or are new things or corpuscles; they correspond to the "soul," which is only the occasion for projecting the basic structure of the monad. Heidegger insists on the distinction between analogy and identity. The soul supplies the pattern for the monad's structure, not its content, which is why subsection c can say that the soul is one possible modification of the monad and not its essence.

*Monadology* §30 states: "In thinking of ourselves in this way, we thereby also grasp the thought of being, of substance, of the simple and the composite, of the immaterial, and of God himself, by conceiving that what is limited in us is contained in him without limits." Heidegger inserts *via eminentiae*, the way of eminence. The whole of general and special metaphysics, being, substance, and God, is derived from self-reflection, with God reached by removing the limits of the finite self. This completes the circle of §4. The divine ideal of knowledge was itself projected from the self, with its limitations removed.

For the whole problem of the guiding function of self-reflection and self-consciousness, Heidegger adds the letter to Queen Sophie Charlotte of Prussia, *On What Is Beyond the Senses and Matter* (1702). The thought of myself, conscious of objects of sense and of my own activity connected with them, adds something to the objects of sense. Thinking of a color is quite different from reflecting on that thought at the same time, just as the color differs from the "I" that thinks it. Since other beings can also have the right to say "I," or it could be said for them, I understand from this what is called substance in general. Consideration of myself also gives me other metaphysical concepts, cause, effect, activity, similarity, and even the basic concepts of logic and morals. Finally: being itself and truth cannot be understood from the senses alone; this concept of being and truth is found rather in the "I" and the understanding than in the external senses and the perception of external objects. The letter makes the thesis of the whole First Main Part explicit in Leibniz's own words: the basic concepts of logic, and being and truth themselves, have their source in the I. What remains open is what this "I" is, the gap Heidegger fills in the next paragraphs.

For all his differences from Descartes, Leibniz holds with him that the self-certainty of the I is primary, and he sees in the I, the *ego cogito*, the dimension from which all basic metaphysical concepts must be drawn. But the guiding function of the ego remains ambiguous in several ways. First, the subject, as that to which the problem of being is posed at all, can become a question to itself. Second, the subject can take itself as an exemplary being, insofar as, as a being, it supplies with its own being the idea of being in general. Third, the subject can come into view as what understands being: as a being of a particular kind, it has an understanding of being in its very being, where being means not only the existence of Dasein. The three senses must be kept apart, because Leibniz's error lies in fusing them. The first is the subject as questioner, the second as specimen, the third as the place of the understanding of being. Leibniz takes the second path, reading the being of all beings off the being of the self; Heidegger's fundamental ontology takes the third. The qualifier "where being means not only the existence of Dasein" blocks the misreading that fundamental ontology projects Dasein's way of being onto everything.

On the knowledge of being in general, Leibniz writes: "I should like to know how we could have the idea of being if we were not beings ourselves and did not thus find being in ourselves" (*New Essays* I, 1, §23; compare §21, *Discourse* §27, *Monadology* §30). A note refers to Mahnke, who brings being and subjectivity together, though misleadingly. Leibniz explicitly asks about the idea of being and answers, following Descartes, that we would not have it if we were not beings ourselves and did not find being in us. We must be, certainly, in order to have the idea, and Leibniz means this. But more is meant: metaphysically speaking, it is our very essence that we cannot be what we are without the idea of being. "That is: understanding of being is constitutive for Dasein" (p. 109). Heidegger converts Leibniz's empirical-genetic claim into an ontological one. It is not that we find the idea of being by inspecting ourselves as beings, but that our way of being consists in understanding being, which is the thesis of *Being and Time* §4.

It does not follow that we draw the idea of being by going back to ourselves as beings. Its nearest origin is rather an indifferent one: the undifferentiated presence-at-hand of world and ourselves. Heidegger adds that the idea of *mana* in primitive thought is also to be illuminated from this undifferentiatedness and from being delivered over to beings. We ourselves are the source of the idea of being, but this source must be understood as the "primal transcendence" (*Urtranszendenz*) of Dasein. That is what it means to draw the idea of being from the "subject": understanding of being belongs to the subject only insofar as it is transcending. Only from transcendence do the various articulations of being arise. The idea of being in general, however, is a difficult and ultimate problem. This paragraph is the first appearance of transcendence as the answer to the Leibnizian problem, and it sets the agenda of the Second Main Part. The self is the source of the understanding of being, but not as a being inspected in reflection; it is so as transcendence, as the surpassing toward world within which beings are first understood as beings at all. The reference to *mana* draws on Cassirer's treatment of mythical thought, which Heidegger had reviewed; the undifferentiated power of *mana* reflects a stage where the understanding of being has not yet separated self and world.

In the ambiguous guiding function of the ego, the subject itself remains ontologically unclarified overall. Leibniz poses and solves the problem of being, the basic problem of metaphysics, by going back to the subject. Despite bringing out genuine ontic phenomena, this return to the I remains ambiguous in Leibniz, as in his predecessors and successors, because the "I" itself is not grasped in its essential structures and specific mode of being. The verdict extends to the whole modern tradition, Descartes to Hegel and Husserl. All return to the subject, and none determines the subject's mode of being, which is why the return is ambiguous.

For this reason Leibniz in particular gives the impression that the monadological interpretation of beings is simply anthropomorphism, a universal animation by analogy with the "I." But it should not be taken so externally and arbitrarily, especially since Leibniz tries to ground the analogizing metaphysically: "since the nature of things is uniform, our own essence cannot differ infinitely from the other simple substances of which the whole universe is composed" (to de Volder, 30 June 1704). Heidegger adds that the grounding given here is itself a general ontological principle that needs grounding. Leibniz's justification appeals to a principle of continuity or uniformity, and Heidegger points out that such a principle presupposes the very understanding of being it is meant to support.

Instead of merely and crudely diagnosing anthropomorphism, one must ask the reverse question: which structures of one's own Dasein are supposed to become relevant for interpreting the being of substance? How are these structures modified so as to become fit to make every being, all levels of being, monadologically intelligible? To resume the central problem: how is drive supposed to give unity to substance, and how must drive itself be determined? The reversal is the interpretive key to subsection c. Heidegger does not ask whether Leibniz projects the self onto things but what in the self's structure he projects and in what modified form. The answer will be the ecstatic, anticipating structure of the self, reduced to representing, and this answer will connect the monad to the temporality of Dasein.

##### c) The Structure of Drive

If drive, or what is determined as pressing insofar as it presses, is to give unity, it must itself be simple: it cannot have parts in the sense of an aggregate. The first constitutive of unity must be an indivisible unity. Leibniz writes to de Volder that what can be divided into several things already existing in act is aggregated from several, and what is aggregated from several is one only for the mind and has only a reality "borrowed from its contents"; hence there are indivisible unities in things, for otherwise there would be no true unity in things and no unborrowed reality, which is absurd. *Monadology* §1 states that the monad is simply a simple substance that enters into composites, simple meaning without parts. The phrase "borrowed reality" connects with §4. An aggregate has its thinghood only on loan from its components, and the regress of loans must end in something whose reality is its own. The simple is required as what has reality originally.

If substance simply unifies, there must already be a manifold that it unifies, or the whole problem of unification would be senseless. What unifies, and whose essence is unification, must therefore be essentially related to a manifold. The manifold must lie in the monad itself as simply unifying: as simple and unifying, the monad must prefigure the possible manifold. Heidegger has turned a difficulty into a structural requirement. A unity without a manifold would not unify anything, so the simple must contain multiplicity in some form without having parts.

Because what simply unifies is drive, and as pressing it is supposed to carry the manifold in itself, to be manifold, the manifold must have the character of pressing, of movedness in general. A manifold in motion is what is changeable and changing. The manifold within drive must have the character of what is pressed. What is pressed is what is pressed upon (*be-drängt*), and what is pressed upon in drive is drive itself. Hence drive contains self-overtaking, reversal, change, movedness: drive is what changes itself in its pressing; it is what is pressed. The morphology carries the argument. *Drängen* (pressing), *Gedrängtes* (what is pressed), and *Bedrängtes* (what is pressed upon, beset) show that the object of drive's pressing is drive itself. Drive has no external material to work on; its manifold is its own succession of states, which is why the monad needs no windows.

As first constitutive, drive is supposed to be simply unifying and at the same time the origin and mode of being of what changes. "Simply unifying" must be determined further. The unity is not supposed to be a subsequent gathering of what has been collected, but an original organizing unification. The constitutive principle of unification must be earlier than what is subject to possible unification. What unifies must be ahead, reaching out in advance toward something from which every manifold has already received its unity. What simply unifies must be originally reaching out and, as reaching out, encompassing in advance, so that every manifold already manifolds itself within what has been encompassed. The two terms *ausgreifend* (reaching out) and *umgreifend* (encompassing) describe a unity that is not a result but an anticipation. Heidegger is describing the structure of the horizon: a unity that is ahead of the manifold and within which the manifold first appears as manifold.

In this sense what unifies surpasses in advance what it unifies in its own course: it is "pre-eminent substance" (*substantia prae-eminens*; to de Volder, 20 June 1703). The "pre-" does not mean being present beforehand, but the structural reaching ahead and encompassing. The gloss prevents the temporal prefix from being read as prior presence-at-hand. The monad is "before" its states as their anticipating horizon, not as an earlier state.

It is therefore required that the primitive force, drive as the first constitutive of original unification, reach out and encompass. Leibniz expresses this by saying that the monad is, at the ground of its essence, representing (*vor-stellend*, *re-präsentierend*). The hyphens mark Heidegger's reading. *Vor-stellen* is "setting before," *re-präsentieren* "making present again"; both are read not as mental acts but as the structure of reaching ahead that the unifying drive requires.

"The innermost metaphysical motive for the representational character of the monad is the ontological unifying function of drive" (p. 112). This motive remained hidden to Leibniz himself. In substance, only this can be the motive for the character of representation, not the reasoning that the monad as force is something living, that soul belongs to the living, and that representing belongs to soul. That would be an external transfer of the psychic onto beings as such and, in that form, metaphysically unjustified. Heidegger derives perception from the problem of unity rather than from the analogy with the soul. This is his "more original" retrieval of Leibniz: perception belongs to every monad because unifying requires anticipating, not because everything is secretly alive.

Because drive is to be what originally and simply unifies, it must reach out and encompass; it must be "representing." Representing must be taken very broadly, structurally, not as a particular faculty of the soul. The monad in its metaphysical essence is therefore not a soul; on the contrary, the soul is a possible modification of the monad. Essentially representing drive is not an occurrence that sometimes also represents or even produces representations; the structure of the pressing occurrence itself reaches out, "is ecstatic," and in this sense pressing is a representing. This representing is not a mere staring but perception (*perceptio*), a unifying in advance of the manifold in the simple, *dans le simple* (*Principles of Nature and Grace* §2). Leibniz defines perception to des Bosses as "nothing other than the expression of the many in the one" (1706), and writes later that perception never concerns an object in which there is no variety or multitude. The word "ecstatic" connects the monad to *Being and Time*, where temporality is the "ecstatic" standing-out of Dasein toward future, past, and present. Heidegger reads perception as a reduced form of this ecstatic structure. The definition "expression of the many in the one" supplies the textual anchor: perception is by definition a unifying of a manifold.

Like representing, "striving" also belongs to the structure of drive; the pairing goes back to Aristotle's *noēsis* and *orexis*, the basic capacities of all living things. Besides perception (representation), Leibniz explicitly names a second capacity, appetite (*appetitus*). He must emphasize appetite separately only because he does not grasp the essence of active force radically enough at once, despite marking it off clearly from active potency and from action. Force still appears to be something substantial, a core then endowed with representing and striving, whereas drive is in itself a representing striving or a striving representing. Appetite does, however, have a special meaning and is not synonymous with drive; it means a distinct, essential, constitutive moment of drive, as perception does. Heidegger criticizes Leibniz for residual substantialism: listing perception and appetite as two faculties of a force treats the force as a thing that has properties. Yet he retains appetite as a distinct moment within drive, and the next paragraphs specify it as the tendency of transition.

Insofar as drive unifies originally, it must already be ahead of every possible manifold, equal to it in possibility, which is to say it must already have exceeded and overtaken it. Drive must in a way carry the manifold within itself and let it be born within itself in pressing; that is its "world" character. The task is to see the essential origin of the manifold in drive as such. The quotation marks around "world" are a promissory note. The monad's anticipatory containment of all its possible states is Leibniz's version of what the Second Main Part will analyze as the world toward which Dasein transcends.

As overtaking in advance, drive is originally unifying unity, and in this way the monad is conceived as substance. "Substances are not wholes that contain parts formally, but total things that contain partial ones eminently" (to de Volder, 24 January 1704). Drive is the "nature," the essence of substance; as drive, substance is in a certain way active, and as active it is always originally representing (*Principles of Nature and Grace* §2). The scholastic distinction between containing "formally" and "eminently" states the thesis. A whole of parts contains them formally, as they are; the monad contains its states eminently, in a higher mode, as their anticipating unity.

In the same letter Leibniz continues: "If nothing is active by its nature, nothing at all will be active; for what reason for action could there be if not in the nature of the thing? You add the limitation that a thing could be active by its nature if the action always stayed the same. But since all action contains change, we have what you seemed to deny, a tendency to internal change, and the temporal following from the nature of the thing." Heidegger comments: the activity of the monad as drive is eo ipso drive to change; drive by its nature presses toward something else, and self-overtaking lies in it. In what presses, as pressing, the manifold arises. Substance is "subject to succession" (*successioni obnoxia*); drive delivers itself over to the manifold succession, not as something other than itself, for the succession is what drive as drive seeks to press out, and it subjects itself to temporal sequence not as something alien, since it is this manifold itself. "Time springs from drive itself" (p. 115). This is the most important sentence of §5 for the course as a whole. Leibniz's own phrase, "the temporal following from the nature of the thing," supports Heidegger's claim that the being of the monad is inherently temporal. The Second Main Part will reverse the order and show that drive, transcendence, and world are possible only on the ground of temporality.

In drive lies the tendency to pass from one to another, the tendency to overcome each stage of drive, and this transition tendency is what Leibniz means by appetite. Perception is an equally original determination of the monad. The tendency is itself representing, unifying from an overtaking unity in advance, and what it unifies is nothing but the transitions from representing to representing that are pressed out and press themselves in drive. Perception and appetite are now defined in relation to each other. Appetite is the passing from state to state; perception is the anticipating unity within which those states belong together. Neither is prior, which is why Heidegger calls them equally original.

Leibniz states his final determination to de Volder: "considering the matter accurately, one must say that there is nothing in things but simple substances and in them perception and appetite." The principle of change is internal to all simple substances, since there is no reason it should belong to one more than another, and it consists in "the progress of perceptions of each monad, and the whole nature of things has nothing beyond this." To seek beyond this and ask why there is perception and appetite in simple substances is to seek "something ultramundane" and to call God to account for why he willed something we conceive. Heidegger identifies the progress of perceptions as the original element of the monad, the representing transition tendency, drive. For the genesis of the doctrine he adds a passage from the first draft of a letter to de Volder (19 January 1706): it suffices for Leibniz to assume what is usually conceded, that there is a certain force in what perceives to form new perceptions from earlier ones, which amounts to saying that from some earlier perception a new one sometimes follows. What philosophers ancient and modern usually admit for the soul's voluntary operations, Leibniz holds to occur always and everywhere, sufficing for all phenomena with great uniformity and simplicity. The draft shows the step by which Leibniz generalized: the self-generation of mental states, admitted for voluntary acts of the soul, is extended to all beings. Heidegger's analysis explains why this generalization is not arbitrary. What it generalizes is the structure of unifying drive, not the psychic content of willing.

The question "how is drive, as drive, unifying?" required penetrating into its essential structure. Drive is originally unifying, not by grace of what it unifies and not as a piecing together of it; it anticipates and encompasses, and so it is perception. This perceiving encompasses and is turned toward a manifold that is itself laid out in drive and springs from it; drive overtakes itself, is an onrush (*Andrang*), a manifold of stages that are always themselves representing. Drive is the progress of perceptions; pressing to overtake itself, it is appetite, the tendency of transition, internal tendency toward change. The summary fixes the three structural moments, anticipating unity, self-generated manifold, and transition, which together correspond to the structure Heidegger will identify in temporality.

In an overview of substantiality: substance is what constitutes the unity of a being. What unifies is drive, taken in the determinations just shown, representation and tendency of transition, which means drive develops the manifold within itself. The sentence is the positive answer to the question posed against Descartes and Spinoza: substance is not what needs nothing else but what unifies itself.

Drive as this unifying element is the nature of a being. At the same time, each monad has its own original constitution (*propre constitution originale*), given with creation. What makes each monad this particular one, and how is this individuation itself constituted? Appealing to creation is only a dogmatic explanation of the origin of what is individuated, not an elucidation of individuation itself. Answering this question must clarify the essence of the monad further. Heidegger sets aside the theological explanation as he did Leibniz's appeal to God's choice, and asks for a structural account.

Individuation, too, must take place in what fundamentally constitutes the monad's essence, in drive. Where can and must the ground of each monad's particular character lie? Which essential character in the structure of drive makes individual singularization possible? How far is the original unifying, precisely in unifying, a self-singularizing? The final question anticipates the answer: the monad singularizes itself by unifying.

The link with creation was set aside only as a dogmatic explanation. The metaphysical sense expressed in characterizing the monad as created is finitude. Heidegger translates the theological category into an ontological one, as he did with God's knowledge in §2. To be created means to be finite, and finitude can be determined structurally.

Formally, finitude means limitation. How far is drive limitable? If finitude as limitation belongs to its essence, it must be determined from drive's basic metaphysical feature, unification, and indeed representing, overtaking unification. In this representing unification lies a having-in-advance of the unity toward which drive, as representing and tending to transition, looks. In drive as representing appetite lies, as it were, a "point" on which attention is directed in advance: the unity itself, from which it unifies. This eye-point or point of view, *point de vue*, viewpoint, is constitutive for drive. The eye-point, what is represented in it in advance, also regulates all pressing in advance. Because it is always what is represented in advance in the free movement of representing movedness, the pressing is not pushed from outside. Perception and appetite are therefore determined in their pressing primarily from the point of view. Leibniz's "point of view" becomes an ontological structure. The unity drive anticipates is not everywhere but from somewhere, and this "from somewhere" is at once the monad's finitude and its individuality. It also guides drive from within, which makes the monad self-determining.

Something that reaches out in itself, as drive does, and does so in such a way that it holds itself and is in this very reaching out, has in itself the possibility of grasping itself. In pressing toward something, what presses always traverses a dimension; it traverses itself and is thus open to itself, as an essential possibility. On the basis of this dimensional self-openness, what presses can also grasp itself explicitly, and beyond perceiving it can present itself along with it, perceive itself as well: apperceive. Leibniz writes (*Principles of Nature and Grace* §4; compare *Monadology* §21 ff.): it is well to distinguish perception, the interior state of the monad representing external things, from apperception, which is consciousness or reflective knowledge of this interior state, which is not given to all souls nor always to the same soul. Heidegger adds "awakening, wakefulness" to Leibniz's "consciousness." Self-consciousness is derived from the ecstatic structure of drive rather than presupposed. What reaches out always passes through itself and is therefore open to itself, and apperception makes that openness explicit. This reverses the Cartesian order, in which self-consciousness is the foundation of everything else.

In the point of view, the whole universe is, as it were, envisaged in a definite perspective of what is and what is possible, but refracted in a definite way, according to the stage of the monad's pressing, its capacity to unify itself in its manifoldness. This "itself" expresses that in the monad as representing drive there lies a certain co-representing of itself. This self-disclosure can have different degrees, from complete transparency to stupor and captivation (*Betäubung und Benommenheit*). No monad lacks perception and appetite, and so a certain self-openness, although this need not be an explicit co-representing of oneself. Every monad has this self-openness, if only in the lowest degree. Accordingly, the particular point of view, and with it the possibility of unifying, the unity, constitutes the distinctiveness of each monad. "Captivation" (*Benommenheit*) is the term Heidegger will use in the 1929–30 course for the animal's mode of being, its openness to its environment without the manifestness of beings as such. Leibniz's gradation of monads by degree of self-disclosure is a precursor of that analysis.

Precisely insofar as the monad unifies, which is its essence, it singularizes itself. The inner possibility of individuation is grounded in its essence as drive. In singularizing, in pressing from its own perspective, it unifies only, according to its possibility, the universe represented in advance within it. Each monad is thus in itself a "concentrated world" (*mundus concentratus*; to de Volder, 20 June 1703). Each drive concentrates the world within itself in its pressing, in its own way. Heidegger's answer to the question of individuation: the monad is this monad because it unifies the universe from this point of view, and what it unifies is always the whole universe. Individuality and universality are not opposed but two aspects of the same perspectival unifying.

Because each monad, in its own way, is the "world," insofar as it presents it, every drive stands in agreement (*consensus*) with the universe, and on the basis of this agreement of each representing drive with the universe, the monads themselves stand in connection. The idea of the monad as representing drive tending to transition implies that the world belongs to it in a perspectival refraction, so that all monads, as unities of drive, are oriented in advance toward the pre-established harmony of the whole of beings. The hyphen in *voraus-gestellt* (set in advance) links pre-established harmony to representing (*vor-stellen*): the harmony is what every monad has set before itself in advance. The whole universe lies in each monad as possibility. Pre-established harmony is reinterpreted structurally. It is not a divine arrangement added to independent monads but the consequence of each monad's representing the same universe.

This pre-established harmony, as the basic constitution of the actual world, is what stands over against the central monad, God, as what is pressed for. God's drive is his will, and the correlate of the divine will is the optimum. Leibniz writes to Bernoulli: one must distinguish between what God can and what he wills; he can do all things, he wills the best. Actual things are nothing but the best of the possibles, all compared. Possibles are what do not imply contradiction. The citation closes the circle with §§2–3: possibility is non-contradiction, actuality is selection of the best, and God's will, his drive, is the principle of existence. The principle of the best, which in §3 appeared among the meanings of the principle of reason, is now located in the drive of the central monad.

The singularization accomplished in drive as unification is essentially always the singularization of a being that belongs to a world. Monads are therefore not isolated pieces that would first make up the universe as a sum. Each, as drive, is the universe in its own way; it represents the world from a point of view. Talk of the monad as a "little world," a microcosm, precisely misses the essential point, since each monad is the universe in such a way that, pressing, it represents the whole world in its unity, though never grasping it totally. Each monad, according to its degree of wakefulness, is a "world-history presenting the world." The universe is therefore multiplied, in a way, as often as there are monads, just as the same city is represented differently according to the different positions of observers (*Discourse* §9). The rejection of "microcosm" is precise. A microcosm is a miniature copy, a small world beside the large one, whereas the monad is the one world from a standpoint. "World-history" (*Welt-Geschichte*) reads the monad's successive perceptions as the happening of world, which prepares the Second Main Part's claim that world is not a sum of things but something that temporalizes itself in Dasein's transcendence.

This clarifies Leibniz's favorite image for the whole essence of the monad: the monad is a living mirror of the universe. One of the most important passages is in the letter to de Volder of 20 June 1703: entelechies must differ, not be entirely alike; indeed they must be principles of diversity, since each expresses the universe differently according to its own way of viewing, and it is their very office to be so many living mirrors of things, or so many concentrated worlds. The image needs the analysis that precedes it. Without drive, "mirror" suggests a passive reflecting surface; with it, the mirror is a living, active presenting.

The sentence contains several points. First, the differentiation of monads is necessary; it belongs to their essence, since as unifying, each from its point of view, they singularize themselves. Second, the monads are therefore themselves the origin of their differences, by virtue of their perceptive-appetitive way of seeing. Third, this unifying presentation of the universe in a singularization is precisely "what is at stake" (*worum es … geht*) for each monad in its being, drive. Fourth, the monads are the universe in a concentration, "concentrations of the universe" (to de Volder, 1705); the center of the concentration is the particular drive determined from its point of view. Fifth, the monad is a "living mirror" (*Principles of Nature and Grace* §3, *Monadology* §§63 and 77, draft letter to Remond, July 1714), an "active indivisible mirror," an active, indivisible, simple mirroring. "Mirror," *speculum* (from the same stem as *spectare*, *species*), means a letting-see. But the monad lets see as pressing: it mirrors as representing, and only in this doing does the disclosure of world take place. Mirroring is not rigid copying; as such, it presses toward new, prefigured possibilities of itself. In having the one universe in advance at a point of view, from which the manifold first becomes visible, it is simple. The third point uses the exact formula of *Being and Time* for Dasein, a being for which its being is at stake. Heidegger thereby indicates which structure of Dasein Leibniz modified into the monad: self-concerned, world-disclosing being. The etymology of *speculum* as "letting-see" connects mirroring to phenomenology's *legein* as letting-be-seen and to truth as disclosure.

From here the essence of finite substance can be grasped more sharply in a respect not yet noticed. Leibniz says (to de Volder, 20 June 1703): every substance is active, and every finite substance is passive, and resistance is connected with passion. Substance is active, drive. In every finite drive, which always operates in a perspective, there is necessarily something resistant that stands against drive as such. Pressing toward the whole universe from a point of view, drive is so and so much not; it is modified by the point of view. Drive is related to resistance precisely because it can be the whole universe in possibility but is not. Insofar as the monad is the whole from a point of view, it is finite by virtue of this relation to the universe: it relates to a resistance, to something it is not but could be. This passivity, in the sense of what drive does not press out, belongs to the finitude of drive. Finitude is not a limit placed on the monad from outside but the difference between what it is in possibility, the whole universe, and what it actually presses out from its standpoint. This structure, possibility exceeding actuality, is the Leibnizian prefiguration of the thesis that Dasein's being-possible exceeds its actuality.

This negativity, purely as a structural moment of finite drive, characterizes what Leibniz means by prime matter (*materia prima*). "Prime matter is essential to every entelechy and never separated from it, since it completes it and is itself the passive potency of the whole complete substance. For prime matter does not consist in bulk or impenetrability and extension" (to des Bosses, 16 October 1706). Prime matter is reinterpreted as the passive side of finite drive, not as stuff. Matter enters the monad not as a material component but as the structural "not" of finitude.

By virtue of this essential original passivity, the monad has the inner possibility of connection (*nexus*) with secondary matter (*materia secunda*), with mass, with determinate resistance in the sense of material mass and weight (compare the correspondence with the mathematician Johann Bernoulli and with the Jesuit des Bosses, teacher at the Jesuit college in Hildesheim). This structural moment gives Leibniz the foundation for making the connection of the monad with a material body metaphysically intelligible and for showing positively why extension cannot constitute the essence of substance. Heidegger does not pursue this or the further development of the monadology and its related principles. The critique of Descartes that began §5 is now complete. Extension belongs to secondary matter, which is grounded in the passivity of finite drive, so extension is derivative of the monad's finitude.

The guiding problem is the rootedness of logic in metaphysics, of the theory of judgment in the theory of substance, of the identity theory in the monadology. The monadology determines the substantiality of substance, the unity of a being as a being. Unity is thereby conceived as originally unifying: *monas* proves to be drive. Drive is representing and tends toward transition. How does it unify, and how does it singularize itself in unifying? It unifies as anticipating encompassing, and so in a view to unity, under a point of view. Each time a point of view guides, and under it the whole is represented. Each monad is thus a perspective of the universe, a concentrated world, and, as monad, stands in agreement with the others. That is why monads have "no windows" (*Monadology* §7): because they need none. There is no influx, not only because it would be inexplicable but because it is superfluous. The derivation of windowlessness from superfluity rather than impossibility is Heidegger's point. Monads need not receive anything because each already contains the universe in its perspective; the doctrine is the positive consequence of drive, not an embarrassment.

Leibniz summarizes (to des Bosses, 19 August 1715): "I do not believe a system is possible in which monads act on one another, since no mode of explaining it seems possible. I add that influx is also superfluous, for why should one monad give another what it already has? For this is the very nature of substance, that the present is pregnant with the future and that from one all can be understood, unless God intervenes by miracle." The formula "the present pregnant with the future" (*praesens gravidum futuro*) joins the monad's temporality and its intelligibility. Because every future state is contained in the present, everything can be understood from one, which is the complete concept of §1 grounded in drive. At this point the identity theory of truth and the monadological theory of being meet: the predicate is in the subject because the future is in the present.

The interpretation of the monadology was needed to obtain the genuinely metaphysical foundation of Leibniz's logic, and it was obtained in the monadological constitution of the being as substance. The result would still need more detailed consideration, since the ultimate basis has not yet been reached: the problem of the being as essence and existence, as a possible that is, as such, a possible actual. With regard to the economy of the course, §6, "The Basic Conception of Being as Such: Essence and the Striving toward Existence," is skipped; Heidegger refers to the discussion of essence and existence as basic articulations of being in the summer 1927 course (published as GA 24, *The Basic Problems of Phenomenology*). Discussing Leibniz's conception of essence and existence would require far-reaching considerations and detailed discussion of texts, especially since Leibniz addresses the problem in only a few places. The omission leaves a gap exactly where §2 located the difficulty of truths of fact, "identical but not necessary." Leibniz's doctrine that every possible strives toward existence (*conatus existentiae*) would have extended drive from actual monads to possibles as such, and its absence means that §7 draws the conclusions from the monad alone.

#### §7. Theory of Judgment and Conception of Being. Logic and Ontology

The characterization of Leibniz's logic started from his theory of judgment, following a connection established in philosophy since antiquity: judgment is supposed to be the proper bearer and place of truth. More precisely, the assertion is what stands in the alternative of being true or false, insofar as in it something is said about something, determined as something. By contrast, the mere representing of something, a direct simple intuiting such as a perception, is supposed to be neither true nor false; it is either performed or not. If instead of the judgment "the blackboard is black" I perform the intuition "the blackboard, black," I "assert" nothing, and so there is no true or false. Since truth is the distinguishing character of knowledge as such (false knowledge is no knowledge of something), and truth has its seat in the assertion, knowing is equated with judging. "Today's theory of knowledge is logic" (p. 124), and the question of the essence of truth moves into the theory of judgment. Heidegger reconstructs the traditional chain in order to break it. The chain runs: truth is located in judgment, knowledge is essentially true, so knowledge is judgment. The whole Second Main Part will contest its first link, showing that the truth of assertion is founded in a more original openness, which is why he takes care to note that on the traditional view simple intuition is neither true nor false.

The question is how a theory of the essence of judgment should be approached, what it means to clarify "judgment" in its essence. Judging is a human comportment; as judging about beings, it is related in itself to beings. This relation-to Heidegger calls intentionality; judging about something is intentional in itself. A mere intuiting of something, the perception of the wall, is also a being-related-to. What characterizes the intentionality of judgment? Judging about something, asserting about something, is in itself a determining, such that what is judged about is determined in the judging. In judging the black blackboard as black, black is placed in relation to blackboard, so there is a further relation, one that determines what is judged about. Not only is the judging Dasein related to what it judges about, but what it judges about is itself articulated relationally. "The intentional relation of asserting is in itself a relating relation" (p. 124): as intentional, it also performs a relating in the sense of determining something as something. The intentional relation of the assertion to what it is about is "forked." Heidegger represents this crudely with a diagram: from judging Dasein a relation runs to what is present at hand, and within it a branch of determination. The analysis restates in the vocabulary of intentionality what §1 called the double sense of inclusion. The forked relation is Heidegger's version of the "apophantic as" of *Being and Time* §33: the assertion both points at a being and articulates it, and these are one act, not two.

The assertion has a "referent intentionality" (*referente Intentionalität*): an originally unitary forked relation, the unity of synthesis and diairesis, not two or more unforked relations side by side or in sequence. I do not first perceive the blackboard and then black and then combine the perceptions. The "is" of the judgment "A is B" contains at once the "is" of the relation to beings and the forking. The rejection of an atomistic account of judgment matters for the later argument. If judgment were an assembly of separate perceptions, its unity would be an achievement of the judge, as in Leibniz's composition; if it is originally unitary, its unity must be grounded in something prior to the act of judging. The copula carries both relations, which confirms the Introduction's claim that the copula points back to being.

The question of the essence of truth, understood as located in judgment, became the question of the essence of judgment, and judgment showed first the structures of intentionality and forking, as structures of the judging Dasein. What and how is Dasein itself, such that it can assert about something and must assert in the manner of forked intentionality? Where is the inner possibility of judgment grounded, as this kind of comportment of Dasein? What is needed is clarification of the constitution of the being of Dasein, first in this respect, ultimately an ontology, a metaphysics of Dasein. The destruction of Leibniz's logic ends where §9 will begin. The ground of judgment is sought not in the monad but in Dasein, and the phrase "metaphysics of Dasein" is Heidegger's name, in 1928, for fundamental ontology with its metaphysical consequences.

What does it mean that truth has its place in the assertion? Is truth a "property" of judgment, as color is a property of a material thing? This too can be decided only when it is clear how truth relates to judging Dasein and to Dasein as such. Only when the inner possibility of truth is clarified can the possibility of knowledge, and so of judging and thinking, be made intelligible, "not the other way around!" (p. 126). The exclamation reverses the modern order stated at the start of the First Main Part, where thinking came first and being followed. Truth is now prior to judgment, and truth must be clarified from Dasein.

For the concept of logic as such it follows that, generally speaking, its task is to clarify the essence of truth. If this clarification can be carried out only as metaphysics, as ontology, then logic must be understood as the metaphysics of truth. The formula "metaphysics of truth" appears here for the first time as Heidegger's thesis. It does not subordinate logic to an external discipline but redefines logic by its proper theme.

Leibniz's theory of judgment was characterized in such a way that it led to the character of truth. Judgment is *connexio*, more precisely *inclusio*; the subject–predicate relation has the character of identity; and being true means for Leibniz nothing but being identical. The grounding, clarification, and securing of truth take place by reduction to identities, made possible by the belonging together of determinations as compatible in the unity of what is judged about, the unity of the subject. This conception concerns not only essential truths but existential ones too. In idea, all possible assertions determining an individual being must be reducible to a nexus of pure identities; they are determinations of what belongs together. The recapitulation joins §§1–4: inclusion, identity, compatibility, and the unity of the subject. The emphasis on existential truths marks where the metaphysical ground is needed most. Only for the individual is the unity of its determinations not guaranteed by a finite definition.

To be a possible whole of what belongs together, the manifold of what belongs together needs a unity of belonging. This unity must be prior, for only so can it function as the standard for whether a together belongs or whether a supposed together does not. The together of a manifold, as a belonging together, needs a unity that regulates in advance. Reduction to identity, as a whole of mutually compatible and belonging determinations, is metaphysically possible as a way of judging about beings only if the being itself is constituted as a being by an original unity. Leibniz sees this unity in the monadic constitution of substance. Therefore the monadic structure of beings is the metaphysical foundation of the theory of judgment and truth as identity theory. "The destruction of Leibniz's theory of judgment with respect to the metaphysical basic problems is thereby carried out" (p. 127). What must be retained above all is that each monad is a mirror, in possibility the universe. It is thus in idea correlated with the universe as the object of absolute divine knowledge. The metaphysical basis is now obtained for the idea of knowledge as intuition, for the ideal of knowledge as adequate intuitive knowledge, as absolute distinctness, "that is, the absolute wakefulness of the monad." The argument of the First Main Part is complete. Judgment as identity presupposes a unity that regulates in advance which determinations belong together, and the monad's unifying drive is that unity. The last sentence joins §4 and §5: the ideal of adequate intuition is the monad's wakefulness raised to the absolute, which is why God can be called the central monad.

This logic of truth is therefore possible only on the ground of the monadological metaphysics of substance. Logic accordingly has metaphysical foundations by its essence; indeed, as a radical consideration can show, it is itself nothing other than the metaphysics of truth. The conclusion generalizes from Leibniz. What holds for his logic is claimed to hold for logic as such, which is the thesis the Second Main Part must establish from the phenomena themselves.

Leibniz himself never explicitly raised the problem of the metaphysical grounding of logic. On the contrary, the tendency repeatedly breaks through to develop logic from itself and metaphysics from logic, while the traditional concepts of metaphysics and logic are retained in their basic features despite all changes in the content of the problems. For the course, the orientation toward Leibniz's logic and its connection with metaphysics serves first to make visible a horizon of possible problems in this field, so as to remove the strangeness of the purely systematic discussions to follow, and above all to destroy the prejudice that logic is something free-floating, firm in itself. Heidegger distinguishes his result from Leibniz's self-understanding. The foundation was there in Leibniz's thought, but Leibniz ran the dependence the other way. The destruction exhibits what his logic actually rests on against the direction of his own derivations.

There is an often-advanced argument by which one believes one can prove conclusively the priority of logic over metaphysics. It has the further advantage of deciding their relation on the basis of quite general ideas of logic and metaphysics, without entering into their specific problems. Heidegger's thesis is that logic is grounded in metaphysics and is itself nothing but the metaphysics of truth. The argument rests, first, on the prejudice that logic is free-floating, something ultimate, "thinking" as such; and second, on the general argument rooted in this prejudice, that logic has priority over all sciences. The target is the Neo-Kantian and logicist orthodoxy of the period, for which logic is the first science because every science thinks.

The argument's advantages are that it is general and formal, not entering into the content of metaphysics and logic, and that it is immediately and vulgarly plausible: metaphysics, as a science, is knowing and thus thinking; it therefore presupposes the science of thinking, logic; logic is thus the presupposition of metaphysics, since without it metaphysics can be neither grounded nor carried out. But this holds of every science and even of every knowing and thinking. So "logic" too presupposes logic. This consequence gives pause, since it leaves open whether the argument decides anything about the relation of logic and metaphysics, when it holds of both equally. If so, its scope must first be examined. The self-application is the first crack. An argument that proves logic presupposes logic proves nothing about the relative order of disciplines; it only points to the fact that all thinking uses rules.

The argument runs: metaphysics, as philosophical knowledge, as knowing, is thinking; thinking presupposes logic; therefore logic must be grounded first, as the foundation of metaphysics, not conversely. Since the argument applies to every science, one concludes that logic provides the presupposition for all sciences. But the very applicability of the argument to any science is itself an argument against the thesis that logic is the presupposition of all sciences, and of metaphysics in particular. For what does "presupposition" mean here? The question isolates the equivocation. "Presupposition" can mean a condition of performing an activity or a ground of the possibility of what is performed, and the argument trades on confusing the two.

Every science, and every prescientific thinking, makes use as thinking of the general formal rules of thought; in carrying out thinking, the use of the rules is unavoidable. Why this is so is left open. But does it follow from the unavoidability of using rules that science is grounded in logic? Not at all. First, the unavoidability of using rules does not mean the unavoidability of logic. The use of rules does not necessarily require the science of the rules of thought, still less a grounded knowledge of them in the sense of traditional logic. Otherwise the thoughtful grounding of logic itself would be internally impossible, or superfluous; the fully developed logic would have to exist already wherever there is thinking. The distinction between using rules and knowing them scientifically is the core of the rebuttal. People reasoned validly long before Aristotle; if using the rules required logic, logic could never have been founded.

Even if one granted that the unavoidability of using rules in scientific thinking meant the unavoidability of logic for science, and for metaphysics, it would not follow that logic is the foundation of science as such. The performance of thinking and the use of rules are required, among other things, by the essence of science, but the essence of science does not have its inner possibility in the performance of thinking and the use of its rules. On the contrary, the unavoidability of using rules can be grounded only from the inner essential possibility of science, that is, metaphysically. Not only the use of rules but the rules themselves require metaphysics for their grounding. One could object again that this metaphysical grounding of the rules of logic presupposes scientific thinking and the rules themselves and necessarily uses them. The objection is the argument's last resort, the claim that any grounding, including the metaphysical, is already thinking and so already under logic.

So the priority of the rules of thought cannot be rejected, however one twists and turns. In fact, there is no escaping this argument. But there is even less escape from the question whether the conditions of performing thought are prior to the essence of thought and of that in which it is grounded as a comportment of Dasein, or whether, conversely, the essence of Dasein and of thought first makes possible the conditions of performance and the manner of their necessary use. Thought and the use of rules may be unavoidable for the performance of all thought, including the grounding of metaphysics, but it does not follow that this grounding consists in the use of rules. It follows only that the use of rules itself needs grounding, and that the plausible argument is incapable of carrying out any grounding at all. The argument from the unavoidability of rule-use can only appeal to this fact; it cannot even make its inner possibility a problem, let alone solve it. Heidegger concedes the argument's premise and denies its conclusion. That thinking always uses rules is a fact; what makes the fact possible is a further question, which the argument cannot ask because it only ever repeats the fact.

Logic is therefore, first, not even a condition of the performance of thought, but only a science of the rules as such, preserved in a tradition. Second, if it is the science of the rules, it turns out that logic in its traditional form can neither clarify nor ground these rules in their essence. Third, since the inner possibility of something, as grounding, is prior to its factual performance and its conditions, the disclosure of the inner possibility of thinking as such is the presupposition of "logic" as the science of the rules of thought. Fourth, the whole problem of the possible priority of metaphysics over logic or logic over metaphysics cannot itself be posed, discussed, and solved by logic, unless logic itself is conceived as the metaphysics of truth. If a discussion is possible in the manner of metaphysics, then the use of rules is, among other things, a condition of carrying it out. The fourth point is decisive: the question of the order of disciplines is not a logical question, since formal logic has no means of asking what grounds it. Heidegger concedes, finally, that metaphysics uses the rules too, but as a condition of performance, not as its ground.

This suffices to characterize the popular argument for the priority of logic. The argument is "a classic, for all sophistry" (p. 131), because it gives, and can always give, the appearance of pressing toward an ultimate grounding and refusing to rest content with anything penultimate, since it lets every thoughtful grounding turn into the observation that, as grounding, it thinks. Even while creating this appearance it appeals to something for which it cannot take responsibility, and yet, and this is what makes it sophistical, it creates the impression that this formal argumentation is the strictest and that grounding is satisfactory only in its sense. Heidegger classifies the argument by the Platonic criterion of the *Sophist*: sophistry is the production of the appearance of rigor. The argument looks radical because it never stops regressing, but its regress appeals to a fact it cannot ground.

It would be a mistake to think that because the argument is formal and empty it could be refuted formally in the same way. The only refutation consists in showing why the argument is possible and why, under certain presuppositions, it is even necessary. By first securing its justification, one makes visible that it lives off something it not only cannot give itself but believes it must even deny. Refutation by explanation is Heidegger's method throughout. A misunderstanding is overcome by exhibiting its ground, and the ground here is the finite, transcending Dasein whose thinking always already moves within an understanding of being, which the argument presupposes and denies.

Because this argumentation is not arbitrary but has metaphysical roots, it imposes itself again and again, precisely where one seeks a supposedly radical grounding. Leibniz therefore comes close repeatedly to such a grounding of metaphysics in logic, most clearly and forcefully in *Primae veritates*. Present-day logic shows a further inversion: not only is metaphysics reduced to logic, but logic itself to mathematics. Present-day logic is logistic, mathematical logic, logic by mathematical method. The reference to *Primae veritates*, where Leibniz derives the principle of reason and the main principles of his metaphysics from the definition of truth (§3), gives the Leibnizian form of the argument. The remark on logistic places Russell and Couturat's logicist Leibniz at the end of the line: the tendency Heidegger has traced culminates in a logic that takes its method from mathematics, the very model of knowledge that Leibniz, in the 1694 article, admitted could not serve metaphysics.

Although the previous considerations allow the titles "metaphysics" and "logic" to be used with more definite content, the problem of their relation cannot be decided by general discussions, especially because there are not two fixed, secured disciplines that could simply be played off against each other. What is needed now is to take up a central basic problem of logic against the background of the mainly historical considerations so far, and to clarify it in a way that shows concretely how logic is possible only as the metaphysics of truth. Here, as in every philosophical task of laying foundations, "laying foundations does not mean slipping another foundation under a finished discipline; laying foundations is always a new projection of the ground plan" (p. 132). Every laying of foundations for philosophy must transform it, but transformation is possible only by grasping and holding onto what is essential. The architectural image defines the method of the Second Main Part. Logic is not to be preserved and underpinned but redesigned from its ground, and the central problem chosen for this is the principle of reason, the principle that §3 showed to be the unclear center of Leibniz's logic.

To close the First Main Part, Heidegger fixes the results. The aim as a whole is a philosophical logic, the search for the metaphysical foundations of logic. The destruction of Leibniz's logic served as a concrete introduction to the basic metaphysical problems (in principle this would also be possible with Plato or Aristotle). It revealed the connection between the identity theory and the monadology, and this connection justifies the thesis that logic is grounded in metaphysics and is itself nothing but the metaphysics of truth. A critical preliminary discussion addressed the common objection from the priority of logic. A necessary condition of performance is not identical with the ground of the inner possibility of what is performed. Pointing to this necessary condition provides no grounding; it cuts off the question and blocks the way. The characteristic blindness of the argument shows itself in the fact that it recurs again and again. With this Heidegger comes to the Second Main Part. The remark that Plato or Aristotle would have served as well shows that Leibniz was a choice of exemplary material, not the object of the course. The final distinction, between a condition of performance and the ground of possibility, is the formal principle under which the Second Main Part will investigate the principle of reason: it will ask not which rule governs grounding but what makes grounding possible at all.

### Second Main Part: The Metaphysics of the Principle of Reason as the Basic Problem of Logic

Heidegger begins by laying out the problem so as to obtain the divisions of the discussion. Leibniz is generally regarded as the discoverer of the principle of reason; he recognizes two basic principles of truth and knowledge, the principle of contradiction and the principle of sufficient reason or of determination. Yet this central doctrine of his logic and metaphysics is the most obscure. It has not been clarified how the principle of sufficient reason relates to the principle of contradiction; it is not transparent, even roughly, what the principle of reason itself is grounded in; its connection with the basic metaphysical doctrine, the monadology, is questionable; it is entirely problematic what "principle" means here, what the character of a principle (*Grundsatz*) belongs to the principle of ground; and one must ask whether the principle belongs primarily to the theory of judgment and logical truth or has another root. The last question aims at the concept of a metaphysical principle as a rightly understood "logical" one. The five questions take up exactly the loose ends of §3, where the ranking of the principles remained unclear and the principle of reason was glimpsed as "the demanded disclosure of identity." The fourth question is the sharpest. A *Grund-satz* is literally a "ground-proposition," so asking what makes the principle of ground a principle is asking about ground twice over.

To bring these questions closer as clearly posed problems, a more radical approach to metaphysics than Leibniz's is needed. Relying on the preceding analysis, Heidegger first tries to fix the starting point of the problem of the metaphysics of the principle of reason by orienting himself on Leibniz, seeing that the principle functions in Leibniz in quite different meanings. Whether these formulations can be grounded uniformly within Leibniz's system and determined in their connection is a historical question that does not concern him. The disclaimer marks the shift from destruction to systematic inquiry. Leibniz now supplies material, not the object of interpretation.

The most general formulation of the principle is the vulgar one: nothing is without reason (*nihil est sine ratione*); positively, everything has its ground. *Ratio* is related to *reor*, to hold something to be so; *ratio* is that with regard to which I hold something to be something, why I hold that it is thus and so. *Ratio* here means first ground in the sense of the basis of a true assertion: ground related to knowledge, ground of knowledge. Aristotle says (*Posterior Analytics* A 2, 71b 9 ff.) that we think we understand something without qualification when we think we know the cause on account of which the thing is, know that it is its cause, and know that the thing cannot be otherwise. To understand is to know the ground of something, and to know it in the way and respect in which it grounds. To know is to have grasped the grounding ground of something in its grounding. The etymology of *ratio* from *reor* places the ground on the side of holding-true, which is where §8 will begin, while the Aristotelian definition already shows that knowing the ground means knowing it as grounding, which exceeds any psychological reason for belief.

*Aitia* is usually translated as cause, and cause understood as the ground of something's actual occurring. A tile falls from the roof, caused by bad weather that damaged the roof. But that this thing falls at all has its ground in its being a material thing determined by weight: that is its essential ground, its possibility. Aristotle has ground in this sense in view in another definition of genuine knowledge (*Metaphysics* Z 1, 1028a 36 ff.): we think we know each thing most of all when we know what a human being or fire is, rather than its quality, quantity, or place, since we know each of these too only when we know what the quantity or quality is. The tile example separates two grounds of one event: the weather explains that the tile fell now; heaviness explains that it can fall at all. The second is the ground of possibility, the *possibilitas* of §4.

Here ground is that to which grounding determination goes back, where it halts in its return as at something irresolvable, or from which, as the first and original, grounding starts. Ground means *archē*, beginning and point of departure: the first from which grounding knowledge begins and to which it first appeals. This concept of ground as *archē* can be taken more broadly to include cause and essential ground: the first from which a being, insofar as it is this being, takes its essence, and the first from which a being, insofar as it is actualized in just this way, begins its actualization, its coming to be. So understood, ground is the *ti estin*, the what-being, the *logos*. This meaning of ground as essence is also meant by *ratio*, and in this sense in Christian Wolff *causa* is a particular mode of *ratio*; here *ratio* is not primarily related to knowledge, "or perhaps to ontological knowledge?!" (p. 137). *Archē* is the unifying concept: beginning of knowing, of essence, and of coming-to-be. The parenthetical question anticipates the Second Main Part's result. The ground of essence is related to knowledge after all, but to the prior understanding of being, not to propositional knowledge.

Aristotle gathers these three main concepts of ground as *archē*, of which *aition* is one, in *Metaphysics* Δ 1 (1013a 17 ff.): common to all principles is being the first from which something is, comes to be, or is known. *Archē* is ground for what-being, that is, essence; ground for being-present-at-hand (that-being), that is, cause; ground for being-true, that is, argument (the grounding of a proposition). If one adds to what-being, presence, and truth, as essentially belonging with them, Dasein (the existence of the human being) and its action, there is also the ground of action: the motive, the "for the sake of which" (*hou heneka*) of the *Nicomachean Ethics*.
