Heidegger adds a fourth ground to Aristotle's three, and the addition is significant. The "for the sake of which" will reappear in §11 as the basic character of world and the core of freedom, so the motive, listed last, will turn out to be the origin of the others.

Four concepts of ground emerge roughly, four modifications of what can be meant by ground, and correspondingly four ways of grounding, of laying and giving ground: essence, cause, argument (in the sense of "a truth"), and motive. The formal-general characterization of ground is "the first from which." But this neither exhausts the idea of ground nor radically grounds and clearly grasps the four main concepts in their connection; their origin and order remain obscure. The formula "the first from which" gives only the formal unity of the four. Their real unity, the source from which they spring, is what the Second Section will seek in transcendence.

One thing has been achieved: the scope the vulgar formulation of the principle of reason can have, and mostly has, in its generality. Nothing is without ground: nothing, be it what-being, presence, truth, or action, is as such without its ground. Every way of being has its ground. Here something new and essential appears: the interlocking of the idea of being as such and the idea of ground as such. "Ground belongs to being" (p. 138). The claim is formulated without restriction to beings. It is not that every being has a cause, but that being as such, in each of its ways, is bound up with ground, which is the thesis the course will investigate.

This thesis, the principle of reason taken most broadly, itself demands grounding, and evidently it can be obtained only by clarifying the essence of being as such. Since that is the basic question of metaphysics, the principle of ground proves to be a basic problem of metaphysics, within which the metaphysics of truth, logic, is included. The move completes the title of the Second Main Part: the principle of reason is "the basic problem of logic" because logic is the metaphysics of truth and truth is one of the modes of being that have ground.

The connection between the problem of ground and the problem of being was made visible through a first clarification of the general formulation of the principle. But one must beware of taking this vulgar formulation as the exclusive guide, because the character of its generality is completely obscure. This obscurity is connected with the complete lack of clarity, since antiquity, about the principle-character of this principle, which was never made a problem. The principle-character, as a problem, points to *principium*, to *archē*, which is to say, to ground. The principle-character of the principle can itself be obtained only by clarifying the essence of ground as such. The reflexive structure is the methodological core of the course. The principle of ground cannot be grounded like other principles, because what it means to be a principle, a first ground, is precisely what it is about.

Schopenhauer was the first to present the principle of sufficient reason in all its previous formulations in a unified way. That remains a merit, especially since it was done in a doctoral dissertation, *On the Fourfold Root of the Principle of Sufficient Reason* (1813; second edition by Schopenhauer 1847; third edition by Frauenstädt 1864, with additions from Schopenhauer's copy, though with the wildest outbursts deleted). The trouble with the second edition is that it was neither more radical philosophically nor more rigorously grounded, but only "larded with tasteless attacks on Hegel, Schelling, and university philosophy" (p. 139). As a first orientation and a relatively unified unrolling of the problem, the work is always useful. But it is completely inadequate in scholarly solidity, historical presentation, and philosophical grounding, where it is "a flat Kantianism and simply superficial," and it is less philosophical incapacity than ressentiment that struck Schopenhauer blind and entangled him in an astonishing unfreedom. Since the problem of ground is discussed neither in logic nor in metaphysics, and Schopenhauer is little regarded, the small work is easy to obtain, "provided it does not become fashionable overnight in philosophy to concern oneself with the principle of reason." Heidegger acknowledges the only prior monograph on the principle and dismisses it, and the charge of "unfreedom" is not rhetorical ornament. The Second Main Part will locate the origin of ground in freedom, so a treatment of ground distorted by ressentiment fails on its own subject.

In chapter II, Schopenhauer surveys earlier doctrines, mainly to show how the concept of ground was gradually differentiated, first into the ground of being (*causa*) and the ground of knowledge (*ratio*). His §9 on Leibniz states that Leibniz was the first to have a clear concept of this distinction, distinguishing *ratio cognoscendi* from *causa efficiens* in the *Principles of Philosophy* and treating both as applications of the principle of sufficient reason, which he formally established as a main principle of all knowledge. Heidegger sets aside the fact that this characterization is extremely brief and essentially untenable; for a survey in a dissertation, it may suffice. Schopenhauer's organizing distinction is the one Heidegger has just rejected as insufficient. It divides ground by its field of application instead of asking what ground is.

More troubling, and characteristic of Schopenhauer's philosophizing and perhaps of the post-Hegelian age generally, is how Schopenhauer at sixty found it necessary to correct the twenty-five-year-old author of the dissertation. The second edition's §9 says Leibniz first formally established the principle as a main principle of all knowledge and science, indicated but did not expressly bring out or discuss its two main meanings; the main passage is *Principles of Philosophy* §32, and a little better in the French version, the *Monadology*. What is acceptable as external report comes from the early period and is owed to the philosophical milieu. What Schopenhauer adds substantively from his own philosophy is nothing but the irritated tone. This tone, which takes quite other forms toward Schelling and Hegel and became customary among others, cannot hide "the boundless superficiality with which these blustering epigones of the nineteenth century believed they had overcome the great and genuine philosophy from Plato to Hegel" (p. 140). The judgment on the post-Hegelian nineteenth century, a line from Plato to Hegel followed by epigones, is characteristic of Heidegger in this period and frames his own work as a retrieval of the "great" tradition.

Schopenhauer's historical orientation, precisely regarding Leibniz, means he grasped the problem only externally, not at the root. This is most palpable in §5, where he formulates the principle generally using Wolff's formula, which goes back to Leibniz, "nothing is without a reason why it is rather than is not" (*nihil est sine ratione cur potius sit quam non sit*), and translates simply: "nothing is without a reason why it is." So it remains in later editions. But the formula says something quite different: not "why it is," but "why it is rather than is not." Schopenhauer ignored the "rather than." This is not a mere translation error, which would not be worth pointing out; it shows that Schopenhauer moved in the old track and picked up the problem externally without grasping it. For this "rather than" (*potius quam*, *mallon ē*) is precisely what is central to the problem of the principle of reason. This can be seen only if one tries to root Leibniz's logic in his metaphysics and to understand this metaphysics philosophically beyond the stock schemata of the history of philosophy. The *potius* is the hinge of the Second Main Part. A ground that explains why something is "rather than" not presupposes an alternative, a space of possibility within which one option is preferred, and so connects ground with choice and freedom, as the following paragraphs develop.

The abbreviated vulgar formula is as general as it is indeterminate. Leibniz nowhere set up a single principled formula covering all modifications of the principle and its applications, but three formulations can be set out schematically that roughly make visible the problem behind the genuine form of the principle: there is a reason why something exists rather than nothing; why this exists rather than something else; why it exists thus rather than otherwise. The principle of reason is "the principle of the 'rather than,'" of the priority of something over nothing, of this over that, of thus over otherwise. The weight of the principle rests on actual beings, on facticity in the broadest sense, and in the factual a definite choice has been made as against what else was possible. The principle in this form thus rests on the connection of possibility and actuality. In *On the Radical Origination of Things* (1697), Leibniz writes that from the infinite combinations and possible series, that one exists through which the most essence or possibility is brought to existence; there is always in things a principle of determination drawn from the maximum or minimum, so that the greatest effect is achieved at the least cost. What is factually found is the best realization of everything possible. The three formulas correspond to the three questions §2 left open about truths of fact: that, what, and how something exists. The principle of reason in this form is a principle of existence, and it points directly to the unexecuted §6 on essence and the striving toward existence.

The whole treatise shows that in the background and center stands the idea of createdness, and that the ultimate reason of things is "the one ruling being beyond the world" (*unum dominans extramundanum*): "for a sufficient reason for existing cannot be found in any single thing, nor in the whole aggregate and series of things." Merely pointing out that Christian dogmatic theology stands behind this conception gains little; on the contrary, it evades the real problem. That always happens when one thinks one has solved a problem by calculating with psychological probability which drives were involved in posing and solving it. One may rest content with satisfying such curiosity about the psychology of worldviews, but must see that this searching of intellectual history for motivating drives would have no possible object, no justification, and no possibility of being carried out if problems were identical with the drives that help determine how they are handled. The polemic is aimed at the history of ideas as practiced in Dilthey's wake and at psychoanalytic reductions, as in the earlier note against asking "from what psychological complex" Leibniz's views arose. A problem is not the motive of the person who poses it, and the problem of the *potius* survives the removal of its theological setting.

The principle of sufficient reason as a principle of the "rather" remains even if the specifically theological interpretation is left out. A brief indication shows that a central problem of metaphysics lies hidden here. The idea of ground (as essence, cause, truth or argument, and motive) stands in the most original and closest connection with being. Now a connection of quite another kind announces itself: between ground and priority, between grounding and pre-ferring (*Vor-ziehen*), freedom of choice, ultimately the inclination toward the good (*propensio in bonum*), the connection of ground and freedom. The concept of "rather" contains a moment of preference. We know possibilities of preference only in the field where value and disvalue, higher and lower, are decided. Appealing to the dimension of value as the horizon of preferring, esteeming, and rejecting is no solution, since the idea of value is as obscure in its metaphysical genesis as that of the *agathon*, the good. Yet the connection between *agathon* and *on* already presses itself upon ancient philosophy. The hyphen in *Vor-ziehen* ("to draw forward," to prefer) reads preference as a drawing-ahead, and the "ground–freedom" pair from the Introduction's program is here derived from the principle itself. The rejection of value philosophy as a solution is consistent with the earlier genealogy of Rickert's "ought" from Descartes's volitional judgment.

The inner connection between the problem of being and the problem of preference shows itself especially in Plato's *Republic*, where the idea of the good lies "beyond being" (*epekeina tēs ousias*). Heidegger lists six determinations. First, the idea of the good is the last in the knowable (517b 8 f.), but so that it completes everything, holding all beings as beings enclosed; being has an inner relation to the good, and this idea is hard to see. Second, it is the cause of everything right and beautiful (517c 2): the basic determination of all order and belonging together, and since belonging together (*koinōnia*) is an essential determination of being, the idea of the good primarily sustains it. Third, it gives birth to light in the visible and to the lord of light, the sun (517c 3). The cave allegory would need detailed interpretation here: light is the presupposition of seeing, and the seeing of the understanding also needs light; what originally gives light for all knowledge of beings, all understanding of being, is the idea of the good. Fourth, it rules directly in the intelligible, providing truth and reason (517c 3–4); the inner possibility of truth and reason is grounded in the idea of the good. Fifth, it is the beginning, ground, and cause of everything (511b 7). Sixth, it is "still beyond being" (509b 9), surpassing beings and their being. What is meant by the idea of the good? Here it must suffice to point to the interlocking of the idea of being, the idea of ground in its several senses, and the idea of preference, of what is most preferable, the good (compare §11b). A note refers to the summer 1926 course *Basic Concepts of Ancient Philosophy* (GA 22). Heidegger reads the good as the Greek name for what makes being, truth, and order possible, and as "beyond" in the sense of transcending. The cross-reference to §11b, "The Phenomenon of World," signals that the *epekeina* will be interpreted as the "for the sake of which" that characterizes world, so that Plato's good becomes the ancient anticipation of transcendence.

It has become clear that there is a general, vulgar formulation of the principle of ground, which conceals the many-layered character of the problem, and that the problem of ground seems to be innermost akin to the problem of being as such. At the same time Heidegger asserts that the problem of ground is the central problem of logic. The discussion is therefore divided into three sections: laying free the dimension of the problem; the problem of ground itself; and the principle of reason and the basic problems of logic as the metaphysics of truth (see §14). The third section was not given as a separate division; its task is taken up in §14. The plan mirrors the claim of the First Main Part: one must first find the dimension in which ground can be seen (truth and transcendence), then analyze ground, then return to logic.

The principle of reason is not to be posited in one of its formulas and then proved. What must first be brought to light is what the principle says and can say. This clarification of its content must also clarify its character as a principle, and only then can one see what possibility of proof exists for it and what kind of proof it demands. It would be unmethodical and pointless to discuss, before clarifying its sense, whether the principle is immediately evident, follows from the essence of reason, rests on experience, or is a practical postulate. Perhaps none of these conceptions captures the essence of this principle, which is much richer than can be squeezed into these common distinctions. The four rejected options correspond to rationalism, Kantian critique, empiricism, and Kant's practical philosophy. Heidegger refuses the whole set of standard epistemological alternatives, anticipating the result that the principle of reason is neither a truth among truths nor a rule of thought but an expression of the being of Dasein.

#### First Section: Laying Free the Dimension of the Problem

Heidegger holds to the understanding of the idea of ground that formed first and has remained dominant, although the whole problem is always already more or less announced in it. It is no accident that the idea of ground first presents itself as "cause" and as "argument" (ground of holding-true, ground of proof), both designated as *aitia*. First, because the idea of cause and causation manifests itself initially and forcefully, on account of our being delivered over to the world, in the producing of one being from another, that is, in the know-how related to it, *technē*. Second, because beings show themselves at the same time as what is spoken of, in *logos* or *epistēmē*, where "truth" is at stake. *Logos* so understood, with its "what is it," is related to *idea*, to the *eidē*; and from *technē*, through the *aition* as *archē*, one is also referred to the *eidos*. The two sources of the concept of ground, production and discourse, are the two comportments in which Heidegger's Aristotle and Plato interpretations located the Greek understanding of being. Both lead to the *eidos*, the look that guides production and is said in the definition.

*Logos* and *technē*, taken very broadly, are the comportments in which beings as such first reveal themselves, so that the idea of being first forms within this horizon. Since the problem of ground is most closely bound up with the problem of being, it becomes intelligible why the first exposition of the problem of ground moves in the same dimension as the basic problem of metaphysics. Put differently, the problem of ground belongs to the basic problem (*Grund-problem*) of metaphysics. The hyphenated *Grund-problem* plays on the double sense: the problem of ground is the fundamental problem. The point is that the history of the concept of ground follows the history of the understanding of being, since both arise from the same comportments.

To win the dimension of the problem of ground means to open the field of view within which the phenomenon of ground becomes visible, within which one can understand what ground means and where the idea springs from. In keeping with the essence of philosophy, none of its problem domains lies directly in the line of sight of the common everyday understanding. A particular way into the specific problem field is always needed; Heidegger does not discuss its character, although it necessarily pre-figures what can properly be seen only afterward, but goes along a stretch of it. He starts from the vulgar opinion, or a conception close to it, because every such opinion has its hidden and perhaps distorted truth. The procedure is that of *Being and Time*: begin from the everyday understanding, which is not simply false but a disguised form of what is to be uncovered, and follow it until it gives out.

In traditional logic the principle of reason also appears, in some hidden place beside and below many others; it is counted, after the principles of identity, contradiction, and excluded middle, among the laws of thought. Heidegger chooses as his starting point the most vulgar and crude formulation possible, to push on from there to a new problem and at the same time to show how many meanings the principle can take on, precisely because it was never seriously made a problem. The starting point is chosen for its crudeness. If even the textbook formula leads into the problem of truth, the connection between ground and truth is not an artifact of Leibniz's system.

##### §8. The Principle of Reason as a Rule of Thought

The task is to make clear what it means to formulate the principle of reason as a rule of thought, whether this expresses the original sense of the principle, and whether it even clarifies the idea of ground; if not, this principle of logic looks poor as a principle of the supposedly fundamental science. Heidegger starts from the formulation in Sigwart's *Logic* (§32). The irony of the last clause is directed at the claim, refuted in §7, that logic is the first science.

Sigwart states that the fourth of the so-called laws of thought would express "the entirely general property of all judging, that in believing in the validity of a judgment there lies at the same time the belief in its necessity." The vulgar interpretation of the principle says that the human being cannot judge without having a ground for the assertion, or in Sigwart's words, that "no judgment is pronounced without a psychological ground of its certainty." What does ground of certainty mean? Sigwart, the leading logician of the late nineteenth century, psychologizes the principle: it becomes a fact about belief. Heidegger will show that even this formulation leads back to truth.

Certainty must be distinguished from the character of assertion discussed so far, truth. For being-true, one kind of ground has been named: the "argument," the ground of knowledge. Now there is talk of a ground of certainty. How do the ground of truth and the ground of certainty relate, and what does certainty mean in distinction from truth? The distinction between truth and certainty is the axis of the modern turn, since Descartes replaced the first with the second, and Heidegger will show that certainty presupposes truth.

The certainty of a piece of knowledge consists in my being assured of the truth of an assertion. "Certainty therefore always presupposes truth!" (p. 148). This assuring oneself of truth rests on something, on the basis of which it holds to the truth in question as a legitimate possession of knowledge. What gives the possession its legitimacy? The insight that what has been taken into possession is truth, which again means that it is grounded. Are the ground of truth and the ground of certainty then one and the same? Being certain about a truth is precisely insight into, the accomplished taking-insight into, the ground of the truth. Heidegger reverses the Cartesian priority in one line. Certainty is a mode of appropriating truth, so it depends on truth and its ground, not the reverse.

The ground of certainty is therefore the ground of truth, seen as ground, in its grounding of truth. The ground of certainty is the ground of truth that has become evident. They are materially the same and formally different only by the moment of being known and grasped. The formula makes evidence a relation to ground. To be evident is to see why something is true.

From this it follows first that holding a true assertion to be true, appropriating truth, must ultimately hold to the ground of truth in order to be sure of itself. Certainty as a mode of holding-true, of appropriating truth, is co-determined by what is appropriated, truth, which itself has "ground." Certainty and its securing depend on the ground of truth. The dependence is essential, not psychological. It is a matter of what certainty is, not of how people come to be certain.

Does the proposition quoted above formulate this essential connection, or does "no judgment is pronounced without a psychological ground of its certainty" mean something else? Evidently something else, if only because it is not a statement about an essential connection but an assertion of fact. Positively, it says that in every uttering and performing of judgments there is a psychological ground of certainty. That cannot mean that every judgment is evident, performed with insight into the ground of its truth, since many judgments are made and accepted as certain without insight. But the lack of insight into the grounding ground of truth does not exclude that the performance of judgment and holding-true has its motive. The motive for being certain and holding true, the motive of conviction, is not simply the ground of evidence. Judgments that lack the ground of certainty need not lack a motive of conviction. The thesis can at most mean that every performance of judgment contains a motive for holding-true, not that a ground belongs to the essence of truth and so to its appropriation as certainty. Heidegger separates three things that Sigwart runs together: the ground of truth, the evident ground of certainty, and the psychological motive of conviction. Only the first two concern the essence of truth.

Even this last interpretation can be doubted. Does every judgment have a motive for holding-true? Insufficiently grounded and even ungrounded assertions are made and held true, without it being possible to say that the holding-true is without motive; but a motive may also be lacking, so there are motiveless, ungrounded assertions. Even these are not wholly groundless, since the motiveless, ungrounded assertion is still "caused." The chain of weakening ends in the causal sense of ground. Even an assertion made for no reason has a cause, which shows that the vulgar formula slides among all four senses of ground distinguished earlier without noticing.

What does the proposition say, then? Only that people usually have motives when they judge. It is a statement about the average, habitual factual behavior of human beings in judging. It says nothing about the essence of ground, whether of truth or of certainty, but only characterizes the usual way of relating to motives for judgment. The "law of thought" turns out to be an empirical generalization about behavior.

If matters rested with this finding, nothing more would need to be said against it, except that philosophically nothing has been gained. But the finding not only takes the place of the principle of reason; one believes it can say more, since one lists it in logic as the science of the rules of thought. In this proposition, which records the fact of a common way of judging, one sees the foundation for a proposition of quite another character, namely the demand that all thinking and judging should have a ground. Because it is customary to have a psychological ground when judging, judging as such should always look for grounding. Thinking should ground itself and be grounded. One bases this normative proposition on that factual finding and further believes that the normative proposition expressing a norm is the genuine formulation of the principle of reason. One goes further still and identifies it with the proposition that every assertion needs proof, the demand that all propositions be absolutely provable and require proof. The sequence moves from fact to norm to the demand for universal proof, which is the rationalist ideal of §2, all truths provable a priori, now masquerading as a rule derived from habit.

Heidegger sets aside for now that a general norm for thinking is being grounded by appeal to empirical facts, an a priori proposition grounded empirically. Husserl's critique of psychologism in the first volume of the *Logical Investigations* shows the absurdity of this attempt. But that argument has only relative and negative force, since the question remains what a priori propositions are at all, and whether and in what sense such a normative proposition is a priori. Heidegger grants Husserl's refutation but denies that it settles anything. Anti-psychologism shows that logic cannot rest on psychology; it does not show what the a priori is, and that is the question the course pursues.

It is far more essential to ask how the normative proposition "all thinking should ground itself" is connected with the general principle of reason. It is easily seen to be a special case of the general proposition that all human comportment, as free, has its motive; hence also comportment as thinking. (Rightly understood, this proposition is an essential statement about comportment as such, not about how human beings usually behave.) Yet the normative proposition intends more than the demand that holding-true be grounded, because trying to meet this demand leads to exhibiting the ground of the truth to be held true. This brings back the connection between the ground of truth and the ground of certainty, which can now be grasped more clearly. Why should every holding-true, every appropriation of truth, have its ground? Only because it is one comportment among others? Or also and precisely because what is held true, the true proposition itself, as true, has a relation to something like ground? The parenthesis introduces the connection between motive and freedom as an essential one. That free comportment has motives belongs to what freedom is. But the principle of reason for thinking cannot be reduced to this, because the truth itself, not just the act of holding it, is related to ground.

Does truth as such have a relation to ground? In what sense, and what does "ground" mean here? If ground belongs to the essence of truth, is this essential connection the ground of the possibility of the demand that a true judgment be grounded? Does the postulate of a ground of certainty then follow only from this demand? And what does it mean that a demand follows from the essence of truth? The last question points to the result of §9: the demand for grounding is not a norm imposed on thought from outside but something that arises from the essence of truth, and so from Dasein.

The discussion of one conception of the principle has led to the insight that the problem of ground is somehow connected with the phenomenon of truth, so that the dimension indicated by the title "truth," "essence of truth," is also the one in which the problem of ground can be posed. In judging, a manifold of "grounds" has been distinguished that concern different things, so that "ground" itself means different things. All these kinds of grounds are somehow related to ground and truth. The title "ground" has always been somehow understood but not explicitly clarified. Even the indication that ground is somehow connected with truth gains little until the essence of truth itself has been sufficiently clarified with regard to the guiding problem. The first dimension is found: truth. §9 will show that the essence of truth leads further, to transcendence, which is why the "dimension of the problem" is not yet fully laid free.

##### §9. The Essence of Truth and Its Essential Relation to "Ground"

###### a) The Essence of Propositional Truth

Exhibiting the essential connection between truth and ground does not mean pointing out the grounds of particular truths; it means showing how truth as such is related by its essence to something rightly called "ground." This essential relation provides the inner possibility for every truth being in principle groundable, and it anchors the legitimacy of the demand, necessary in certain respects, that true propositions be grounded. The distinction answers the questions with which §8 ended. The demand for grounding is legitimate only because truth as such is related to ground, and the task is to find that relation, not to supply grounds.

The essential relation of truth to "ground" becomes visible only when the essence of truth itself is sufficiently clear. A general characterization of the essence of truth is needed first, and beyond that an account of the inner possibility of this essence. To clarify the inner possibility of the essence of truth is at the same time to characterize the dimension in which the problem of ground must be posed. The structure is two-staged: the essence of truth (subsection a), then its inner possibility (subsection b), which will turn out to be transcendence.

It has often been emphasized that truth has its place in the judgment, the assertion, and that the essence of truth is to be taken from its essence, provided the traditional thesis is valid. Leibniz's theory of judgment showed that truth is determined from the judgment: judgment means nexus, connection, inclusion, identity, the belonging together of subject and predicate, of concepts and representations; already in antiquity judgment is a synthesis of thoughts (*synthesis noēmatōn*). Truth is equated with this belonging together. What rightly belongs together is valid; truth is the validity of a connection of representations. Among other things, and derivatively, truth can be characterized this way. But does this characterization reach the essence? What is valid is what rightly belongs together. By what is it decided what belongs together, what an assertion must be measured by to be true? What determines the legitimacy of what belongs together? When truth is equated with identity, the question stops halfway. "It is characteristic of the mathematical way of thinking that Leibniz defines truth as identity for his purposes, without asking precisely here about the *possibilitas* of this *identitas*" (p. 154). Heidegger turns Leibniz's own concept of possibility (§4) against his definition of truth. Identity is a result whose possibility has not been examined, and the "validity" of Lotze and the Neo-Kantians inherits the same halfway stop.

What determines the legitimacy of the connection of representations, belonging together as accord? The assertion is in accord, it "fits," if the relation of representations agrees with what is judged about. Accord (the identity of the nexus) draws its legitimacy and validity from the agreement of what is thought in judging with what it is about, the object. The regulation of accord takes place in and through agreement. Truth as validity, belonging together, identity rests on agreement between representation and object, between *noēma* and *pragma*. Since antiquity this agreement has been the character of truth, determined as *homoiōsis*, *adaequatio*, conformity to, measurement against. But why and how does this character of conformity belong to the essence of truth? Why is the essence of truth determined along the thread of conformity? Where does this traditional determination of the essence of truth, the idea of *adaequatio*, spring from? German makes the argument audible: accord (*Einstimmigkeit*) is grounded in agreement (*Übereinstimmung*), and both are forms of "fitting" (*stimmen*). Internal consistency presupposes correspondence with the thing, so Leibniz's identity theory points back to the correspondence theory, and the question becomes where correspondence comes from.

Truth as identity is thus traced back to truth as *adaequatio*. It has also become clear that the attempt to grasp the essence of truth along the thread of the judgment does not straightforwardly succeed, or at least does not come out unambiguously. So far one conception has only been exchanged for another that also refers to the assertion: the connection of representations agrees. Where does the question stand now? At the agreement of representation with objects, at the subject and its consciousness in relation to what lies "outside consciousness." Truth as agreement concerns this relation of representations to objects. How is it to be determined? With this question one arrives at "a playground for all possible theories" (p. 155). Can the essence of truth be settled in this doubtful area? Should the clarification of truth be grounded on a doubtful theory of knowledge? The regress has reached the subject–object relation, and Heidegger refuses to found truth on epistemology, which is itself built on the unclarified relation.

Both defenders and opponents of the traditional characterization overlook that it is only a starting point for clarifying the essence of truth, one that, as the history of the concept of truth shows, by no means guarantees that it will be exploited rightly and can instead become the starting point for mistaken theories. "The determination of truth as *adaequatio* is the starting point, not yet the answer; the point of departure for posing the problem, not yet the solution!" (p. 155). Heidegger neither accepts nor rejects the correspondence theory. It correctly indicates a phenomenon and wrongly presents itself as an explanation of it.

Yet the path the tradition has taken from early on, through the assertion, the *logos*, cannot simply be rejected because it leads to difficulties. Everything depends on going this path rightly, so that it leads beyond where it at first means to lead, to the essence of truth as a character of the assertion. This is the method of *Being and Time* §44: follow the traditional thesis until it points beyond itself.

What does it mean to go this path rightly, to interpret the assertion as such rightly? A right interpretation takes the assertion, before all explanation, as it gives itself, avoiding the hasty construction of a theory before holding fast to what belongs to it and grasping it as what it primarily shows itself to be. But what does "gives itself primarily" mean here? As what does the assertion show itself to us when we relate to assertions before all theory of judgment, before all philosophical questions? The phenomenological maxim, to the things themselves, is applied to the assertion. The question is what the assertion is in the living understanding of speech, prior to any theory of it.

One might answer: an assertion is a pronounced judgment, a proposition uttered in speaking, a vocalization in words. These present themselves as sounds, tones, noises; from them we come to the verbal sound, the word, then meaning, thinking, representing, which takes place in the soul, and the representation in the soul is related to the object outside. Aristotle already has this sequence: noise, sound (*phthongos*, *phōnē*), verbal sound, word, meaning, thinking, representing, soul. This seems a strict and exact start from what is given first, from which one ascends step by step, and it leads to the problem of the agreement of representation and object. The more exactly one follows the relations between noise, sound, word, meaning, thinking, and representing, the more scientific the explanation of what is then called the agreement of thought with its object. The layered model is the construction of the opening of *De interpretatione*, adopted by empiricist philosophy of language. Its appearance of exactness comes from beginning with what is physically simplest, which Heidegger will show is not what is phenomenally first.

Does this start from vocalization capture what is given first? By no means. When someone in the lecture hall says "the blackboard is black," within an immediately given context of question and answer, what are we directed toward in taking in the assertion? The vocalization? The representation the speaker performs, of which the sounds are "signs"? No: toward the black blackboard itself, here on the wall. In perceiving the blackboard, or presentifying it, thinking of it and nothing else, we perform the assertion along with the speaker. What gives itself first in the assertion is what it is about. This is the phenomenological core. Understanding an assertion is not decoding sounds into ideas and then relating ideas to things; it is being directed at the thing along with the speaker.

Is much gained by this, and can anything be done with it? Certainly not if one means constructing hasty theories, since the procedure cuts them off. But something is gained if every genuine clarification of a phenomenon begins by grasping and holding fast to what gives itself. Only now do the difficulties of adequate interpretation begin. The primary givenness must be held, and from there one must ask back toward the full structure of the phenomenon. The phrase "ask back" (*zurückfragen*) names the method of the rest of the section: from the given assertion back to what makes it possible.

What gives itself primarily in naturally hearing and understanding an assertion? In what respect do we grasp it? In view of what it is about. This means that we grasp the assertion as an assertion about something. The "about" is the first structural moment, the intentionality of the assertion already named in §7.

This has been mentioned already, but now it is interpreted more sharply. In following an assertion about the blackboard, we do not first transpose ourselves into the speaker's soul and then somehow relate from there to the object outside. We always already comport ourselves toward the beings around us. Asserting does not first establish the relation; on the contrary, the assertion is possible only on the basis of an always already latent comportment toward beings. The asserting I, Dasein, is always already "with" (*bei*) the beings it asserts about. A first result: asserting about something is not an original relation to beings but is possible only on the basis of an already-being-with beings, whether merely perceiving or some practical comportment. "Asserting about is possible only on the basis of dealing with" (p. 158). The claim reverses the order of the layered model. The relation to beings is not the end of a chain beginning in sound but its presupposition; assertion is a modification of a prior involvement. "Dealing with" (*Umgang mit*) is the term of *Being and Time* for everyday practical involvement.

What does this mean for the problem? Can the essence of truth be characterized along the thread of the assertion? If truth means agreement, conformity to beings, then this asserting self-conforming is evidently founded in our having, as it were, already come to terms with beings in dealing with them, in the fact that beings we are not, but deal with somehow, are uncovered for us as beings. In the end an assertion can be true, conforming in its content to what it is about, only because these beings are already somehow uncovered. "An assertion about something is true only because dealing with it already has a certain truth" (p. 158). The popular argument against *adaequatio*, which points to its superfluity or impossibility, rests on the presupposition that the agreement of the assertion would first have to establish the subject–object relation. The rescue of *adaequatio* is exact. The correspondence of a statement with its object is possible because the object is already disclosed, so correspondence does not have to bridge a gap between inner and outer. The critique of correspondence theories (how can an idea match a thing?) assumes the gap that the analysis has shown not to exist.

The truth of the assertion is rooted more originally, in already-being-with. This happens "already," before asserting, and since when? Always already, as long as Dasein exists. This already-being-with belongs to the existence of Dasein, to its way of being. "Always already" is the temporal formula of the a priori in Heidegger. The priority of being-with over assertion is not a priority in time but belongs to Dasein's way of being.

Existence is the title for the way of being of the being we ourselves are, human Dasein. A cat does not exist but lives; a stone neither exists nor lives but is present at hand. Being-with belongs to existence, taken in the sense that what Dasein, as existing, is with is uncovered in and for this being-with. Being-with is not being present alongside, as a bench stands next to a house. A bench does not exist; it has no "being at the house," since that would mean that in the bench's presence as bench the house appeared to it as house, showed itself to it. The bench example distinguishes spatial juxtaposition from existential "being-with." The preposition *bei* in *Sein-bei* means "with," "at," in the sense of dwelling with and being familiar with something, not spatial proximity, and the test is whether the other shows itself.

Being-with as a character of existence is disclosive, a letting-encounter. It is disclosive as such, not occasionally but essentially. "This being-disclosive is the genuine sense of being-true" (p. 159). Going back to the original meaning of the concept of truth shows the elementary understanding that shaped the Greek word *a-lētheuein*. The Greeks saw this character of truth, though they did not let their insight become philosophically fruitful but covered it with theories. The negativity in the concept of truth as *a-lētheia* must be made an explicit problem. The hyphen in *a-lētheia* marks the privative alpha: truth as un-concealment, wrested from concealment. Heidegger announces that this negativity will be a problem, and the Second Main Part does take it up: the nothing of world and temporality (§§11–12) is where the "not" of unconcealment is lodged.

Already-being-with, dealing-with, is disclosive in itself. As the disclosure of what is present at hand in the broadest sense, this disclosing is an uncovering (*Entdecken*). Asserting about what is present at hand uncovers it in a mode of its own, as determining something as something. This is the genuine sense of synthesis (*symplokē*, *connexio*). This "something as something" is indissoluble in itself but nevertheless founded; it is possible only on the basis of the disclosing that already lies in dealing-with. The uncovering the assertion performs always depends on and feeds on the original uncovering that lies in dealing. Terminology follows *Being and Time*: "disclosing" (*Enthüllen*) is the general term, "uncovering" (*Entdecken*) the disclosure of beings not of Dasein's kind. The Leibnizian *connexio* of §1 is reinterpreted as the as-structure: the predicate "belongs" to the subject because the assertion lets the being be seen as something, on the basis of a prior uncovering.

Several results have been obtained. Being-true means being-disclosive. The truth of the assertion is not primary but founded in the disclosing of being-with, of dealing; this kind of disclosing is uncovering. Being-true as disclosing belongs to the existence of Dasein as such. The three theses relocate truth from the proposition to Dasein, which is the reversal announced in §7: truth must be clarified from Dasein before judgment can be understood.

This raises a fundamental question. If truth belongs to the assertion not primarily but derivatively, and only because it is an essential determination of being-with as a basic character of existence, does being-true belong to existence only insofar as Dasein relates to what is present at hand, or does truth belong equally originally to the other essential structures of Dasein? Is being-true only disclosing as uncovering, or must it be conceived still more originally and broadly? The question anticipates the disclosedness of Dasein to itself and to world. Uncovering beings is one mode of truth; a more original truth belongs to Dasein's understanding of its own being and of world.

It is also already clear that if the truth of the assertion is not original, and ground stands in an essential relation to truth, then the original problem of ground cannot be grasped from propositional truth either. This rules out the approach of traditional logic, which sought the principle of reason among the rules of propositions. The problem of ground must be sought where the more original truth lies.

###### b) Intentionality and Transcendence

Before pursuing the essence of truth more radically, Heidegger appropriates what has been gained more clearly, precisely with regard to traditional and still dominant opinions that determine logic through and through. The subsection is a confrontation with epistemology and with phenomenology's own concept of intentionality.

One might think that there is being-with, and alongside it presence-at-hand; being-with belongs to the subjectivity of the subject, and subjectivity is characterized by the thesis of the subject–object relation, understood as a co-presence of subject and object such that neither is without the other: with every subject an object is present, and with every object a subject. The two theses are the common formula of correlativist epistemology, which Heidegger takes apart one by one.

On the second thesis, "no object without subject": "object" is ambiguous. It means what is present for itself, and this present-for-itself taken in its objectivity as object, in which objectivity it is meant and grasped precisely as a being present of itself, which in its presence does not need to be grasped. For grasping what is present at hand as present at hand involves a withdrawing release of what is present; grasping has the character of leaving what is grasped to rest in itself and understands itself as a receiving. If "object" means what is present for itself, what does not need to be grasped, the thesis is false: such a being does not need a subject, and its presence does not posit the presence of a subject. The thesis also misjudges the essence of the subject, taking it merely as grasping. The subject can be what it is, Dasein exists, without grasping beings as objects; objectification, let alone theoretical thematization, does not necessarily belong to being-with. If one takes the object with regard to its objectivity, it is true that this objectivity depends on a grasping subject. The analysis of grasping as "withdrawing release" rejects idealism from within the act of knowing. To know something as present at hand is to let it be independent of the knowing; knowing includes the acknowledgment of its object's independence.

On the first thesis, "with every subject an object is present": this has become doubtful too. It says nothing about the subjectivity of the subject. The subject can very well be without an object, but that does not mean it could exist without a being-with, which belongs to being a subject as such. With the existence of a Dasein, as soon as Dasein exists, "something happens, history has begun," namely "the unheard-of fact that beings encounter and can encounter another being" (pp. 161–62), without the subject specifically directing itself toward them. The sentence treats the existence of Dasein as an event in the history of beings as a whole: with Dasein, beings first become manifest. This is the metaphysical weight of transcendence that the appendix on metontology will draw out.

Appealing to the subject–object relation to characterize subjectivity leaves out what is essential and misses what is decisive. It leaves out the character of this "relation between," which is precisely what needs clarifying, and it misses the genuine concept of subjectivity by overlooking that the "relation to" belongs to the essence of subjectivity. Even the thesis that relation to possible objects belongs to subjectivity does not settle everything, since one can hold it without grasping the right ontological sense of the claim that disclosive being-with belongs to existence. Heidegger distinguishes his position from the phenomenological thesis of intentionality, which already says relation belongs to consciousness. The difference lies in the ontological sense: what kind of being has this relation, and what is its disclosure.

The theme is the essential connection between truth and ground, which requires clarifying truth, first along the traditional thread of truth as propositional truth. An interpretation of assertion showed it to be founded in already-being-with, where truth lies more originally. This being-with, as an existential, is itself a problem, precisely because of the apparent self-evidence of starting with a subject–object relation. The summary identifies "being-with" as an existential, a structure of existence, which must now be clarified against its epistemological disguise.

Strangely, the problem addressed by this thesis does not move. It is as old as philosophy and appears already in Parmenides. That the soul, thinking and representing, consciousness, relates to objects, or conversely that beings lie over against (*antikeitai*) representing, intuiting, and thinking, arose early and easily from the prephilosophical understanding of Dasein and kept this general, indeterminate form for a long time. The problem offers the seductive look of the self-evident and simple. It is discussed from antiquity through the Middle Ages and modern philosophy up to Hegel's dialectic; in Kant especially the relation of consciousness to the object becomes a problem, as when he writes to Marcus Herz (21 February 1772): "I asked myself: on what ground rests the relation of what we call representation in us to the object?" The letter to Herz uses the word "ground," so Kant's founding question of the critical philosophy is itself a question of ground, which is why it belongs in this course.

Characteristically, Kant and his successors, and today's epigones even more, ask too hastily about the ground of the possibility of consciousness's relation to the object, without first sufficiently clarifying what is meant by this relation whose possibility is to be explained, between what it holds, and what kind of being it has. The charge is methodological: the question of possibility has been asked before the phenomenon was described.

What "relation" means remains unclarified. This lack of clarity rebounds on what stands in relation. It shows itself above all in the indeterminacy of the concept of the subject, and on the other side in the naivety and lack of problems regarding the being of beings. Their "in-dependence," for example, is on the one hand only a negative determination, and on the other it can mean only independence from the subject, so it must be explained, indeed made a problem, precisely in relation to the subject. The hyphen in "in-dependence" makes the point: the realist's key term is defined by reference to the subject it claims to exclude, so realism is not an escape from the relational problem.

Two things must be observed about the "relation" of subject and object: the simplicity of the problem in its breadth and originality, and correspondingly that it can be grasped only as a whole or not at all. That is the difficulty, since the self-evidence of the starting point tempts one to regard the approach to a solution and its conditions as equally self-evident. The problem's simplicity is the simplicity of a fundamental phenomenon, not of an easy one. It cannot be solved piecemeal by explaining first the subject, then the object, then their link.

Epistemology in the second half of the nineteenth century and in recent decades repeatedly based its questions on the subject–object relation, but idealist and realist explanations alike had to fail because what was to be explained was insufficiently determined. How much the prior clarification of the phenomenon determines the whole problem shows itself here: the first clarification reaches so far that, where it is actually carried out, a problem in the sense of idealist or realist epistemology disappears altogether. This is *Being and Time* §43 in brief. The problem of the external world is not solved but dissolved, because being-in-the-world is already the relation to beings that realism tries to prove and idealism tries to construct.

The most recent attempts conceive the subject–object relation as a "relation of being." Here the inadequacy, misdirection, and misunderstanding of the central problem come to light. Nothing is gained by this title as long as it is not said what kind of being is meant and the way of being of the beings between which the relation holds remains obscure. One thinks that leaving the being of the relation and the ways of being of subject and object undetermined poses the problem as free of prejudice as possible. The opposite is true. The earlier formulation is more critical in not speaking of a relation of being, since being, in Nicolai Hartmann and Max Scheler too, is taken as presence-at-hand. This relation is not nothing, but it is not a being as something present at hand. Hence Hartmann is pushed into relapsing into "critical realism," "probably the most unphilosophical of all ways of posing this problem" (p. 164). The target is Hartmann's *Metaphysics of Knowledge* (1921). Calling the relation a "relation of being" while tacitly understanding being as presence-at-hand makes it a relation between two things, which is the very model the phenomenon contradicts.

One of the main preparatory tasks of *Being and Time* is to bring this "relation" radically to light in its original essence, with a fundamental intention (§§12 and 13 as first introductory characterizations). By another route and with another intention, Max Scheler reached similar insights and planned a large treatise "Idealism–Realism," partly with regard to Heidegger's investigations; parts II and III appeared in the *Philosophischer Anzeiger* (1927–28). The plan for this confrontation came to nothing. Heidegger records the convergence with Scheler as the background to the memorial earlier in the course.

In their last long conversation, in December 1927, they agreed on four points. First, the problem of the subject–object relation must be posed entirely anew, free of previous attempts. Second, it is not a question of so-called theory of knowledge; it must not be posed primarily with regard to a subject grasping an object, and this grasping may not be presupposed at the outset. Third, it is centrally important for the possibility of metaphysics and stands in the most intimate relation to its basic problem. Fourth, and most essential, the moment has come, precisely given the desolation of the public philosophical situation, to venture again the step into genuine metaphysics, to develop it from the ground up. That was the mood in which they parted, the glad mood of a promising struggle; fate willed otherwise. Scheler was optimistic and believed he already had the solution, whereas Heidegger is convinced that the problem has not yet even been posed and worked out radically and totally. His essential intention is to pose the problem first and work it out so that the whole Western tradition is concentrated in its essentials into the simplicity of one basic problem. The four points are the program of the Second Main Part: transcendence instead of epistemology, as the basic problem of metaphysics. The last sentence states Heidegger's ambition in 1928, a single problem, being and transcendence, in which the whole tradition is gathered.

In the treatise, Scheler rejects both idealism and realism as impossible and tries to give a reason why both had to fail. Characteristic of both as their "first falsehood" (*prōton pseudos*) is the lack of clarity about the relation between essence and existence. This shows that the problem depends on a central question of general ontology, only Scheler strangely believed this question settled, whereas here all the problems begin, and can begin only when the basic problem of being as such is unrolled. However clearly Scheler rejects the traditional attempts and roughly sees the ground of their failure, he takes over from tradition, unexamined, theses even less grounded and problematized than those treated in epistemology. That Scheler saw no problem in the genuine sphere of the problem led him, as will be shown, to misunderstand the central problem in Heidegger's attempt. Scheler's diagnosis is right, Heidegger says, but his treatment takes the distinction of essence and existence as given, the very distinction the unexecuted §6 and GA 24 put in question. The promised criticism of Scheler comes in §11c, on Scheler's reading of care.

Their meeting was nevertheless no accident, partly because Heidegger learned from Scheler, but above all because both attempts grew out of phenomenology, especially out of the understanding of intentionality. Here again is a title and concept so self-evident that one no longer lingers over it and takes it, even at the preparatory stage, for the solution, a sure key to every door. What it means must itself be made a problem. Heidegger turns now to phenomenology's own central concept and subjects it to the same scrutiny as the subject–object relation.

Heidegger gives a brief orientation. Already in Aristotle the "relatedness" of intuiting and thinking, *noēsis*, to the *noēma* comes out. In the nineteenth century Franz Brentano put the concept of intentionality at the center of his *Psychology from an Empirical Standpoint* (1874). "From an empirical standpoint" is essential for how the problem is posed there: his methodological principle is to bring the psychic into view before all natural-scientific explanation. What characterizes everything psychic is "the intentional inexistence of the object": everything psychic relates to something. Following the traditional division of faculties, Brentano distinguishes three irreducible basic classes of psychic phenomena, three ways of relating: presentation, judgment, and interest (love and hate). He aims at a classification of psychic phenomena; the concept of the psychic itself remains untouched, and intentionality remains an internal affair of psychology without fundamental significance. Brentano's descriptive psychology discovers intentionality but places it inside a region, the psychic, whose being is not questioned. That region-bound status is its limit.

Husserl, with his intentional concept of consciousness in the Fifth Logical Investigation, freed the problem from this narrowness. He prepared a new stage by showing that intentionality determines the essence of consciousness as such, the essence of reason as such. With his doctrine of the immanent intentionality of the *cogitationes* he also connected the problem with the basic questions of modern philosophy since Descartes. But as Brentano left the concept of the soul untouched, so Husserl, in his idealist theory of knowledge, did not further ask about the being constituted as consciousness. The insight into intentionality does not go so far as to see that grasping this structure as an essential structure of Dasein "must revolutionize the whole concept of the human being" (p. 167). Only then does its central philosophical significance become clear. Scheler, from his initially realist position, took up intentionality not so much as a determination of reason as such but as a structural moment of acts that essentially belong to a person. By conceiving intentionality as an essential moment of personality, he saw its decisive significance. Although he did not make it a further problem, this insight let him see beyond the alternative of idealism and realism. But he did not make personality itself a metaphysical problem, leaving it as the center of intentional acts. The criticism of Husserl is exact: intentionality is described without asking about the being of the intending entity, which is left as "consciousness." Scheler's advance was to tie intentionality to a being, the person, but the person remains a mere center of acts. Heidegger's step is to ask about the being of that being, Dasein.

If the self-relating that intentionality names is rightly understood, it cannot be taken as a character of representing that remains within the psychic, as it could seem in Brentano. Otherwise one must assume a sphere of the psychic in which representations occur that are intentionally directed at images, which also belong to the psychic, and one faces Brentano's problem of how the psychic sphere relates to the physical. But the intentionality of a perceiving is directed not at an image in the soul that then has to be related to what is present at hand, but at what is present at hand itself. Only by holding onto this natural sense of intentionality does one have the basis for an appropriate approach. One objects that then the problem of the subject–object relation disappears altogether. Certainly, "but that is precisely the purpose of the concept of intentionality: to eliminate this pseudo-problem" (p. 168). Heidegger defends the realist sense of intentionality from its immanentist interpretation. Perception reaches the thing itself; the image theory recreates the gap intentionality was meant to close.

Intentionality itself must nevertheless become a problem. It is related to beings themselves and in this sense is an ontically transcending comportment, but it does not originally constitute this relation-to, which is founded in being-with beings. This being-with is, in its inner possibility, grounded in turn in existence. This makes visible the limits of the previous interpretation and function of the concept of intentionality, and its fundamental significance. The concept brings not only a modification of the traditional concept of consciousness and spirit; the radical grasp of the phenomenon it means, in an ontology of Dasein, leads to a fundamental, "universal" overcoming of that position. From here the previous concept of intentionality proves to be a narrowed conception, insofar as it is understood as a comportment toward what is present at hand. This also explains the tendency to take self-apprehension as an inward-directed ontic intentionality. Owing to this narrowing, intentionality is conceived primarily as "meaning" (*Meinen*), understood as an indifferent character of knowing. So all directedness receives primarily the character of knowing, as in Husserl, who characterizes the basic structure of all intentional comportment as *noēsis*; all intentionality is first a knowing meaning, on which other modes of comportment toward beings are built. Scheler first made clear, especially in the essay "Love and Knowledge," that intentional comportments are quite different and that love and hate even found knowing, taking up motives from Pascal and Augustine. The narrowing has three aspects. Intentionality is modeled on the relation to present things, it is then turned inward to explain self-consciousness, and it is modeled on theoretical knowing. The criticism of Husserl's founding of all acts on objectifying acts (the doctrine of the *Logical Investigations* and *Ideas*) is decisive, and Scheler's priority of love over knowledge is Heidegger's ally against it.

Heidegger fixes three points about the previous concept of intentionality: it is only an ontic transcendence; it concerns existing comportment toward beings only in a certain restriction; and it comes into view only in a narrowing theoretical conception, as *noēsis*. "Ontic transcendence" is a precise term. Intentionality goes beyond the subject to beings, but it presupposes a more original surpassing that is not directed toward beings at all.

Underlying the whole problem of the "relation" of "subject" to "object" is, undiscussed, the problem of transcendence, the term first taken quite vulgarly: a being (Dasein) passes over to another being (Dasein or something present at hand), such that in this transcending, that to which Dasein transcends is disclosed to it in a very broad sense. The first task was to grasp this nearest aspect of transcendence, its vulgar concept. The vulgar phenomenon of transcendence is the transcendence in which Dasein immediately and essentially moves. This is precisely hard to see, as the discussion has shown, and it requires freedom from prejudice and sharpness of sight; the preparatory work was done by developing the problem of intentionality. "Transcending" is taken in its etymological sense, *trans-scendere*, to climb over. The "vulgar" concept is the one in which Dasein passes over to other beings, which is intentionality correctly understood.

For a radical posing of the problem, it is essential to lay free the original phenomenon of transcendence. This problem is not identical with that of the subject–object relation but is more original in scope and kind, and it is immediately connected with the problem of being as such (compare *Being and Time* §§12 and 13, and, made visible at increasingly original levels, §§69 and 83). The transcendence of Dasein is the central problem, with the aim of clarifying not "knowledge" but Dasein and its existence as such, and this in turn with a fundamental-ontological intention. The cross-references trace the concept through *Being and Time*: being-in-the-world (§§12–13), the temporality of transcendence (§69), and the closing question about the meaning of being (§83). Transcendence connects the analytic of Dasein with the question of being.

The problem of transcendence as such is not identical with the problem of intentionality. Intentionality, as ontic transcendence, is itself possible only on the basis of original transcendence, being-in-the-world. This primal transcendence makes possible every intentional relation to beings. But this relation happens in such a way that, in and for comportment, beings are in the "there" (*Da*). It is grounded in a prior understanding of the being of beings. This understanding of being first grants the possibility that beings manifest themselves as beings; "it carries the light ahead, in whose brightness beings can show themselves" (p. 170). If original transcendence (being-in-the-world) makes the intentional relation possible, and this relation is ontic, and the relation to the ontic is grounded in the understanding of being, then there must be an inner kinship between original transcendence and understanding of being as such; "indeed, in the end they are one and the same." The identification of transcendence and understanding of being is the key thesis of the course. The image of light carried ahead recalls Plato's good, which gives light (§8's introductory section), and prepares the reading of the *epekeina* as transcendence in §11b.

Earlier (subsection a) it was shown that being-true as being-disclosive belongs to being-with what is present at hand; "uncovering" what is present at hand is a basic form of comportments of dealing. The essence of truth as a whole can therefore be clarified only as the problem of transcendence as such. The conclusion completes the "laying free of the problem dimension": ground leads to truth, truth to transcendence. The next section examines transcendence through fundamental ontology.

##### §10. The Problem of Transcendence and the Problem of *Being and Time*

The understanding of being forms the basic problem of metaphysics as such; "what does 'being' mean?" is the basic question of philosophy. Heidegger does not present the posing of the problem and its "retrieval" in *Being and Time*; instead he sets it out from outside in guiding theses, so as to fix the "problem of transcendence." The section is Heidegger's own retrospective commentary on *Being and Time*, a year after its publication, and its guiding theses address the misunderstandings the book had already provoked.

First, a general characterization. The problem begins with fundamental ontology as the analytic of the existence of Dasein. This analytic is carried out with a fundamental-ontological intention, and only with it; this intention regulates its starting point, execution, limits, and the way particular phenomena are made concrete. The understanding of being is to be brought to light from Dasein's way of being, which is primarily existence. This constitution of Dasein's being is such that the inner possibility of the understanding of being essentially belonging to Dasein can be shown in it. The analytic is therefore not anthropology or ethics but concerns this being in its being as such; it is a preparatory analytic, and the metaphysics of Dasein itself is not yet at the center. The restriction answers the reading of *Being and Time* as philosophical anthropology or as an existentialist ethics of authenticity. Every structure the analytic exhibits is chosen and limited by its function of making the understanding of being intelligible.

The guiding theses begin. For the being that is the theme of the analytic, the neutral title "Dasein" was chosen rather than "human being." It designates the being to which its own way to be is, in a definite sense, not indifferent. The choice of term avoids the anthropological, biological, and theological connotations of "man," and fixes the being by its formal structure: its being matters to it.

The peculiar neutrality of the title is essential, because this being must be interpreted before every factical concretion. Neutrality also means that Dasein is neither of the two sexes. But this sexlessness is not the indifference of an empty nothing, the weak negativity of an indifferent ontic nothing. Dasein in its neutrality is not an indifferent Nobody and Everybody but "the original positivity and potency of the essence" (p. 172). The thesis answers the objection that the analytic abstracts from embodiment and sexuality. Neutrality is not the removal of such determinations but the origin from which they arise, as the following theses explain.

Neutrality is not the nullity of an abstraction but the potency of the origin, which carries within itself the inner possibility of every concrete factical humanity. "Potency" (*Mächtigkeit*) is Heidegger's word for the positive character of the origin. The neutral is not what remains when everything particular has been removed but what makes every particular possible.

This neutral Dasein is never what exists; Dasein exists only in its factical concretion. But neutral Dasein is the primal source of the inner possibility that springs up in every existing and makes existence inwardly possible. The analytic always speaks within Dasein about the Dasein of those who exist, but not to the Dasein of existences; the latter would be absurd, since one can speak only to someone existing. The analytic of Dasein therefore lies before all prophecy and worldview-preaching; it is not wisdom either, which is only laid out in the structure of metaphysics. Against this analytic as a "system of Dasein" stands the prejudice of life-philosophy, which springs from fear of the concept and testifies to a failure to understand the concept and "systematics" as intellectual and yet historical architectonic. The distinction between speaking "about" and speaking "to" separates the analytic from edification. The analytic addresses no one and exhorts no one, which answers the reading of *Being and Time* as a call to authenticity. The rebuke of life-philosophy, Dilthey's heirs and Jaspers's *Psychology of Worldviews*, defends the conceptual rigor of the analytic.

This neutral Dasein is therefore not the egoistic individual, not the ontically isolated individual; the egoity of the individual is not placed at the center of the problem. But the essential feature of Dasein, belonging to itself in its existence, must be included in the approach. The neutral approach means a peculiar isolation of the human being, not in the factical-existentiell sense, as if the philosopher were the center of the world, but "the metaphysical isolation of the human being" (p. 172). *Being and Time*'s "mineness" is not solipsism. The isolation in question is the singling out of Dasein as the being that understands being, not the withdrawal of the individual from others.

Dasein as such harbors the inner possibility of factical dispersion into bodiliness and thus into sexuality. The metaphysical neutrality of the human being, isolated most inwardly as Dasein, is not the empty abstraction from the ontic, a neither-nor, but "the genuinely concrete of the origin, the not-yet of factical dispersion." As factical, Dasein is always, among other things, split into a body and, together with that, split into a particular sexuality. "Splitting" and "division" sound negative at first, like "destruction," and taken ontically these negating concepts immediately connote something of lesser value. But here something else is at issue: the characterization of the multiplication (*Mannigfaltigung*, not "manifoldness") that lies in every individuated factical Dasein as such. It is not the idea that a great primal being is ontically split into many individuals, but the clarification of the inner possibility of multiplication that lies in every Dasein itself, for which bodiliness is one organizing factor. Manifoldness is not a mere formal plurality of determinations; it belongs to being itself. The essence of Dasein, in its metaphysically neutral concept, includes an original dispersal, which in a quite definite respect is dispersion. A rough indication: as existing, Dasein never relates only to one object, and if it does, only by disregarding other beings that appeared before and appear together with it. This multiplication does not happen because there are several objects; it is the other way round. The same holds for Dasein's relation to itself, according to the structure of historicity in the broadest sense, insofar as Dasein happens as extension. Another essential possibility of Dasein's factical dispersion is its spatiality. The phenomenon of Dasein's dispersion into space shows itself, for example, in the fact that all languages are primarily determined by spatial meanings. This can be clarified only when the metaphysical problem of space is posed, which becomes visible only after passing through the problem of temporality ("radically: metontology of spatiality"; see the appendix). The thesis is the most concentrated treatment of embodiment and sexuality in Heidegger's Marburg period. It parallels the monad of §5, whose unity contains a manifold that it generates itself. Dasein's multiplication, the fact that many beings show up together, precedes the plurality of objects, just as the monad's perceptions arise from its drive. The reference to historicity as "extension" (*Erstreckung*) recalls *Being and Time* §72, and "metontology of spatiality" is the first appearance of the term that the appendix will explain.

This transcendental dispersion belonging to the metaphysical essence of neutral Dasein, as the binding possibility of its factical existentiell splitting and division, is grounded in an original character of Dasein: thrownness. Dispersion is thus traced to thrownness, Dasein's being delivered over to beings.

This thrown dispersion into the manifold, to be taken metaphysically, is the presupposition for Dasein as factical to be able to let itself be carried by beings it is not, with which it first identifies precisely on the basis of dispersion. Dasein can let itself be carried, for example, by what we call nature in a very broad sense. Only what is essentially thrown and caught up in something can let itself be carried and embraced by it. This also holds for the absorption of primitive, mythical Dasein in nature. Mythical Dasein, in its being carried, has the peculiarity of not being aware of itself as regards its way of being, which does not mean that it lacks self-consciousness. It belongs to the essence of factical dispersion, however, that thrownness and being caught up remain deeply hidden to themselves, and precisely thereby the simplicity and "care-lessness" of an absolute being carried comes into Dasein. The analysis connects with the earlier remark on *mana* and with Cassirer's mythical thought. Myth is a mode of thrownness in which Dasein is so absorbed in nature that its own being remains hidden, and the hyphenated "care-lessness" (*Sorg-losigkeit*) names this as a privative mode of care.

The essentially thrown dispersion of Dasein, still understood as wholly neutral, shows itself among other things in Dasein's being being-with Dasein. This being-with does not arise from factical being-together, nor is it explained merely from a supposedly more original generic being of sexually divided bodily beings. Rather, this generic striving together and generic union has as its metaphysical presupposition the dispersion of Dasein as such, being-with as such. This basic metaphysical character of Dasein can never be derived from generic organization, from living together.
