Factical bodiliness and sexuality explain only, and only within the limits of the essential contingency of all explanation, how far the being-with of a factical Dasein is pushed in a particular factical direction, in which other possibilities are screened off or remain closed. Heidegger rejects any biological derivation of sociality. Being-with is not a result of sexual difference or of the species; sexual union presupposes being-with, not the other way around.

Being-with, as a genuine relation of existence, is possible only in such a way that each co-existing one can be and is genuinely himself. This freedom of being with one another presupposes the possibility of self-determination of a being of the character of Dasein as such, and it is a problem how Dasein, as essentially free, can exist in the freedom of factically bound being-with-one-another. Since being-with is a basic metaphysical determination of dispersion, this dispersion is ultimately grounded in the freedom of Dasein as such: "the basic metaphysical essence of metaphysically isolated Dasein is centered in freedom" (p. 175). But how is the concept of freedom to be grasped metaphysically? It seems too empty and too simple. Yet ontic inexplicability does not exclude ontological-metaphysical understanding. Freedom is the title for central problems (in-dependence, binding, regulation, standard), some of which will be touched on in clarifying the concept of world (§11b). The chain of theses has led from neutrality through dispersion and thrownness to freedom, which the Second Section will identify as the origin of ground. The "problems" listed under freedom, binding and standard, anticipate §11c, where freedom gives itself the binding "for the sake of."

These theses state what the analytic of Dasein deals with. Two further guiding theses clarify how it proceeds.

This metaphysics of Dasein, at first as analytic, can be obtained only in a free projection of the constitution of being itself. Because Dasein always exists as itself, and being-a-self and existing are only in their enactment, the projection of the basic ontological constitution of Dasein must spring from the construction of an extreme possibility of an authentic and whole ability-to-be of Dasein. The projection is directed at Dasein as a whole and at the basic determinations of its wholeness, although ontically it is only as existing. The winning of the metaphysical neutrality and isolation of Dasein as such is possible only on the basis of "the extreme existentiell engagement of the one projecting" (p. 176). The thesis explains the role of authenticity and being-toward-death in *Being and Time*. They are not ethical ideals but methodological requirements. Only by projecting Dasein's extreme possibility can the analyst grasp the whole structure, and this projection requires the philosopher's own engagement.

This engagement is necessary and essential for the metaphysical projection, but precisely for that reason, as a single existentiell comportment, it is not authoritative and not binding among the manifold concrete possibilities of factical existing. For the metaphysical projection itself reveals the essential finitude of the existence of Dasein, which is understood existentially only in "the inessentiality of the self," which becomes concrete only, as can be grounded metaphysically, through and in the service of the possible whole, which reveals itself in metaphysical questioning in a quite distinctive way. How far the metaphysical projection and the philosopher's existentiell engagement nonetheless contain an existentiell guidance, an indirect one, is a separate problem. The paragraph limits the claim of the previous one. The philosopher's engagement is a condition of the projection, not a model for others, because the projection reveals that the self is inessential except in service of the whole. The "inessentiality of the self" corrects the individualist reading, and the "indirect guidance" leaves open, in the manner of Kierkegaard's indirect communication, what bearing the analytic may have on life.

With regard to the metaphysical neutrality and isolation of Dasein, the ontological interpretation of its structures must be concrete. Neutrality is not the indeterminacy of a vague concept of consciousness in general; genuine metaphysical universality does not exclude concretion but is in one respect the most concrete, as Hegel already saw, though he exaggerated it. But the concreteness of the analysis of phenomena of Dasein, which gives the metaphysical projection direction and content, easily tempts one first to take these phenomena by themselves, and second to absolutize them, in their extreme formulation conditioned by fundamental ontology, on the wrong side, as existentiell. The more radical the existentiell engagement, the more concrete the ontological-metaphysical projection; but the more concrete this interpretation, the easier the fundamental misunderstanding that the existentiell engagement as such is what is essential and unique, whereas it reveals itself precisely in the projection in its personal irrelevance. Heidegger diagnoses the "existentialist" reading of his book as a structural temptation: the more concrete the analysis, the more it looks like a personal confession, although its concreteness serves the universal.

The existentiell engagement of fundamental ontology brings with it the appearance of an extremely individualistic, radical atheism; that is the worldview interpretation one reaches for. One must examine whether it is justified, and if so, what metaphysical, fundamental-ontological sense it has. One must not lose sight of the fact that such a fundamental-ontological clarification decides nothing; it is meant to show precisely that nothing can be decided in this way; nevertheless there is always the factical necessity of the "presupposition" of a factical situation. The "atheism" of the analytic is methodological: it brackets the question of God because fundamental ontology cannot decide it. Heidegger neither affirms nor denies the worldview reading but reduces it to a question about the sense of a neutral analysis, while conceding that every philosopher philosophizes from a factical situation.

These theses indicate the intention underlying the analytic of Dasein and what it requires in execution. Its basic aim is to exhibit the inner possibility of the understanding of being, which means at the same time the inner possibility of transcendence. The identity of understanding of being and transcendence stated in §9b returns as the aim of the whole analytic.

Why is the preparatory analytic of Dasein, aimed at disclosing the possibility of the understanding of being, an exhibition of the temporality of Dasein? Why does the metaphysical projection of Dasein move toward time and its radical interpretation? Because relativity theory deals with time or the principle of objective time measurement? Because Bergson, and Spengler after him, deal with time? Because Husserl worked out the phenomenology of inner time-consciousness, Kierkegaard speaks of temporality in the Christian sense in contrast to eternity, or Dilthey sees the historicity of Dasein as central, and historicity is connected with time? Would the analytic have been projected toward time because it seemed a good idea to melt these together and, as the phrase goes, "think them to the end"? That is "little Moritz's" idea of philosophy, that five authors make a sixth. Heidegger adds that he dealt with Kierkegaard before there was any dialectical literature, and with Dilthey when it was still indecent to name him in a philosophy seminar. Moreover, "thinking to the end" has its own difficulty: to think something to the end, let alone Kierkegaard, Husserl, Bergson, and Dilthey together, one must first have the end toward which one thinks, and there remains the question why just these. The polemic answers the reception of *Being and Time* as a synthesis of contemporary sources. "Little Moritz" is the naive schoolboy of German jokes. Heidegger's point is that a synthesis requires a guiding question to select its sources, and that question, not the sources, determines the turn to time.

The analytic of Dasein as temporality, aimed at disclosing the inner possibility of the understanding of being, is determined by nothing but the matter of this basic problem of metaphysics, more precisely by the basic insight that the understanding of being stands in an original but initially completely obscure and enigmatic connection with time. The insight is stated without proof; the following paragraphs give "indications" of it from the history of ontology.

Only when the analytic of temporality has obtained its direction from the basic metaphysical problem can the previous interpretation of time from Aristotle through Augustine to Bergson be raised and appropriated in its decisive content, and it would be a strange naivety to refuse these aids, even if they are only indirect, like the one found in Aristotle, which determined every later problem of time, not least Bergson's. The sources are admitted, but in the right order: the problem selects the tradition. Aristotle's *Physics* IV is named as the decisive source, as in GA 24.

Why is time connected with the understanding of being? This does not lie open. And yet there are indications of this connection, because nothing accessible is absolutely hidden; otherwise it would not be accessible to finite Dasein. Before following them, Heidegger offers a reminder that sharpens the problem of the understanding of being. The parenthetical principle, that nothing accessible is wholly hidden, is the hermeneutic premise of the historical indications that follow: the connection of being and time must have left traces in the tradition's concepts.

Parmenides recognized and fixed early the correlation between *einai* and *noein*: "for thinking and being are the same" (fragment 3). Misunderstandings must be removed first. In the nineteenth century there were attempts to claim this sentence for various epistemological positions. It was seen as "the first gleam of idealism," as if Parmenides meant that the subject first posits beings as beings, or thought as Kant is understood, that objects conform to knowledge. All this contains a certain core of truth, since in Parmenides it is first said that being is related to the subject. But what is essential is this: the *einai* correlated with *noein* is not yet clearly distinguished from *on*, but that certainly does not mean that *on* is a being only insofar as it is caused and produced by a *noein*; no causal ontic dependence or "positing" is meant. It would be equally hasty to find in Parmenides the so-called criticist prejudice, an epistemological motive in the sense of the Copernican turn, which also rests on a misunderstanding of Kant. The parenthetical remark on Kant is characteristic of the 1928 reading: the Copernican turn is not an epistemological idealism but an ontological thesis about the conditions of the possibility of the objectivity of objects, which is why reading Parmenides as proto-Kantian is doubly wrong. The same point was made in GA 19 about Parmenides and the absence of idealism among the Greeks.

In opposition to such interpretations, one points out that there is no such idealism in all of ancient philosophy. But the interpretation of Parmenides' thought as "realism" is equally untenable, since at issue is not a position on the relation of beings as such to the existing subject but "a first dawning of the genuine metaphysical problem of being as such" (p. 180). The question is not whether the subject posits beings or conforms to them as knower, but how the human being understands anything like being at all. Those who do not think idealistically in epistemology believe themselves superior, especially today, to so-called criticism and imagine themselves the advocates of the medieval and ancient tradition, whereas they are only the reverse side of idealism, think epistemologically too, and are even less able to grasp the problem. The target is the neo-scholastic and realist revival of ontology (Hartmann, Heimsoeth, and Catholic philosophy), which Heidegger reads as epistemological realism in ontological dress.

If one understands anything of the basic problem of ancient philosophy and grasps it at its root, it cannot be a matter of positions and standpoints in the sense of realism or idealism, not only because as epistemological questions both are equally untenable, but because the basic problem (being) is not yet epistemology at all and lies before every problem of knowledge. To see this, one must really have grasped and concretely seen through the basic problems of ancient metaphysics. The priority of the question of being over epistemology is also a priority in the history of philosophy. The Greek question was ontological before the modern question was epistemological, and the latter is a narrowing of the former.

A more accessible consideration in the *Theaetetus* (185a ff.) shows how Plato develops Parmenides' thesis about *noein* and *einai* as the problem of the relation of being to the soul, *psychē*. Socrates tells Theaetetus that being, otherness, sameness, and equality cannot be grasped by hearing or seeing, yet one says "they are," though one neither sees nor hears it. When one says "salty," one knows which faculty to rely on, taste. For being there are no bodily organs; the soul itself, through itself, seems to consider what we say of everything insofar as it is. Heidegger comments: all primary determinations of being are obtained not through bodily organs but by the soul itself, purely from itself, relating to being according to its inner freedom. The soul stretches itself out toward being from itself, that is, the soul itself, purely for itself, understands something like being in the manner of *eporexis*, reaching out. The *Theaetetus* passage is a classical source for the a priori, and Heidegger's gloss contains two anticipations: the soul relates to being "according to its inner freedom," and it "stretches out" (*eporexis*) toward being. Both prefigure transcendence as freedom and as surpassing, and "reaching out" recalls the monad's drive.

For Parmenides' approach and its development in Greek philosophy, Heidegger holds fast: first, *on* is not derived ontically from *noein* or *legein*, which are rather a *dēloun*, a making manifest. Second, it is not an epistemological thesis about reversing the relation of measure in knowing. Both misinterpretations rest metaphysically on the subject–object relation and take the problem too lightly. As the *Theaetetus* passage showed, what is at issue is the problem of being, though only in its beginnings, and it is oriented to the "subject" as *psychē*, where what we call subjectivity is still fluctuating. We must also distinguish what of it is explicitly known under titles like *noein*, *nous*, *logos*, *psychē*, *noēsis*, and *orexis*, and how it nevertheless functions otherwise, in *technē* and *praxis*, which is also recognized, though not in its ontological function. Positively, for the problem of being, it has emerged that there is some special connection between being and subjectivity (Dasein). The *dēloun* is the Greek name for disclosure, and the relation of being and thinking is one of disclosure, not production. The remark about *technē* and *praxis* recalls the course of 1924–25: the Greeks had the relevant phenomena of Dasein's comportment but not their ontological role.

After this reminder, Heidegger follows the indications of the connection of time and understanding of being, a more external one (a) and one pointing more to the center (b). The historical material now serves the thesis of the connection of being and time.

(a) With regard to time, being is divided into regions: the intratemporal (nature and history), the extratemporal, and the supratemporal, the latter two as the atemporal. The classification is the usual one of the period: nature and history in time, mathematical and logical objects outside time, God above time.

One can object that this indication proves as much or more that beings, the extratemporal and supratemporal, are without time, that not everything is "in time." Certainly. But the question is whether this exhausts the relation of beings to time. The question is quite another: not whether beings are in time or not, but whether the being of beings is understood with regard to time. It emerges that the extra- and supratemporal are meant ontically as not in time, but this "a-temporal" is only a particular mode of relation to time, as motionless rest is a mode of motion, only here there is a still more radical relation. It must be clarified why and how this relation is possible, and with what inner necessity even the vulgar understanding of the being of beings goes back to time. Further, the relation to time in question is not exhausted, indeed not reached at all, by time in the sense of intratemporality. This understanding of being itself needs clarification. Even beings not "in time," and precisely these, become intelligible in their being only on the basis of time, but for this time must be grasped more radically. "Being is understood from a relation to time, but the problem of this relation of being and time is the 'and'" (p. 182). A note refers to the Cologne lecture of January 1927, "Kant's Doctrine of the Schematism and the Question of the Meaning of Being." The comparison with rest as a mode of motion is Aristotelian. Even the timeless is determined by negation of time, so it presupposes an understanding of time. The "and" of *Being and Time* is thus named as the problem of the whole work.

(b) The question is how far a connection between being and time has already been seen. After the first rough indication comes a second, which is itself twofold. The subdivision uses Greek letters, preserved here as run-in lead-ins.

**(α)** The terminological title for the being of beings, though often used for beings themselves, is *ousia*, "beingness." It is what makes beings beings, *on hē on*, being. *Ousia* has a double meaning, not accidental, which first appears sharply in Aristotle but can be found everywhere in Plato. *Ousia* is being in the sense of the mode of existence, presence-at-hand; *Theaetetus* 155e 4 ff. speaks of those who think nothing exists but what they can grasp firmly with their hands and do not admit actions, comings-to-be, and everything invisible into the domain of *ousia*, of presence. *Ousia* is also being in the sense of the mode of essence: what-being, content, essence, what makes something what it is, whether it "exists" or not. The Latin *essentia* (since Boethius) therefore does not capture *ousia*, which is richer and also means *existentia*. Aristotle wants to give both meanings by distinguishing first *ousia*, this being as it exists, that-being, and second *ousia*, what-being, essence. Both meanings are oriented to time. What properly "exists," being as existence, shows itself in what is always (*aei on*), what is never not in any now, what is "there" at any time. Essence means the what, the *idea*, what determines every being in advance as a being and is therefore, as what is in the highest degree, all the more always. The relation to time shows itself not only in this character of constant duration, the "always," but more originally, though more hidden, in another. *Ousia* is also an ontic title for what is always available in everyday Dasein: things of use, house and yard, property, possessions, what is always to hand in daily use, what is first and mostly always present. The temporal meaning of *ousia* stands out even more clearly in this prephilosophical meaning. What is present in this sense is not only and not so much always, but present in every now; present, however, as a temporal character in the sense of presence (*Anwesenheit*). *Ousia* is often only an abbreviation for *parousia*, presence. The *para*, as the title for being present with, the constant presence of something in nearest nearness, occurs in all Plato's main ontological problems. Beings are what is always present, in constant presence. Constancy and presence have a temporal character in a sense that is at first problematic. Comparing Thomas's formulas mentioned earlier (present intuition, everything subject to it presentially, God's being as pure act) shows in principle the same concept of being. This is the central historical claim of *Being and Time* §6 and GA 24: *ousia* means constant presence, and presence is a temporal determination. The prephilosophical sense of *ousia* as property, the estate at one's disposal, is Heidegger's favored evidence, since what is at one's disposal is what is present for use. The comparison with Thomas closes the circle with §2 and §4: God's timeless intuition is the highest form of the same understanding of being as presence.

**(β)** In another respect too, the relation of being and time came to light, though it was not made a problem but only taken for granted. Ancient ontology (Aristotle) says of what determines beings as beings, of being (as *idea* and *genos*), that it is "prior" (*proteron*) to beings, and prior in a unique way; as prior by nature (*proteron physei*) it is distinguished from prior in knowledge (*proteron gnōsei*), prior for us. Being is prior to beings; this "prior to," ascribed to being, is a distinguishing "determination" that does not concern knowledge as the order of grasping beings. Being is prior, the essentially "earlier," it is from earlier, in the language of later ontology: a priori. "All ontological questioning is a questioning about and determining of the 'a priori'" (p. 184). The *a priori*, literally "from what is earlier," carries a temporal meaning in its very name, and Heidegger's argument turns on taking this seriously.

"Prior to" is evidently a determination of time: no prior without time. Earlier than every possible "prior to," however, is time. Therefore if being is prior, a priori, it stands in an original connection with time. What "prior," that is, what time, means here remains obscure, and completely enigmatic if one tries to manage with the vulgar concept of time. One sees at once that this does not work, and the Greeks already rejected it with the distinction mentioned. The vulgar concept of time would make the a priori an earlier moment in a sequence, which is absurd for being. The Greek distinction between prior by nature and prior for us shows that they already knew the "prior" was not chronological.

Being is not prior for us, in the sense that it is known by us earlier than beings. We first always grasp beings and mostly remain there without grasping being as such. In the order of being grasped, being is not the earlier but the latest. And yet it is prior by nature (which is why there are beings as objects); it is prior of itself. What this means is obscure and ambiguous; all this is ontologically or metontologically unclarified and is therefore understood ontically. But being is not a being, although it is ranked as what is in the highest degree (*ontōs on*). The "prior" does not mean being's being present earlier than other beings as a kind of being. The prior therefore belongs neither to the order of being grasped nor to the order of presence; it is neither logically nor ontically prior, neither. "And yet!" The "and yet" marks the dead end that the next paragraph resolves. If being is neither logically nor ontically prior, the priority must be of a third kind.

It has often been said that in all grasping of beings, being is understood in advance; the prior understanding of being gives light, as it were, to all grasping of beings. "In advance," "prior": is that not the "earlier"? Certainly. But it was said that the earlier does not concern the order of grasping, and now there is talk of a prior understanding of being, an understanding in advance; the *proteron* of *ousia*, of the *idea*, is not a *proteron gnōsei*. But note that *gnōsis* here always means knowledge of beings, and rejecting this *proteron* means, rightly understood, only negatively: being is not a being, and its being grasped does not belong to the order of grasping beings. In the end, being is indeed earlier in regard to being grasped in a broad sense, before all grasping of beings. And being gives itself in a way totally different from the grasping of beings. Being gives itself "in itself" in an original sense; it is prior by nature and for us, only rightly understood, not as something ontic among others. "Being is the only 'in-itself,' and the genuine one; hence the originality of the understanding of being, that is (as will be shown), freedom" (p. 186). Heidegger resolves the dilemma by distinguishing understanding from knowing. Being is prior for understanding, which precedes all knowing of beings, though it is last for knowing. The claim that being is "the only in-itself" inverts the realist's in-itself, which belongs to beings. The identification of the originality of understanding with freedom anticipates §11c.

Being is earlier, neither ontically nor logically, but earlier in an original sense that lies before both, before each in a different way; it is not ontically or logically but ontologically earlier. But this is the problem: how being is "earlier," how it relates originally as being to time. "Being and time, that is the basic problem!" As long as it is not posed or relatively solved, the use of the title "a priori" remains without right or justification, as does talk of the a posteriori and the distinction as such. The whole Kantian and Neo-Kantian vocabulary of the a priori is thus placed under a question it cannot answer: what kind of "earlier" the a priori is.

Being is the earlier in an obscure sense. It lights up in a way when one points to something Plato saw above all in his doctrine of recollection (*anamnēsis*). Being is what we recollect, what we let be given to us as something we understand as such, what has already and always been given to us; what is never strange but always familiar, "ours." Being is thus what we always already understand and need only recollect in order to take it as such. In grasping being we grasp nothing new but something fundamentally familiar, that in whose understanding we always already exist when we relate to what we now call beings. This recollection concerns being and thus reveals an original temporal relatedness of being: always already there and yet only ever grasped in coming back to it again. This is not the vulgar recollection of ontic events, of beings, but the metaphysical recollection in which that original temporal relatedness of being announces itself. In this metaphysical recollection the human being understands himself in his genuine essence, as the being that understands being and on the basis of this understanding relates to beings. Recollection has the temporal structure of the a priori: what is always already there is grasped only by returning to it. Heidegger reads Plato's myth as a temporal figure of the understanding of being, anticipating the having-been and repetition of *Being and Time*.

According to Plato (*Phaedrus* 249b 5–c 6), a living being that has never seen the truth can never take on the form of a human being. For the human being, in accord with its way of being, must understand and know in such a way that it addresses what it knows with regard to its being (*kat' eidos legein*). The human being can have truth about something only by understanding beings in their being. Understanding being is recollection of what our soul once saw, when it traveled with the god and looked beyond what we now call beings. Plato sees in the phenomenon of recollection a relation of the understanding of being to time, but can make it clear to himself only through a myth. The myth of the prenatal vision says in narrative form what the analytic must say conceptually: understanding of being belongs to what the human being is, and it has the temporal character of the "already."

So much for the indications of a connection between the problem of being and time. The survey closes the historical part of §10.

The problem of being is the basic problem of philosophy, and most closely connected with it is the guiding problem of transcendence, to which the question of the essence of ground and the essential connection of ground and truth led, according to the theme: logic as the metaphysics of truth, guided by the problem of ground. If the problem of being is central, as such and for this question in particular, if the relation of being and time exists, and if this relation has remained hidden and been taken as self-evident, then this central problem of being and its relation to time must really be posed. With regard to its fundamental significance for philosophy as such, it must be posed and worked out radically and universally. The two demands structure the rest of §10. Radicalization concerns the subject of the understanding of being; universalization concerns the scope of what "being" covers.

First, the radicalization of the problem of being. A relation has appeared between *einai* and *psychē*, between *einai* and *chronos*, and in recollection between *psychē* and *chronos*. The relation between being and soul must be grasped more originally, likewise between being and time, which means clarifying the relation between soul and time. The three relations form a triangle, and the third side, soul and time, is the one Heidegger's analytic of temporality addresses.

Being and soul: this means showing how the understanding of being lies in what soul means, in the being whose being is primarily characterized by soul, which requires an original and appropriate interpretation of Dasein. Because it is undertaken with regard to the question of being as such, this interpretation is also metaphysical, ontological; the task is to bring to light the specific peculiarity of the being of Dasein so as to make visible how precisely this being, in its peculiarity, includes something like an understanding of being. In attempting an ontologically original and appropriate interpretation of Dasein, it turns out that the tradition did not interpret this being metaphysically in an original and appropriate way, and still less in connection with the basic problem, not from negligence or incapacity but for reasons lying in the essence of the genesis of the understanding of being itself. The last clause is important. The tradition's failure to interpret Dasein is itself grounded in how the understanding of being arises. Because being is first understood from what is present at hand, Dasein too is interpreted as something present, a substance or a subject, as §5 showed in Leibniz.

An ontologically original interpretation of Dasein means at the same time an original interpretation of time, which is also interpreted like something present at hand, namely, according to the traditional concept of time, from the now. The traditional concept of time is itself an instance of the understanding of being as presence: time is a sequence of nows, present moments.

An original interpretation of time must be undertaken as much as one of Dasein, an original clarification of the connection of both. That time is in the soul (*en tē psychē*, *in anima*) is an old insight (Aristotle, Augustine). But time is regarded as something present that is somehow present in the soul. This remains completely unclarified in Kant (as the problem of the connection of time and the "I think") and his successors. Recently Bergson tried to grasp the concept of time more originally, and he made the interweaving of time and consciousness clearer than anyone before him. But the essential remains undecided with him, and does not even become a problem. He develops his interpretation of time on the basis of the traditional concept of consciousness, Descartes's thinking thing. The basic metaphysical problem of the original connection between Dasein and temporality is not posed, still less the problem of being as such, for which the former is to be the preparation. The problem of Kant is precise: in the first *Critique* time is the form of inner sense and the "I think" is timeless, and their relation remains unresolved. Heidegger's Kant book will argue that the transcendental imagination is original time and the root of both. Bergson is credited and criticized in one sentence: he connects time with consciousness but leaves consciousness as the Cartesian thinking thing.

If being has an original relation to time, and if the understanding of being belongs originally to the essence of Dasein, to its inner possibility, then time must co-determine this inner possibility of Dasein; it must be shown, with regard to and guided by the problem of being, that temporality is the basic constitution of Dasein. This transforms the concept of time itself. An original basic stance toward the history of metaphysics as such arises from this. Being is the a priori; if the a priori is a basic character of being, and a determination of time, and time is connected with being such that the understanding of being is rooted in the temporality of Dasein, then there is an inner connection between the a priori and temporality, that is, the constitution of the being of Dasein, the subjectivity of the subject. Then it is not an arbitrary idealist prejudice, as is readily proclaimed today, that the problem of the a priori in Plato and Aristotle, as in Descartes, Leibniz, Kant, and German Idealism, is most closely bound up with the problem of the subject, however obscure the connection may be. The argument vindicates the subjective orientation of the tradition against its realist critics, but on a new basis. The connection of the a priori and the subject is not idealism but the consequence of the a priori's temporal character and the temporality of Dasein.

Second, the universalization of the problem of being. Here too the problem has become strange today and is made even stranger by a movement that seems to renew ontology and metaphysics. It is a widespread misunderstanding of ontology, coming from Kantianism, to think that posing an ontological problem means deciding epistemologically for realism, since realism lets beings in themselves count. The "movement" is the ontological revival in phenomenology and Hartmann.

Interest in ontology today was awakened above all by phenomenology, but Husserl and Scheler too, and the others even more, did not see the scope of ontology. Here too, as everywhere today (in Rickert, for example), ontology is understood as a study that makes the being-in-itself of things in their so-called independence from the subject a problem. The question of subjectivity is supposed not to belong to ontology but to epistemology. Ontology is supposed to mean emphasis on the object, after the subject alone had counted. In this sense ontology is connected with the epistemological position of realism as opposed to idealism. Ontology should abstract from the subject as much as possible, whereas the basic necessity is to make subjectivity a problem. In today's understanding, ontology is first not a science of being but of beings, and second of objects, of nature in the broadest sense. The critique names the two errors that the course's concept of ontology corrects: ontology is about being, not beings, and it must include the being of the subject.

In the renewal of ontology one sees a return to medieval realist scholasticism and to Aristotle, who appears as a disguised church father. In this sense one also looks for the ontological motive in Kant. In Scheler, N. Hartmann, and Heimsoeth, the idea arises that in Kant, beside his epistemological-idealist considerations, there are some of a so-called ontological-realist character, tendencies to let the objective world still stand. This idea of ontology is absurd and neither Aristotelian nor Kantian. Heimsoeth's work on the metaphysical motives of Kant (1924) is the target. Heidegger rejects the attempt to find a realist Kant alongside the idealist one, because it keeps the epistemological frame.

Precisely what the supposedly ontological interpretation of Kant leaves as epistemology is the genuine ontology; metaphysics is not to be set up against epistemology. What must be shown is that the Analytic of the *Critique of Pure Reason* is the first attempt since Plato and Aristotle to make ontology really a philosophical problem. Such an undertaking is thought impossible because Kant is a criticist, of the Kantian opinion that knowledge does not conform to the object but the object to knowledge. This is the thesis of *Kant and the Problem of Metaphysics* (1929): the Transcendental Analytic is a laying of the foundation of metaphysics, an ontology of the being of objects, not a theory of knowledge.

The ontological problem has primarily nothing whatever to do with the notorious pseudo-problem of the reality of the external world and the independence of what is in itself from the grasping subject. Rather, the ontological problem consists precisely in seeing that this so-called epistemological question cannot be posed at all unless it has been clarified what being-in-itself of what is present at hand means. That cannot even be posed as a problem, let alone solved, unless it is clear how the question of the meaning of being as such must be posed. The priority is strict. The external-world problem presupposes a concept of being-in-itself, which presupposes the question of being.

This rejection of a widespread error, which makes all so-called interest in metaphysics fundamentally barren, remains negative. To develop the universalization of the problem of being positively means showing which interconnected basic questions the question of being includes. What basic problems are meant by the simple title "being" when one asks about being and time? The next paragraphs enumerate the four basic problems of GA 24.

The basic ontological problem is not only not identical with the question of the "reality" of the external world; that problem presupposes a genuine ontological one: clarifying the way things are there (their that-being) and their regional constitution. But the being of material things of nature is not the only one; history is, works of art are. Nature is in different ways: space and number, life, human Dasein itself. There is a manifold of modes of existence, and each is at the same time a mode of beings of a definite content, a definite what-being. The title "being" is meant in a breadth that covers all possible regions. The problem of the regional manifold of being includes, precisely when posed universally, the question of the unity of this general title "being," of how the general meaning "being" is modified into the various regional meanings. This is the problem of the unity of the idea of being and its regional modifications. Does the unity of being mean universality in another form and with another motive? The problem is in any case the unity and universality of the idea of being as such. Aristotle posed it, though he did not solve it. What is essential everywhere is how the universality of the concept of being is grasped. The allusion is to the Aristotelian problem of the analogy of being, the unity of the many senses of "is" in relation to *ousia*, which Brentano's dissertation had put at the start of Heidegger's own path.

The regional manifold is only one respect in which the problem of being must be universalized from the outset. In clarifying *ousia*, it emerged that being means both that-being and what-being. This articulation of being has been accepted since antiquity and worked with as self-evident, without ever asking where the inner possibility of this articulation of the idea of being lies, where its origin is. Why is every something that is, whatever its content, determined by a what-being and a possible that-being? Even the formal something, of which we say it has no definite content, is distinguished precisely by lacking a definite content (the problem of the formal is not taken up here). Being means not only the manifold of regions and their modes of existence and essence, but this idea of being with regard to its essential articulation into existence and essence. This articulation is a basic problem of ontology: the problem of the basic articulation of being. This second problem is the topic of the unexecuted §6, and its origin will be sought in temporality.

The two problems mentioned concern being itself, without noting that being, as it is articulated into essence and existence and regionalized, is always the being of beings. Being as such and in every meaning is being of beings. Being is distinguished from beings, and only this difference, this possibility of difference, grants an understanding of being. In other words, the understanding of being involves carrying out this distinguishing of being and beings. This difference first makes something like ontology possible. The difference that first makes understanding of being possible is therefore called the ontological difference. The ontological difference is presented here, as in GA 24, as enacted in the understanding of being itself. It is not a distinction drawn by the philosopher afterward but what understanding being consists in.

The indifferent term "difference" is used deliberately, since it is precisely the problem in what way what is distinguished, being and beings, is different or even separated. With the problem of the ontological difference, the original problem of being and the center of the question of being press forward. It hardly needs saying that the ontological difference must again be taken in the full breadth of the problems of basic articulation and regionalization. The caution against "separated" guards against reading being as a being beside beings, the error the preceding discussion of the *proteron* identified.

Most closely intertwined with the problem of the ontological difference and the others is the one toward which the course has been striving all along, as it were from the opposite direction: the inner connection of being and truth, the truth character of being. The question of the original connection of truth and being, the problem of the veritative character of being, belongs to logic as metaphysics. "Veritative" (*veritativ*) names the connection of being and truth. The course arrives at it "from the opposite direction," from the logic of judgment to being, whereas GA 24 approached it from being.

The general title "being" includes four basic problems: the ontological difference, the basic articulation of being, the veritative character of being, and the regionality of being and the unity of the idea of being. The four correspond to the four chapters of the planned second part of GA 24.

The problem of being has thus been made clear as central, universal, and radical. There is an insight into what it means to ask about the inner possibility of anything like an understanding of being, which is the essential distinction of human existence. In characterizing vulgarly understood, ontic transcendence, as it lies in intentionality, it was seen that comportment toward beings presupposes understanding of being, that ontic transcendence is itself grounded in original transcendence, primal transcendence, which therefore relates to the understanding of being. The problem has now been shown, and that means, working backward: the problem of transcendence, and so the problem of truth, and so the problem of ground, can be posed only in the dimension prefigured by the problem of being as such. The problem of transcendence must be posed as universally and radically as the problem of being. It is not a problem restricted to the relation of the subject to the things independent of it, nor a question about a particular region of beings. Nor may one stop or begin with a subject–object relation fallen from the sky; as for the problem of being as such, for transcendence the subjectivity of the subject itself is the central question. The chain ground, truth, transcendence, being is now complete, and the direction is reversed: from being back to transcendence. The phrase "fallen from the sky" repeats the complaint of §9b against the unexamined starting point of epistemology.

Three theses follow. First, beings are in themselves the beings they are and as they are, even if, for example, Dasein does not exist. Second, being "is" not; there is being only insofar as Dasein exists. Transcendence lies in the essence of existence, that is, the giving of world before all being toward and with intraworldly beings. Third, only insofar as existing Dasein gives itself something like being can beings manifest themselves in their in-itself, that is, can the first thesis be understood and known at all. The three theses are Heidegger's answer to the realism–idealism dispute. The first grants realism its point about beings; the second places being, not beings, in dependence on Dasein; the third shows that the realist's thesis is itself intelligible only within Dasein's understanding of being. The German idiom *es gibt* ("there is," literally "it gives") becomes a thesis: being is not a being but is "given" in transcendence, which "gives world."

In a note, Heidegger adds that because being is not and therefore is never something co-existing with beings, the question what being is in the being-in-itself makes no sense and has no right. One could still ask what, in beings, corresponds to being, which is not but "only" is given. Being gives itself originally and in itself when it makes its beings accessible, and with regard to these beings one cannot further ask in itself about their being in itself. We only ever know beings, never being that is. This becomes clear only from transcendence and the ontological difference. The note forbids reifying being. Being is not a property of beings to be discovered in them; it is the accessibility of beings, which is why it can be understood but not known as an object.

With this, what the First Section was to achieve has been reached: the laying free of the problem dimension for the problem of ground, which is none other than the domain of a central direction of questioning of metaphysics as such. Before the Second Section develops the problem of ground itself within this dimension, the problem dimension and the way it is worked out are characterized further, in an appendix. This dimension and its discussion are called fundamental ontology. The appendix that follows is the course's statement on the idea of fundamental ontology and its "turn" into metontology.

###### Appendix: Characterization of the Idea and Function of a Fundamental Ontology

Fundamental ontology means the laying of the foundation of ontology as such. It includes, first, the grounding that exhibits the inner possibility of the question of being as the basic problem of metaphysics, the interpretation of Dasein as temporality; second, the unfolding of the basic problems included in the question of being, the temporal exposition of the problem of being; and third, the development of this problematic's self-understanding, its task and limit, the "turn" (*Umschlag*). The three parts match the plan of *Being and Time*: the first is Part One, Divisions One and Two; the second is the unpublished Division Three, "Time and Being," worked out in part in GA 24; the third is new. It names a reversal that fundamental ontology must undergo when it understands itself.

For what follows, a quite general characterization must suffice. The aim is that fundamental ontology not be conceived too narrowly or one-sidedly. The guiding questions are: why is fundamental ontology, in its starting point, an existential analytic? What does "existence" mean here? And how far does the existential analytic receive its sense only from the full concept of metaphysics, as "metaphysical history" and "*humanitas*"? The three questions indicate that the analytic of Dasein is not self-sufficient. Its meaning depends on the whole of metaphysics, including its bearing on history and on the human being.

With fundamental ontology one grasps, only in a certain respect, the inner and hidden life of the basic movement of Western philosophy. Its basic features have repeatedly shown themselves from the beginning. They must be brought to light as tangibly as possible and not left in indifference, not because these problems were touched on earlier and always, not because of their great age, which gives them a certain dignity, but, on the contrary, because fundamental ontology grasps problems that belong in their very problematic to the existence of the human being, to the metaphysical essence of Dasein as it becomes visible to us; and only for this reason did these problems come to light at the beginning of Western philosophy in their definite, concrete form. The priority is explicitly reversed. The problems are not important because they are old; they are old because they belong to Dasein, and so they appeared as soon as Dasein began to philosophize.

"Fundamental ontology is always only a retrieval (*Wiederholung*) of this old, early beginning" (p. 197). But the early hands itself down to us in retrieval only if we give it the possibility of transforming itself, since these problems demand this by their essence. As will be shown, all this has its ground in the historicity of the understanding of being. It is characteristic that tradition, the externalized handing on, denies the problem precisely the possibility of transforming itself in retrieval. Tradition hands on fixed propositions and opinions, fixed ways of questioning and discussing. This external tradition of opinions and free-floating standpoints is now called "the history of problems." Because this external tradition and its treatment in the history of philosophy deny the problems life, which is to say transformation, and try to smother them, the struggle is against it. The distinction between retrieval and tradition is that of *Being and Time* §§6 and 74. The "history of problems" (*Problemgeschichte*) is Windelband's and Hartmann's method, which treats problems as timeless items with a history of solutions. Heidegger's objection is that problems live only by being transformed in each retrieval, and a history of fixed problems kills them.

It is not antiquity that is to be overcome, if "criticism" is in place in this regard at all (which it is not primarily, though it is demanded with every situation), but its poor advocates are to be fought. That happens only through the effort to give these basic problems, the *metaphysica naturalis* lying in Dasein itself, an opportunity for transformation. "That is what I understand by destruction of the tradition. It is not a matter of clearing away these two millennia and putting oneself in their place" (p. 197). The definition of destruction confirms the guard set at the head of this commentary. Destruction is not demolition but the liberation of the tradition's problems from the fixed forms that block their transformation. Kant's phrase *metaphysica naturalis*, metaphysics as a natural disposition, locates the problems in Dasein.

However resolutely one must find one's way back into the simple force of the central problems grasped in their universality and radicality, it would be fatally wrong to absolutize these problems and so nullify their essential function. Human beings tend, not only today and not by accident, either to misjudge what is central in philosophy in favor of what is interesting or near at hand, or, once the central has been grasped, to absolutize it just as blindly, to fix a possible stage of the problematic of origin and make it an eternal task, instead of temporalizing and preparing the possibility of new origins. This requires not foreseeing them but, owing to the finitude of Dasein, only ever working on factical possibilities. "Because philosophizing is essentially a matter of finitude, every concretion of factical philosophy must also fall victim to this factical element" (p. 198). Heidegger applies to his own work the finitude he ascribed to Leibniz's in §5a ("every philosophy fails at its innermost core"). Fundamental ontology is not to be absolutized; it is a stage that must itself be transformed.

The peculiar shortness of breath of questioning and thinking cannot be removed, but effort is needed not to fall victim to it unawares. We are seldom able to run through the inner course of a problematic as a whole and keep it alive and capable of transformation, or, if we can, we lack the strength to take a completely new breath for other equally essential possibilities. Or if that is possible, the working out is harder, because detaching oneself from what came before is at bottom an inner impossibility. What remains is the breaking open of horizons each time; the essential is always left to the future, as the genuine inheritance. What is refutable and what the spirit of the age discusses is not the essential. Heidegger adds that if Kant had been only what his contemporaries, who refuted him after a fashion, took him to be, things would be bad for him indeed. The image of breath captures the limits of finite thinking. The "genuine inheritance" is what a philosophy leaves open for the future, not the doctrines that can be refuted, which is the principle Heidegger applied to Kant and Leibniz throughout the course.

"The finitude of philosophy does not consist in its coming up against limits and being unable to go further, but in its harboring, in the simplicity of its central problematic, a wealth that demands ever new awakening" (p. 198). Finitude is redefined positively. It is not a boundary but inexhaustibility, the same structure Heidegger ascribed in §5a to historical objectivity, which lies in the inexhaustibility of possibilities.

Regarding fundamental ontology, it must be noted above all that precisely the radicality and universality of this central problematic, and it alone, leads to the insight that these problems are central, but for that very reason never the only ones in their essentiality. In other words, fundamental ontology does not exhaust the concept of metaphysics. The thesis is the turning point of the appendix. The more radical fundamental ontology becomes, the more clearly it sees that it is not the whole.

Since there is being only when beings are already in the "there," fundamental ontology latently tends toward an original metaphysical transformation, which becomes possible only when being is understood in its full problematic. The inner necessity that ontology turns back to where it began can be made clear from the primal phenomenon of human existence: the being "human being" understands being; in the understanding of being lies the carrying out of the distinction between being and beings; there is being only when Dasein understands being. The possibility that there is being in understanding presupposes the factical existence of Dasein, and this in turn the factical presence of nature. Precisely in the horizon of the radically posed problem of being it shows itself that all this is visible and can be understood as being only if a possible totality of beings is already there. The argument turns the three theses of §10 around. Being "is given" only in Dasein's understanding, but Dasein exists only factically, among beings, and ultimately within nature. So the analysis of being leads back to beings as a whole as its own presupposition.

From this arises the necessity of a peculiar problematic that has beings as a whole as its theme. This new way of questioning lies in the essence of ontology itself and results from its overturning, its *metabolē*. Heidegger calls this problematic "metontology." In the domain of metontological-existentiell questioning lies also the domain of the metaphysics of existence; only here can the question of ethics be posed. "Metontology" is formed on the model of *metabolē* and of "metaphysics": ontology "turned over" onto beings. The placement of ethics in metontology answers the charge that *Being and Time* has no ethics. Ethics belongs not to the neutral analytic but to the question of beings as a whole and Dasein's factical existence among them.

The positive sciences also have beings as their theme, but metontology is not a summary ontics in the sense of a general science that compiles the results of the individual sciences empirically into a so-called world picture, from which a view of world and life is then derived. Something like this is in a way alive in prescientific Dasein, though that has another structure; the possibility and structure of the natural world-view is a problem of its own. That people keep attempting a summation of ontic knowledge and pass it off as "inductive metaphysics" points to a problem that necessarily presses forward again and again in history. The distinction guards metontology against confusion with the "inductive metaphysics" of Külpe, Driesch, and Becher, which synthesized scientific results into a worldview. Metontology is not a summary of sciences but a questioning of beings as a whole in the light of the understanding of being.

Metontology is possible only on the ground and in the perspective of the radical ontological problematic and in unity with it; precisely the radicalization of fundamental ontology drives the overturning of ontology out of itself. What is apparently separated here into "disciplines" and given titles is one, just as the ontological difference is one or the primal phenomenon of human existence. To think being as the being of beings and to grasp the problem of being radically and universally means at the same time to make beings in their totality a theme in the light of ontology. The unity of fundamental ontology and metontology mirrors the unity of the ontological difference. Being is always the being of beings, so the question of being is always also a question about beings as a whole.

It would be external pedantry to think that, once fundamental ontology had been found as a discipline, another with a new title would be set beside it as a supplement. Moreover, fundamental ontology is no fixed discipline that, once the child has a name, would occupy once and for all an empty place in a supposed system of philosophy and would only need to be supplemented and extended in order (as the layman or positivism imagines) to bring philosophy to a happy end in a few decades. Besides, that "place" is occupied in every philosophy, each time transformed. The irony targets the reception of *Being and Time* as a new discipline with a research program. Every philosophy has a fundamental ontology in the sense of an implicit understanding of being, transformed each time.

One must not confuse the pedantry of a schema with the rigor of questioning, and must be clear that in the analytic one only takes possession of what already underlies every analytic approach as an original unity and wholeness, a synthesis that we have not explicitly performed beforehand but that has, as it were, always already been performed on and with us insofar as we exist. The analytic articulates a whole it does not produce. The "synthesis" is Kant's word, used for the unity of Dasein's being that precedes every analysis, which the course will identify with temporality.

We need not only the analytic as such; we must always, as it were, give birth to the illusion that the task at hand is the only and necessary one. Only one who understands this "art of existing," treating what one has grasped as the only thing in one's action while remaining clear about the finitude of this doing, understands finite existence and can hope to accomplish something in it. This art of existing is not self-reflection, an uninvolved hunt to track down motives and complexes from which to gain reassurance and a dispensation from action, but is solely the clarity of action itself, the hunt for genuine possibilities. The paragraph unites finitude and commitment. A finite being can act only by treating its task as absolute while knowing it is not, and this knowing is not psychological introspection, which Heidegger again rejects, but the lucidity of action.

It has emerged that the basic problem of metaphysics, in its radicalization and universalization, requires an interpretation of Dasein in terms of temporality, from which the inner possibility of the understanding of being and thus of ontology is to become clear, not so that this inner possibility is merely known, but because it is understood only in accomplishment, in working out the basic problematic (presented in the four main problems) itself. This whole of the laying of the foundation and working out of ontology is fundamental ontology; it is, first, the analytic of Dasein and, second, the analytic of the temporality (*Temporalität*) of being. This temporal analytic, however, is at the same time "the turn (*Kehre*), in which ontology itself runs back explicitly into the metaphysical ontics in which it always implicitly stands" (p. 201). Through the movement of radicalization and universalization, ontology is to be brought to the overturning latent in it. There the turning takes place, and it comes to the overturning into metontology. *Temporalität* (the temporality of being) is distinguished from *Zeitlichkeit* (the temporality of Dasein), as in GA 24. The word *Kehre* appears here in 1928, in a precise sense: the turn from the analysis of being back to beings as a whole, internal to fundamental ontology. This is not yet the later "turning" of Heidegger's thought, but it shows that a turn was planned within the project of *Being and Time* itself.

Fundamental ontology and metontology in their unity form the concept of metaphysics. This only expresses the transformation of the one basic problem of philosophy itself, touched on above and in the Introduction with the double concept of philosophy as *prōtē philosophia* and *theologia*. And this is only the respective concretion of the ontological difference, the concretion of the enactment of the understanding of being. "Philosophy is the central and total concretion of the metaphysical essence of existence" (p. 202). The appendix returns to the Preliminary Remark of the main part, which defined metaphysics as ontology and theology, being as such and beings as a whole. Fundamental ontology and metontology are the retrieval of that ancient double concept, grounded now in the ontological difference as Dasein enacts it.

This necessarily general indication of the idea and function of fundamental ontology was needed both to see the breadth of the horizon of the problem and not to lose sight of the narrow path along which the concrete treatment of the following problem must move. The Second Section returns from this broad horizon to the specific problem of ground, approached through transcendence.

#### Second Section: The Problem of Ground

##### §11. The Transcendence of Dasein

###### a) On the Concept of Transcendence

The question is about the essence of ground, the inner possibility of anything like ground at all. Its exposition showed that it can be posed and answered only in the dimension of the problem of being as such. The stations of the return into this dimension are indicated by the titles: ground, principle of reason, law of thought, judgment, assertion, truth, intentional comportment, ontic transcendence, ontological transcendence, ontology, problem of being as such, metaphysics. The list is a map of the First Section, from §8's rule of thought to §10's problem of being. Each station is the ground of the one before it, so the order itself exhibits the thesis that the problem of ground leads back to being.

The concrete consideration of the problem of ground starts from the problem of being as such, but this is prepared by the analytic of Dasein, which discloses transcendence as a basic phenomenon. To avoid going the whole way again from the beginning, Heidegger starts with the problem of transcendence, treating it not only and primarily with a view to the analytic of Dasein but with reference to the problem of ground. It will turn out that in sufficiently interpreting transcendence one comes, as it were of itself, upon the phenomenon and essence of ground. The claim sets the expectation for §11: ground is not a separate topic to be added to transcendence but will emerge from its analysis, as freedom in §11c.

The given problem perspective must be held fast but also screened off, so as to concentrate a discriminating look on the phenomenon of transcendence. What is the general meaning of the word, what does the verbal concept mean, and what the philosophical term? The procedure moves from the ordinary word to the technical term, as §8 moved from the vulgar formula of the principle to its philosophical sense.

The word comes from *transcendere*: to climb over, step over, pass over to. Transcendence therefore means the passing over, the surpassing; the transcendent designates that to which the passage is made, what demands a passage in order to be accessible and graspable, the beyond, the opposite; the transcending is what carries out the passage. The verbal meaning includes, first, an action in the broadest sense, a doing; second, formally, a relation, the passage to something from something; and third, something that is passed over, a limit, barrier, gap, something "lying between." This is only a general clarification of what lies in the common sphere of representation when we understand the word. The third moment carries the error Heidegger will contest. If transcendence crosses a barrier, one must ask what the barrier is, and the epistemological answer, a wall between inner and outer, is wrong.

The terminological use in philosophy can be characterized by two main meanings to which all variants go back. Holding to the concept of the transcendent and characterizing its philosophical meaning by its decisive counterconcepts, the term means the transcendent in distinction from the immanent, and the transcendent in distinction from the contingent. The two counterconcepts organize the next paragraphs: immanence yields the epistemological concept, contingency the theological one.

The transcendent in contrast to the immanent: the immanent is what remains within, what remains in the subject, within the soul, consciousness. The transcendent is then what does not remain inside but is outside, what lies outside the soul and consciousness. What lies outside the limits and the enclosing wall of consciousness has, spoken from the innermost court of consciousness, climbed over the wall and stands outside. Insofar as this consciousness knows, it relates to the outside, and so the transcendent as what is present outside is at the same time what lies opposite. The architectural image, a courtyard within walls, dramatizes the spatial metaphor inherent in "immanence."

The subject is represented here as a capsule, with an inside, a capsule wall, and an outside. No one holds the crude opinion that consciousness really is a capsule, but what is essential in the analogy and belongs to this concept of the transcendent is that a barrier between inside and outside must be crossed. The inside is first and properly restricted by the barrier and must first break through it, must "de-restrict the restriction." The "capsule" image is Heidegger's shorthand for every model of the subject that treats relation to the world as something to be achieved. The wordplay on *Einschränkung* and *Entschränkung*, restricting and de-restricting, returns at the end of the subsection, where even talk of intentionality as "always already de-restricted" is shown to presuppose a prior restriction.

Transcendence itself is then regarded as the relation that somehow maintains traffic between the inside of the capsule and the outside by leaping over or penetrating the capsule wall. The problem then arises of explaining the possibility of this traffic. One tries to explain it causally, psychologically or physiologically; or by somehow calling on intentionality; or one considers the undertaking hopeless, stays in the capsule, and tries to explain from inside how to understand what supposedly comes in from a being outside in the representations. The three options are causal realism, the phenomenological appeal to intentionality taken as a bridge, and immanentism. Heidegger's inclusion of intentionality among the capsule theories is pointed: intentionality understood as a property of consciousness reaching out still presupposes the capsule.

In each case a different standpoint of immanence is taken, and the concept of consciousness varies accordingly. But however and wherever the problem of transcendence is posed, in explicit or implicit orientation on the counterconcept of immanence, the capsule representation of the subject, of Dasein, underlies it in principle. Without it the problem of crossing a barrier would be senseless. It is already clear that the problem of transcendence depends on how the subjectivity of the subject, the basic constitution of Dasein, is determined. Does the capsule representation have any right from the start? If not, why is it so persistent? The question of persistence is taken seriously. The capsule model is not an arbitrary mistake but has a ground, which Heidegger locates, as in §7, in the tendency of Dasein to understand itself from what is present at hand.

Transcendence is not a determination that one ascribes to a subject in addition; the question will be whether the essence of subjectivity becomes graspable first of all precisely through transcendence rightly understood. The reversal is the thesis of the subsection. Transcendence is not a relation a finished subject enters into but what makes the subject a subject.

This concept of transcendence, with immanence as its counterconcept, first makes possible what is called epistemology, so Heidegger calls it the epistemological concept of transcendence. If it falls as unjustified, resting on a hasty approach, epistemology as such falls with it. This does not mean that knowledge is not a problem of philosophy. From the epistemological concept Heidegger distinguishes the theological concept in the broadest sense. The qualification matters. Heidegger does not deny that there are problems about knowing; he denies that they can be posed as crossings of a boundary around consciousness.

Transcendence in contrast to contingency: the contingent is what touches us, directly concerns us, with which we ourselves are on a level, what is of our kind and lineage or beneath it. The transcendent, by contrast, is what lies beyond all this as what conditions it: the unconditioned, but at the same time what is properly unattainable, the exuberant (*das Überschwängliche*). Transcendence is surpassing in the sense of lying beyond conditioned beings. Heidegger glosses *contingens* by its root *con-tingere*, to touch together: the contingent is what touches us, what is on our level. "Exuberant" renders Kant's *überschwänglich*, the term for what exceeds all possible experience.

Transcendence is again a relational concept here, not between subject and object but between conditioned beings as such, including subjects and all possible objects, and the unconditioned. Conversely, the concept of transcendence here is essentially determined by the conception of that toward which transcendence transcends, what lies beyond the contingent. Lying beyond also expresses a difference of degree in being, better, the infinite difference between created and creator, if one inserts, as is not necessary, the Christian God for the transcendent. Insofar as the transcendent in this second concept always somehow means the unconditioned, the absolute, and this is predominantly called the divine, one can speak of a theological concept of transcendence. The two concepts differ in their point of orientation. The epistemological concept is defined from the subject's side, the theological from the goal's side. Neither asks about transcending as a way of being.

The two concepts can be coupled, as has always happened and keeps recurring. If the epistemological concept is presupposed, explicitly or not, a being outside the subject and opposite it is posited. Among these opposite beings is one that towers above all, their cause, which is opposite and at the same time surpasses everything conditioned opposite. The transcendent in this double sense is the eminent, the being that exceeds all experience. The question of the possible grasping of the transcendent in the epistemological sense becomes entwined with the question of the possibility of knowing the transcendent object in the theological sense. Indeed, the latter question is in a way the motive of the former. The problem of the existence of the external world and its knowability is therefore entwined with the problem of the knowledge of God and the possibility of proving his existence. The historical pattern is Descartes's *Meditations*, where the proof of God secures the external world. Heidegger suggests that theological interest motivated the epistemological problem, the same diagnosis §2 gave for Leibniz's ideal of knowledge.

All theological metaphysics, and all systematic theology, moves within this entanglement of the two problems of transcendence. If the difference between the rational and the irrational is then distributed between the contingent and the transcendent, the confusion can no longer be surpassed. This tangle of half-posed and falsely posed problems rolls on in ontological philosophy and systematic theology; it is passed from hand to hand, which only confuses the state of the entanglements and gives them new names. The allusion to the rational and irrational points to Rudolf Otto's *The Idea of the Holy* and the theology of the irrational numinous, which Heidegger treats as a further layer of confusion.

It is not worthwhile for philosophy to pursue or disentangle this confusion. Heidegger mentions it for another reason and with a view to the central problem. Roughly seen, in Kant too, and especially in him, the entanglement of the two problems of transcendence is a driving motive of his questions in every period of his philosophizing. Yet it is fundamentally wrong to base an interpretation of Kant on these vulgar connections. One must see how Kant tries to free himself from these entanglements, which he only partly succeeds in doing, and without transcendence becoming central for him. Kant is the test case because both concepts are present in him, as the problem of the thing in itself and as the problem of God, and yet he points beyond them.

Heidegger gives a brief indication of how Kant approaches the problem of transcendence, starting from a passage where Kant fixes the double sense of the expression "outside us" in connection with the distinction between appearance and thing in itself (the note refers to the winter 1927–28 course on the *Critique of Pure Reason*, GA 25). Kant writes (A 373, missing in B): since the expression "outside us" carries an unavoidable ambiguity, sometimes meaning something that exists distinct from us as a thing in itself, sometimes what belongs merely to outer appearance, he will distinguish empirically external objects from those that might be called so in the transcendental sense by calling them things "to be found in space." The passage comes from the Fourth Paralogism of the first edition, Kant's refutation of empirical idealism, which he rewrote for the second edition. Heidegger chooses it because it shows Kant separating two senses of "outside" that the capsule model confuses.

"Outside us" thus means first the independent being itself, and second this same being, but thought as a possible object of an absolute knowledge not possible for us, thought as outside our possibility of grasping. "Outside us" means once what we ourselves are not, what is present at hand, which as such can show itself only within the ecstatic transcendence of thrown Dasein as being-in-the-world. But "outside us" can also mean what we not only are not, but what lies outside accessibility by finite ecstatic transcendence, namely appearances insofar as they are considered in themselves. "Outside us" then means outside "us" in the sense of the whole finite Dasein and its possibilities as finite. The appearance itself has "two sides" (A 38, B 55): the thing itself as a being existing for itself, and its showing itself to me, a finite subject. First, the object is considered in itself, apart from the way it is intuited through affection; it is intuited in the infinite understanding, the original intuition (*intuitus originarius*). Second, one attends to the form in which the being becomes accessible to a finite subject; then what is in itself is an appearance (compare A 251 f.). Heidegger reads the Kantian distinction as a distinction between two kinds of knower, not two kinds of object: the same being is thing in itself for an infinite, creative intuition and appearance for a finite, receptive one. This is the reading of *Kant and the Problem of Metaphysics* §5, and it carries forward the contrast between divine and human knowledge from §§2 and 4.

One of the crudest misunderstandings of the otherwise most substantive and advanced interpretation of Kant, that of the Marburg School, is that it took the thing in itself as something separate and tried to interpret it away. Kant says in the *Opus postumum* that the thing in itself is not another being than the appearance; both express only "another relation (*respectus*) of the representation to the same object." The same being can be the correlate of an original or a derivative intuition; the difference lies "merely in the difference of the relation in which the subject is affected." The Marburg Neo-Kantians (Cohen, Natorp) treated the thing in itself as a limit concept to be dissolved into the infinite task of knowledge. Heidegger's appeal to the *Opus postumum* shows Kant himself treating it as the same object under a different relation, which makes the difference a matter of the knower's finitude.

Kant's transcendence is oriented first to two possibilities of being-grasped for what is in itself, to two essentially different kinds of intuition. This conception is essential because it is not set out with reference to a psychological theory; the immediate relation of the subject to beings themselves is taken as basis. The concept of the thing in itself cannot be removed by dissolving it epistemologically; this concept (as correlate of an absolute understanding) falls only if it can be shown that the presupposition of an absolute understanding is not philosophically necessary. The condition stated is exactly the question Heidegger raises about "construction" in §2. If finite knowledge must be measured against an infinite ideal, the thing in itself remains; if philosophy can understand finitude without that measure, it falls. The next NB poses the question directly.

One must distinguish the thing itself as appearance and as thing in itself. Starting from the rightly understood concept of the thing in itself, the concept of appearance as "finite" object must be deduced. Starting from the appearance, the "X" must be shown immanent in it as the thing itself, which is not "in itself" in the strict sense. The two tasks run in opposite directions: from the infinite correlate to the finite object, and from the finite object to what in it points beyond finite access.
