The "X" is Kant's transcendental object.

Two questions are noted. How far must "infinity" be presupposed as a guiding thread? And how far does Kant show the still more fundamental problematic function of the ontic for ontology? Ontology is grounded in the ontic, and yet from what is so grounded the transcendental problematic is exposed, which also first makes the ontic transparent in its function. The first question returns to the theological ideal. The second anticipates metontology: ontology rests on the facticity of beings, yet it is ontology that makes that facticity intelligible, a circle the appendix to §10 had named as the "turn."

Kant's *Critique of Pure Reason* as a whole circles around the problem of transcendence, "which in the original sense is precisely not an epistemological problem but the problem of freedom" (p. 210), without Kant having radically secured this phenomenon of transcendence from the ground up. One must read him according to what he wanted to say. If one tallies him up in terms of Hume, Leibniz, Wolff, and so on, the essential falls through the gaps between these divisions. The identification of transcendence with freedom is announced here before it is argued. The remark about tallying Kant up against his sources repeats the objection to "little Moritz" in §10: philosophy is not a sum of influences.

Against both concepts of transcendence, the epistemological and the theological, it must be said in principle: transcendence is neither a relation between an inner and an outer sphere, such that what is surpassed in it would be a barrier belonging to the subject that separated it from the outer sphere, nor primarily the knowing relation of a subject to an object, belonging to the subject as an addition to its subjectivity. Still less is transcendence simply the title for what is exuberant and inaccessible to finite knowledge. The three negations dispose of the capsule model, the subject–object relation, and the theological beyond.

Positively, transcendence is, first, the original constitution of the subjectivity of a subject. The subject transcends as subject; it would not be a subject if it did not transcend. "To be a subject means to transcend" (p. 211). Dasein does not exist and then occasionally carry out a passage; existing means originally surpassing. "Dasein itself is the passage." Transcendence is not some possible comportment (among others) of Dasein toward other beings but the basic constitution of its being, on the basis of which it can relate to beings at all. A long note adds that the problem of transcendence must be taken back into the question of temporality and freedom, and only from there can it be shown how far the understanding of being as the overpowering, as holiness, belongs to transcendence itself, as essentially ontologically different. The task is not to prove the divine in its "existence" ontically, but to clarify the origin of this understanding of being from the transcendence of Dasein, the belonging of this idea of being to the understanding of being as such (compare Scheler's idea of such a genesis). Only from the essence of being and transcendence, and from the full dispersal belonging to transcendence (§10, sixth thesis), can this idea of being as overpowering be understood, not by interpretation toward an absolute Thou, nor as the good, value, or the eternal. Still to be thought: being and the *daimonion*, understanding of being and the *daimonion*; being as ground; being and nothing, anxiety. This is deliberately not treated in the course, because here, "amid today's violently inauthentic religiosity," the dialectical illusion is especially great. Heidegger would rather accept "the cheap reproach of atheism, which, if it is meant ontically, is even completely justified." But is not the supposed ontic faith in God at bottom godlessness? And is not the genuine metaphysician more religious than the usual believers, members of a "church," or even the "theologians" of every confession? The main text states the thesis in its sharpest form: Dasein does not have transcendence, it is transcendence. The note is among the most important in the course. It proposes to derive the understanding of the holy from transcendence, an ontological genealogy of the divine, rather than proving God's existence. It rejects the dialogical theology of the "absolute Thou" (Buber, Ebner), value theology, and eternity. The admission that the charge of atheism is "justified" if meant ontically, with the counter-question about godlessness in ordinary faith, recalls the §10 theses on the appearance of atheism in fundamental ontology.

Only because Dasein, as existing, is in a world can it learn to relate to beings in this or that way and come to terms with them; but it does not first reach beings in this way. Learning presupposes world. Every acquired way of dealing with things moves within a prior familiarity, so no learning can explain how Dasein first reaches beings.

Second, transcendence does not mean the crossing of a barrier that first shut the subject into an inner space. What is surpassed is beings themselves, which can become manifest to the subject precisely on the basis of its transcendence. Because the passage exists with Dasein, and beings that Dasein is not are thus surpassed, such beings come to appear as such, in themselves. Transcendence, which has leapt over beings, and nothing else, in advance, first makes it possible that what was leapt over stands over against Dasein ontically as a being and, as standing over against, can now be grasped in itself. This is the decisive reinterpretation. What transcendence passes over is not a wall but beings as a whole, and passing over them is what lets them appear. Beings are surpassed toward something else, world, and seen from there they show themselves as beings. "In itself" is thus a result of transcendence, which answers the realist worry of §10's first thesis.

What Dasein surpasses in its transcendence is not a gap or barrier "between" itself and objects; it is the beings among which it also is, as factical Dasein, that are surpassed. Objects are surpassed in advance, more precisely the beings that can later become objects. Dasein is thrown, factical, through its bodiliness entirely in the midst of nature, and precisely in the fact that the beings in whose midst it is and to which it belongs are surpassed by it lies transcendence. In other words, as transcending, Dasein is beyond nature, although as factical it remains embraced by it. "As transcending, that is, as free, Dasein is something alien to nature" (p. 212). Dasein is both in nature and beyond it. The equation of transcending with being free, and the claim that freedom makes Dasein "alien" to nature, anticipate §11c. Heidegger is not denying Dasein's natural embodiment, which the tenth thesis of §10 affirmed; he is saying that its relation to nature as nature is not itself natural.

Third, where the subject transcends to, as subject, is not an object, not this or that being at all, whether a particular thing, a being like itself, or another living being. The object, the being that can have the character of encounter, is what is surpassed, not the whither. "What the subject transcends toward is what we call world" (p. 212). The distinction between the surpassed (beings) and the whither (world) is the ontological difference in the language of transcendence: Dasein goes beyond beings to world, which is not a being.

Fourth, because transcendence constitutes the basic constitution of Dasein, belongs primarily to its being and is not an added comportment, and because this original being of Dasein, as passage, surpasses toward a world, the basic phenomenon of Dasein's transcendence is called being-in-the-world. The term of *Being and Time* is here defined as transcendence, which confirms that §§12–13 of the book were a first presentation of transcendence.

Insofar as Dasein exists, that is, insofar as a being-in-the-world exists, beings (nature) are already leapt over, and beings thereby receive the possibility of manifesting themselves in themselves. Insofar as Dasein exists, objects have always already become accessible to it, though in what mode of possible objectivity they are grasped remains quite undetermined and variable; there are different stages of possibilities in which things themselves can be uncovered in their being-in-themselves. The last clause prepares the reference to Scheler. There is no single privileged way in which beings are "in themselves"; every mode of access, everyday or scientific, presupposes the same transcendence.

Scheler did not really see this inner connection, but he was the first to gain insight into what he calls "the Dasein-relativity of things" (where "Dasein" means presence-at-hand). According to Scheler, it is a misleading formulation of the problem to posit things and then ask about their objectivity. There are stages of discoverability for beings, different possibilities in which they manifest themselves in themselves, and one cannot say, for example, that the physical grasp of the sun is the genuine knowledge as opposed to the natural grasp of the sun. Heidegger credits Scheler with the anti-scientistic point that scientific knowledge is not the only or the primary access to what things are. The parenthetical note on terminology prevents confusion: Scheler's *Dasein* means existence in the traditional sense.

Insofar as Dasein, as being-in-the-world, exists, it is always already outside with beings; indeed this way of speaking is still skewed, since "already outside" presupposes that it was once inside. Even saying that its intentional comportment is always already de-restricted toward beings presupposes that it was once restricted. What is meant by this transcendence cannot be brought together with previous conceptions and is hard to see at all, given the current entrenched distortion of the problem. Neither Bergson (least of all) nor Dilthey, and not Husserl either, sees the problem and phenomenon; Husserl opposed it vigorously from the start two years ago. Heidegger corrects his own formulation from *Being and Time* §13, which said Dasein is "always already outside." The correction shows how deeply the capsule image is embedded in the language. The remark about Husserl refers to their disagreements over the *Encyclopaedia Britannica* article in 1927, where Husserl rejected being-in-the-world as a relapse into the natural attitude.

If transcendence in the sense of being-in-the-world is the basic metaphysical constitution of Dasein, a metaphysics of Dasein, especially one with a fundamental-ontological aim, must point to this basic constitution from the start. The attempt is required to make this constitution visible first by starting from the traditional concept of transcendence, the epistemological subject–object relation. Hence in *Being and Time* the investigation begins, after the exposition in a first chapter, with the second chapter: "Being-in-the-World in General as the Basic Constitution of Dasein"; §§12 and 13 give a prefiguration of the phenomenon and a first familiarity with it. Anyone with even a slight notion of method could infer that this basic constitution is central for a metaphysics of Dasein, recurs constantly, and comes to light more originally, that is, ever more centrally, as a problem as the interpretation progresses. So after the first characterization, the attempt is made to articulate its structural moments and bring it closer again as a whole. Since the whole investigation aims to exhibit temporality as the metaphysical essence of Dasein, transcendence itself will evidently become graspable only from there, but as the basic constitution it must come into view as central throughout the investigation. The analysis of anxiety (§40), the problems of Dasein, worldhood, and reality, the interpretation of conscience and the concept of death, all serve the progressive working out of transcendence, until it becomes a problem again explicitly in §69: "The Temporality of Being-in-the-World and the Problem of the Transcendence of the World." Transcendence is still, indeed only now, a problem there. The problem must not be underestimated, and one needs a long breath, so as not to run out of it precisely where the problem begins. That intentionality is grounded in transcendence is stated there (p. 363, note) and fixed as a basic ontological problem. The paragraph is a reading guide to *Being and Time*: transcendence is the red thread from §12 to §69, and the book's analyses of anxiety, death, and conscience are stages of it, not freestanding existentialist themes.

Transcendence, being-in-the-world, is nowhere equated or identified with intentionality; whoever does so, as repeatedly happens, only shows how far they are from understanding the phenomenon, which is indeed not easy to grasp. What hinders its understanding is not so much its content and structure as the fact that we are burdened with prejudices and want to be done with things too quickly, or do not take seriously enough what cannot be grasped with the hands. So the effort and expenditure of concrete interpretations cannot be great enough, and the path cannot be laid out strictly enough, to make this basic phenomenon of transcendence visible (compare *Being and Time*, p. 351 ff.). Heidegger is far from convinced that he has worked it out so that one need only look, as at the blackboard, to "establish" it. Establishing in this sense is not at issue at all. That a more radical interpretation than his previous ones is possible will become clear from the second part; that it is necessary is shown by the fact that even Scheler did not see what is meant by transcendence (the note cites Scheler's "Idealism–Realism"). Heidegger admits the incompleteness of *Being and Time* and announces a more radical interpretation, which §§11b–c and 12 deliver: transcendence as world, freedom, and temporality. The phrase "what cannot be grasped with the hands" echoes the *Theaetetus* passage on those who admit only what they can grasp.

Heidegger now tries to clarify this basic constitution further, which means first rejecting obvious misunderstandings. One might be surprised that, if transcendence means being-in-the-world, so much is made of it, as a problem. What it says is surely the most evident thing there is: a factically existing Dasein, an actually existing human being, is there as an actual being among other beings, stands on the earth, walks under trees, and moves among other people. Dasein is in a world, that is, the factically existing human being occurs among other beings, within the totality of the rest. This is so clear that one does not see what problem remains; one could at most say that the thesis that Dasein is in a world by its basic constitution, by its essence, is evidently false. For it is equally evident that it is not essentially necessary that this or that human being exists; it does not lie in the idea of the human being that it actually exists, is in a world; it is only possible that a being like human Dasein is in a world. To say that the essence of Dasein lies in being-in-the-world is plainly absurd. The objection is the one the opening of §11b answers: it takes "world" as the sum of present beings and "in" as spatial inclusion, and so it treats being-in-the-world as a contingent fact.

It is indeed right that it does not belong to the essence of Dasein that it factically exists; it is precisely its essence that this being can also not exist factically. The cosmos can be without human beings inhabiting an earth, and presumably the cosmos was long before human beings ever existed. The concession is important for the metontology of the appendix: Dasein's existence is contingent, and nature precedes it.

How can one then claim that being-in-the-world belongs to the essence of Dasein? If the thesis is still to be true, being-in-the-world must mean something else. What it means is already fixed in principle by the emphasis that being-in-the-world is the basic constitution of Dasein. Negatively, this contains what is decisive. When I say of Dasein that its basic constitution is being-in-the-world, I state something that belongs to its essence, but I disregard whether a being of this essence factically exists. The thesis is not a statement of its factical existence; I do not establish that my Dasein factically occurs, nor that by its essence it must also factically exist. I say: if Dasein factically exists, its existence has the structure of being-in-the-world; Dasein is by its essence being-in-the-world, whether it factically exists or not. The solution distinguishes an essential statement from an existential claim. Being-in-the-world is a structure of Dasein's possibility, not a report of its actuality.

Dasein is not being-in-the-world because it happens factically to exist; on the contrary, it can factically exist only as Dasein because its essence is being-in-the-world. Two things must be kept strictly apart. First, the factical statement of existence, that this particular Dasein now exists; here it is decided whether a Dasein factically exists or not. Second, the metaphysical statement of essence, that being-in-the-world as constitution belongs to the essence of Dasein, whether it factically exists or not; here it is not decided whether it exists, but what belongs to the inner possibility of Dasein if it is to be able to exist. Being-in-the-world, transcendence, characterizes the basic way of being, the existence of Dasein, and says nothing about whether it exists, analogous to the statement "body is extended," which is an essential statement and not an assertion of fact, and says nothing about whether this or that body is present. The statement of transcendence about existence is "an existential (ontological) statement, not an existentiell (ontic) one" (p. 217). The terminological distinction between existential and existentiell is that of *Being and Time* §4. The comparison with extension, Descartes's essence of body, is instructive: just as extension says what a body is without asserting that bodies exist, being-in-the-world says what Dasein is without asserting that it exists.

All this can serve only to determine the sense of the thesis in principle and ward off a wrong interpretation. What it means in content is far from clear, and it requires patience, step-by-step progress, and above all the will to see what the indication is meant to point to. The statement that it belongs to the essence of Dasein to be in the world as factically present among others differs from the statement that being-in-the-world belongs to the essence of Dasein, which characterizes its essential constitution. "World" means something fundamentally different in each. What is decisive is, first, what world (as the "wherein") means, and second, what being-in means here. The two questions set the agenda: §11b answers the first, and the "in" will be answered by freedom and temporality.

The thesis that Dasein has being-in-the-world as the basic constitution of its being is meant as an essential statement. This implies that Dasein "has" something like world by its essence and does not first get world by factically existing, nor by other beings of its kind and of other kinds also factically being (or its being among them), but conversely: it can exist as this particular Dasein only insofar as, as Dasein at all, it has something like world. "Having" world is not possessing an object. It is the prior familiarity with a whole within which particular beings can be encountered.

It becomes clear that the difficulty of seeing and understanding this basic constitution of transcendence lies in the fact that the concept of world means something peculiar. Heidegger now tries to clarify transcendence as being-in-the-world in its structure by clarifying and determining the concept "world." §11b is therefore the determination of the "whither" of transcendence.

###### b) The Phenomenon of World

The aim is to bring what "world" designates closer as a determinacy of transcendence as such. Transcendence is being-in-the-world. Because world belongs to transcendence as such, it is a transcendental concept in the strict sense. "Transcendental" in Kant means roughly "ontological," though concerning the ontology of "nature" in the broadest sense; here the term is equivalent to "fundamental-ontological." Heidegger adopts Kant's term but widens its scope. Kant's transcendental concerns the conditions of objects of nature; Heidegger's concerns the conditions of Dasein's understanding of being as such, of which the ontology of nature is one region.

The expression "world" is ambiguous, but not by accident. The traditional concept must first be followed in the directions of its ambiguity, to see what it means and how in each case. From this one can gather indications of what is meant by the concept of world that properly belongs to transcendence. The characteristic ambiguity becomes graspable above all by looking at the history of the concept, though only a few characteristic indications can be given. The historical survey follows the method of §8's opening: the ambiguity of a concept is a trace of the phenomenon it half-grasps.

In ancient philosophy, precisely in its decisive beginnings (Parmenides and Heraclitus), something strange appears at once. The Greek expression for "world" is *kosmos*. It does not mean what one usually thinks, present beings as such, heavenly bodies, stars, earth, a particular being. Nor does *kosmos* mean all beings together; it does not mean beings themselves at all and is no name for them. *Kosmos* means "condition," a state; it is the title for a way of being, not for beings themselves. *Kosmos houtos* is this particular condition of beings, this world of beings as distinct from another. Beings themselves remain; their overall condition, their world, can become another, or one can hold that the world of beings always stays the same. To express this how of being, Heidegger uses (as already in his Freiburg lectures) the verb "to world" (*welten*). This basic meaning of *kosmos* was first pointed out in principle by Karl Reinhardt (*Parmenides and the History of Greek Philosophy*, 1916). The verb *welten* first appears in Heidegger's 1919 war emergency semester course ("es weltet"). Reinhardt's philological finding supplies the Greek warrant: *kosmos* is an adverbial notion, the order or arrangement of beings, not their sum.

The basic meaning appears in several Presocratic fragments. Melissus (fragment 7): it is impossible for being to be re-worlded, for the world that already worlded before does not perish, and the one not yet worlding does not arise. Parmenides (fragment 4) speaks of *kata kosmon* with regard to being: use your understanding instead of your eyes and see what is far as present (in its genuine being); for it will not cut apart the holding-together of beings, neither scattering them entirely with regard to their overall constitution, *kata kosmon*, nor putting them together otherwise. Anaxagoras (fragment 8): the things in one world (*ta en tōi heni kosmōi*) are not wholly separated or cut off with an axe; that is, what worlds as a whole in a certain way, has a certain basic condition and connection. *Kosmos*, as the how of wholeness, underlies every dismemberment; dismemberment does not destroy world but is possible only on its basis (every "part" of the world is finite). Heraclitus (fragment 89): for those awake there is one common world, whereas each sleeper turns to a world of his own. Here world is related to waking and sleeping as peculiar basic modes of factical Dasein. Wakefulness is a state of Dasein in which beings manifest themselves as one and the same for all in the same world character; beings show themselves in a thoroughgoing accord accessible to everyone and to which everyone holds. In sleep, beings that show themselves have for each individual a world character of their own, a wholly particular way in which they world. Heidegger's translations make *kosmos* adverbial throughout. The Heraclitus fragment is decisive, because it relates world to Dasein's modes of being, waking and sleeping, and so shows world to be relative to Dasein without being subjective. The remark that every "part" of the world is finite recalls Kant's reflection, quoted below, that the world is finite because its members limit each other.

From these examples several things follow. First, world is a title for the way of being of beings (although the concept is not yet explicitly an ontological problem). Second, world means the wholeness, the unification, and the possible dispersal of beings. Third, this way of being is changeable, or not; world stands in connection with movement, change, and time. Fourth, world is somehow relative to Dasein and the way it happens to exist, and fifth, with regard to whether the world is common, one and the same, letting beings manifest themselves in the same concordant way for all, or for each individual in his own way: everyone's world or one's own world. The five points supply the outline of the transcendental concept: a how of being, a whole, bound up with time, relative to Dasein, shared or private. The pairing of unification and dispersal recalls the monad (§5) and Dasein's dispersion (§10, sixth thesis).

Further evidence will confirm and differentiate these meanings, and as a whole will make clear what this preparatory consideration is about: that the concept of world has a peculiarly universal character (as a wholeness relative to dispersal) and yet is essentially related to human Dasein. This is only the preliminary stage for the properly transcendental meaning that the interpretation of transcendence will assign. The paradox of the concept, universal yet related to Dasein, is the problem the transcendental interpretation must solve.

Concepts like "world," which express a condition of beings, the how of beings (they can world thus or so), are easily and often, indeed mostly at first, used to designate beings themselves. "River" (*Fluß*) means what flows, this particular river flowing here, the Lahn; but it also designates the how of being of beings, flowing as opposed to rolling. The Greek *physis* likewise means nature in a material and a formal sense, as the principle of movement; the ending *-sis* already indicates a how. Correspondingly, "world" means primarily and properly the how of beings, and at the same time these beings themselves. Both meanings can go together undistinguished: world is beings in a certain how. The Lahn is the river at Marburg, where the course was given. The linguistic point explains the persistence of the ontic concept: a word naming a way of being slides into naming the beings that have it.

When Paul, in 1 Corinthians and Galatians, speaks of "this world" (*kosmos houtos*), this means this overall condition of beings, and not only or primarily nature, the "cosmic" in our sense. "This world" means this condition and situation of human beings, this way of their Dasein, their comportment toward goods and works, toward nature and everything, the manner in which they value goods. Indeed *kosmos* is directly the title for the way human Dasein is, its disposition, way of thinking: "the wisdom of the world." This *kosmos*, this how, is determined by its relation to the *kosmos* that has already begun, "the age to come"; the condition of all beings is seen with regard to the *eschaton*, the final state. Again *kosmos* means a how, essentially related to Dasein and time. *Kosmos* is the world in the sense of humanity, the community and society of human beings in their disposition turned away from God, that is, in their basic stance toward themselves and all beings. "Worldly" then becomes the expression for a basic kind of human existence. Compare 1 Corinthians 1:28: God chose the base things of the world, the things that are not, to bring to nothing the things that are. The Pauline material recalls Heidegger's 1920–21 course on the phenomenology of religion. World as a way of existing, oriented to an eschatological future, supplies the temporal and existential dimension that Greek *kosmos* lacked. The verse chosen, God choosing "the things that are not," foreshadows the "not" of world in §11c.

With regard to the principle, the formation of the Christian concept shows how directly "world" means, first, a way of being, and second, primarily with relation to human beings, their relation to beings. The two features are the ones that distinguish the transcendental concept from the cosmological.

For Augustine, *mundus* means the whole of creation. But just as often *mundus* stands for *mundi habitatores*, the inhabitants of the world, those who settle into it; and this does not mean merely that they are there besides mountains and rivers, but that this settling-in is primarily determined by certain basic attitudes, valuations, ways of conducting oneself and taking things, by "disposition": being with the world in one's heart (*corde esse cum mundo*). The inhabitants of the world are the lovers of the world, the impious or carnal. The just are not called "world," because although they dwell in it in the flesh, they are with God in their hearts. "World" is thus the title for the God-averted way of relating to beings in the midst of which the human being exists. It is, first, a collective designation for the human community existing in this way, and second, primarily the way of this particular existing, in which and for which all beings show themselves in a particular valuation and involvement. "World is the how, not the what" (p. 223). Augustine's *dilectores mundi* add the affective dimension: world is constituted by love and valuation, not by perception. "Involvement" (*Bewandtnis*) is the term of *Being and Time* §18 for the relational being of equipment.

For Thomas Aquinas too, world is sometimes synonymous with universe, the totality of creatures, but also with *saeculum*, insofar as "by the name of world the lovers of the world are signified" ("children of the world"). *Mundanus* equals *saecularis*, worldly minded as opposed to spiritual; hence *astutia mundana*, worldly cunning. The medieval double sense preserves both the cosmological and the existential meaning.

The meaning *mundus* takes on in modern metaphysics shows in the following. Metaphysics is divided into general and special metaphysics, and the latter into cosmology, psychology, and theology. Baumgarten (*Metaphysica* §351) defines general cosmology as the science of the general predicates of the world, and world (§354) as the series (multitude, whole) of actual finite things that is not part of another. In this artificial definition all determinations are used indiscriminately and taken together in the external sense of summation: world is simply the sum of what is actually present. The distinctions antiquity already had are completely lost here. Only Kant makes the essential distinctions. School metaphysics represents the low point: world as a sum of actual things, the purely ontic concept.

Kant understands world clearly as a how, a wholeness in an ontological-metaphysical sense. This is shown by his remarks on Baumgarten, by the distinction between world and nature, and by the division of philosophy according to the scholastic concept and the world concept. The three points structure the rest of the Kant discussion.

First, from Kant's marginal notes to Baumgarten's *Metaphysics*: No. 4085: "The idea of the world is not arbitrary. For just as I must think a part that is not a whole in turn, so I must think a whole that is not a part." World must necessarily be thought insofar as we think a whole; Kant does not further ground why we must think a whole. No. 4329: "The world is finite, because it is a whole of beings that limit one another." No. 4521: "The present concept of the world serves rather to put in order the idea of a substantial whole and an isolated one, than to prove the existence of any particular cause." No. 4522: "The world that cannot be part of another whole is the world in the transcendental understanding. The world that is not part of an actual whole is the world in the physical understanding. Because the possible is distinguished from the actual only in that in the former the conditions are not considered in thoroughgoing determination, a whole world that has no conditions outside itself except in the absolutely necessary being must also have no other limits of actuality than the inner conditions of possibility. From a world in this sense one could infer a single cause and its all-sufficiency, and further the unity of this world. But from a world that is not the absolute universe, one can infer no more than from a substantial composite in general, and finally from a mere multitude of finite things no more than from a single finite thing." Heidegger's bracketed insertions, "a substantial whole and an isolated one [an ontological concept of world]" and "another [more original] whole," mark where Kant's notes point beyond the ontic concept. No. 4329 recalls the Leibnizian doctrine of §5c that finite substances limit one another.

The last remark, also directed against dogmatic theoretical metaphysics, makes the Kantian concept especially clear. World means not only wholeness, as the wholeness of a particular together of ontically present things, so that world reaches only as far as the single whole of what actually is, but the wholeness whose universality is determined by the whole of inner possibilities. Kant clearly goes beyond the ontic concept of world as a series of actual finite things to a transcendental concept. This is also the positive work and the metaphysical content of Kant's Transcendental Dialectic. Only from such a concept of world could one infer an absolute cause, if this inference had any sense at all. But this transcendental concept of world is an idea, so the inference is essentially impossible. One cannot infer from something possible, not even from the whole of possibility, to something actual, but at most to the idea of an absolute ground: the transcendental ideal. Heidegger reads the Transcendental Dialectic positively, as the working out of a transcendental concept of world, not merely as a critique of illusion. World as the totality of possibilities connects with Leibniz's pure possibles in God's understanding (§2) and the *omnitudo realitatis*.

In another note (No. 3799) Kant summarizes: "If the concept of the world signified the whole of all possible things, namely those possible in connection with the all-sufficient ground, it would be more fruitful." The prephilosophical, externally ontic concept of world as series says nothing at all; it even falls essentially behind the ancient concept of *kosmos*. On the other hand, the latter does not coincide with Kant's transcendental concept of world either. In the Transcendental Dialectic this concept has yet another meaning, which can only be indicated here. The ranking places Baumgarten below the Greeks, and both below Kant.

Second, Kant says (A 418–19, B 446–47): "We have two expressions, world and nature, which sometimes run into one another. The first means the mathematical whole of all appearances and the totality of their synthesis, in the great as well as in the small, that is, in their progress both through composition and through division. The same world is called nature insofar as it is considered as a dynamic whole, and one looks not at aggregation in space or time, so as to bring it about as a magnitude, but at the unity in the existence of appearances." What is striking is that the concept of world is again restricted to the totality of beings, yet understood not ontically but ontologically, in distinction from nature. As a formula: world is the mathematical whole and nature the dynamic whole of appearances, that is, of beings in themselves accessible to us finite beings. Accessible beings stand under these two ontological, transcendental determinations. To understand them rightly, the titles "mathematical" and "dynamic" must be clarified. The passage is from the Antinomy of Pure Reason, where world is the subject of the cosmological ideas.

Kant uses this division throughout the *Critique*, dividing the categories into two classes: mathematical categories of quantity and quality, and dynamic categories of relation and modality. Since the ontological principles are articulated along the guide of the categories, Kant speaks of mathematical and dynamic principles: the former are the Axioms of Intuition and the Anticipations of Perception, the latter the Analogies of Experience and the Postulates of Empirical Thought. The (mathematical) principles of intuition are capable of intuitive certainty, the (dynamic) principles of thought of discursive certainty; in each case the certainty is "complete" (A 162, B 201). The division by kind of certainty recalls §4: intuitive versus discursive knowledge, though now both are finite.

What Kant means by "mathematical" and "dynamic" has, in content and directly, nothing to do with mathematics or with the dynamics of physics. But the titles are chosen to designate something (categories, principles, ideas) that, in its possible application, insofar as it is ontological, can be so named with regard to its use in determining the ontic. Kant says (A 160, B 199) that in the application of pure concepts of understanding to possible experience, the use of their synthesis is either mathematical or dynamic, since it concerns partly the intuition alone, partly the existence of an appearance. He adds (A 162, B 201–2) that he has in view neither the principles of mathematics nor those of general (physical) dynamics, but only those of pure understanding in relation to inner sense, and that he names them "more with regard to their application than for the sake of their content" (compare the division of *conjunctio* into *compositio* and *nexus* in the note to B 202). The names are borrowed from the region they found. Ontological concepts are named after the ontic sciences to which they apply, a pattern Heidegger now explains.

The exemplary being for Kant's general ontology is nature, beings in themselves as mathematical natural science discovers them. Therefore the ontological determinations as such have a possible application to these beings and can receive their names from what they can be applied to. "Mathematical" and "dynamic" are not primarily ontological titles; with them something ontological is designated from the beings it founds ontologically. The choice of these determinations shows that with his general ontology Kant had a positive interest in providing the ontological foundation for "physics" as the science of beings as such, but that does not mean a philosophy of science of physics. The distinction between an ontological foundation and a philosophy of science is aimed at the Marburg reading of the *Critique* as a theory of natural science, rejected also in §4.

Since the what-content of a natural thing is determined by its extensive and intensive magnitude (in Descartes only by extension), and magnitude is what is determined mathematically, with space and time as pure intuitions as the a priori foundations of these determinations, the mathematical categories coincide with the categories of the essence of nature, its *essentia*. The categories Kant calls dynamic, by contrast, concern the being "nature" not in its what-being but in its that-being, the mode of existence. Dynamic categories are categories of "existence." The mathematical principles are ontological-essential principles, the dynamic ones ontological-existential principles. Heidegger maps Kant's division onto the scholastic distinction of essence and existence. The mapping supports the claim of GA 24 and of the unexecuted §6 that the articulation of being into essence and existence runs through the whole history of ontology, Kant included.

As world and nature refer to the same thing, so do mathematical and dynamic wholeness. World is the wholeness that represents the sum of those a priori determinations that say what belongs to a possible being in its what-content. Nature is the corresponding wholeness delimiting the mode of existence. Nature is understood here (compare the note at A 418, B 446) in the original sense of *physis*, as the how of presence (nature considered formally) of what we also call nature materially. World designates the region nature with regard to the essential content of its material character; not even the essential and existential wholeness of beings, nor, as in the notes on Baumgarten, the wholeness of possibilities (the *omnitudo realitatis*). "World is the wholeness of the a priori what-content of what belongs to nature, irrespective of whether it exists or not!" (p. 229). In the *Critique*, then, "world" has narrowed to the essence-side of nature, which is narrower than the notes to Baumgarten.

There is a difficulty: an ontology of the mode of existence is also a priori; this mode also belongs to nature, only it remains undecided whether it is actualized. This is a pervasive difficulty in traditional metaphysics and ontology and in the consideration of essences, especially in Husserl. By excluding the actual (in the phenomenological reduction), the what is brought out; but excluding the actual does not exclude actuality, the mode of existence and its essential connection with essential content in the narrower sense. Essence here is ambiguous: it means the a priori of *essentia* and of *existentia*. The criticism of Husserl is precise. Bracketing actual existence does not bracket the essence of existence, and the reduction therefore tends to treat essence as what-content alone, obscuring the a priori of existence, which is where the analytic of Dasein, whose essence is existence, begins.

This concept of world as mathematical wholeness, as transcendental ideal, without including existence, indicates that Kant's concept of world, though defined in each of its meanings, remains unclarified as a whole and still too narrow. The verdict prepares the third Kantian usage, which points toward the existential concept.

Third, another use Kant makes of the concept of world can now be understood. Kant distinguishes a double concept in the essence of philosophy, already met several times in this course: general and special metaphysics, metaphysics as preparatory and as final purpose, *prōtē philosophia* and *theologia*. Philosophy as special metaphysics, as metaphysics proper, is philosophy "according to the world concept"; it deals with the questions: What can I know? What ought I to do? What may I hope? But why is this metaphysics proper called philosophy according to the world concept (*in sensu cosmico*)? It deals not with nature but with the human being, with his existence and its essence (compare the fourth question, "What is the human being?", in the Introduction to the *Logic* lectures). And yet it is philosophy according to the world concept. Here again the peculiar other relation of "world" to the existence of human Dasein and to the essence of the human being as a whole appears, together with the significance of Kant's first concept of world as wholeness. Kant says (A 839, B 867 note): "World concept here means the one that concerns what necessarily interests everyone; accordingly I determine the aim of a science according to scholastic concepts if it is regarded only as one of the skills for certain arbitrary ends." Hence, according to Kant, "logic," and in a certain way "ontology" too, are propaedeutic (*Logic*, Introduction III). The "world concept" of philosophy joins the two senses of world, totality and human existence. Philosophy in the cosmic sense concerns the whole of what matters to every human being, which is the Augustinian sense of world transposed into Kant's architectonic. That logic is merely propaedeutic for Kant is the premise of the whole course: logic belongs to the scholastic concept until it is grounded in metaphysics.

The expression "worldview" (*Weltanschauung*), which already occurs in Kant though not in today's sense, points in the direction of this use of "world." By worldview is meant not viewing the world as nature; "view" (*Anschauung*), as singular representation, means representing, thinking, knowing the one whole of what interests every human existence as such. The title must be taken in this sense in Karl Jaspers's *Psychology of Worldviews* (third edition 1925), whose task is to show "what the human being is." Heidegger links Jaspers to Kant's world concept, giving the book's project a transcendental reading. Heidegger had reviewed the book in 1919–21.

In today's meaning, we also think that a worldview involves a particular stance, or gives the means to take one, as a particular existentiell stance. Philosophy itself never gives a worldview and does not have the task of giving one. On the contrary, it is a possible form of existence that, in itself and in its idea, does not yet need a worldview, because it moves in the possibilities of worldviews (for how far it still needs "engagement," compare the eleventh thesis of §10). "Worldview philosophy" is an absurd title; it can only mean discussion of the essence of worldview, its inner necessity, and its possible forms. But the expression "scientific philosophy" is equally absurd at bottom, because philosophy lies before all science, which it can do only because it already is in an eminent sense what "science" can be only in a derivative sense. The alternative, either scientific or worldview philosophy, is as external as any combination of the two. The paragraph rejects both options of the 1920s debate: Husserl's "philosophy as rigorous science" and the worldview philosophies of Dilthey's school. Philosophy is prior to science, and it is prior to worldview because it moves within the space of possible worldviews without choosing among them, the same position as §10's twelfth thesis on the "decision" fundamental ontology cannot make.

What Kant means by philosophy according to the scholastic concept or the world concept coincides only in a certain way with this distinction, because the idea of metaphysics is not positively grounded and exposed in the same way as the laying of the foundation itself. The reservation is that Kant's "philosophy in the cosmic sense" remains a program, while his foundation, the Analytic, is worked out.

Summarizing the orientation on the history of the concept: "world" as a concept of the being of beings characterizes the wholeness of beings in the totality of their possibilities, but this is essentially related to human existence, taken in its final purpose. To prepare the phenomenological clarification of the transcendental concept of world, and as a systematic summary, four concepts of world can be distinguished (compare *Being and Time*, p. 64). First, an ontic concept: "world" is simply the title for what is present in its togetherness, for "nature," the prephilosophical naive concept, which can be called ontic-natural. Second, a (in a certain way) ontological concept correlated with the first: "world" as the region nature, the totality of what belongs to a nature as such. The summary formula holds the two strands of the history together: totality of possibilities, related to human existence. The first two concepts correspond to *Being and Time* §14's first and second concepts.

Third, again an ontic concept: "world" now means not nature but existing human beings as existing, as in "the great world," "woman of the world," "man of the world." This prephilosophical concept is, as distinct from the ontic-natural, the ontic-existentiell (or human) concept. Looking ahead, fourth, an ontological concept of world that does not mean human society ontically but ontologically the metaphysical essence of Dasein as such, with regard to its basic metaphysical constitution: transcendence. The fourth concept is the transcendental concept that the rest of §11 develops.

The transcendental concept is evidently related to the others in its own way. Yet none of the concepts named under one to three, nor their sum, exhausts the concept of world as constitutive of transcendence. The transcendental concept is not an addition or synthesis of the others but their ground.

Regarding the two prephilosophical ontic concepts, something essential must be noted. Take the third, existentiell concept, as in the expression "fashionable society" (*die vornehme Welt*). This does not mean merely a collection or even a society of well-dressed individuals; constitutive for "world" is that we mean these people precisely in their conduct: to them as world belongs also everything among which they move, the hotels, the racing stables, and so on. It would be a complete failure even of the prephilosophical concept to think that it designates (as in the first) only present things and things of use in particular, and that this concept must be supplemented or replaced by saying that world is a human concept designating the human environment or distant world. "World" designates neither things in isolation nor human beings, but what is essential even in the prephilosophical concept is the relation of human Dasein to things, or things in relation to human beings. The example is well chosen: "the fashionable world" includes its hotels and racehorses. World is neither the people nor the things but their relational whole, which is what the transcendental concept makes explicit.

Even the prephilosophical ontic concept has as its primary meaning not a particular being but the how of the existing of Dasein, namely its comportment toward beings and toward itself, taken in its totality. The way human beings exist is to determine themselves in and from the whole. "Dasein's being-in-the-world means being in the whole, with regard to the how" (p. 233). Even if it happens to fit itself into particulars and expect everything from them, this very expecting testifies to existence and its wholeness. Existing is always from a whole, even when one clings to a particular thing. Clinging to one thing is a way of relating to the whole, a narrowing that presupposes it.

World therefore cannot be understood as the ontic context of useful things, the things of historical culture as distinct from nature and natural things, though the analysis of useful things and their context offers a foothold and path for first making the phenomenon of world visible. World is not beings as equipment, what human beings busy themselves among, as if being-in-the-world meant moving among cultural things, nor is it a multiplicity of human beings. All these beings belong to what is called intraworldly beings, but not to world itself. What and how is world? The correction is aimed at readers of *Being and Time* §§15–18, who took the equipment analysis as a theory that world is the workshop of tools. The analysis of equipment was a way into the phenomenon, not its definition.

Since the problem cannot be unrolled in its full breadth and there is not enough time to develop the connections concretely and evenly, Heidegger proceeds with the help of a historical orientation, if only vague, on Plato's doctrine of ideas. Conversely, working out the original phenomenon of transcendence first gives the perspective for a renewed penetration of Plato's doctrine of ideas and for clarifying participation (*methexis*) and the between (*metaxy*). The reciprocity of interpretation is characteristic: Plato helps show world, and world helps interpret Plato.

The last considerations showed that the character of wholeness belongs somehow to the concept of world, and world is supposed to be constitutive for the transcendence of Dasein. Dasein transcends beings, and the leap-over is a leap to world. But the beings leapt over in transcendence are not only those Dasein itself is not; in transcendence Dasein leaps over precisely itself as a being, more precisely, this leap-over makes it possible for Dasein to be something like itself. Only in the leap-over of itself does the abyss open up that Dasein is for itself in each case, and only because this abyss of selfhood is open through and in transcendence can it be covered over and made invisible. Self-transcendence is the key addition. Dasein surpasses itself as a being toward world, and only thus becomes a self. The "abyss" (*Abgrund*) anticipates *On the Essence of Ground*, where freedom is the "abyss of ground." The self is an abyss because, having surpassed itself as a being, it has no being to rest on.

The question becomes unavoidable: what is world, toward which Dasein transcends? How does this wholeness relate to Dasein itself? Is world a realm of ideas in a "place beyond the heavens" (*hyperouranios topos*), which a reason sunk into Dasein beholds? Or is world the whole of ideas innate in the subject? These questions already show how the problem of the concept of world as a transcendental one is entwined with the problem of the subjectivity of the subject and with the basic ontological question of being as such. The two options, Platonic realism of ideas and Cartesian innatism, reproduce the realism–idealism alternative on the level of world.

If the phenomenon named by the transcendental concept is central, it must always have come to light in all genuine philosophy in some form, however veiled and not grasped as such. That the conception of the doctrine of ideas was motivated by transcendence, not yet disclosed as such, and by the phenomenon of world cannot be doubted. But it can be shown just as clearly that the doctrine of ideas could not reach the concept of world, because the ideas and the comportment toward them counted only as an intensification of a certain grasping of beings, namely intuition. The beholding to which everything here goes back has as its correlate a particular, quite one-sided concept of being, and the recourse to a beholding consciousness indicated in *idea*, *theōria*, *intuitus*, and the intuition of essences is so little fit to solve the problem of transcendence that it does not even allow one to see the phenomenon. The lineage *idea*, *theōria*, *intuitus*, *Wesensschau* runs from Plato through Thomas's divine intuition (§2) and Leibniz's adequate intuition (§4) to Husserl. Heidegger's criticism is that transcendence modeled on seeing is transcendence modeled on a relation to present beings, which cannot reach world.

The connection between ideas and seeing, *theōria*, intuition (indicated by the word *idea*), is essential, because it expresses the origin of the doctrine of ideas. Insofar as being is ascribed to the ideas themselves, they are, as Aristotle already saw, only a doubling of beings; moreover, as what always is, removed from change, they are what is beheld in pure contemplation. The doctrine of ideas thus means the doubling of a basic kind of comportment toward beings, theoretical intuition, and is coupled with it. If the doctrine of ideas relates to the phenomenon of transcendence, and the idea is correlative to intuition, then there lies in it the tendency to grasp the problem of transcendence along the thread of intuiting. This is already prepared in antiquity and later leads to orienting the problem of transcendence on the epistemological subject–object relation. The genealogy of the epistemological concept of transcendence reaches back to Plato: modeling the relation to the ideas on intuition turns transcendence into a relation of seeing, and modern epistemology inherits this.

Because intuiting as *aisthēsis* is connected with grasping the beautiful, one tends to grasp or ground transcendence not only by measuring it against *noein* but by leaning on aesthetic comportment. This tendency is strengthened by the view that in aesthetic intuition things are seen most originally as they are, a view resting on the mistaken opinion that in aesthetic intuition the subject is at the same time disinterested in the being intuited. The aesthetic model, with Kant's "disinterested pleasure" and Schopenhauer's will-less contemplation, is rejected as a variant of the theoretical model. Heidegger denies that aesthetic contemplation is disinterested.

Just as little as transcendence, not being an ontic comportment, can be imposed on intuition in the theoretical or aesthetic sense, so little can it be imposed on practical comportment, whether handcrafting and using or any other. The central task is to go back, in the ontology of Dasein, behind these divisions of comportments to their common root, a task that need not be simple. Transcendence lies before every possible mode of comportment, before *noēsis*, but also before *orexis*. The rejection of the practical model is aimed at the "pragmatist" reading of *Being and Time*. Transcendence precedes the division of theory and practice, as the monad's drive preceded the division of perception and appetite (§5c).

That the phenomenon of world is bent around or posited ontically as a present realm of ideas accessible to mere intuition has, among other things, a main reason in the fact that transcendence was taken early and primarily in the sense of *theōrein*; transcendence was not sought in its original rootedness in the genuine being of Dasein. Yet this, as genuine action, *praxis*, was of course not unknown to antiquity. If the problem of transcendence is now posed in connection with the problem of freedom, freedom must not be taken in the narrow sense in which it belongs to *praxis* as distinct from *theōria*. Up to Kant's doctrine of the primacy of practical reason, and above all in Neo-Kantianism, the problem of freedom remains ambiguous. In the horizon of the division of faculties (imagination, intuition, thought) and the positing of an either-or of receptivity and spontaneity, one must understand Kant's effort to take theoretical comportment back into practice as giving the practical priority over the theoretical. But Kant was on the way to grounding both more originally, although he never posed the problem radically and quite consciously. The problem is the common root of intuiting, *theōrein*, and acting, *praxis*. The "common root" echoes Kant's "common but unknown root" of sensibility and understanding (A 15, B 29), which *Kant and the Problem of Metaphysics* identifies with the transcendental imagination and ultimately with time. Here the root is freedom, which §12 will show to be temporal.

The idea correlates with an *idein*, a seeing. It lies in the starting point of Western philosophy as such that intuition, mere beholding, is the basic act in which transcendence is, as it were, primarily at home. Even when later the theoretical comportment is apparently replaced by the practical (primacy of practical reason), the ancient starting point remains guiding. The genuine phenomenon of transcendence cannot be housed in any particular comportment, theoretical, practical, or aesthetic; as comportments toward beings, all are possible only on the basis of transcendence itself. Reversing the priority of theory and practice does not escape the model, because it still locates transcendence in a comportment toward beings.

Although Plato did not seek transcendence in its genuine rootedness, the connection between the transcendent meant by the *idea* and the root of transcendence, *praxis*, comes to light under the unavoidable compulsion of the phenomena. The idea is correlative to intuition, but there is a passage in Plato according to which beyond beings and *ousia*, beyond the ideas, *epekeina tēs ousias*, lies the idea of the good. Here a transcendence appears which, since the ideas are already transcendent relative to changing beings, must be claimed as the most original. The *epekeina* is the point where Plato's thought exceeds the model of seeing. The good is not seen as an idea among ideas; it is beyond them, as what they are for.

What the idea of the good means cannot simply be determined from what we have of Plato. That it still has a mythical character in the end is as certain as that this remark does not uncover the philosophical motive. Nor does the path especially obvious today, simply reinterpreting the idea of the good as the concept of value, lead there. The concept of value, which within certain limits to be fixed has its necessary function, springs from the same traditional metaphysics, unclarified and ungrounded in its genuine ontological foundations. The rejection of value is consistent with the Second Main Part's opening, where the idea of value was called as obscure as the *agathon*.

What must be learned to see in the idea of the good is the character that Plato and especially Aristotle call the *hou heneka*, the "for the sake of which" (*das Umwillen*), that for the sake of which something is or is not, is thus or otherwise. The idea of the good, which lies beyond beings and the realm of ideas, is the "for the sake of"; it is the genuine determination that transcends the totality of ideas and at the same time organizes them in their wholeness. The "for the sake of," as *epekeina*, surpasses the ideas, but determines them precisely in surpassing and gives them the form of wholeness, *koinōnia*, belonging. If the character of the *hou heneka* in the highest idea is kept in view, the connection between the doctrine of ideas and the concept of world appears: "the basic character of world, by which the wholeness receives its specifically transcendental form of organization, is the for-the-sake-of" (p. 238). World, as that toward which Dasein transcends, is primarily determined by the for-the-sake-of. The good as "for the sake of which" gives the ideas their wholeness, as world gives beings theirs. This is the interpretation promised in §8's introductory section. The *hou heneka* also appeared there as the fourth concept of ground, the motive, which now proves to be the basic character of world.

A for-the-sake-of is essentially possible only where there is a will. Insofar as transcendence, being-in-the-world, constitutes the basic constitution of Dasein, being-in-the-world must also be originally grown together with, or have grown out of, the basic determination of the existence of Dasein: freedom. "Only where there is freedom is there a for-the-sake-of, and only there is there world. In short: the transcendence of Dasein and freedom are identical!" (p. 238). Freedom gives itself its inner possibility; a being that is free is in itself necessarily transcending. The identification closes §11b and completes the thesis Heidegger announced in §11a, that Kant's problem of transcendence was "the problem of freedom." The argument turns on the German: *Umwillen* ("for the sake of") contains *Willen* ("will"). §11c must now explain how freedom, world, and the for-the-sake-of hang together, and freedom will prove to be the origin of ground.

###### c) Freedom and World

"But now the riddles begin anew!" What is the inner connection between Dasein's freedom, being-in-the-world, and the primary character of world, the for-the-sake-of? The identity of transcendence and freedom asserted at the end of §11b must now be explained, and the subsection proceeds by clarifying the for-the-sake-of first.

In the formulation so far, the for-the-sake-of seems only a formal determination: Dasein exists for the sake of something, and it now seems necessary to fix in content what Dasein exists for the sake of. What is the final end for the sake of which the human being exists? This would seem to be the decisive question, but only apparently. The question is ambiguous. It seems to go directly at the whole, but it is premature. It presupposes that it can somehow be decided objectively, whereas in the end it lies in the sense of the question itself that only the questioner, each for himself, can pose and decide it in the genuine sense. If this is so, it must be shown why. The metaphysics of Dasein must make clear why Dasein, in accordance with the essence of its being, must itself take over the question and answer about its final end, and why the search for an objective answer is in itself a misunderstanding, indeed the misunderstanding, of the human essence as such. Heidegger refuses to supply a content for the for-the-sake-of, a highest good or final purpose, and he gives a reason. The question "what is man for?" cannot be answered from outside, because the being it concerns is one whose being is its own to take over. This is the ontological ground for the refusal, in §10, of prophecy and worldview.

Unlike truth about what is present at hand, truth about what exists is truth for what exists. This truth consists only in being-true as existing. Questioning must be understood accordingly, not as inquiring about something but as questioning for someone, in which it is already asked how matters stand with the questioner. The distinction extends §9a. Truth about present things is uncovering; truth about existence is a mode of existing, disclosedness of oneself, which cannot be delivered as information.

Before hastily trying to answer the question of the final end of the human being with an answer that is in the end none at all, and before trying to fill the formal for-the-sake-of with content, the for-the-sake-of itself must be pursued further, to avoid an inadequate construction of it as the constitutive of worldhood. The warning recalls the "construction" of §2, where the ideal of divine knowledge was projected as the measure of finite knowledge. An objective final end would be another such construction.

The existence of Dasein is determined by the for-the-sake-of: what distinguishes Dasein is that, in its being, this being is concerned in a specific way with its being itself. The being and ability-to-be of Dasein are what it exists for the sake of. One will object at once that this gives the for-the-sake-of a content and fixes the final end of Dasein: it is Dasein itself. And this determination is extremely one-sided; it is extreme egoism, "the most blatant megalomania," to claim that all beings, nature and culture and whatever else there is, are present only for the individual human being and his egoistic ends. If this were the sense of the ontological thesis, it would be madness, and one would not see why an analysis of Dasein was needed to assert this palpable nonsense. On the other hand, Kant said that the human being exists as an end in itself. The objection is the one Heidegger's formula from *Being and Time*, that Dasein is the being for which its own being is at issue, had provoked. The reference to Kant's formula of humanity shows that the thesis is not eccentric.

Matters are not so simple when a metaphysics of Dasein asserts that it belongs to the essence of Dasein that its own being is at issue for it. Then the correction that points out how many people sacrifice themselves for others and are absorbed in friendship and community is entirely superfluous, because it wants to correct something it cannot correct. The thesis is not an ontic claim that all existing human beings in fact use, or should use, everything around them for their particular egoistic ends. The ontological thesis, that it belongs to the essence of Dasein that its own being stands in its for-the-sake-of, does not exclude that a human being is in fact concerned with the being of another; on the contrary, it states the metaphysical ground of the possibility that anything like Dasein can be with others, for them, and through them. If this thesis stands at the beginning of an ontological analytic of Dasein and in immediate connection with the thesis of transcendence, it is a simple requirement of the most primitive method to ask at least whether an ontic, worldview thesis preaching so-called individualistic egoism is or could be expressed in it. The argument turns on the distinction between existential and existentiell from §11a. Only a being for which its own being matters can care about another's being, because caring about another is a way of being that one takes over as one's own.

What is at issue in this thesis is not an existentiell, ethical egoism but the ontological-metaphysical characterization of the "egoity" of Dasein as such. Only because Dasein is primarily determined by egoity can it factically exist for another Dasein and with it as a Thou. The Thou is not an ontic duplicate of a factical I; nor can a Thou exist as such and be itself as Thou for another I unless it is Dasein at all, grounded in egoity. The egoity belonging to Dasein's transcendence is the metaphysical condition of the possibility that a Thou can exist and an I–Thou relation can be. The Thou too is most immediately Thou when it is not simply another I but "you yourself are." This selfhood is its freedom, and this is identical with the egoity on the basis of which Dasein can be egoistic or altruistic at all. "Egoity" (*Egoität*) is a technical term for the neutral structure of selfhood, prior to the distinction of I and Thou. Heidegger's argument against the dialogical philosophy of Buber and Ebner is that the Thou presupposes the same selfhood as the I; otherwise the other would be a mere object or a copy of me.

That we can make the I–Thou relation a problem at all shows that we transcend the factical I and Thou and grasp the relation as a relation of Dasein as such, in its metaphysical neutrality and egoity, though mostly without suspecting these self-evident presuppositions. No analysis of possible I–Thou relations, however varied and interesting, can solve the metaphysical problem of Dasein, because it cannot even pose it; from its first step it presupposes the whole analytic of Dasein in some form and constantly makes use of it. Today the I–Thou problem is of worldview interest for quite different motives, sociological, theological, political, biological, ethical, which give the relation special significance, but the philosophical problem is thereby covered up. The argument is transcendental: to thematize the I–Thou relation is already to understand the being of both terms, so the relation cannot be the foundation. The list of motives places dialogical thought among the worldview interests the analytic sets aside.

A new characteristic difficulty thus appears in the problem of subjectivity and in every ontology of Dasein. The first concerned the illegitimate cutting off of the self-enclosed subject from all objects, the mistaken opinion that starting from an initially worldless subject is the most presuppositionless. The present difficulty concerns the opinion that starting from a subject, even an ultimately transcending one, is individualistic, egoistic subjectivism, and that the more radically an ontology of Dasein is made a problem and task, the more extreme the individualism must be; or rather, the more obtrusive the appearance of such individualism and the more difficult to grasp and hold fast to the ontological intention. The two difficulties are symmetrical. The capsule model isolates the subject from things; the individualist reading isolates it from others. Both mistake the ontological structure for an ontic self-enclosure.

When we say that Dasein is essentially in each case mine, and the task is to determine Dasein ontologically with regard to this character, it does not mean that the essence of myself as this factical individual, or of any other, should be investigated. The object of the question is not my individual essence but the essence of mineness and selfhood as such. Likewise, when the "I" is the object of ontological interpretation, it is not the individual I-ness of myself but I-ness in metaphysical neutrality, which is called egoity. Even so there is a danger of misunderstanding: must not Thou-ness become a theme in the same way, and be taken together equiprimordially with I-ness? Certainly this is a possible problem. But I-ness as the counter-phenomenon to Thou-ness is still not metaphysical egoity. The title "I" always pushes toward isolating myself, in the sense of a corresponding cutting off from the Thou, whereas egoity does not mean the factical I as distinct from the Thou but the I-ness that underlies the Thou too and prevents taking the Thou factically as an alter ego. Why is a Thou not simply a second I? Because being-I as distinct from being-Thou does not touch the essence of Dasein; a Thou is one only as itself, and the "I" likewise. That is why Heidegger usually uses "selfhood" (*Selbstheit*) for metaphysical I-ness, egoity: "self" can be said equally of I and Thou, "I myself," "you yourself," but not "you-I." The grammatical test is neat. "Self" is common to the first and second person, so it names the structure prior to their distinction. This is why *Being and Time* speaks of the self rather than the ego.

Pure selfhood, taken as the metaphysical neutrality of Dasein, is at the same time the expression for the metaphysical isolation of Dasein in ontology, which must never be confused with an egoistic-solipsistic exaggeration of one's own individuality. How the latter nonetheless has a function in the engagement was indicated in the eleventh thesis of §10. Because selfhood is the basic character of existence, and existing always means an ability-to-be, one of its possibilities must serve to work out ontological selfhood concretely; hence the path of an extreme construction was chosen (*Being and Time* §64). Heidegger was never for a moment of the opinion that this problematic and its inner task would be quickly grasped today or must be grasped by the crowd. What Hegel wrote in the preface to the *Phenomenology of Spirit* holds today even more: "It is not pleasant to observe that ignorance, and a crudeness without form or taste that is incapable of holding its thought to a single abstract proposition, still less to the connection of several, assures us that it is now freedom and tolerance of thought, now genius." The reference to §64 of *Being and Time*, "Care and Selfhood," confirms that authentic resoluteness was the "extreme construction" through which selfhood was made concrete, not a moral ideal. The Hegel quotation is aimed at the reviewers.

Being for the sake of itself is an essential determination of the being of the being we call Dasein. This constitution, now called briefly the for-the-sake-of, provides the inner possibility for this being to be itself, for selfhood to belong to its being. To be in the manner of a self means to be, in one's being, fundamentally toward oneself. This being-toward-oneself constitutes the being of Dasein and is not a subsequent capacity to observe oneself in addition to existing. Being-toward-oneself is existing itself, but it must be taken in its original metaphysical breadth and not fixed on some comportment or capacity, still less on a mode of grasping, knowing-about, apperception. Being-toward-oneself as being-a-self is the presupposition for the various possibilities of ontic comportment toward oneself. The contrast with §5c is exact. There apperception grew out of the monad's self-openness; here Heidegger insists that self-relation is not a cognitive act but a way of being, prior to any self-knowledge.

Only because this being is determined by selfhood in its metaphysical essence can it, as factical, explicitly choose itself as a self; but in this "can" lies that it can also forgo the choice. What does this possibility, grounded in selfhood, of explicitly choosing oneself or forgoing this choice mean? What is essentially chosen along with it in this explicit self-choosing? The possibility of forgoing the choice is inauthenticity in *Being and Time*'s vocabulary. Heidegger now asks what is co-chosen, which leads to the claim that choosing oneself is choosing being-with.

Here lies the origin of "possibility" as such: only from freedom, only a free being, can understand being as transcending, and must, in order to exist as such, that is, to be "among" and "with" beings. The claim that possibility originates in freedom answers the question about the origin of the modal concepts, which §4 left with Leibniz's pure possibles in God's understanding. Possibility is first Dasein's ability-to-be, and possibility in general is understood from there.

These metaphysical, ontological theses are constantly exposed to being taken ontically-existentielly. A main reason is that the metaphysical horizon of the problem is not held fast, and this is a particular danger at the present stage. It was said that Dasein chooses itself. Unawares one substitutes for "Dasein" the common concept of the isolated, egoistic subject and interprets Dasein's self-choosing as a solipsistic-egoistic withdrawal into oneself, whereas in the genuine metaphysical sense the reverse is the case. Dasein, and only it as Dasein, is to choose itself (Dasein). It has been pointed out repeatedly, to the point of tedium, that this being as Dasein is always already with others and always already with beings not of Dasein's character. In transcending, Dasein transcends every being: itself, every being of its kind (co-Dasein), and beings not of Dasein's character. In choosing itself, Dasein genuinely chooses precisely its being-with others and its being with beings not of Dasein's character. The self is not a private interior but a way of being-in-the-world. Choosing oneself is therefore choosing the whole of one's relations, to others and to things.

Explicitly choosing oneself essentially involves full self-engagement, not toward where one is not yet but toward where and how, as Dasein, one always already is insofar as one exists. How far this succeeds factically is not a question for metaphysics but a question and matter for the individual. Only because Dasein, on the basis of its selfhood, can explicitly choose itself can it commit itself for the other, and only because Dasein, in being toward itself, can understand anything like "self" at all can it listen to a "you yourself." Only because Dasein, constituted by the for-the-sake-of, exists in selfhood is anything like human community possible. These are primary existential-ontological essential theses, not ethical theses about the ranking of egoism and altruism. The existential-ontological phenomenon of genuine self-choice brings out most radically the metaphysical selfhood of Dasein, which means transcendence as the transcending of one's own being, of beings as being-with others, and of beings in the sense of nature and useful things. Methodologically this again indicates extreme existential-ontological construction. Self-choice is not a move into a new place but a taking over of where one already is. The ground of community is selfhood, not the reverse; this answers the collectivist and communitarian critics, but equally denies that the analytic takes sides in ethics.

In a note Heidegger adds that Kierkegaard speaks much of choosing oneself and of the individual, and if the point were to say again what Kierkegaard said, it would be not only superfluous but would necessarily fall essentially behind Kierkegaard's intention. His intention is not Heidegger's but fundamentally different, which does not prevent but obliges one to learn from him what he gives. Kierkegaard never penetrated into the dimension of this problematic at all, because it was not essential for him, and his writing had a quite different basic intention requiring other means and paths. Heidegger distances himself from the existentialist reading at its source. Kierkegaard's "choice of oneself" (from *Either/Or*) is religious-existentiell; Heidegger's is ontological, and to confuse them misses both.
