The thesis that the for-the-sake-of-itself belongs to the essence of Dasein is ontological; it states something about the essential constitution of Dasein in its metaphysical neutrality. Dasein is for the sake of itself, and in this for-the-sake-of lies the ground of the possibility of an existentiell, egoistic or unegoistic "for my sake," but equally originally of a "for his sake" and of every ontic "for what reason." As constitutive of the selfhood of Dasein, the for-the-sake-of has this universal scope; it is that toward which Dasein, as transcending, transcends. Every particular purpose, mine or another's, is a specification of the for-the-sake-of. This is the fourth concept of ground from the Second Main Part's opening, the motive, now shown to be rooted in the structure of world.

In the context of the question of transcendence, starting from the problem of world through the realm of ideas and the *epekeina tēs ousias*, the for-the-sake-of was found as the basic character of world. It must be grasped as the metaphysical structure of Dasein, not with regard to the particular egoistic purposes of a factically existing individual. This for-the-sake-of, as the metaphysical constitution and basic structure of world, must be pursued more sharply, so as to make being-in-the-world as transcendence understood. The summary keeps the ontological level in view before the decisive step.

The for-the-sake-of is what it is in and for a will. This does not mean the existentiell-ontic act of will, but again the metaphysical essence, the inner possibility of will: freedom. In freedom such a for-the-sake-of has always already sprung up. Holding the for-the-sake-of before itself lies in the essence of freedom. Something like a for-the-sake-of is not present somewhere, with freedom merely relating to it; freedom itself is the origin of the for-the-sake-of, but again not as if there were first freedom and then also a for-the-sake-of: the for-the-sake-of is at one with freedom. It is inessential how far a being determined as free is factically free, or how far it knows about its freedom; nothing is said about how far it is free or only latently free, captivated or caught up by others or by beings not of Dasein's character. "Only a free being can be unfree" (p. 247). Freedom and the for-the-sake-of are co-original, so freedom is not the choice among given ends but the opening of the dimension of ends as such. The final sentence prevents an empirical objection: unfreedom, captivation, and constraint are modes of freedom, not counterexamples.

Here too the interpretation of freedom must be turned out of the traditional perspective, whose aim is primarily to emphasize, against a compulsory mechanical sequence, the beginning-of-itself, *sua sponte*, spontaneity. But this "of itself" remains undetermined without selfhood, which means one must take transcendence back into freedom, indeed seek the basic essence of transcendence in freedom itself. Kant's cosmological freedom, the power to begin a causal series of oneself, presupposes a "self" it does not clarify. Heidegger reverses the order: selfhood, and thus transcendence, is what makes spontaneity intelligible.

In other words, world, primarily characterized by the for-the-sake-of, is the original whole of what Dasein, as free, gives itself to understand. Freedom gives itself to understand; it is the primal understanding, the primal projection, of what it itself makes possible. In projecting the for-the-sake-of as such, Dasein gives itself the original binding. Freedom makes Dasein, in the ground of its essence, binding for itself, more precisely, gives itself the possibility of binding. The whole of the binding lying in the for-the-sake-of is world. According to this binding, Dasein binds itself to an ability-to-be toward itself as an ability-to-be-with others in an ability-to-be with what is present at hand. "Selfhood is free obligation for and toward oneself" (p. 247). The concept of freedom now includes law. Freedom is not the absence of binding but the giving of binding, which is Kant's autonomy transposed from the moral law to world. World is the whole of what binds Dasein, which is why it is not a sum of things but the space of what matters. The three abilities-to-be (toward self, with others, among things) are the three dimensions of transcendence in the preceding paragraph.

As free, Dasein is world-projection. This projecting is projected only in such a way that Dasein holds itself in it, such that this free hold binds, placing Dasein, in all dimensions of its transcendence, in a possible leeway of choice. Freedom holds this binding over against itself. World is held against freedom within freedom itself. "World is the free counter-hold (*Widerhalt*) of the for-the-sake-of of Dasein" (p. 248). Being-in-the-world is therefore nothing other than freedom, freedom no longer taken as spontaneity but in the indicated constitution of its metaphysical essence (which is far from fully determined). *Widerhalt* is a coinage: what freedom holds over against itself, as resistance and support. World is not given to freedom from outside, yet it is not arbitrary either; it is freedom's own counterpart, the leeway (*Spielraum*) within which choices are possible. This is the Heideggerian form of the Kantian thought that the will gives itself the law it obeys.

As transcendence, the free counter-hold of the for-the-sake-of has, as indicated, the character of leaping over the beings that are factically and actually there. World, as the whole of the essential inner possibilities of Dasein as transcending, surpasses everything actual. Actual beings, whenever and however encountered, and precisely in their being-in-themselves, reveal themselves only as a restriction, as a possible realization of the possible, as the insufficient in relation to an excess of possibilities within which Dasein, as free projection, always already holds itself. The priority of the possible over the actual reverses Leibniz's God, who chooses the best actual from the possible. For Dasein, the actual is always less than the possible it has projected. The echo of §5c is deliberate: there the finite monad was "so much not," passive against the universe it represented only in part.

Dasein is excessive in itself, determined by a primary insatiability (*Ungenügsamkeit*) with all beings, both metaphysically as such and existentially in its factical individuation. This primary insatiability can show itself in a particular ontic, existentiell comportment; only because of it is being-at-ease-with, existentiell self-satisfaction or dissatisfaction, possible; this dissatisfaction must not be confused with insatiability in the metaphysical sense. The essence of freedom, which surpasses everything factical and actual, its surpassing character, shows itself precisely in despair, in which one's own unfreedom closes over Dasein sunk into itself. Even this complete factical unfreedom is an elementary testimony to transcendence, for despair consists precisely in the despairing person's seeing the impossibility of the possible. He testifies once more to the possible by despairing of it. The analysis of despair is Kierkegaardian in material (*The Sickness unto Death*) but ontological in intention. Despair is possible only for a being that has possibilities, so even the loss of freedom testifies to freedom. The distinction between metaphysical insatiability and existentiell dissatisfaction repeats the pattern of the egoity argument.

The peculiar surpassing of the factically existent that lies in world as such, and so in transcendence and freedom, corresponds to the *epekeina*. World itself is surpassing (*übertrifftig*); a being of Dasein's character is distinguished by a "swinging over" (*Überschwung*); world is the free, surpassing counter-hold of the for-the-sake-of. *Überschwung* ("swinging over," "surge") names the dynamic of transcendence. Heidegger connects it with Plato's "beyond being": the good was *epekeina* because it surpassed the ideas, and world surpasses beings.

Only insofar as Dasein, in its metaphysical essence, freely swings over by holding the for-the-sake-of-itself before itself does it become, as what swings over toward the possible, metaphysically the occasion for beings to manifest themselves as beings. A being of Dasein's essence must have opened itself as freedom, that is, world must be held against it in swinging over, a being must be constituted as being-in-the-world, as transcending, if this being and beings as such are to become manifest. Seen metaphysically from being-in-the-world, Dasein as factically existing is nothing other than "the existing possibility of the world-entry of beings" (p. 249). Only when, in the totality of beings, beings become more fully beings in the existence of Dasein, that is, when temporality temporalizes itself, is there an hour and day of the world-entry of beings. World-entry in turn gives the possibility of the disclosability of beings. "World-entry" (*Welteingang*) is the course's most striking metaontological coinage. With Dasein, beings enter world, that is, become manifest. The phrase "beings become more fully beings" (*seiender*) in Dasein's existence is the metontological thesis of the appendix: Dasein's existence is an event in beings as a whole. The "hour and day" ties this event to temporality, the theme of §12.

Before clarifying transcendence in its inner possibility so as to see from there the rootedness of the essence of ground in it, transcendence is made more graspable by a brief characterization of world-entry. The inner possibility is announced for §12.

Insofar as transcendence could be made visible, it must have become clear that world does not mean beings, neither individual objects nor the totality of objects standing over against a subject. Where one wants, particularly in the realist faction, to express transcendence as a subject–object relation, one often likes to say that the subject always already presupposes the "world," meaning the objects that are. First, this thesis is far from even seeing the genuine phenomenon of transcendence, not to mention more. The target is the realist argument that every knower presupposes the existence of the external world, a variant of Dilthey's and Scheler's arguments from resistance.

What does it mean that the subject "presupposes" objects that are, presupposes that they are? No intelligible sense can be attached to it, quite apart from the fact that we nowhere encounter such presupposing. Does it mean that "we" assume in advance that objects are? By agreement? By what right do we make this assumption, and how would we even come to it? Only on the presupposition of the isolated subject. And do beings show themselves to us as something we let be present, as it were, on request? There is no trace of such a presupposition anywhere. All that is apt in talk of presupposing the "world," the objects, is that factically existing Dasein always already encounters what is present at hand, has already encountered beings in advance. But these beings, and their already being, do not rest on a presupposition, on a metaphysical fraternization: let us presuppose that there are beings, and then try to exist in their midst. Precisely this encountering of what is present speaks most sharply against our having presupposed presence. It means, on the contrary, that as existing we have no need first to presuppose objects. The irony of "metaphysical fraternization" shows the absurdity of treating the being of beings as a postulate. The encounter with beings is the refutation of the need to presuppose them.

Yet beings (present things) could never be encountered if they did not have the opportunity to enter a world. Hence Heidegger speaks of the possible and occasional world-entry of beings. When and how is this possibility realized? World-entry is not a process in present things, in the sense that they change and push forward on the basis of this change. World-entry of what is present at hand is "something" that happens with it. World-entry has the character of happening, of history. World-entry happens when transcendence happens, that is, when historical Dasein exists; only then is a being-in-the-world of Dasein existent. And only when this is existent has what is present at hand already entered world, become intraworldly. Only Dasein as existing gives the occasion for world-entry. World-entry is not a change in beings but an event that "happens with" them, a historical event in the sense of *Geschichte*, the happening that constitutes history. History begins with Dasein because world-entry does.

Intraworldliness is therefore not a present property of what is present at hand in itself. What is present at hand is the being that and what it is even if it does not become intraworldly, even if world-entry does not happen with it and there is no occasion for it. Intraworldliness does not belong to the essence of what is present at hand as such; it is only the transcendental condition, in an original sense, of the possibility that what is present can manifest itself in its in-itself, that is, of existing Dasein's experiencing and grasping it in its in-itself. World-entry and its happening are the presupposition not for the present thing first becoming present and coming into what reveals itself to us as its presence, but only for its manifesting itself precisely in its not needing world-entry for its own being. The argument dissolves the realism dispute from within. Beings do not need world to be; they need it to be manifest, and to be manifest precisely as not needing it. This is the first of §10's three theses, beings are what they are even if Dasein does not exist, now explained.

Transcending Dasein, as being-in-the-world, factically gives beings the occasion of world-entry, and this giving of occasion on the part of Dasein consists in nothing other than transcending. "Giving occasion" is deliberately modest. Dasein does not create beings or their being-in-themselves but lets them enter the open.

If intraworldliness is not a property of intraworldly present things as present, where does it belong and how is it? Evidently it belongs to world and is only with it; it happens only insofar as being-in-the-world happens. There is world only insofar as Dasein exists. Is world then something "subjective"? Indeed! Only one must not again substitute a vulgar, subjectivist concept of "subject," but see that being-in-the-world, which as existent gives present things world-entry, transforms the concept of subjectivity and the subjective from the ground up. The concession that world is "subjective" is bold, but the subject has been redefined as transcendence, so world is subjective in the sense of belonging to the being of Dasein, not in the sense of being in consciousness.

When Dasein exists, world-entry has at once already happened, in such a way that at bottom nothing happens to the present thing entering. It remains so utterly untouched that, precisely on the basis of world-entry, it can in turn concern, strike, and move existing Dasein. But if nothing happens to what enters in the happening of world-entry, is world itself nothing? Indeed: world is nothing, if "nothing" means not a being in the sense of the present at hand, and more, nothing in the sense of not a being, nothing of what Dasein as such transcends; but it also transcends itself. World is a nothing, no being, and yet something; nothing that is, but being. So world is not a nothing in the sense of the *nihil negativum*. What kind of *nihil* is world, and at bottom, then, being-in-the-world itself? "World is nothing" is the thesis that will be developed in *What Is Metaphysics?* (1929) as the nothing that nothings. Here it follows from the ontological difference: world is not a being, and "nothing" names what is not a being yet is not simply null. The scholastic *nihil negativum*, the self-contradictory nothing, is excluded; §12's title names the alternative, the *nihil originarium*.

This leads to the question of the inner possibility of transcendence itself, of being-in-the-world as the swinging-over toward the surpassing counter-hold, in which Dasein gives itself to understand itself in its metaphysical essence, so as to bind itself originally as freedom in this self-understanding. "The inner possibility of transcendence, I assert, is time as original temporality!" (p. 252). The claim links §11 and §12: world as the free counter-hold of the for-the-sake-of is possible only temporally, and the "nothing" of world will prove to be the nothing of ecstatic temporality.

##### §12. Transcendence and Temporality (*nihil originarium*)

As free, Dasein projects itself upon the for-the-sake-of-itself as the whole of the essential possibilities of its ability-to-be. Holding this for-the-sake-of-itself before itself and existing in this holding, this being in its way uses itself for itself. By what was said, this using-oneself-for-oneself cannot mean solipsistic self-interest. Only because Dasein as such, as free, uses itself for itself does it, as factical, essentially stand before the choice of how, in the ontic-existentiell sense, it uses itself for others and for itself. Or, which is only another possibility of freedom as unfreedom, Dasein behaves undecidedly and lets things take their course depending on circumstances. That we are factically and existentielly only rarely free speaks not against the metaphysical essence of Dasein but for it. It is a false opinion to think one grasps freedom most purely in its essence by isolating it as free-floating absolute arbitrariness. On the contrary, freedom must be conceived in its finitude, and one must see that exhibiting its boundness does not impair freedom or curtail its essence. The opening recapitulates §11c and adds the thesis of finite freedom. Freedom is not the power to do anything whatever; it is bound by the world it projects. Rare actual freedom is evidence for essential freedom, as despair was.

What has been characterized as freedom, being-in-the-world, transcendence, must now be taken as it was shown. None of it is an apparatus hidden in an isolated subject and its interior; freedom itself transcends; in it the passage beyond beings takes place and has always already taken place, and we always encounter these beings as being-in-themselves in such a way that we come back to them in freedom, from the origin and within it. All ontic comportment toward beings, of Dasein's kind or not, does not first transcend when it sets out intentionally to relate itself to objects; the intentional relation is only the particular factical way of appropriating what has already been leapt over, that is, disclosed, on the basis of transcendence. "Coming back" (*zurückkommen*) to beings from world is the counter-movement of transcendence. Intentionality is the return from the surpassed to particular beings, which §9b stated in terms of founding and which is here given its dynamic form.

One must learn to see, regardless of entrenched directions of questioning, how Dasein, on the basis of its metaphysical constitution, of being-in-the-world, is always already beyond all beings in possibility, and in this being-beyond does not run into absolute nothing; on the contrary, precisely in this being-beyond it holds binding as world before itself, and only in this counter-hold can and must it hold onto beings. With regard to this basic phenomenon of transcendence, temporality must now be grasped. The warning against "absolute nothing" anticipates the *nihil originarium*: what lies beyond beings is not an empty void but world, which binds.

What is temporality itself, and how can it be brought closer? One can speculate about time in many ways, begin anywhere and try to analyze and interpret it. What is at issue here is not the usual isolated philosophical speculation about time, from which one can always reach other philosophical problems somehow. Time, as shown, stands in an admittedly still obscure relation to the understanding of being as such. It therefore claims a central systematic function in metaphysics as such, and the interpretation of time must be guided primarily from there. The path to it is not simple. The one Heidegger took is not the only one, but each is long and full of obstacles. For present purposes he chooses another procedure: to point more directly and dogmatically to the essence of time, which means switching off the vulgar way of questioning about time. "Dogmatically" means without the long preparation of *Being and Time*, Division One. The course presents results, and the commentary should read them as compressed statements of the analyses in *Being and Time* §§65–69 and GA 24 §§19–21.

Heidegger sets out the main lines of the vulgar conception of time. First, time is itself something present somewhere and somehow, in motion, flowing away, as we say, passing. This first feature, time as a present flowing thing, is Aristotle's "number of motion" reified.

Second, this time, as what passes (in a way the exemplar of transience as such), is something "in the soul," in the subject, inside consciousness, so an inner consciousness is needed to get hold of time. The possibilities of conceiving and interpreting time therefore depend essentially on the particular conception of soul, subject, consciousness, Dasein. The second feature, time in the soul, is Aristotle's and Augustine's, and it ties the theory of time to the theory of the subject, which is why a new concept of Dasein must bring a new concept of time.

Third, time is something that passes away in the soul, yet it does not really belong to the center of the soul. Time has always been seen together with space; what we experience with the senses is in space, spatial, and the same holds for time: time belongs to sensibility (also in the phenomenological conception of Husserl and Scheler). However this is understood, it remains distinct from spirit and reason, which themselves are not temporal but extratemporal. (In Kant, it can be said without exaggeration, spontaneity as freedom stands directly beside time.) The third feature is the one Heidegger's Kant interpretation targets: time assigned to sensibility, reason and freedom exempt from it. His thesis will be that freedom itself is temporal.

Fourth, since Plato time has been distinguished from eternity, which is thought more or less theologically. The temporal then becomes the earthly as opposed to the heavenly. From here time takes on a certain worldview character, which has contributed to the fact that the phenomenon of time has never really been seen in its metaphysical significance. The contrast of time and eternity carried the valuation of the temporal as lower, which blocked its metaphysical role, and §2's analysis of the *nunc stans* showed the effect on the theory of knowledge.

These features, familiar to the vulgar and the philosophical understanding alike, cannot be simply arbitrary, freely invented. The essence of time must make such conceptions possible, indeed suggest them. Nevertheless, none of them reaches the metaphysical essence of time. The principle is the same as with the vulgar formula of the principle of reason: vulgar conceptions have their "hidden truth" and must be explained, not dismissed.

If one makes things easy and simply construes what the fundamental-ontological analytic exhibits about time from these vulgar perspectives, instead of first appropriating it from itself and its own intention, everything falls into confusion. Heidegger fixes the main characters of the metaphysical essence of time positively in five points, which do not simply correspond to the four just named. The warning is against reading *Being and Time*'s temporality as a psychological theory of lived time.

First, the ecstatic character belongs to the essence of time. Second, together with this ecstatic constitution, time has a horizonal character. Third, time neither passes nor stands; it temporalizes itself (*zeitigt sich*); temporalizing is the primal phenomenon of "movement." Fourth, time is not relative to sensibility but more original than it, and than spirit and reason, where the concept of reason must be the only one we know, that of finite reason. Fifth, methodologically: because time constitutes the metaphysical connection of Dasein, it becomes ungraspable precisely when Dasein is posited in some theoretical thematization, as a psychic whole, a knowing-willing subject, self-consciousness, or a unity of body, soul, and spirit. The analytic of Dasein must choose as its guiding horizon the one that remains guiding for Dasein's being-toward-itself in its being-with others and its relation to beings not of Dasein's kind, in factical existing, before, apart from, and despite all theory. The five points answer the four vulgar features. The fourth point, that time is more original than reason, is the thesis of *Kant and the Problem of Metaphysics*. The fifth, the methodological one, restates the priority of existence over theory. "Temporalizes itself" (*zeitigt sich*) is Heidegger's verb for the way time is: not a thing that is, but a happening that brings itself about.

The classical literature on the problem of time is: Aristotle, *Physics* IV, 10–14; Plotinus, *Enneads* III, 7; Augustine, *Confessions* XI; Kant, *Critique of Pure Reason* (Transcendental Aesthetic, Deduction and Schematism, Analytic of Principles, Antinomies); Hegel, *Encyclopedia* (with a preliminary stage in the *Jena Logic*) and *Phenomenology of Spirit*; Bergson, all writings; Husserl, only brief remarks in *Ideas* I; compare O. Becker's contributions to the phenomenological foundation of geometry (1923); and now Husserl's *Lectures on the Phenomenology of Inner Time-Consciousness* themselves, appearing in the *Jahrbuch* vol. IX. The investigations of Aristotle and Augustine are the essential and authoritative ones for the later tradition; Kant then, more unconsciously than quite clearly, pushed the problem furthest into the dimension of the genuinely philosophical problematic. The Husserl lectures appeared in 1928 under Heidegger's own editorship, as the editorial note records, which gives his critical remarks below particular weight.

The discussion is restricted to the most important points concerning the first two characters. Only ecstasis and horizon are treated; temporalizing and the relation to reason are left to the reader of *Being and Time* and the Kant book.

On the first: time has an ecstatic character. To make this intelligible requires a brief reminder of the vulgar conception of time, more precisely of our ordinary comportment toward time. That we speak here of a comportment toward time already indicates that it gives itself as something among many others to which we relate daily (like space). The index of how we relate to time is the fact that we usually carry a watch, a time-measurer. We measure time not from mere curiosity, determining how much time it is, but in such a way that we reckon with it. Reckoning with it means here not primarily determining it numerically but taking it into account, using time, using it rightly, not losing time. Everyone knows time, knows what he means when he speaks of time, and yet seldom has a concept of time. This preconceptual acquaintance with time was not acquired by taking over something like time from others and placing it in ourselves. "We do not somehow come to know time!" What we learn is only the manner of measuring time, the factically concrete ways in which we best deal practically with time. But each Dasein discloses time in itself, and nonetheless time long remains something alien. "Only rarely do we take possession of the time that possesses us in a metaphysical sense, only rarely do we become master of this power that we ourselves are, only rarely do we exist freely" (pp. 257–58). The starting point is the watch, as in *Being and Time* §81 and GA 24 §19. The point that we never learn what time is echoes Augustine's "if no one asks me, I know." The last sentence joins time and freedom: to own one's time is to exist freely.

However much we busy ourselves with time daily, more or less consciously or even pedantically, however often we speak of it, name and address it, it remains indifferent and distant to us. This is partly why we are blind to its ownmost essence, and why, when we want to grasp time, we take it like everything first accessible to us, like things. The reification of time follows from the everyday understanding of being as presence-at-hand.

What is essential is to keep the view free for the total peculiar essence of what discloses itself as time. Only one of the phenomena can be set before our eyes here, the one that brings us most immediately to grasp the ecstatic character of time, and it should be interpreted at least relatively concretely (compare GA 24, Part II, and *Being and Time*, Part I). The phenomenon chosen is the expressed "now," "then," "back then."

We measure time with numbers and stretches, quantities: from now until then, from now until back then, from then until then. We name time itself with "now," "then," "back then." It is essential to see how these expressions speak of time. When I say "now" quite unreflectively and immediately, it always means: now the door slams, now I move on to the analysis of the structure of time. By "now" we do not really mean it itself thematically; we do not dwell on it, meaning that this now has such and such properties, follows a just-now, and is followed by a right-away; rather, "now the door slams": we mean the door and that it slams now. We do not mean that the now and the door's slamming coincide, but this peculiar relation between the two. And this relation does not arise because both are simultaneous; the relation belongs to the now as such, except that what happens in this now is arbitrary, not prescribed in the now. When we say "now," we are not thematically directed at the now as an isolated now-thing; the now itself guides and pushes us on to what is happening in it. This pointing-on function belongs essentially to the now. The now is essentially a "now, when this and that," a "now, where." Likewise the other expressions of time: "then" is always, mostly unexpressed, a "then, when," and "back then" always a "back then, as." The structure is datability. Every now is a now-when, referred to something happening, which is what *Being and Time* §79 calls the datability of world-time. The slamming door, a sound in the lecture hall, makes the point that the "now" is never experienced as a bare instant.

These time-namings have a pointing-on character: pointing on to what is now, then, back then. But looked at more closely, the phenomenal situation is the reverse. We are not directed toward the nows in order then to be pointed on to the things and events "in" time; we are occupied with things, related to them, and then assign to them these time-determinations, more or less fixed in numbers. The door slams, now: now the door slams. But does the now not lie before everything, unthematically? However that may be, in saying "now" I do not make the now a thematic object; I do not address it as I do the door or the blackboard. The correction reverses the direction of reference: we are with things and then date them. The unanswered question, whether the now lies unthematically before everything, is the lead into the next step.

This indicates that we do not come upon now, then, and back then as we do upon present things, yet we say "now" with a peculiar immediacy. We do not have a so-called series of nows spread out before us from which we pick out, snatch, and name one. We are not specifically directed at time, and yet we have it immediately "at hand," even when we do not expressly say now or back then. Again the most elementary example is the use of a watch, and one must not reflect on a conscious use. It belongs to the essence of using a watch that when I read the time I do not ask "what time is it?" but, for example, "how much time do I still have?" I do not state the now but read the time directly, saying "now" latently. Where do we get what we mean and understand, though unthematically, in saying now, back then, then? "How much time do I still have?" shows that clock time is read from the future, from what remains to be done.

Consider "then." We never use "then" in isolation but, for example, in arranging and organizing: then this should happen, then that may follow; or in planning deliberation: then perhaps this will occur and accordingly that must be done. Or when we ask in such deliberation, "what then?", something toward which we are related in planning is also meant, only it remains undetermined and indeterminable. We say "then" within our being related to what we relate to in planning, deliberating, pro-viding; in general, we say "then" out of a way of existing in which we await something coming, something to be done. Only in such awaiting (*Gewärtigen*) can I say "then." Awaiting, we say of ourselves then and then, without being directed specifically at these thens. We are directed at what will or should be then. These thens are nothing in themselves, such that awaiting would only be the way of grasping them, as perception is access to a material thing (the blackboard); awaiting itself, as awaiting, gives the then. The then is what awaiting expresses itself as, what lies in it. In awaiting, and only in it, the thens slumber, as it were. "Awaiting" (*Gewärtigen*) is the inauthentic future of *Being and Time* §68. The then is not an object that awaiting perceives but what awaiting expresses, which moves time from the objects to the way of existing.

But just as we do not find the then in the object that will or should be then, so we do not find it inside the soul as a psychic process or its property. Neither in objects nor in subjects (in the traditional sense), neither here nor there, but, as it were, "underway from subject to object!" We already know this underway: as the passage, transcendence. This indicates where, in the end, what we express as a time-character "is." The locating of time "underway" is the decisive connection: time is where transcendence is, neither inside nor outside.

The then arises from and in an awaiting and can be determined more or less unambiguously within limits; these possibilities are not essential now. What was set out for the then holds correspondingly for back then and now. "Back then" is always the expression of a retaining (*Behalten*) of what was formerly; it does not matter how far and how precisely we remember it; we may also have forgotten it, so "back then" is equally the expression of a forgetting. Correspondingly, "now" is the expression of being toward what is present, called having-present, or in general, presenting (*Gegenwärtigen*). The inclusion of forgetting as a mode of retaining is significant: the past is present in Dasein even when forgotten.

Returning to the then: it springs from awaiting as such and is neither a property of the object nor of the subject. But this has far from exhausted its essential character; something essential has been overlooked. The then that springs up in awaiting and can be expressed is always understood as "not yet now" (but just then). The then as such, whatever then I may choose, points back to a now; more precisely, it is understood from such a now, though unexpressed. Conversely, every back then is "no longer now," and as such, by its structure, a bridge to a now. This now is the now of the particular presenting or retaining, in which a "then" and "back then" is expressed. So a peculiar connection, still obscure in its essence, appears between back then, then, and now. In the vulgar understanding, we call the mere succession of nows (now, not yet now: right away; no longer now: just now) time. We also know in a way that time is not an accumulation of nows but a continuum, and not a rigid but a flowing one (the "flow of time"). However time so conceived may be determined, and whether this can succeed from this standpoint at all, it has become clear that then, back then, and now, in their unity, spring from awaiting, retaining, and presenting, which must evidently be unified themselves and among themselves. The now-sequence is derived from the unity of the three modes of existing.

If what we find as sprung from this is called time, then what is its origin and lets it spring forth must be called time even more so, in an original sense. "And time is then originally this awaiting, retaining, and presenting" (p. 262). The argument from origin: if the derivative is called time, the source is time in a more original sense. This is the step from world-time to original temporality.

In a note, Heidegger adds that Bergson was the first to work out the connection between a derived and an original time, but went too far in saying that derived time is space. Bergson thereby blocks his way to understanding derived time, since by not seeing derived time as springing forth he misjudges its essence. By remaining with derived time, he also fails to clarify original, genuine time in its essence. Nevertheless, Bergson's analyses of time belong to the most intense we have. It has become a commonplace that Bergson (like Dilthey) is vague and must be revised more exactly. But Bergson's "images" are the expression of the effort to grasp the phenomenon within the domain he has as his theme. The defect is not supposed vagueness (Bergson is perfectly clear in what he sees) but the far too narrow domain of the problem, which "more exact" revision would not remove either. "As is well known, there are exact follies in philosophy too." The note acknowledges Bergson's *durée* as the precursor of the distinction and rejects its spatialization of clock time. The defense of Bergson against the charge of vagueness is directed at the logicist critics, Russell among them.

Again: awaiting, retaining, and presenting are not merely the way of grasping then, back then, and now, the mode of consciousness of them, but themselves the origin. Awaiting is not a mode of consciousness of time but in an original and genuine sense time itself. The repetition hammers the point against the reading of temporality as time-consciousness, which the next paragraph addresses in Husserl.

Husserl, in the *Lectures on the Phenomenology of Inner Time-Consciousness* (from the Göttingen lecture of 1904–05) appearing in the *Jahrbuch* vol. IX, investigated the problem of time in a quite definite context, characteristic of the direction of his view. He speaks of "inner time-consciousness" and analyzes as forms of this consciousness above all memory, presentification, re-presentification, expectation. These phenomena were suggested to him by a quite different problematic, that of intentionalities as such, that is, by an analysis of consciousness as consciousness-of (though Aristotle already discusses memory and hope). In connection with interpreting perception, phantasy, image-consciousness, and memory, the problem of time also arises: memory is conceived as a certain knowing of the past. Time thus still remains a flow of nows, just-nows, and right-aways, to which a certain knowing corresponds. Compared with all previous interpretations, it is Husserl's merit to have seen these phenomena for the first time with the help of intentional structure; a glance at contemporary psychology or epistemology suffices to see what an essential step was taken. Nevertheless, as regards the problem of time, basically everything remains as it was, so much so that time is taken as something immanent, remaining something inner "in the subject"; hence the title "inner time-consciousness." Husserl's whole investigation is motivated by his seeing the primary and original consciousness of time in the knowing of a mere sensation datum; the investigation accordingly revolves throughout around the temporal course of a tone. These lectures are essential (apart from the concrete analyses of memory, perception, and so on) for working out intentionality more sharply beyond the *Logical Investigations*. "What Husserl still calls time-consciousness, consciousness of time, is precisely time itself in the original sense" (p. 264). As Husserl's editor, Heidegger gives a precise assessment: an advance in the description of intentionality, but time remains immanent, a flow of nows with a correlative consciousness, analyzed on the model of hearing a tone. His thesis inverts Husserl's: retention and protention are not consciousness of time but time itself.

Original time is deliberately called temporality, to express that time is not also present but that its essence is temporal. "Time 'is' not, but temporalizes itself" (p. 264). Every attempt to stretch it into some concept of being must therefore fail. To try to master it with the help of dialectic is, like all dialectic, an evasion. Time does not "be," for the same reason being does not "be" in §10's second thesis. It is the source of being's intelligibility, not a being. The rejection of dialectic is aimed at Hegel's treatment of time as the negation of negation, which Heidegger criticized in *Being and Time* §82.

Temporality is the unity of awaiting, retaining, and presenting, originally unifying itself in its temporalizing. We came upon this unity by going back from the nearest graspable expressed time (then, back then, now), considering what is essential in it, namely that the then is a "then, when," that is, essentially determinable from some being. This character of time is called its datability. In the "then, when," a pointing-on happens in the manner of pointing beyond over beings, which can themselves occupy a when and so date the then. If this pointing beyond belongs to expressed time as an essential structure, it must bring it along from original temporality. How can this pointing-beyond be made visible in original temporality? Datability is the trace of transcendence in world-time. The then "points beyond" to beings because original temporality has already leapt over beings.

Starting again from the then as awaiting: comportment toward what we say will be "then" is, for example, hoping, fearing, or expecting. These are called intentional comportments toward the future. Hoping is not fearing, and expecting is not necessarily hoping or fearing and can itself show various modifications: tense impatience, indifferent letting-come. But none of these comportments would be possible, this directing oneself toward what will somehow be "then" would have no open direction at all, if Dasein, which hopes, fears, and so on, did not as Dasein as such stretch itself into something then-like, quite apart from what may then come toward it from there. What was called awaiting is nothing other than the "rapture" (*Entrückung*) into the then-like that underlies those comportments, which has already leapt ahead beyond all possible beings of which we can and must say they are "then." Because the then and every particular then is essentially only the expression of this awaiting that raptures itself in advance beyond all beings in possibility, the then, corresponding to this rapture, is in its own structure pointing over, datable. Intentional comportments toward the future (hope, fear) presuppose an openness toward the future as such, a rapture that is not directed at any particular future being. This is the temporal form of the founding of intentionality in transcendence (§9b).

Conversely, awaiting is, as we say, ecstatic. The ecstasis named here, stepping out of oneself (*ekstasis*), is a kind of *raptus*: Dasein does not become awaiting gradually by running through, one after another, the beings that factically come toward it as future; this running through only runs gradually through the open lane that the *raptus* of temporality itself has cut. The same holds correspondingly for retaining and presenting, so these three basic phenomena are called the ecstases of temporality. Temporality itself is the ecstatic unity unifying itself in ecstatic temporalizing. The image of the "open lane" (*offene Gasse*) is exact: the future is not assembled from anticipated events; events are anticipated within an openness already cut. This corresponds to world as the whole within which beings enter.

Awaiting means a being-ahead-of-oneself; it is the basic form of being-toward-oneself, more precisely, it first makes this possible at all. Awaiting means understanding oneself from one's own ability-to-be, one's own ability-to-be again understood in its essential metaphysical breadth, to which being-with and being-with-things belong. Awaiting my own ability-to-be as mine, I have already come toward myself in and through awaiting. This coming-toward-oneself from one's own possibility, lying in the being-ahead, is the primary ecstatic concept of the future (*Zukunft*, coming-toward). Heidegger illustrates the structure, as far as possible, with a diagram, the question mark signifying the horizon that remains open. The etymology *Zu-kunft* as "coming toward" is that of *Being and Time* §65. The future is primary because the for-the-sake-of, which characterizes world, is futural: Dasein comes toward itself from its possibility.

This coming-toward-oneself does not extend to a momentary present of oneself but to the whole of one's having-been (*Gewesensein*). More precisely, and this is the thesis, this having-been temporalizes itself only from and in the future. Having-been is not a remainder of oneself left behind for itself, nor what Bergson likes to demonstrate in various images: the future unrolls as it were while the past rolls up on another spool. Such a representation would be right insofar as it indicated the immanent connection of future and having-been, but misleading because what has been is not something remaining for itself, an accumulating ballast I drag behind me, to which I could relate this way or that; my having-been "is" only according to the way the future temporalizes itself, and only in it. What has been is no longer what is present, and so one might conclude vulgarly that nothing can be changed about it, it is done. Not so; the having-been of what has been becomes having-been first and constantly in each future. The very fact that we say we cannot get rid of the past already shows a particular mode of our having-been. For the essence of temporality this expresses that the ecstasis of the future, as coming-toward, extends immediately, without break, and primarily into having-been. The priority of the future over the past is the thesis of *Being and Time* §65. The past is not a fixed deposit but is taken up anew in every futural projection, which is why "we cannot get rid of the past" is itself a way of having-been, not a report on a fixed fact.

For certain reasons the prevailing mode of having-been is forgetting, which creates the appearance that what has been "is" no longer there and that Dasein, understanding itself from its ability-to-be, could come back to itself only as what is momentarily present, which has just slammed the door on having-been behind it. But slamming the door is itself a way of temporalizing having-been, which brings what has been into being in its way. And only in the ecstatic unity of future and having-been does presenting temporalize itself. This basic kind of temporalizing emerges when temporality is interpreted in itself. The slamming door returns from the earlier example as an image of forgetting. Forgetting is not the absence of the past but a mode of it, and presenting arises from the unity of future and having-been.

If, on the contrary, like the vulgar and traditional interpretation, one starts from expressed time, the nearest is always the present: here is the now, and no-longer-now and not-yet-now are the two arms by which time as now stretches into non-being. This image and the analysis guided by it become inevitable once one overlooks the ecstatic character of temporality and does not ask about the unity of temporality as one that temporalizes itself ecstatically. The traditional priority of the now is explained, not dismissed. From expressed time, past and future appear as non-being, which is the Aristotelian aporia of *Physics* IV, 10.

This wards off an obvious misunderstanding, namely thinking that temporality is a threefold rapture, but such that the three ecstases somehow converge in a substance, as a living being can stretch out feelers in different directions and draw them in again. The whole of the raptures does not center in something that is for itself free of rapture, unecstatically present, the common center for the start and departure of the ecstases. The unity of the ecstases is itself ecstatic. They need no bearer and no pillars, like the arch of a bridge; if one may speak of the "being" of the ecstases at all, one must say that their being lies precisely in the free ecstatic swing. Bergson addresses this phenomenon with his "élan," and saw something essential here too, but he transferred it too quickly and in a general metaphysical way to all the different formations of beings, without attending to the ecstatic structure of time and its horizonal character. The élan thus has only an ontic character and is directed, as it were, forward. The rejection of a substantial center contrasts with Leibniz's monad in §5c, where Heidegger criticized residual substantialism, force as a core endowed with perception and appetite. Temporality has no core.

Temporalizing is "the free oscillation (*Schwingung*) of the original whole of temporality; time swings itself out and swings itself back" (p. 268). And only because there is swing is there throw, facticity, thrownness; only because there is oscillation is there projection (compare the problem of time and being indicated in *Being and Time*). The play on *Schwung*, *Wurf*, *Entwurf*, *Geworfenheit* (swing, throw, projection, thrownness) derives thrownness and projection, the two poles of Dasein's being, from temporality's swing. The reference to "time and being" points to the unwritten Division Three.

For a first grasp of the unity of temporality, it is essential to exclude the idea of anything thing-like, present, lying between having-been and future; but equally one must not smuggle in a personal center, an ego-core, and the like; the essence of time lies in the unified ecstatic oscillation. To this peculiar unity of time belongs the unity of its horizon. The exclusion of the ego-core applies against Husserl's transcendental ego and Scheler's person. The unity of temporality is not held by a subject; it is the self-unifying of the ecstases.

On the second point: what is meant by the horizonal character of the ecstases? By horizon we understand the circle of the field of vision. But horizon, from *horizein*, is not primarily related to looking and intuiting; it simply means what bounds, encloses, the enclosure. And the ecstases are no knowing about, no consciousness, still less a seeing. The etymology is chosen to free the horizon from the model of vision criticized in §11b.

Each ecstasis encloses itself, precisely as *ekstasis*. One might think the reverse is the case: rapture is precisely the leap over every barrier. Certainly, nothing determinate lies in awaiting as such; for itself it cannot and never can decide unambiguously what can be expected on its basis and how. Yet the rapture as such gives itself something beforehand: the futural as such, futurity in general, that is, possibility pure and simple. The ecstasis does not produce from itself a particular possible but the horizon of possibility in general, within which a particular possible can be expected. Note that the ecstasis leaps over every being, and the horizon is not located in the sphere of the subject. Because it represents no particular being, the horizon is nowhere; it is neither spatially nor temporally localized in the ordinary sense. It does not "exist" at all but temporalizes itself. The horizon shows itself in and with the ecstasis; it is its "ecstema" (formed analogously to *systēma* from *systasis* or *synthēma* from *synthesis*). Corresponding to the unity of the ecstases in their temporalizing, the unity of the horizons is original. "Ecstema" is Heidegger's coinage for the correlate of an ecstasis, as the product of a process is named by the *-ma* ending. The horizon of the future is possibility as such, which connects temporality with §11c's thesis that possibility originates in freedom.

This ecstematic unity of the horizon of temporality is nothing other than the temporal condition of the possibility of world and its essential belonging to transcendence. Transcendence has its possibility in the unity of ecstatic oscillation. This oscillation of the self-temporalizing ecstases is as such the swinging-over, seen with regard to all possible beings that can factically enter a world. The ecstematic temporalizes itself, oscillating, as a worlding; only insofar as something like ecstatic oscillation temporalizes itself as a temporality does world-entry happen. The identification completes the chain: world is the ecstematic unity of the horizons of temporality, and "worlding" is the temporalizing of this unity. The verb *welten* from §11b is now given its temporal meaning.

World-entry is grounded in the temporalizing of temporality. "That there is something like temporality at all is the primal fact (*Urfaktum*) in the metaphysical sense." The world-entry of beings is primal history (*Urgeschichte*) pure and simple. From this primal history a problem domain must be unrolled which we are now slowly beginning to approach with greater clarity: the mythical. The metaphysics of myth must be understood from this primal history, with the help of a metaphysical construction of primal time, the time that begins with primal history itself. The "primal fact" is not deducible, which is the metontological element: that temporality temporalizes is a fact about beings as a whole. The remarks on myth, following §10's eighth thesis, refer to Cassirer's *Philosophy of Symbolic Forms* II (1925), which Heidegger reviewed in 1928; myth is understood as the mode of Dasein closest to the primal event of world-entry.

The aim was to point to the inner possibility of transcendence through the ecstatic and horizonal constitution of time as temporality. How transcendence is to be determined more concretely can be exposed only from the phenomenon of care. It would have to be shown how facticity and individuation are grounded in temporality, which as temporalizing unifies and individuates itself in the metaphysical sense, as the principle of individuation. This individuation is the presupposition for the original *commercium* between Dasein and Dasein. "Care" is the structural whole of *Being and Time*, whose meaning is temporality. The principle of individuation connects with §5c, where the monad individuated itself by unifying from a point of view; *commercium* is Leibniz's and Kant's term for interaction between substances.

"Now you see a certain correspondence to Leibniz's monadology, but also the difference" (p. 270). The interpretation of the monadology was already guided by the interpretation of Dasein as temporality, above all by insight into the essence of transcendence. The interpretation given was deliberately exaggerated in two respects: first, insofar as the basic determinations of the monad, representation and appetite, were taken as intentional structures, and second, insofar as these were made clear as transcendental structures, in their relatedness to universe, "world," which is why the monad can be determined as a concentrated world. The essential difference between Leibniz's interpretation of the monad and Heidegger's interpretation of Dasein as temporality is this: in Leibniz the effect of the genuinely metaphysical sense of his conception is prevented by his basing his conception of the monad, the I, in principle on Descartes's *ego cogito*, and taking the monad as a substance enclosed in its sphere, only incorporating the whole world into this immanence and its content. Hence Leibniz can say the monads need no windows, because they already have everything inside. "We would say, conversely: they have no windows, not because they have everything inside, but because there is neither an inside nor an outside, because temporalizing (the drive) means in itself the ecstatic happening of world-entry, insofar as transcendence is already in itself the possible leap over the possible beings that can enter a world" (p. 271). So time is no concentrated world but, conversely, essentially an opening and un-tensing (*Ent-spannen*) into a world. Heidegger will not pursue the comparison further, above all not the question whether, as the monadology is laid out as an interpretation of the totality of beings, the interpretation of Dasein as temporality could be conceived universally-ontologically, "a question I myself cannot decide, which is still completely obscure to me." The passage is the key to the whole course. It confirms the reading proposed at the head of this commentary: the interpretation of §5 was a deliberate retrieval, reading Dasein's temporality into the monad. The difference lies in the capsule. The monad contains the world within; Dasein is outside in world, since there is no inside. "Windowless" becomes a characterization of transcendence, not of enclosure. The closing admission, that Heidegger cannot decide whether temporality could be a universal ontology like the monadology, marks the limit of fundamental ontology that the appendix to §10 had named as the turn to metontology.

It must now have become clearer how world can be said to be a nothing. What kind of *nihil* is it? Insofar as we deal with it at all, make it a problem, and try to show it to be essential to transcendence, it must be something. If it is a *nihil*, it is no *nihil negativum*, the simple, absolute empty negation of something. World is nothing in the sense that it is no being. No being, and yet something that "there is" (*es gibt*). The "it" that gives this non-being is itself not a being but self-temporalizing temporality. And what this temporalizes as ecstatic unity is the unity of its horizon: world. "World is the nothing that temporalizes itself originally, that which springs forth in and with temporalizing pure and simple; we therefore call it the *nihil originarium*" (p. 272). The title of §12 is now explained. The *nihil originarium*, original nothing, is the nothing that is the source, as opposed to the *nihil negativum*, the nothing of contradiction. The *es gibt* ("there is," "it gives") is given its subject: temporality. This is the direct precursor of the "it gives" of *Time and Being* (1962) and of the nothing of *What Is Metaphysics?*

The origin (*origo*) of transcendence is temporality itself, such that with temporalizing, transcendence, and that is, world-entry, already happens. Only with the temporalizing of temporality, with the happening of world-entry, is there time in the vulgar sense, and only insofar as world-entry happens and intraworldly beings become manifest for Dasein are there also intratemporal beings, those that run their course "in time." The derivation of vulgar time and intratemporality from temporality through world-entry completes the account begun with the watch.

The peculiar inner productivity of temporality thus shows itself, in the sense that the product is a peculiar nothing: world. Kant first came upon this original productivity of the "subject" in his doctrine of the transcendental productive imagination. He did not succeed in exploiting this insight in its radical scope, for with it he would have had to bring his own edifice to collapse. On the contrary, this great intuition was basically lost again. Nevertheless, this first advance to the transcendental imagination, which for him stands in an obscure connection with time, is "the first moment in the history of philosophy in which metaphysics tries to free itself from logic," a logic that itself has never found its own essence but remained an external, formalized schooling. But this moment passed. Perhaps the genuine happening in the history of philosophy is always only the temporalizing, at wide intervals and in throws, of such moments, which never become manifest for what they really are. This is the thesis of *Kant and the Problem of Metaphysics*, which Heidegger was then writing: the transcendental imagination is original time, and Kant "shrank back" from it in the second edition. Its connection with the course's theme is stated outright: Kant's discovery was the first attempt of metaphysics to free itself from logic, which is the course's program. The remark about moments (*Augenblicke*) applies the temporal structure to the history of philosophy itself.

This productivity of the subject (taken in the transcendental sense) has, as always when something is philosophically central, already come forth somewhere in all genuine philosophy. As an example Heidegger names Heraclitus (fragment 115): "to the soul belongs a *logos* that increases itself." Dasein is the being that enriches itself from itself in the manner of understanding. The original inner possibility of enrichment lies essentially in Dasein; it always has the character of being-richer-than, of swinging-over. Heraclitus's self-increasing *logos* is read as the swinging-over of transcendence: Dasein's understanding always exceeds what is actually given. The fragment closes §12 on the note of excess (*Überschwung*) with which §11c defined Dasein's relation to beings.

##### §13. Transcendence Temporalizing Itself in Temporality and the Essence of Ground

Temporality temporalizes itself primarily from the future. The ecstatic whole of temporality, and thus the unity of the horizon, is primarily determined from the future. This is the metaphysical expression of the fact that world, grounded in nothing other than the ecstatic wholeness of the horizon of time, temporalizes itself primarily from the for-the-sake-of. This for-the-sake-of is always the for-the-sake-of of the will, of freedom, of transcending being-toward-oneself. But this has the inner possibility of such coming-toward-oneself in the manner of binding only in the ecstatic temporalizing of original awaiting, in the future, in which, better, as which the recursiveness (*Rückläufigkeit*) of Dasein constitutes itself. The future, however, is by its essence no isolated or isolating ecstasis; the more originally futural, the more recursive temporality is, and in this way the constitution of the whole of temporality and the temporalizing of its ecstematic horizon take place. The opening joins §11 and §12: the for-the-sake-of (§11b) and the future (§12) are the same phenomenon seen from world and from time. "Recursiveness" names the coming-back that belongs to the future, the swing from possibility back to having-been, which is where ground will be located.

Transcending toward world, being-in-the-world, temporalizes itself as temporality and is possible only so. World-entry happens only when temporality temporalizes itself, and only then can beings manifest themselves as beings. Since this is possible only on the basis of the understanding of being, the possibility of the understanding of being must lie in the temporalizing of temporality. In and with the temporalizing of temporality and the world-entry of beings, these enter "into time": they are grasped and determined as intratemporal. Because the event of the world-entry of beings is the primal event and in its essence temporalizing, it is natural to carry out, even in prephilosophical comportment toward beings and in the preontological vulgar determination of being, this determination with regard to time. In the completely enigmatic and by no means accidental tendency to understand beings as intratemporal, extratemporal, and supratemporal, the metaphysical primal history of Dasein as temporality documents itself. As temporality, Dasein thereby sets itself the task of understanding itself in its temporalizing. The answer to §10's first "indication" arrives here. The division of beings into temporal, extratemporal, and supratemporal is not an accident of classification but a trace of the fact that the understanding of being is temporal.

In other words: metaphysics belongs to the nature of the human being. Human Dasein therefore has by its essence a pre-dilection (*Vor-liebe*) for metaphysics. We can also say that all existing is already a philosophizing. Philosophy, as has always been seen, cannot be derived from anywhere but must ground itself. This "itself" comes from the fact that philosophy belongs essentially to the self-hood of Dasein. This does not mean that a factical development of philosophy is absolutely necessary, just as, conversely, the business of philosophical scholarship or the appearance of literature calling itself philosophical does not guarantee the existence of philosophy, as distinct from philosophizing. "But everything genuine needs, in order to exist, the semblance." There is neither a philosophy in purity in itself nor a sophistry ruling alone; both belong together in each particular and variously possible historical "formation." Kant's *metaphysica naturalis* returns, now grounded in temporality. The hyphen in *Vor-liebe* reads "predilection" as a love that is "ahead," futural. The remark that genuine philosophy needs its semblance, sophistry, recalls the course of 1924–25 and §7's classification of the argument for the priority of logic as sophistical.

The path of philosophy's self-grounding is not pursued further; the intention here is another, though no less essential. The essential connection between transcendence and temporality has just been clarified in its main features. The surpassing of beings in all dimensions in transcendence is grounded in the ecstatic constitution of temporality. Surpassing toward world means nothing other than this: the ecstatic unity of temporality, as a unity of rapture, has a horizon temporalized primarily from the future, the for-the-sake-of: world. Transcending is being-in-the-world. The metaphysical clarification of transcendence was undertaken in order to uncover the essence of ground. The essence of ground became a problem because grounding and groundability belong to truth. Truth, rightly understood and taken universally, is the theme of logic. The clarification of the essence of ground, or the way to it, is to give not only insight into this essence but also to show that logic is nothing other than the metaphysics of truth. The summary retraces the chain from §8 backward: logic, truth, ground, transcendence, temporality. The course now returns along it.

What is the essence of ground? As important as, indeed more decisive than, a bare, seemingly reassuring, merely knowable answer is familiarity with the path of working it out, because the answer can be given only by walking and retrieving the path of the question. Nevertheless an answer will now be attempted, with all the reservations such an answer contains when taken as a definition and isolated thesis. The warning echoes the appendix to §10: understanding is in the accomplishment, not the result.

In asking about the essence of ground, one seeks not a particular ground for something but insight into what ground as such means, how ground as such is inwardly possible, and that always means metaphysically necessary. The question can also be formulated: why do we ask, not merely factically but essentially, as Dasein, about the why? Why is there anything like a why and a because? "Because Dasein exists, that is, because transcendence temporalizes itself!" Transcending is ecstatic being-toward-oneself in the manner of being-for-the-sake-of-oneself. "The for-the-sake-of, as the primary character of world, that is, of transcendence, is the primal phenomenon of ground as such" (p. 276). Because we are in the manner of transcending existence, of being-in-the-world, and this is temporalizing, we ask about the why. The answer to the course's question is here. Ground is originally the for-the-sake-of. The four concepts of ground (essence, cause, argument, motive) were listed in §8's introduction with motive last; the order is now reversed, and the "for the sake of which" is the origin of the others.

But this thesis is still open to misunderstanding. It does not mean that because we happen factically to be actual, we are also interested in our whence and whither. The question is where, metaphysically, this interest arises at all, which is always related to all beings, not only to ourselves. In the essence of existence as transcendentally determined, in the rapture of Dasein into the for-the-sake-of-itself, lies the origin of anything like ground. If the for-the-sake-of as such is the primal phenomenon of ground, it transcends all beings, according to all their different modes of essence and existence. The misunderstanding is anthropological: that we ask "why" because of our curiosity about ourselves. Heidegger insists the "why" extends to all beings, because the for-the-sake-of transcends all beings.

The for-the-sake-of is nothing free-floating; it temporalizes itself in freedom. Freedom, as ecstatic self-projection upon one's own ability-to-be, understands itself from this and at the same time holds it before itself as binding. It is thus the origin of anything like ground. "We can say quite lapidarily: the metaphysical essence of Dasein existing transcendingly is freedom. But freedom, as transcendental freedom, is freedom toward ground" (p. 276). Being free is understanding oneself from one's own ability-to-be; "oneself" and "own" understood not individualistically or egoistically but metaphysically, in the basic possibilities of transcending Dasein: the ability to be with others, the ability to be with what is present at hand, the factical existentiell ability to be toward oneself. "Freedom toward ground" (*Freiheit zum Grunde*) is the course's formula, reworked in *On the Essence of Ground* (1929) as "freedom is the ground of ground." Its three dimensions repeat those of §11c.

Understanding oneself from the for-the-sake-of means understanding oneself from the ground. In accordance with what was called the inner dispersal of Dasein (§10), this must already have multiplied itself into the basic possibilities of itself. The essence of ground differentiates itself into different kinds of "grounds" (for example the four causes) not because there are different beings, but because the metaphysical essence of Dasein as transcending has the possibility of first providing world-entry to different beings. And because Dasein transcends itself, it is itself groundable for its own self-apprehension in different ways according to the different possible directions, but never in only one. Factical Dasein as a being has various possibilities of ontic understanding and knowing (historical, biological, psychological, cosmological). But the multiplicity of possible grounding, or the diversity of possible understanding of Dasein, must be interpreted in itself as a multiplicity that belongs together. It must be shown how the essence of Dasein as factical requires different ways of questioning, knowing, grounding, and proving, not only with regard to itself but in the whole breadth of its possible transcendence. The multiplicity of grounds, Aristotle's four causes among them, is derived from Dasein's dispersal, not from the plurality of beings. The consequence for the sciences follows: the plurality of approaches to the human being (history, biology, psychology) is grounded in transcendence and must be understood as a unity, which is the program of the note on the sciences below.

This problem has a guiding thread only when it has become clear where the essence of ground as such is rooted: in the freedom of Dasein as freedom toward ground. Only this "is" and understands itself as the origin of binding. "Because the for-the-sake-of is the for-the-sake-of-itself that recoils back, freedom toward ground is essentially and necessarily the ground of ground" (p. 277). "Ground of ground" is the precise formula. Freedom grounds grounding because the for-the-sake-of returns upon Dasein itself, the "recursiveness" of the opening paragraph.

Hence we ask about the why in the form: why the why? It seems one could go on asking about the why of the why of the why, and so on. Such further questioning has the appearance of a radicalism that stops nowhere, but it is only an appearance, and really thoughtlessness. In the question about the why of the why, two whys are not simply formally connected in a way that could be formally iterated. The questioning why, the first why that asks about the second, is itself grounded in what is asked about, the why it puts in question. So in the end the questioning why is what is to be determined, and this determination is nothing other than the essence of the why asked about. The infinite regress is refuted exactly as the argument for the priority of logic was in §7. It has the appearance of radicalism, but the questioning is grounded in what it questions, so the regress circles back into the essence of ground rather than continuing formally. This is the "circle" of understanding, as the next paragraph says.

From the general clarification of the essence of ground, two domains of problems have already been set off: first, the clarification of the origin of the multiplicity of grounds, the forms of ground, the dispersal of ground; second, the interpretation of the essential recursiveness of ground into a ground (thrownness into itself). This recursiveness of ground, this reversal of Dasein, is closely connected with the first, as the "circle" in understanding shows (compare *Being and Time*). Neither group of problems is pursued here. Instead the essence of ground is clarified one step further, so as to reach the goal of the course: to make logic intelligible as the metaphysics of truth. "Thrownness into itself" connects ground with finitude: freedom grounds, but it finds itself already thrown into having to ground. This is the "abyss" of *On the Essence of Ground*, only indicated here.

The primal phenomenon of ground is the for-the-sake-of belonging to transcendence. Freedom, holding the for-the-sake-of before itself, giving it to itself, and binding itself in it, is freedom toward ground. Being free is understanding oneself from possibility. The analysis of the concept of world showed that the phenomenon of world contains what was called a surpassing. Freedom, as ecstatic being toward the possible, is thus in itself a swinging-over into possibilities. Since freedom (taken transcendentally) constitutes the essence of Dasein, Dasein as existing is by essential necessity always "further" than any factical being. On the basis of this swinging-over, Dasein is always above beings, as we say, but precisely so that it first experiences beings in resistance as that against which transcending Dasein is powerless. The powerlessness is to be understood metaphysically, as essential: it cannot be refuted by pointing to the mastery of nature, to technology, "which today rages like an unchained beast into the 'world'; for this mastery is the genuine proof of the metaphysical powerlessness of Dasein, which gains freedom only in its history" (p. 279). The pairing of excess and powerlessness is the finitude of transcendence. Dasein surpasses beings toward possibility and for that reason experiences them as resistant, beyond its power. The remark about technology is one of Heidegger's earliest on the subject: technological mastery is not a counterexample to finitude but its expression, a reading that anticipates the later essays.

In a note Heidegger adds that the natural sciences and the human sciences are not two different groups of sciences distinguished by concept formation and mode of proof, or by the fact that one deals with hydrogen sulfide and the other with poems, but, as basic possibilities of the free confrontation of the metaphysical essence of Dasein with its world, they are in themselves one and the same. The target is the Neo-Kantian division of sciences (Windelband, Rickert) by method. Heidegger grounds both kinds of science in transcendence, which makes their unity prior to their difference.

Only because, in factical intentional comportment toward beings of every kind, we first swing over from possibilities and then come back and toward them, can we let beings themselves be what and how they are. Conversely, because transcending Dasein, as factically existing, is always already engaged with beings, and so, metaphysically, powerlessness toward beings is manifest with transcendence and world-entry, Dasein, which (metaphysically) can be powerless only as free, must hold to the condition of the possibility of its powerlessness: freedom toward ground. "And therefore we essentially place every being as a being under the question of its ground" (p. 279). "Letting beings be" (*sein lassen*) appears here, the formula of *On the Essence of Truth* (1930). The demand for grounding is derived from powerlessness: because beings resist, Dasein must ask why they are as they are, and it can ask because it is free.

We ask about the why in comportment toward beings of every kind because in us possibility is higher than actuality, because with Dasein itself this being-higher comes into existence. This being-higher of the possible over the actual exists only when temporality temporalizes itself. But if one sees in the temporalizing of temporality the being of what is more fully a being than other beings, then Aristotle's thesis holds: "actuality is prior to possibility" (*Metaphysics* Θ 8, 1049b 5), precisely because possibility is higher than actuality. *Being and Time* §7 had said, "higher than actuality stands possibility." Heidegger reconciles this with Aristotle's priority of *energeia*. The actuality that is prior is the temporalizing of temporality, the event in which possibility is higher. The paradox is deliberate: the most actual is the happening of the priority of the possible.

By its metaphysical essence Dasein is the one who asks why. The human being is primarily not the "No-sayer" (as Scheler said in one of his last writings), nor the Yes-sayer either, but the why-asker. Only because he is this can and must he say not merely yes or no, but essentially yes and no. The apparent harmlessness of traditional school logic under the title of positive and negative judgment moves at bottom in this dimension. Scheler's *The Place of Man in the Cosmos* (1928) defines the human being as the one who can say No to reality. Heidegger places the "why" before affirmation and negation, and so derives the logical division of judgments into affirmative and negative from the question of ground, bringing the course back to the theory of judgment with which it began.

##### §14. The Essence of Ground and the Idea of Logic

Logic, it was said at the beginning of the course, is knowledge of the *logos*, of the assertion, whose basic character is truth. It then emerged that propositional truth is primarily founded on comportments without the character of assertion, such as intuiting. These have the character of disclosing and, as ontic truth about beings, are grounded in the understanding of being, in what makes the disclosability of beings possible. That is world-entry, the happening of a being-in-the-world.
