Understanding of being is transcendence; all understanding of being, whether unthematically preontological or thematically, conceptually ontological, is transcendental. This understanding of being and its essential basic modes is the disclosing that lies in the ecstatic unity of temporality, in the temporalizing breaking open of horizons. This disclosing is metaphysically original being-true, the truth that transcendence itself is: *veritas transcendentalis*. It is the condition of the possibility of every ontic-intentional truth. The scholastic *veritas transcendentalis*, the truth of being as a transcendental property convertible with *ens*, is retrieved as the truth of transcendence. The chain of §9 is now complete: propositional truth, founded in uncovering, founded in the disclosedness of being, which is temporality.

With the happening of transcendence, of transcendental truth, beings are already uncovered, although precisely from the essence of truth as such and from the metaphysical beginning of the happening of transcendence it shows itself that initially and for a long time beings are concealed, and that truth, with reference to this primary concealment, must be called un-concealment. Beings are absolutely concealed as long as no world-entry happens. A deep insight therefore lies in the Greek word for truth, *a-lētheia*. Beings must first be torn from concealment; it must be taken from them, and it is taken through temporalizing temporality's giving the occasion of world-entry. That this interpretation of the peculiarly privative, negative character of the Greek concept of truth is no etymological game can be shown everywhere in Presocratic philosophy and in Plato and Aristotle. As evidence, one sentence of Heraclitus suffices (fragment 123): *physis kryptesthai philei*, beings in themselves and their essence love to conceal themselves, to remain in concealment. The "negativity" of *a-lētheia* announced in §9a is now explained. Unconcealment is privative because beings, apart from world-entry, are concealed. Truth is wrested from concealment by the happening of world, and the negativity is the *nihil originarium* of §12 seen from the side of beings.

Truth lies in the essence of transcendence; it is originally transcendental truth. If the basic theme of logic is truth, logic itself is metaphysics, if the problem of transcendence is, as Heidegger tried to show, the fundamental theme of metaphysics. The thesis of the course is now stated as a consequence: logic is the metaphysics of truth because truth is transcendental and transcendence is the basic theme of metaphysics.

In the traditional and today customary emphasis on the form of judgment as the center of logic, only this much is right: truth is thereby placed at the center. But then one must ask radically about the essence of truth. The point is not to convert metaphysics into logic or conversely; it is not a matter of distributing disciplines at all; the disciplines themselves are the problem. The last clause repeats the refusal of §7 and the appendix to §10. Heidegger is not proposing a new ranking of disciplines but questioning the frame in which disciplines are ranked.

Traditional logic is the science of asserting, of thinking, with the main aim of determining the laws of thinking and asserting. The character of these laws (natural laws, normative laws, or laws of quite another kind) remains uncertain. The usual laws in the usual order are the principle of identity, the principle of contradiction, the principle of reason. For Leibniz, because he sees the essence of truth in identity, the primal truth is identity, A = A. The recapitulation returns to §§2–3, where identity was the primal truth and the principle of reason was glimpsed as more original.

"Our thesis is: the first principle of logic is the principle of reason" (p. 282). This thesis is not simply a reversal of the traditional order; it is spoken from the radicalization of logic into metaphysics. The principle of reason is not a rule and norm of asserting; it is the first principle of logic as metaphysics. Its primary theme is transcendental truth, transcendence. What sense does the principle of reason have with regard to this, and what is the character of such "propositions" that we call principles (*Grund-sätze*)? The priority of the principle of reason over identity, glimpsed in §3 when Leibniz derived it from the definition of truth, is now asserted outright. It is justified not by reordering rules but by grounding logic in transcendence.

These questions can be answered only with regard to the essence of ground. The primal phenomenon of ground is the for-the-sake-of. The origin of "ground" lies in freedom as freedom toward ground. The for-the-sake-of is the primary character of world. World-projection in freedom is nothing other than the temporalizing of the understanding of being. If ground, as for-the-sake-of, is the primary character of world, and world is being understood in the understanding of being, which gives beings world-entry, lets them be understood as beings, then "ground" belongs essentially to being. From this follows the genuine metaphysical sense of the principle of reason: "The basic character of ground as such belongs to the essence of being as such" (p. 283). Only from this follows, in several stages of formulation, the rule that asserting about beings, because it asserts about beings disclosingly, must ground itself. The metaphysical sense of the principle confirms and grounds what the Second Main Part's opening asserted from the vulgar formula: "ground belongs to being." The logical rule, that assertions must be grounded, is derived from this in stages, which answers the question of §8 about how a demand can follow from the essence of truth.

The nexus of grounding in the positive sciences is formed primarily not by the chains of proof of ontic argumentation; these have their foundation in ontology. Here, in the understanding of being, lie the primary grounds, more precisely, the tendency toward grounding. Because science is ontic (deals with beings), it must ground. Sciences ground because their objects are beings, and beings, as manifest, stand under the question of ground. Their proofs rest on an ontological projection of their region, the thesis of *Being and Time* §3 on the regional ontologies that found the sciences.

Once the metaphysical content of the principle of reason has been brought out transcendentally, it can easily be read off afterward from the vulgar formula, "nothing is without reason": every being has its reason. Every being has its ground; that means: because ground belongs to the being of beings, beings, insofar as they disclose themselves as beings, must ground themselves. But "ground" is here conceived much more broadly and radically than the traditional concept of *ratio*. The vulgar formula is not false. It is read now as a statement about the being of beings, not about the causes of events or the justification of beliefs.

This also yields the propositional character of this principle. It is the first principle (*Grund-satz*) pure and simple, because it is the principle of ground. All principles are grounded in the principle of reason, in quite different ways, not merely because they share its formal character. Above all one must not think of a linear deduction of the other principles from it (as Fichte tried with the principle of identity). If these principles are grounded in the principle of reason, it means that they too can be interpreted only from the ground of ground, from freedom, and that is: from temporality. The fourth question of the Second Main Part's opening, what "principle" means for the principle of reason, is answered: it is the principle of principles because ground is what makes anything a principle. The rejection of Fichte's deduction from A = A excludes a system of logic built linearly from one axiom, since each principle must be interpreted from the temporal ground.

In the first characterization of the genuine meaning of the principle of reason in Leibniz, it was shown that the vulgar formula suppresses something essential: the "rather than." A character of preference lies in the principle of reason. What could now be shown positively about the essence and origin of ground from freedom, from transcendence, indicates how far preference does belong to the essence of ground. For this "rather" is only the expression of the surpassing character of world, of the swinging-over of freedom into possibility. How the "rather" character of ground looks concretely, and how the modes of ground, grounding, and founding are multiplied accordingly, requires difficult considerations. The *potius* of §8's introductory section is explained. Ground always selects "this rather than that" because it arises from freedom's projection into a space of possibilities that exceeds the actual. The theological answer of Leibniz, God choosing the best, is replaced by the structure of transcendence.

Ground belongs essentially to the essence of being. With concrete insight into this metaphysical connection, Heidegger has only led his hearers back to where Plato stood when he wrote the sentences of the *Republic* (VI, 509b 6–10) with which he closes: "So you must say that for what is known, not only is its being known present by virtue of the good, but its being and what-being are also assigned to it by that, though the good is not *ousia*, but still beyond *ousia*, surpassing it in dignity and power." Heidegger's glosses: the good provides beings not only with knownness and thus world-entry, but being and what-being are assigned to beings by it. "The for-the-sake-of, however (transcendence), is not being itself, but what surpasses it, and does so by swinging over beings in dignity and power" (p. 284). The course ends where §8's introductory section located the *epekeina*: the good beyond being, interpreted as the for-the-sake-of and so as transcendence. The final gloss distinguishes transcendence from being, as what surpasses and gives it. The course thus ends with the ontological difference in Platonic form, and the claim to have "only led back" to Plato is the retrieval of the tradition that the appendix to §10 called fundamental ontology's task.

### Appendix: Distance and Nearness

"To philosophize means to exist from the ground." Philosophy is neither one science among others nor a production of worldview; it is more original than every science and at the same time more original than every worldview. What matters is only that we meet its demands in the right way, that in philosophizing we always also transform everything and everyone in ourselves and toward ourselves. As long as we waver externally in the doubling of theoretical propositions and practical maxims, we are not yet in philosophy. Logic and metaphysics are grounded in the understanding of being, which is determined by the ontological difference, and this seems to us abstract, dry, and empty; and yet we must ask: what is the understanding of being? The opening sentence joins the results of §§13–14 with the theses of §10 on worldview and science. Philosophy is a way of existing "from the ground," from freedom, and the rejection of the "doubling" of theory and practice repeats §11b's thesis that transcendence precedes the split between *theōria* and *praxis*.

Freedom toward ground is the oscillating in the swinging-over, in what carries us away and gives us distance. The "swinging-over" of §§11c and 12 is now named as distance. Transcendence removes Dasein from beings toward possibility, and this removal is distance.

"The human being is a being of distance!" And only through genuine, original distance, which he forms in his transcendence toward all beings, does true nearness to things begin to rise in him. And only the ability to listen into the distance temporalizes the awakening of the answer of those human beings who are to be near him. The course ends with the paradox of distance and nearness. Nearness to things and to others is possible only because Dasein, surpassing all beings, has distance from them. This is the existential form of the ontological theses: beings manifest themselves "in themselves" only through world-entry, which requires surpassing (§11c), and the other can be a "you yourself" only for a being that can hear a self, which requires selfhood (§11c). "Listening into the distance" names a relation to others that respects their being-other, the opposite of the capsule model and of the fusion of I and Thou. The appendix gives the course's metaphysics its existential form, and in doing so it performs the "turn" that the appendix to §10 described, from the analysis of being back to the factical existence of human beings among beings and among one another.
